Polymarket : Autopsie d'un délit d'initié sur la réintégration de Jensen Huang chez NVIDIA
[!NOTE] Pourquoi cet article et cette analyse ?
Depuis quelque temps, je constate que Polymarket devient un véritable terrain de jeu pour les délits d’initiés, et le climat politique autour de la nouvelle administration Trump rend ce type d’asymétrie d’information encore plus propice. J’ai donc développé un algorithme qui surveille en permanence les marchés les plus sensibles : chaque transaction y est analysée par des modèles statistiques et des outils de traçage de flux on-chain.
J’ai finalisé l’algo il y a deux jours à peine. Dans la nuit même, en voyant l’alerte se déclencher, j’ai mené une première analyse à chaud sur les transactions… avant de voir tomber quelques heures plus tard le tweet de Donald Trump confirmant l’asymétrie. Et vous allez voir que le communiqué officiel de NVIDIA n’était que la cerise sur le gâteau.
Cette enquête documente, à la minute et au hash près, un cas assez probant de délit d’initié sur Polymarket détecté en temps réel.
Question principale
Y a-t-il eu un délit d’initié sur le marché « Will Jensen Huang rejoin NVIDIA by December 31, 2026? » ?
- Le timing : Une accumulation de liquidité achevée en pleine nuit Transactions Polygon, exactement 89 minutes avant la prise de position publique de Donald Trump sur Truth Social ;
- L’asymétrie de gain : 4 648 USDC engagés sur une probabilité marginale de 6,5 %, dénoués en 71 518 USDC suite à la confirmation unanime du Conseil d’Administration de NVIDIA sur Polymarket ;
- Le profil du portefeuille : Un compte jetable Gnosis Safe Proxy créé 81 heures auparavant, dont l’activité est 100 % monothématique.
Question secondaire : En quoi les marchés de prédiction décentralisés constituent-ils désormais le sismographe le plus implacable pour révéler publiquement des délits d’initiés invisibles sur les bourses traditionnelles ?
Réponse courte (avant d’entrer dans le détail)
Sur le strict plan des données on-chain, l’hypothèse du hasard est statistiquement disqualifiée. L’acheteur anonyme opérant sous le pseudonyme ljhggk a méthodiquement balayé le carnet d’ordres jusqu’à 0,065 $, verrouillé sa position avant toute réaction de marché, puis encaissé passivement les deux catalyseurs successifs pour réaliser un gain net de +66 869,34 USDC (+1 438,5 % de ROI).

1 · L’Anatomie du trade : 71 518 parts OTM raflées en pleine nuit
Entre 02:19:11 et 03:28:20 CET (01:19 - 02:28 UTC) dans la nuit du dimanche au lundi 23 novembre 2026, le carnet d’ordres du marché Will Jensen Huang rejoin NVIDIA by December 31, 2026? était totalement assoupi. La probabilité implicite stagnait à 6,5 %, reflétant un consensus quasi-unanime : suite à sa démission du 12 novembre, un retour de Huang avant la fin de l’année paraissait exclu.
C’est à cet instant précis qu’un acteur unique déploie une stratégie d’absorption agressive, découpée en 4 ordres limités successifs :
Le relevé des 4 transactions sur Polygon
Chaque transaction a été retracée. Les 4 transactions ont été incluses dans les blocs sans rejet Registre complet :
[!NOTE] Verdict technique de l’exécution : L’opérateur n’a pas étalé ses achats sur 24 heures pour moyenner son coût. Il a exécuté un achat massif et concentré en pleine nuit européenne/américaine, absorbant 34 % de toute la liquidité vendeuse disponible sur le contrat, ce qui pourrait montrer qu’il a eu l’info quelques heures avant et qu’il n’a pas spécialement eu le temps de l’étaler davantage. Dès son dernier ordre complété à 03:28:20 CET, il s’est totalement arrêté de négocier.
À noter : le compte s’était déjà positionné sur de petits montants sur ce pari dès sa création, mais sans jamais approcher de volumes comparables à ce qui a levé l’alerte cette nuit-là. Cette montée soudaine en conviction renforce encore la suspicion.
2 · La Reconstitution temporelle : 89 minutes d’avance sur le monde réel
Le cœur du dossier réside dans la corrélation temporelle entre la clôture de l’accumulation on-chain et la chaîne des déclarations publiques.

1. Le catalyseur politique : La publication de Donald Trump (+89 min)
À 04:57:00 CET (22:57:00 EST le 22 novembre), Donald Trump publie sur son réseau Truth Social :
« I hope the Board of Nvidia rehires Jensen Huang. It would be the best thing for the Company, and also, for the U.S.A. »
Il ne s’agit pas d’une injonction légale, mais le poids politique est massif : la mise à l’écart de Huang reposait sur des craintes de sécurité nationale. Le soutien explicite de Trump lève immédiatement le principal verrou psychologique. Le contrat bondit instantanément de 0,065 $ à 0,14 $ dans les échanges de la nuit.
2. Le catalyseur corporate : Le Conseil d’Administration de NVIDIA (+11h)
À 14:30:00 CET (08:30:00 EST), exactement une heure avant l’ouverture de Wall Street, NVIDIA diffuse son Communiqué officiel de réintégration. Le Conseil d’Administration confirme avoir voté à l’unanimité la réintégration immédiate de Jensen Huang au poste de CEO et Board Member, précisant avoir reçu l’assurance des plus hauts échelons du gouvernement américain quant à l’absence de risque sécuritaire.
Le contrat binaire explose vers 0,985 $, avant d’être résolu et encaissé à 1,00 $.
3 · L’Empreinte on-chain : Ce que révèle le profil du wallet
Dans le cadre d’un audit de délit d’initié (insider trading), l’analyse on-chain permet d’isoler des critères objectifs d’intentionnalité. Le compte ljhggk coche l’intégralité des marqueurs d’un portefeuille jetable (burner proxy) :
flowchart LR
  EOA["<b>Portefeuille personnel (EOA)</b><br/><code>0x3e0f9ada318fa8...</code><br/>Adresse signataire directe"] -->|Déploiement Safe| Proxy["<b>Gnosis Safe Proxy</b><br/><code>0xdf8616e18da2ad...</code><br/>Âge : 81h · Pseudo : ljhggk"]
  Proxy -->|100% Capital| Trade["<b>Marché Unique : NVIDIA</b><br/>4 648 USDC sur YES @ 6,5 ¢<br/>Zéro diversification"]
  Trade -->|Dénouement| Cashout["<b>Position Résolue</b><br/>71 518 USDC (+1 438%)"]
4 · D’où viennent les fonds ?
En inspectant l’adresse à l’origine de la création du compte Polymarket (0x3e0f9ada318fa8...) sur Polygonscan, on fait un constat surprenant : son historique affiche 0 transaction. Le wallet est totalement vierge et n’a jamais interagi directement avec le réseau Polygon.
Ce n’est donc pas ce wallet qui a directement fourni ou transféré les fonds on-chain.
Le rôle du pUSD et du bridge Polymarket
Sur Polymarket, le fonctionnement des soldes et des ordres repose sur une certaine mécanique :
- Aucun besoin de gaz sur le wallet personnel : L’utilisateur signe ses ordres d’achat hors-chaîne depuis son navigateur. C’est le relais officiel de Polymarket (Relayer) qui diffuse les transactions sur la blockchain et règle les frais de réseau à sa place.
- Le concept de pUSD (Polymarket USD) : Pour négocier sur le carnet d’ordres, la plateforme utilise son propre jeton interne indexé sur le dollar : le pUSD. Ce jeton n’est pas envoyé par un particulier, mais émis automatiquement par le protocole lors d’un dépôt.
- Le contrat de bridge / séquestre : Quand un utilisateur approvisionne son compte, les USDC réels sont déposés dans le coffre de séquestre de Polymarket (Collateral Vault). En contrepartie, le bridge de Polymarket crée (mint) les pUSD équivalents directement sur le contrat proxy unique de l’utilisateur (0xdf8616e1...).
- L’origine des capitaux : Les fonds engagés proviennent très probablement d’un retrait direct depuis une plateforme d’échange centralisée (comme Binance, Kraken ou Coinbase) vers l’adresse de dépôt assignée par Polymarket, d’un autre portefeuille externe via une passerelle cross-chain, ou d’un paiement bancaire direct via le partenaire d’intégration fiat (MoonPay).
Ce montage montre que l’acheteur a utilisé une adresse totalement vierge pour signer les ordres sans laisser d’antécédents directs, en faisant transiter sa liquidité directement par les infrastructures de dépôt de Polymarket.
Conclusion
Le délit d’initié sur Polymarket présente une rupture majeure par rapport aux marchés financiers traditionnels : toutes les traces sont publiques, horodatées et infalsifiables dès la première seconde.

US To Blacklist Asian AI Firm Megaspeed After Nvidia Scandal (bbc.com) 2
			
		 	
				The U.S. Commerce Department plans to blacklist Singapore-based AI infrastructure company Megaspeed and its subsidiary Speedmatrix over suspicions that they helped Chinese companies obtain restricted Nvidia AI chips. The move follows allegations that a former Megaspeed executive had an affair with Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang while working as a Chinese intelligence asset, although Commerce officials say their investigation into the company began nearly two years ago. The BBC reports:  The Department of Commerce announced it planned to add Megaspeed Ltd to a list of entities that are blacklisted from buying or selling goods in the US. The company is mainly a developer of data centers, but in recent weeks a former executive of Megaspeed was accused of working as spy for China while having a romantic relationship with Nvidia head Jensen Huang. [...] When exactly the ban against Megaspeed will go into effect was not specified in the commerce department announcement. The BBC has asked the agency for comment, along with the White House and Megaspeed.
Megaspeed was not well known within the US until last month when media reports emerged that a former Megaspeed executive, Alice Huang, had an affair with Jensen Huang. Alice Huang (no relation) was thought by US intelligence to be working as an asset for Beijing, creating national security concerns within the US. China has denied that she is a spy and warned the US not to use the scandal as a "pretext for imposing a technological blockade" on AI technology.

Worshipping at the Altar of Brendan Gregg
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
10:50:14 UTC
19:50:14 KST

Okay, I am the first to admit that I’m a bit manic with AI. When you are working on a problem that has endless possibilities, it is difficult to constrain yourself to a limited set of features.
Can I just add one more?
I have been amazed at all the fun and useful things that a deterministic VM like Rewind enables. I have been using it to help find heisenbugs, to make profile-guided optimization reproducible, and to make Nix builds bit-reproducible. But the most fun has been using it to understand performance.
Turns out that performance is yet another area where Rewind shines. I have been using it to analyze the critical path of builds.
What is the critical path?
The critical path is the longest chain of dependent work in a program. It is the minimum time that the program can take to complete, regardless of how many resources you throw at it. The only way to make a program faster is to shorten the critical path.
If you worship at the altar of Brendan Gregg, you know he spent years turning performance analysis from a dark art into a science. This is a small offering to him. 🤲
I added a new rewind time command to Rewind that analyzes a recording and tells you where the time went and what would make the run finish sooner. You can drill into any part of that answer and see the source lines responsible for it. It’s like Brendan Gregg’s flame graphs but on steroids for the critical path and with a debugger attached.11All the ideas for the command are either inspired by or directly influenced by Brendan Gregg’s work. Kneel before Zod! 
  Aside: New to Rewind? Start with a flaky build you only catch once and dafuq is a deterministic VM?
Let’s see how it works by analyzing the critical path of a libuv build and test run. The build and test suite takes 78 seconds inside Rewind, and even with 64 cores it still takes 47 seconds. Why?
The usual answer is to look at a CPU profile, and in this case a CPU profile would be almost useless. For two thirds of those 47 seconds, the work the build is waiting on is not computing at all: it is asleep. 💤💤💤
§Where the time goes
Every rewind time starts with two numbers:
$ rewind time 7a29e716
libuv-1.51.0
    78.0 s  elapsed on the VM's one CPU    47.4 s  critical path: the least it can take on any number of CPUs            67% asleep (epoll_pwait 23.1 s, clock_nanosleep 8.53 s, futex 100 ms), 33% computing
The first is how long the run took on the VM’s one virtual CPU. The second is the critical path: the longest chain of work where each step had to wait for the one before.
No number of CPUs makes a run shorter than its critical path, so the gap between the two numbers is all that parallelism can buy you.
The line under the two numbers applies Gregg’s thread state analysis. Here the biggest state is asleep: 23 seconds in epoll_pwait and 8.5 in clock_nanosleep.
A sleeping thread is invisible to a CPU profiler. Gregg’s answer is off-CPU analysis: look at where threads block, not only where they compute.
On a live machine, off-CPU analysis is hard. Linux can trace which thread woke which, and Gregg built wakeup and off-wake profiling on top of that, but you have to be tracing before the slow run happens, the tracing has a cost, and most of the sleeps you capture are noise. Rewind already logs every context switch because it needs them to replay, and now it records every wakeup and who caused it too. All that juicy information is in every recording. 🤤
Here is the whole output for libuv:
$ rewind time 7a29e716
libuv-1.51.0
    78.0 s  elapsed on the VM's one CPU
    47.4 s  critical path: the least it can take on any number of CPUs
            67% asleep (epoll_pwait 23.1 s, clock_nanosleep 8.53 s, futex 100 ms), 33% computing
The critical path by phase
    3.54 s  configurePhase  ██
    1.67 s  buildPhase      █
    42.0 s  checkPhase      █████████████████████
     99 ms  5 other phases
The critical path by process
   on path      pid  process, with what it started

    41.9 s     4076  ├─ make -j8 SHELL=bash-5.3p3/bin/bash VERBOSE=y check

    2.00 s     5013  │  │  ├─ lt-run-tests delayed_accept delayed_accept  asleep in epoll_pwait 2.00 s

    1.47 s              └─ 581 more
What would shorten it
    sooner      pid  if this took no time
    36.1 s           every lt-run-tests, 600 processes
    6.66 s           every cc1, 283 processes
    3.38 s     5412  75 sleeps in clock_nanosleep: lt-run-tests hrtime hrtime
    2.00 s     5013  2 sleeps in epoll_pwait: lt-run-tests delayed_accept delayed_accept
    1.76 s     4189  cc1 /build/source/test/test-fs.c
    1.62 s     5126  30 sleeps in epoll_pwait: lt-run-tests fs_event_watch_dir fs_event_watc…
    1.60 s     5103  16 sleeps in epoll_pwait: lt-run-tests fs_event_close_in_callback fs_ev…
    1.60 s     5508  lt-run-tests metrics_idle_time metrics_idle_time
    1.22 s           every perl, 9 processes
    1.14 s     5510  lt-run-tests metrics_idle_time_thread metrics_idle_time_thread
Look closer
  rewind time 7a29e716 --in PID             one process: its threads, sleeps and waits
  rewind time 7a29e716 --folded             the path for flamegraph.pl or speedscope.app
  rewind time 7a29e716 --perfetto F         every process on a timeline, for ui.perfetto.dev
The second table is the path as a tree of processes based on Gregg’s colony graph idea. Each line is a process with everything it started, and its time on the critical path.22A ps snapshot might miss short-lived process invocations; the recording has every fork, regardless of how brief. 
In this build, make check needs 41.9 of the total 47.4 seconds, and 36.4 of that is lt-run-tests, libuv’s test runner. The test runner invokes 287 tests each spending 1-3 seconds.
All of those tests are on the path, and the path is a single chain, so the runner ran them one after another. Worse, most of each test is spent asleep, waiting on timers.
Drag can be less than time on the path.
The last table answers a different question: how much sooner would the run finish if this part took no time? The critical path method calls this drag.
For the truly magical part, we can jump to any part of the path and see what was happening, invoke gdb and fork the run at that moment. A profiler hands you a stack, sampled at some moment you can never go back to. Every frame in rewind time is a moment in a recording, and Rewind can replay the run to it.
--in looks inside one process,33It can take a pid, a program or a word of the command line.  so let’s dive into the hrtime test that spent 3.38 seconds asleep in clock_nanosleep:
$ rewind time 7a29e716 --in hrtime
lt-run-tests hrtime hrtime (pid 5412)
    3.38 s  on the critical path with what it started, 7% of the 47.4 s path
            100% asleep (clock_nanosleep 3.38 s), <1% computing
What would shorten it
    sooner      pid  if this took no time
    3.38 s           75 sleeps in clock_nanosleep
Look closer
  rewind where 7a29e716 115894 --pid 5412   the source line the first row's time starts at  rewind time 7a29e716 --perfetto F         every process on a timeline
Since this test spent 100% of its time asleep, the step provided on the last line is where it began. rewind where replays the run to that step and asks gdb where the thread was:
$ rewind where 7a29e716 115894 --pid 5412
process 5412 (lt-run-tests), thread 5412, at step 115894
#2 uv_sleep (src/unix/core.c:1869)
    1867
    1868    do
>   1869      rc = nanosleep(&timeout, &timeout);    1870    while (rc == -1 && errno == EINTR);
    1871
arguments:
  msec = 45
locals:
  timeout = {tv_sec = 0, tv_nsec = 45000000}
  rc = <optimized out>
  __PRETTY_FUNCTION__ = <error: Cannot access memory at address 0x7fb172519ae8>
called from #3 run_test_hrtime (test/test-hrtime.c:39)
called from #4 __libc_start_call_main (../sysdeps/nptl/libc_start_call_main.h:58)
🎯 We can see that the test is calling uv_sleep(45) from line 39 of test/test-hrtime.c, and that it is sleeping for 45 milliseconds.
It does that 75 times for a total of 3.38 seconds.44Turns out that the test sleeps on purpose: it is checking how far uv_hrtime moves across a sleep. There is nothing to optimize in this case. 
As the cherry on top: the run is deterministic, so step 115,894 is the same instruction every time you replay it. You can open rewind gdb at that step and investigate to your heart’s content.
The Rewind app’s Time tab shows the same answers. The path becomes a flame graph, colored the way Gregg colors his off-CPU flame graphs: computing is warm, asleep is blue, and a process shades from one to the other by how much of its time it slept.
Clicking a frame picks it and moves the playhead there, so the build log, the process tree and the files all show that moment; clicking a process again zooms into it. When you pick a blue frame, the app replays the run to that part’s longest sleep and shows the stack where it slept:
Under the stack, Show source opens the source panel at that moment on that thread, and Open gdb here opens gdb in a fork of the run at the sleep:
If you’d rather use tools you already know, rewind time can emit the data in their formats: folded stacks for flamegraph.pl and speedscope, or a Perfetto trace for ui.perfetto.dev.
The flame graph below is the real flamegraph.pl output: click a frame to zoom in, or open it on its own.
§Kneeling at the altar
Every idea here predates Rewind. Gregg has been drawing off-CPU and wakeup flame graphs for over a decade, and build systems have reported critical path information potentially even longer.55Bazel’s critical path analysis is a great example of this, and it has been around for years. I have been inspired by his work since I first dabbled in eBPF, back when flame graphs were new. 🙇
What Rewind adds is the data. Lots of it.
Rewind has that data because it can’t replay without it. On a single CPU the only thing that changes from run to run is the scheduler, so every recording already logs every context switch.
This changes the economics of performance analysis. On a live machine, you have to trace before the run, and tracing has a cost. You can only trace a few runs, and you have to pick which ones. Rewind traces every run, so you can analyze any of them later, and do so exactly.
It has just been a manic fever joy to take ideas I have admired for years, point them at a deterministic VM, and watch them work in ways that are hard or impossible on a normal machine.
Please accept rewind time as my offering at the altar of Brendan Gregg.

Google Chrome dev team declares bug report bankruptcy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
09:56:08 UTC
18:56:08 KST

Wed, 18 Nov 2026
Google Chrome dev team declares bug report bankruptcy
[ I wrote this in August 2012 and then forgot about it. ]
About 18 months ago I filed a report for a bug in Google Chrome. It wasn't a very important bug. I reported that when Chrome was asked to inline an image, and the image turned out to be in the obscure and seldom-used X-bitmap format, it came up as a "broken image" icon (which isn't a bug) and failed to display the alt text from the img tag (which is).
I then forgot about it until this week, when I got a notice that the report was being closed because it was "obsolete". I was invited to resubmit the bug report if I considered it valid.
My first draft of this article continued politely "The Chrome dev team botched this in a number of ways." That draft came out boring, weak, and vague, so I'm going to try continuing a different way.
That was a really jerky thing to do. I put in my time and trouble to construct a clear bug report with an easy test case; the bug report contained a link to a page that demonstrated the problem. To see if the problem is still present, all you have to do is click the link in the bug report.
But after I spent my time and effort to submit this bug report, Chrome dev decided to ignore it. After they had ignored it for a year and a half, they decided to pitch it in the trash. But if I want, I can submit it again.
Now why on earth would I want to do that? Does Chrome think I enjoy assembling bug reports? Maybe I do it for fun? No. The bug report has no value to me. It was an expense. It was a donation to someone I thought would appreciate it.
Suppose my neighbor has a boat. Sometimes he lets me use the boat, but it leaks. One day he asks if I will help me fix the boat. I spend the day in his garage painting and sealing some new boards to put into the bottom of the boat. Some time later, I see he has put the new boards into the trash. "What did you do that for?" I ask. He shrugs. "I don't really care if the boat leaks," he says. "But if you want to come over and paint and seal some more boards, how about next week?"
Sorry, what am I supposed to get out of this transaction?
Chrome dev says they have 40,000 bug reports, too many to deal with. So what? Autoclosing them isn't dealing with them either; it's throwing them in the trash. If your project doesn't want to get bug reports, you don't have to solicit them.
Chrome says that a lot of the reports are useless. I'm sure that's true. Most people don't file very good bug reports. A few people do. Who gets pissed off when you throw all the reports into the trash? The people who put in the most effort, that's who. So if you're trying to adopt a strategy that will ensure that your next 40,000 reports are even worse, this is the strategy to adopt.
What should Chrome have done here? There is an easy thing and a hard thing.
The easy thing not to say "We threw away your report, but if you think it was a good one, please start over and resubmit it," which is likely to piss off anyone with any sense. Instead, you stick a button on the web site and say "We threw away your report, but if you think it was a good one, please click the button." I would not object to checking if the report was still valid, and clicking a button to reactivate it.
The harder thing that they should have done was to not close the reports at all. Managing bug reports is hard. Perl was years behind on bug reports for many years. Many of those reports were indeed obsolete. But there is no cost to keeping them around.
Addenda
20261119
- A Mastodon colleague was complaining today about a similar practice, and I wanted to refer him to this article, but couldn't because I had completely forgotten to publish it.
- JWZ complained about something similar in 2003.
20261120
Paul Irish dug up the original bug report that was the subject of this complaint. (And discovered that he himself had replied to it at the time!)

Jevons Paradox in AI: Cheaper Tokens Won't Pay for Capex
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
09:39:50 UTC
18:39:50 KST

Cheaper AI Tokens Won't Pay for $560 Billion of Capex
Updated 24 November 2026
Contents
Key takeaways
- Token volume and token prices are moving at roughly the same speed, so cheaper inference on its own does not grow AI revenue.
- The price of a fixed level of capability collapses each year, while the cost of running frontier models rises; the revenue sits at the frontier.
- Closing the gap between capex and revenue needs money that today pays for labor, and outcome pricing is how vendors are reaching for it.
- The capex and cash flow figures from March have grown worse: five hyperscalers spent $560 billion in the first three quarters of 2026.
In March I wrote that the hyperscalers’ answer to collapsing inference prices is Jevons: cheaper tokens create so much demand that total spending grows anyway.
On volume, they look right. Google processed 9.7 trillion tokens a month in May 2024. In May 2026 it was 3.2 quadrillion, 330 times as many. Amazon, Microsoft, Alphabet, Meta and Oracle spent $230 billion on capex in the third quarter of 2026 alone, almost four times what they spent two years earlier.
But tokens aren’t dollars. Revenue is price times volume, and the price is falling too. So my question is: does demand for tokens grow faster than the price of a token collapses?
The arithmetic
If the price of a token falls 10x and volume grows 10x, revenue is flat. Revenue only grows when volume grows faster than the price falls.
Google’s first-party model APIs went from 7 billion tokens a minute in Q3 2025 to nearly 32 billion in Q3 2026, up 4.6x. Google’s tokens across all products grew 6.7x between the I/O keynotes of 2025 and 2026. OpenRouter went from 0.5 trillion to 128 trillion tokens a week in 20 months, about 27x a year. On the price side, MIT researchers find that the price of reaching a fixed benchmark score falls 5 to 10x a year, and Epoch AI finds that cost per task at fixed performance falls about 13x a year.
Volume and price move at about the same speed. That proves nothing by itself, because Google’s tokens aren’t sold at fixed-capability prices (like the ones Epoch measures). But it lines up with what Mert Demirer, Andrey Fradkin, Nadav Tadelis and Sida Peng found when they used OpenRouter and Azure data to estimate short-run price elasticities “just above one” and concluded that this leaves “limited scope for Jevons-Paradox effects.” At an elasticity of one, a 10x price cut buys 10x the tokens for the same money.
That matches how a lot of buyers behave. Say a bank’s head of operations has $2 million approved for AI contract review, and then the vendor cuts its token prices tenfold. The bank can now review ten times as many contracts, which is great. The budget still says $2 million. To spend more, the head of operations would have to walk into the CFO’s office and argue that the product got cheaper and so deserves a bigger budget, and I wouldn’t want to make that case. So the bank spends the same $2 million. The vendor can say, truthfully, that token volume grew tenfold.
But I guess there’s also a counterargument. Cheaper tokens change what people build. When inference is nearly free, developers design agents that call a model hundreds of times per task, and a recent theory paper argues this can make demand rise faster than prices fall. That may happen.
Two price curves
“The price of a token” hides two curves that move in opposite directions. (1) The price of a fixed level of capability is falling. GPT-4-level answers cost a small fraction of what they did in 2023, and the decline continues. (2) The cost of using the best model available is rising. The same MIT paper finds that the cost of running frontier models on benchmarks rises 3 to 18x a year, because each generation is larger and reasons for longer.
A concrete case: to run the Artificial Analysis benchmark suite, Claude Opus 5.5 at maximum effort used 218 million output tokens, almost three times the median model, at about $10 per task. At low effort the same model used about a tenth of the tokens.
Spending probably follows the frontier curve (at least up until now). In enterprises, open-weight models now handle 19% of tokens but get 4% of spend, while frontier labs take 69% of deployment dollars. Cheap tokens and expensive tokens are close to two separate markets (the cheap one grows fast in volume and barely registers in revenue).
The volume is real
I don’t want to wave the demand away. Google’s token count is the clearest public series we have, and it’s steep.
Agents account for much of it. A paper by Bai, Brynjolfsson, Pentland and co-authors finds that agentic coding tasks use about 1,000 times more tokens than chat about the same code. Anthropic reported in 2025 that multi-agent systems use about 15 times more tokens than chat. OpenRouter dates February 6, 2026 as the last day humans used more tokens on its platform than agents did.
Where the money has to come from
If cheaper tokens alone don’t grow revenue, the money has to come from somewhere else. Bain estimates AI needs $2 trillion of annual revenue by 2030 to fund the compute being built, and that it will still be $800 billion short even if companies reinvest every dollar AI saves them in sales, marketing, support and R&D.
You can already see vendors reaching for that money in their pricing. Intercom charges $0.99 for each customer case its Fin agent resolves, a price set against the cost of a support agent’s time, whatever the tokens cost. Salesforce has changed its Agentforce pricing three times since 2024. The direction is clear. Vendors want to be paid per outcome, and an outcome is priced against a (human) wage.
Workers aged 22 to 25 in the jobs most exposed to AI saw a 13% relative drop in employment compared with less exposed peers. US employers cited AI in about 121,000 announced job cuts in the first ten months of 2026, about a fifth of all announced cuts.
Receipts
Spending grew faster than I expected when I wrote in March. Back then the five companies guided to $660 to $690 billion for 2026. Their SEC filings show $560 billion of cash capex in the first three quarters, with $230 billion in Q3 alone. That excludes finance leases, so the full figure is higher.
The cash flow squeeze I described has arrived. Oracle spent 174% of its operating cash flow on capex in its fiscal 2026 and Amazon 94% in 2025. Microsoft is at 63%, Meta at 60% and Alphabet at 56%, all well above where they were a few years ago.
Who keeps the money
So far the clearest winner is the company selling the equipment. Nvidia reported $116 billion of revenue at a 75% gross margin in its latest quarter, and its revenue has grown in step with hyperscaler capex for three years.
The model labs sit lower. Press reports put OpenAI’s gross margin at 33 to 39%, depending on the quarter. Anthropic’s S-1 shows about an 85% gross margin, but it books training compute under R&D, so the figure only measures the cost of serving existing models. Even so, it suggests the cost of serving tokens may be falling faster than their price.
Markets have priced the split. Since ChatGPT launched, the semiconductor index has roughly tripled while software stocks and the S&P 500 are both up about 70%. Chips have also fallen 41% from their June peak, so the market is less sure than it was in the summer.

I'm not excited about tech anymore
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
07:46:12 UTC
16:46:12 KST

I'm not excited about tech any more. For the longest time I was excited about new devices, new apps, new platforms—all of it. I've gradually lost interest. I don't think I've grown more cynical—I've always been a cynic. Tech is only interesting when it provides value and it only provides value by being useful. Most new tech isn't useful.
If you're a developer, you are—whether you want to or not—staring down incorporating AI into your workflow. Is it useful? It can be. It can also wreak havoc in your codebase and move your team's bottleneck from authorship to code review. Am I excited about AI? No, no, not at all. I'm not excited about an industry that's consumed the entirety of the internet to enrich a handful of people. I'm not excited about an industry that's building data centers wherever it can, sucking up water and power as it goes. I'm not excited about an industry dedicated to deskilling entire professional fields, to devaluing art and creativity, to flooding the internet with worthless slop, sludge, and spam.
I don't care about self-driving cars. I'd rather have folks in a position to make a living driving taxis. I'd rather we invested in public transit and infrastructure.
I don't care about a folding phone. I have an unremarkable phone with a display that works.
I don't want to be recorded in public by your goofy-looking glasses. I don't need VR—the real world is fascinating without a company mediating the experience.
I don't need crypto.1 Money is a store of value that you can exchange for goods and services. Crypto is a bad investment vehicle with a value totally untethered from reality.2
I don't care about your social platform or short-form video. I'd rather read a book or watch a movie. Your social platform is an echo chamber. Your video is unending and uninteresting.
I don't care about your AI search. Your AI search is worse than what it's replacing: it's less accurate, it takes more energy to run, and it provides no benefit to the publishers it draws upon to provide answers. I want to enter a query, get accurate results, and go to the page providing the answer.
Streaming platforms are now effectively cable, just delivered over a different medium. Streaming music means renting your library in perpetuity.
Appliances don't need an internet connection. Appliances don't need a subscription. They just need to work and work reliably. No refrigerator should require a firmware update.
Cars shouldn't require subscriptions.
I don't need to be tracked. I don't need personalized recommendations. I don't need to establish a relationship with a brand. If I need something, I'll do some research and buy it. Every ad, every tracker, every beacon is something new to block.
So, what am I excited about? Well, mostly, things that are boring and things that are stable. Open protocols, standards, and other things that have stood the test of time. I like email.3 I love RSS. I like APIs and integrations. I like decentralized social platforms. I love tools that do one thing and do it well. Examples? I've got those.
- Fastmail: they do standards-compliant email. It's performant and reliable, and they charge a reasonable price.
- Kagi: I search and I get relevant results. The price is fair.
- Navidrome: I can stream my own music and enrich it with plugins and complementary tools like AudioMuse-AI.
- linkding: I save a lot of links. I share some of those links. They all go through linkding. I archive what I save and store the archive in an S3 bucket.
- Obsidian: all of my notes and writing exist as files on my local disk. It's absurdly configurable and even supports alternative syncing methods. I have a theme that aligns with my site's design and a few custom plugins. It does exactly what it sets out to do, and more if you need it.
What else? ActivityPub, ATProto, CoreDNS, Headscale, NextDNS, bunny.net, DNSimple, and on and on.
Do one thing. Do it well. Be useful. Don't exploit people.

Russia May Launch Nuclear Weapon Into Orbit As Soon As Next Month
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Russia May Launch Nuclear Weapon Into Orbit As Soon As Next Month (arstechnica.com) 28
			
		 	
				Western intelligence agencies reportedly believe Russia could launch a nuclear-armed satellite into orbit as early as December, "suggesting the country's development of a satellite-killing weapon of mass destruction is more advanced than previously thought," reports Ars Technica, citing German media reports. Such a launch would violate the 1967 Outer Space Treaty, while a detonation could create radiation belts that damage satellites for years. From the report:  The orbiting nuclear weapon would threaten thousands of satellites in low-Earth orbit, including military and commercial communications networks like Starshield and Starlink. A nuclear-armed satellite would also put at risk Western military surveillance satellites and human spaceflight platforms like the International Space Station. A nuclear detonation in orbit would be indiscriminate, also knocking out Russia's own spacecraft and Chinese satellites.
Reporting from three German news outlets -- public broadcasters NDR and WDR and the Munich-based newspaper Suddeutsche Zeitung -- indicates Russia's launch of a nuclear-armed satellite could be imminent. The report says Western intelligence agencies, including the German Armed Forces and Federal Intelligence Service, believe Russia is preparing to deploy the weapon in space this year. Several NATO countries have exchanged their findings on the matter.
The nuclear-armed satellite, designated Sputnik-S, has been in storage in the greater Moscow area for years, the German news report said. German Space Command believes the satellite could be launched as early as December. Once in orbit, a nuclear weapon could loiter for months or years, ready for detonation on the command of Russian military forces. The Kremlin dismissed the report. "Given the number of qualifiers like 'allegedly' and 'possibly' -- not to mention the fact that the claims are attributed to some unspecified source -- they are hardly worth taking seriously," said Dmitry Peskov, spokesperson for Russian President Vladimir Putin.

2035 Came Early
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
06:30:33 UTC
15:30:33 KST

tl;dr trynix.dev boots GUI programs now. Try solitaire, GL gears, xeyes, a voice, the weather over curl, or Firefox.
I remember watching the talk The Birth & Death of JavaScript by Gary Bernhardt and falling in love with the whimsical yet dreadfully serious unfolding of the story of how JavaScript will come to dominate all software.
Others have noted the inevitability of JavaScript’s dominance, so perhaps it was not surprising that Atwood’s Law holds true.
  Atwood’s LawAny application that can be written in JavaScript, will eventually be written in JavaScript. — Jeff Atwood, 2009
I highly recommend watching the talk if you have not already seen it, but the short and skinny is a lookback from 2035 and how JavaScript came to be the defacto language for all software.
In Bernhardt’s retelling of events, the date is worked back from 2035, however unsurprisingly, large language models have rapidly sped up the timeline. What was once perceived to be the end-state at 2035 has arrived years earlier and you can witness it unfolding at trynix.dev.
One of the most impressive creations I’ve managed to build leveraging nixpkgs-multiverse is https://trynix.dev.
It still baffles my mind that I can run any Nix package, over 300,000 distinct package versions, straight from my browser. Truly insane capability. I was however a little dismayed at the speed of the QEMU emulation in the browser. It was slow enough that the creation tipped more towards novelty than practicality.
I woke on November 20th and saw Domen Kožar sharing a single message in our TacoSprint Discord channel: unkernel.dev
It was the fast browser Linux experience I had sought to achieve, but with Nix.
  Aside What is unkernel? It is Linux compiled to WebAssembly, allowing the kernel and system components to run at wasm speed while only user programs are emulated. It was published by Pooya Parsa on November 19th, 2026.
In an AI induced manic frenzy, I adopted the new engine along with a slough of long-wished features almost immediately.
The proof is in the pudding: unkernel delivers remarkable speed improvements over qemu-wasm,11With a very healthy set of patches applied to make it even faster. especially on first runs where kernel work dominates. The new engine is able to deliver 2x to 32x faster performance depending on the workload.22The benchmark page tracks both engines over time, measured through the real page and shell.
JavaScript & WebAssembly nirvana.
Amazingly not only does the kernel boot quickly, it also enables networking, full-fledged graphical and audio experiences directly in the browser.
Windows. A program’s Wayland surfaces are composited in the page by wland, and they float over the terminal you started them from.
GL. The VM includes a minimal Mesa build with only the virgl driver, compiled to WebAssembly, rendering through your browser’s WebGL2.
Sound. The VM has an ALSA sound card, and can play audio through your browser.
Network. curl, git clone and pip install work. Each TCP connection goes over one WebSocket to a Wisp relay.33Thank you to @_pi0_ for adding trynix.dev to his Wisp relay allow-list. 
The best joke in Bernhardt’s talk is a browser running inside another browser: the Windows GIMP under Wine, in Chrome, in Firefox. Of course, I had to try it with trynix. 😈
Here is Firefox in trynix, and it loading trynix.dev, and then loading Firefox again inside itself.44Do not be too overjoyed. It took more than an hour for this to render.
Sit and bask in that insanity. That is Firefox, on Linux compiled to WebAssembly, in a Firefox tab, loading the site and then loading Firefox again inside itself. 🤯
2035 came early.

India's opposition storms Meta's offices to get its Instagram accounts back
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
06:28:04 UTC
15:28:04 KST

India's opposition storms Meta's offices to get its Instagram accounts back
Last Friday, activists from the Indian Youth Congress, the youth wing of India's main opposition party, forced their way into the building housing Meta's offices in Hyderabad, where they ended up clashing with police among overturned potted plants.
The scene repeated itself the same day in Mumbai, with anti-Meta slogans spray-painted on the building's walls and placards burned in front of the entrance, while in Gurugram, near New Delhi, banners demanded the return of the movement's accounts.
Mumbai police registered a complaint against four of the organization's leaders and detained several protesters.
This anger stems from the November 19 blocking of the Instagram accounts of the youth wing's president and of the party official who oversees it. The Congress accuses Meta of obeying orders from the BJP, Narendra Modi's Hindu nationalist party, and of restricting, along the way, thousands of accounts belonging to journalists, creators and influencers critical of those in power.
The movement had warned on X that it would storm the company's offices across the country if the accounts weren't reinstated. Promise kept, then. And the method pays off, since two accounts were restored the day after the protests, according to the group's social media chief, who is now giving Meta seven days to lift all restrictions targeting opposition voices.
Meta restricted more than 5.4 million posts or accounts in India in the first half of 2026, compared with around 70,000 over the whole of 2025. The government, for its part, issued almost 200,000 blocking orders between March and July, and since February has required platforms to remove flagged content within three hours, compared with 36 previously.
India is also Meta's biggest market by number of users, and this newfound docility looks an awful lot like an effort to protect its bread and butter.
On the BJP side, a spokesperson accuses the protesters of trashing the Hyderabad office and bragging about it on social media. Meta, meanwhile, still hasn't said anything, caught between a government that gives orders and an opposition that kicks down its doors.
Source: The Verge

====================================================================================================

The movement had warned on X that it would storm the company's offices across the country if the accounts weren't reinstated. Promise kept, then. And the method pays off, since two accounts were restored the day after the protests, according to the group's social media chief, who is now giving Meta seven days to lift all restrictions targeting opposition voices.
Meta restricted more than 5.4 million posts or accounts in India in the first half of 2026, compared with around 70,000 over the whole of 2025. The government, for its part, issued almost 200,000 blocking orders between March and July, and since February has required platforms to remove flagged content within three hours, compared with 36 previously.
India is also Meta's biggest market by number of users, and this newfound docility looks an awful lot like an effort to protect its bread and butter.
On the BJP side, a spokesperson accuses the protesters of trashing the Hyderabad office and bragging about it on social media. Meta, meanwhile, still hasn't said anything, caught between a government that gives orders and an opposition that kicks down its doors.
Source: The Verge

====================================================================================================

AMD EPYC Zen 6 vs. Zen 1 Performance: 12.1x Geo Mean in Less Than 10 Years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
05:15:22 UTC
14:15:22 KST

AMD EPYC Zen 6 vs. Zen 1 Performance: 12.1x Geo Mean In Less Than 10 Years, Up To 133x In AI
This month brought the availability of the new EPYC 9006 "Venice" SP7 Zen 6 server processors. We have seen excellent generational gains with the likes of the EPYC 9686F and EPYC 9996 over Zen 5 Turin CPUs as well as sharply outperforming the competition from Intel and NVIDIA. With the first-generation EPYC "Naples" processors having launched nearly ten years ago, I was curious to quantify AMD's server performance gains over the past decade. So up today is a look at how the EPYC 9996 and EPYC 9686F Zen 6 processors compare to the original EPYC Zen 1 flagship with the EPYC 7601 across real-world workloads for performance as well as power efficiency for some pretty epic gains.
The AMD EPYC 7601 launched back in June 2017 as the flagship of the EPYC Naples "Zen 1" processors. The EPYC 7601 was notable for its time with 32 x Zen 1 cores / 64 threads, 2.2GHz base frequency, 3.2GHz max boost frequency, and 64 MB of L3 cache. On the memory side was eight channel DDR4-2666 memory and for connectivity were up to 128 PCIe 3.0 lanes. The TDP of each EPYC 7601 processor is 180 Watts.
Now just shy of a decade later, the EPYC 9006 SP7 processors have begun shipping. With the AMD EPYC 9996 256-core flagship are 256 Zen 6C cores / 512 threads per socket, 2.55GHz base clock, 4.1GHz max boost frequency, and an incredible 1024MB of L3 cache. EPYC 9006 SP7 processors support DDR5 RDIMMs or up to MRDIMM-12800 memory at 16 channels. There is also 96 lanes of PCI Express 6.0. The EPYC 9996 has a 600W default power rating.
Or for the frequency-optimized flagship is the EPYC 9686F with 96 Zen 5 cores / 192 threads with a 4.05GHz base frequency and up to 5.3GHz maximum boost clock. The EPYC 9686F has a 384MB L3 cache size while also having a 600 Watt default power rating.
Over the past ten years of AMD EPYC there has been incredible architectural advancements too not reflected in the key specs alone such as AVX-512 with Zen 4 and further refined in Zen 5, security improvements to reduce the need for software-based security mitigations, and countless other architectural improvements each step of the way from Zen 1 to Zen 6.
For comparing EPYC Zen 1 to Zen 6, I dusted off the Dell PowerEdge R7425 server originally provided by AMD for EPYC Naples testing at Phoronix. Nearly ten years later, the Dell PowerEdge server with the dual AMD EPYC 7601 processors still fires up and runs reliably. The AMD EPYC 7601 2P was running with 16 x 32GB DDR4-2666 memory, Samsung SSD 860 SSD storage, and other stock components of this Naples review unit.
With the AMD EPYC 9686F 2P and EPYC 9996 2P testing was the Nigeria reference server with 32 x 64GB MRDIMM-12800 memory, and Micron MTFDMBQ7T6THM-1BP1JABDC PCIe Gen6 SSD.
The AMD EPYC Naples and EPYC Venice servers were tested on the same software stack with using Ubuntu 26.10 for a very up-to-date open-source software experience. Ubuntu 26.10 ships with the Linux 7.3 kernel and GCC 15.3 compiler by default.
In addition to the raw performance testing for EPYC Zen 1 vs. EPYC Zen 6, the CPU power consumption and performance-per-Watt were also compared using the exposed CPU PowerCap/RAPL sensors of 2P processor configuration.

====================================================================================================

Show HN: Genesis Map – Visualize your agent's changes. See where they can break
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
05:04:41 UTC
14:04:41 KST

English · 简体中文
genesis-launch.mp4
Genesis turns your codebase into a business flow map and shows which of your agent's changes are untested or risky. It runs as a skill inside Claude Code and Codex, and works on projects in Python, TypeScript, JavaScript, Go, Java, Rust and most other common languages. Python gets the most accurate results.
We gave Claude Code and Codex 10 recent commits from 5 open-source Python projects and asked what a user would ask: what does this commit change, could it break anything, and do the tests cover it? Each agent answered every commit three times on its own and three times with Genesis. Every answer was scored against a reference answer built from the code and its tests before the runs.
With Genesis:
- Changes that tests run but would not catch are flagged about five times as often. A test runs such a change but would still pass if it broke, like the 🟡 stock change in the example below. Claude Code flagged 8% of them on its own and 38% with Genesis. Codex flagged 4% and 23%.
- False alarms drop to about a third, and real risks hold steady. So more of the risks an answer raises are real: 64% instead of 44% for Claude Code, 72% instead of 54% for Codex.
- More of the entry points a change affects are named: the routes, tools and commands that reach the changed code. Claude Code went from 32% to 47%, Codex from 15% to 24%.
Keep in mind that the first row says less than it seems. Most changes in these commits are covered, so an answer that called every change covered would already score 82%. For Codex it is higher on only 4 of the 10 commits. The difference is in the changes tests would not catch, and even with Genesis about a third of those are still called covered. Codex names more entry points with Genesis, but a smaller share of them are right. The sample is small, 10 commits with three runs each, and the answers were scored by language-model readers, not people. Method and full results: change-analysis benchmark.
The quickest way is to let your coding agent do it. Open your own project in the agent and paste:
Install Genesis by following https://raw.githubusercontent.com/GenesisAgentInfra/genesis/main/docs/install.md, then review my latest commit with it and tell me how to open the result.
Or install it yourself. You need uv and Git, on macOS or Linux:
uv tool install --python 3.12 "git+https://github.com/GenesisAgentInfra/genesis#subdirectory=packages/genesis_ci"
"$(uv tool dir --bin)/genesis" agent install --agent claude
Use --agent codex for Codex and --agent both for both. Other agents, updating,
uninstalling and troubleshooting are in the install guide.
Now open your own project in Claude Code. It has to be a Git repository with at least one commit. Then ask for one of two things:
/genesis map
/genesis review main
map draws the system as it is now, so you can understand it. review shows what
changed since a revision (here main) and whether a test checks it. You can also just
ask in plain language.
In Codex the skill is $genesis. When it finishes, open .genesis/out/latest/view.html
in a browser. The summary.md next to it is ready to paste into a pull request. The
usage guide explains the page and the other files.
Say you asked your agent for guest checkout. It built that, and changed three other things on the way. Genesis draws the checkout flow and puts all four changes on it:
These are the labels on the page. summary.md calls the same verdicts not reached,
reached only, unknown and covered; the usage guide lists
both.
Click a step to see what changed there, the test verdict with the note that explains it, and the code behind it:
Your agent does the reading, but it doesn't get the last word. Every step it writes has
to quote the lines of code it stands for, and the Genesis CLI checks each quote against
a frozen snapshot of your repo. If one doesn't match, the whole description is rejected
and the agent tries again. For Python, Genesis also parses the code itself, so a branch
the agent skips leaves the result marked partial in summary.md. When you map a
project, the page also shows "Partial" at the top. A review's page does not, so check
summary.md.
In a review, Genesis finds the changes itself by comparing the two versions of the code, so the list does not depend on the agent. What each change means, its test verdict and its risks are the agent's reading. The CLI refuses a verdict only in plain cases: Behavior checked (covered) with no cited test that asserts anything, or, for Python, No coverage found (not reached) when a test calls the changed function directly. In other languages, whether a cited test asserts anything is the agent's word.
The CLI calls no model, sends nothing anywhere, and never runs your code.
- Python gets the most accurate results, because Genesis parses it. Other languages work too: your agent reads the code and Genesis checks each step against the source, but a path the agent misses is not flagged.
- Test verdicts come from reading your tests. Genesis doesn't run them.
- Risks are what your agent noticed while reading. There is no rule engine.
- Install: requirements, which flag for which agent, updating, uninstalling, and what to do when something goes wrong.
- Use: what to ask, where the result is, how to read the page.
- Benchmark: what was measured and the full results.
- AGENTS.md: for a coding agent asked to install, use or change Genesis.
Questions and bug reports go to GitHub issues. Report a security problem privately, as SECURITY.md describes.
Apache-2.0. Bundled components keep their own licenses, listed in NOTICE and THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md. Want to help? See CONTRIBUTING.md.

New York Bans Subscription and Post-Purchase Fees On Cars
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

New York Bans Subscription and Post-Purchase Fees On Cars (wgna.com) 61
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from WGNA-FM: On November 23rd, New York State Governor Kathy Hochul announced Bill S.9348A/A.10411A. This bill aims to put money back in New Yorkers pockets by prohibiting any vehicle manufacturers, dealers, or third-party providers from charging consumers more money for locked services or subscription fees. This specifically targets newer vehicles that may lock pre-installed features (like heated seats or screen compatibility) behind a paywall or subscription service. This is becoming more and more of a problem as years go by, and consumers have been very vocal against the practice, but this is a tangible step that can be taken to end this practice before it gets too out of hand. [...] The bill will go into effect on February 27th, 2027.  "No one should have to pay recurring fees just to use features in a car they already bought. My office has secured millions of dollars for New Yorkers throughout the state who were charged illegal junk fees when purchasing a car," said New York Attorney General Letitia James stated. "Unfair fees are fueling rising costs, and I am proud to work with Governor Hochul and the legislature to bring costs down."

Zero data retention with Enterprise Frontier Safeguards is now in beta
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
03:15:38 UTC
12:15:38 KST

Zero data retention with Enterprise Frontier Safeguards is now in beta
Over the past few months, we've been developing Enterprise Frontier Safeguards (EFS) for our business customers. EFS provides state-of-the-art detection of sophisticated misuse, while remaining compatible with zero data retention (ZDR): neither Anthropic nor any cloud providers retain enterprises' data.
Since we announced EFS in September, we've been developing it further with over 100 of our customers—including a quarter of the Fortune 100 and many companies in industries with strict rules on data visibility (like healthcare, financial services, and law). Leading enterprises like Bridgewater, Salesforce, and Stripe have been evaluating EFS in private beta.
“Enterprise Frontier Safeguards gives us exactly what we asked for: our logs stay in a Wells-managed environment under Wells-managed keys. We keep custody of our data while Anthropic operates the detection. That split is what lets our teams put frontier models to work safely and meet our obligations to customers, employees, and regulators. We helped shape these safeguards because our industry needs them.”
“Where we have regulated or highly sensitive data, having Enterprise Frontier Safeguards with zero data retention will allow us to confidently accelerate our AI deployment across those highly regulated and sensitive client engagements.”
“As a company that runs critical infrastructure, safety matters as much to us as model capability. We need a solution that gives us strong safeguards while allowing us to keep our data in our own account. Enterprise Frontier Safeguards delivers just that.”
“At Stripe, protecting customer data is foundational to how we operate. Anthropic’s Enterprise Frontier Safeguards will enable us to use frontier models with added protection against misuse. It will also let us retain conversation logs in Stripe’s AWS environment, with access and review governed by Stripe’s security controls.”
“With EFS, customers can keep sensitive data in their own environments, protected with their own encryption keys, while still preserving the specialized safety mechanisms frontier capabilities require. That balance, keeping enterprises in control of their data without abandoning frontier-model safety, is critical to broader adoption of advanced AI in security-sensitive organizations like Cisco.”
Now, we're opening up EFS in public beta. In this post, we explain how EFS works in more detail, how it stays compatible with ZDR, and how businesses can join the beta program.
How EFS provides automated safety monitoring with ZDR
Customers who rely on legacy ZDR are protected by Claude's classifiers: our real-time safeguards that screen individual requests to assess whether they might cause harm. But sophisticated attacks generally occur across multiple queries—and any single request might, in isolation, look harmless.
For example, our September threat intelligence report describes an operation against roughly fifty organizations in which an AI agent divided the work of an attack among many other agents running in parallel. It saved the credentials it had harvested between sessions, so that the attackers could easily resume their campaign. Viewed one request at a time, this attack would have appeared as separate, more benign tasks.
To catch attacks like these, we use a second defensive layer, known as automated safety monitoring. This layer looks for patterns of misuse across many requests. Through EFS, Anthropic can provide automated safety monitoring without storing any customer data. Instead, our automated safety monitoring system analyzes data stored entirely on infrastructure controlled by our customers.
When automated safety monitoring finds a risk, it creates a Safety Case for you to review. These cases can be read from your cloud storage or through an API, and can be integrated with security information and event management systems. They do not include inputs and outputs.
Once it's set up, automated safety monitoring can help your team detect intentional misuse that could lead to serious harm (like offensive cyber operations). It can also help detect agents that take actions beyond a user's intentions, such as—in extreme cases—using a software exploit to access an internal database or external system.
Zero data retention and EFS
With EFS, you bring your own storage for storing inputs and outputs. Neither Anthropic nor your cloud provider keeps a copy of your data once it is delivered to storage you control. This provides you the following guarantees:
- Zero data retention. Neither Anthropic nor your cloud provider retains your data. You bring your own storage and store your own data. Inputs and outputs are deleted promptly after they're written to your own storage. You configure your own access policies and audit logging, and encrypt safety data with your own keys.
- No operator human review. Neither Anthropic nor your cloud provider personnel access your inputs and outputs. Only automated safety monitoring can access this data.
- We never train on your data. Inputs and outputs are used only for automated safety monitoring, never for model or classifier training.
You can use EFS through the Claude Platform, Claude Platform on AWS, Amazon Bedrock, or Claude on Google Cloud. When you use EFS through Amazon Bedrock or Claude on Google Cloud (Google Managed), it's operated by your cloud provider, and the end-to-end data flow stays within that cloud's boundary.
You can use Amazon S3 or Google Cloud Storage in your own account for your storage, depending on where you use Claude. Your cloud provider bills you for that storage as usual, including reads, writes, and key operations (plus any applicable network transfer).
Get started
EFS is available on the Claude Platform, Claude Platform on AWS, Amazon Bedrock, and Claude on Google Cloud. Support for EFS in Microsoft Foundry is coming soon.
Eligible customers will be notified with instructions on how to get started. Read our developer docs to learn how to set it up, and our security whitepaper (opens in new tab) for how the architecture works, or deploy it through consulting partners like Accenture, Deloitte, KPMG, and PwC.

Show HN: FireVoice, free open-source voice studio on your own GPU
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
02:58:20 UTC
11:58:20 KST

Just say “Hey FireVoice”
No keys to press. Say it, then say what you want written, in any app. It wakes for your accent too.
New · Start with “Hey FireVoice”
Speak in any app and FireVoice writes it out, clean and punctuated, right where your cursor is. Free, and it runs on your own computer.
Works in every app you type in
Why speak
You already know what you want to say. FireVoice keeps up with you, tidies it as you go, and puts it wherever you are writing.
Most people type about 40 words a minute and speak about 150. Here is one minute of each.
Move the slider to how much you write in a day.
Time saved
≈ 13
hours saved a month
That is about 20 working days a year.
words a day × 22 workdays × (1/40 − 1/150) minutes a word
0 bytes
Speech turns into text on your own computer. It works offline, too.
If it has a text box, FireVoice can write in it. Email, chat, documents, code.
How it works
Press a shortcut or say the words, talk, and finished writing appears in the app you are in. Here is one dictation, step by step.
Hold Ctrl + Win or say “Hey FireVoice”, then talk the way you would to a friend. Ums are fine.
Filler words and repeats drop out; punctuation and capitals go in. All on your computer.
The finished text lands where your cursor is, in any app, and the pill turns into a green tick.
Features
Everything dictation should have done all along, in one small pill.
Names and terms are spelled your way when you dictate, and said right by the voices.
Say a short cue and get the long text: your address, your sign-off, a link.
You said: “uh tell Priya the deck is late, it’s ready Friday, sorry”
Hi Priya, The deck is running late. It will be ready on Friday. Best, Alex
The same words, tidied to suit an email, a chat or your notes.
Speak the language you think in. FireVoice notices and writes it.
Words dictated, time saved against typing, how fast you speak and your streak, on Home.
Everything you dictated, by day. Find that sentence from Tuesday in a second.
Privacy
FireVoice listens, writes and speaks on your own computer. What you say and what it writes never pass through a server, so there is nothing to upload and nothing to wait for.
Speech, clean-up and voices all happen locally, on your own machine.
Pull the cable and keep dictating. Nothing waits on a server.
What you say, what it writes and the files you open stay with you.
Anonymous usage statistics are optional: FireVoice asks once, and you can turn them off in Settings. Never your words.
Voice studio
Natural speech in a library of voices, your own voice cloned from a short sample, voices designed from a few words, dialogue, dubbing and captions. All of it on your computer.
Type or paste anything and hear it read in a natural voice, from a library of voices.
Real clips, made by FireVoice on a home computer.
Movie trailer
“In a world where every voice lives in someone else's cloud... one app keeps yours exactly where it belongs.”
Documentary
“Here, in the quiet of your own computer, a brand new voice takes its very first breath.”
Bedtime story
“Once upon a time, a little computer learned to tell stories. And it never told a soul.”
Villain

Radio DJ

Whisper

Deadpan
“Oh great. Another app that works offline, costs nothing per word, and sounds human. I'm thrilled.”
Sports commentator
“And she writes the whole email without touching the keyboard! Unbelievable! What a dictation!”
FAQ
The short answers about how FireVoice works, what it needs and what stays on your computer.
Almost everywhere you can type. FireVoice puts the finished text where your cursor is, so it works in email, chat, documents, browsers and code editors: Slack, Gmail, Word, Notion, VS Code and the rest. The one exception is a window running as administrator, which Windows keeps apart from other programs.
Built-in dictation writes down what you said, word for word. FireVoice writes what you meant: it drops the ums and uhs, repeated words and false starts, adds punctuation and capitals, and tidies the text to suit where it goes, so an email reads like an email and a chat like a chat. You can also start it by saying "Hey FireVoice", teach it names and terms in the Dictionary, and keep snippets for text you use often.
Yes. FireVoice detects the language you speak and works in many languages, with all kinds of accents. Add the names and terms you use to the Dictionary and it spells them right. "Teach it your voice" helps "Hey FireVoice" learn how you say it.
No. Speech recognition, the writing clean-up and the voices all run on your own computer, so what you say and write is never uploaded. If you agree when FireVoice asks, it shares usage statistics: which features you use, details about the app and your computer, and your approximate location. Never what you say or write. You can turn that off at any time in Settings, Privacy.
Yes. FireVoice is free for everyone right now. There is no card to enter and nothing to unlock.
No. An account is optional, and everything on your computer works without one.
A Windows PC, a Mac with Apple Silicon (M1 or newer, macOS 11 or later), or a Linux computer. Dictation runs on the processor, and faster with a graphics card. For the voice studio, a recent NVIDIA graphics card makes it fastest; without one it still runs on the processor, just slower.
Yes. Because everything runs on your computer, once FireVoice has downloaded what it needs, you can dictate and make voices without an internet connection.
Yes. FireVoice starts when you sign in to your computer (Windows, Mac or Linux) and waits quietly in the tray or menu bar, ready for the shortcut or "Hey FireVoice". You can turn that off in Settings.
Press the shortcut or say "Hey FireVoice", then speak in any program. The pill glows with your voice and turns into a green tick when the words go in. Choose whether the text is typed as you speak or all at once when you stop. Your first dictation comes with a short guided tour.
Yes, for Macs with Apple Silicon (M1 or newer) on macOS 11 or later. The first time, open it with right-click > Open, because it is not yet signed by Apple. When you first dictate, allow the microphone, and allow FireVoice under Privacy & Security > Accessibility so it can type into other apps.
FireVoice is also a voice studio: natural text to speech in a library of voices, your own voice cloned from a short sample, new voices designed from a description, dialogue between several speakers, dubbing into other languages, a voice changer, sound effects, and transcription with captions you can save as SRT.
FireVoice is free. Install it, press the shortcut, and talk.
Windows, macOS and Linux · Free

Hacking radiators for fun and no profit
France
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
03:56:41 CET
11:56:41 KST

Hacking radiators for fun and no profit
My flat has a bunch of electrical radiators, branded Acova. They have a remote. Each. Fixed to the wall right next to it. This means I have to get up from my seat to turn a radiator on. Worse, I might have to get to an entirely different room.
Unlike the TV remote, my radiator remotes are not IR (although an IR variant does exist, and was already hacked), but RF. With no other information, I whipped out my trusty RTL-SDR Blog V3.
As is now usual, Claude helped me quickly detect the signals of the remotes, establishing that they use 868.2 MHz. So I got myself a Heltec V3 for that frequency. Unlike my SDR, it would let me send data, in addition to receiving it.
After more poking with the Heltec, I realized that the remotes seemed to use proper cryptography during pairing, and all communications thereafter were encrypted. That meant that trying to crack the exchanges from sniffed data would most likely not work.
So I took one apart.
Promising! Next to the big black square is a connector with 6 pin holes labelled X1. Most likely a debug port for engineers and technicians working on the product.
After identifying the GND pin with a conductivity tester, and failing to conclusively identify the 3.3V pin, I tried the first guess of Claude for the mapping, with SWDIO on the 4th pin from the top and SWCLK on the 2nd pin from the top. It worked first try!
Fortunately, the MCU did not have any protection enabled, so I was able to dump the whole firmware, and to read memory freely. After I installed Ghidra, Claude was able to analyze the firmware to determine the whole protocol, including the cryptography, as well as dump the session key (determined at pairing).
Interestingly, the firmware is labelled “ComfoRF” and bears marks of the Zehnder Group, of which Acova is a subsidiary. It embeds mbed TLS with various cryptographic primitives, but only ends up using AES-ECB, and implements its own finite-field Diffie-Hellman. Also, it has some logic to use a hardware implementation of AES available on a different variant of the MCU.
With this, I could determine exactly what the MCU was sending to the radio chip. However, I could not read and send commands just yet. The SPIRIT1 radio chip does its own transforms to make the transmission more reliable (FEC, interleaving, whitening), before 2-GFSK modulation. These steps are only partially documented and specific to the SPIRIT1. To use my Heltec module, I would need to know exactly what these steps do.
What I did have were the exact contents of messages going into the radio (read from the MCU using SWD) and of messages going out of the radio (captured using the Heltec). After collecting a few samples, I turned it into a math problem for Opus 5.5, which eventually managed to determine the exact transform used in the SPIRIT1. The real (also generated) prompt was longer, but in essence:
The task. A black-box deterministic function F maps an input byte-string X to an output. I observe pairs (X, Y) where Y is a fixed 64-byte window onto F(X) — the full output may be longer; only the first 64 bytes are seen. Recover F well enough to invert it: given Y, recover X. Approach it however you like; form your own model before reading "What I ruled out."
Data.
- pairs.txt — 5 labelled examples (<input_hex> <output_hex>): input is the full 57-byte record, output the 64-byte window.
- outputs.txt — 1831 unlabelled output windows from the same system.
Observations (verified on the data).
1. Fixed additive mask: Y = M XOR T(X), with M a fixed position-indexed sequence. XORing two outputs cancels M, and the zero runs line up with where the two inputs are equal.
2. Partially linear over GF(2): in some regions Y_i XOR Y_j depends only on X_i XOR X_j; in others it doesn't — so T isn't globally linear.
3. Input-independent invariant block: output bytes 12..27 are identical across every example, even though the inputs differ there. The central anomaly.
4. Leading correspondence: output 0..7 tracks input 0..7, degrading further in.
What I ruled out (read after forming your own view). Modelling T as a GF(2)-linear expansion plus the additive stream M and searching exhaustively: best ~73% of bits, never exact. Observation 3 can't come from a linear expansion, so a non-linear or block-structured component is involved — don't re-tread the linear-only family.
The Claude-generated description of the whole protocol is available in a separate article.
And now the fun begins. I added a separate tab to my TV control panel, dumped the session key of all remotes, and can now control all radiators from my computer, or even my phone. I can even do it remotely, to come back to a warm home!
It took more effort than taking control of the TV, both from me and from Claude, but it worked out in the end. Without Claude, such a project would have likely taken me dozens, if not hundreds, of hours, but it only took about 9 hours of my time, all included.
Now I just need to find a way to control the weather…

Making a dumb TV just smart enough
France
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
03:46:54 CET
11:46:54 KST

Making a dumb TV just smart enough
I own an old, cheap TV. It has no Internet connection, no fancy smart features that make you want to install a Pi-hole, and no ask-me-again-later updates.
However, it is connected to my own desktop computer through an HDMI cable, so I can watch movies and TV series from the couch. Setting it up used to involve going to my desk, enabling the secondary monitor, opening the relevant application, moving its window to the secondary monitor, setting it in fullscreen, going back to the couch, remembering I forgot my wireless keyboard, getting off the couch, grabbing my wireless keyboard, going back to the couch again, and then pressing the Space bar.
The most important quality of a programmer is laziness.
So, of course, I vowed never to get off the couch unnecessarily. And that meant controlling everything from the TV remote.
So, I got myself an IR transmitter and an IR receiver to tinker with:
Then, I fished out an ESP32-C3 Mini devboard, a mini-breadboard, and a handful of jumper wires, and made the most basic device that could solve my woes:
I have played around with Espressif MCUs before, including for early versions of the OmniMorse, but I must admit that Claude is just faster at it than I am. With the help of Opus 5.5, I identified the RC-5 IR codes for each remote button, and set up a few scripts so that a single button press on the remote would do the whole dance I described above.
Problem solved!
But this first success unlocked much more.
I also own a bunch of old consoles. Getting everything hooked up is a chore, and makes a mess of wires. It would be much simpler to run an emulator on my computer and show it on the TV.
Thus, I installed DuckStation, PCSX2, Dolphin, Cemu and ES-DE, configured two Xbox controllers on each of them, and got Claude to start everything from a single button on the remote.
At this point, I was using two of the four colored buttons of the TV remote. So, of course, I had to come up with two more modes. I got Claude to add a Fireplace mode, which just plays 10-hour videos, the most important one being a fireplace. And also Steam, in Big Picture mode.
Soon, there were regular issues with windows opening on the wrong screen, so I switched approaches. Instead of a large desktop on two screens, I resorted to multiseat, which I had always wanted to use, with two separate X sessions, one on each screen. This also allows using both screens at the same time without interference.
But that did mean I could not directly control the windows on the TV from my regular session. So, again, I got Claude to make me an admin panel:
The virtual TV remote actually works, by sending directly through the device I cobbled together. The device also tracks volume being changed from the real remote, so things stay in sync. The rest is just commands to my computer, including a virtual gamepad, and a real-time stream of what is being displayed (mouse clicks work too!). Also, turning on an Xbox controller switches to the emulator or Steam mode, and turning the last one off goes back to the fireplace.
Last, but not least, I took a significant amount of time making (telling Claude to make) everything fast. The whole thing took me about 10 hours in total, most of which was setting up console emulators.
Claude still needs to be kept on a tight leash: it sometimes wanted to go in the wrong direction, left obvious bugs, or suggested fixes that did not address the root cause. However, it saved me a bunch of time on the original problem, and lowered the effort enough to go above and beyond!

Declare is a UI language for you and your AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
02:36:56 UTC
11:36:56 KST

Just as SQL is a domain-specific language for database queries, Declare is a DSL for user interfaces — for taking the custom, fluid experience you have in mind beyond a prototype, into a real app: structured, verifiable, and with the design written plainly in its code.
Declare in 10 minutes →
Run the calendar →
The language, in one file →
01
A calendar that zooms through time, a desktop with real windows: complete programs, each readable end to end and checked by the compiler. Open one, or edit it live in the browser you’re using.
1
Day, week, month and year views are one surface, zooming continuously; a view doesn’t switch so much as become the next one, and every motion can be interrupted. It runs on real data: drag an event to another day, and it’s moved.

lines of code, plus 228 of comments — four views, zoom, drag and edit

over the wire, gzipped — app (structure, logic, styling) and runtime

The acid test for a UI language: multiple applications, windows that drag, resize, layer and minimize into live thumbnails, a magnifying dock, menus that follow focus, and separately loaded apps running inside its windows. Elsewhere that is a project assembled from several libraries; here it is one program.
3529 lines — 2011 code, 939 comment · 264 KB over the wire · every pixel drawn from shapes, no image assets
3
1,000,000 issues · search as you type · edit in place, with undo · not one line of virtualization code
1508 lines — 983 code, 251 comment · 194 KB over the wire, before its data · no dependencies
