Windows that drag, resize and layer; minimizing into live thumbnails; a magnifying dock; menus that follow focus; other programs running inside windows — in a mainstream web stack each of those is usually its own library or its own project, and keeping them coherent (focus, stacking, hit-testing, animation that can be interrupted) is where the effort goes. In Declare they are the language and its library, and the desktop is one program of 2,011 lines of code. That is why it makes a good acid test for any UI toolkit.
Time and change live in the language instead of being bolted on:
- Constraints — any attribute can be a { } expression; the compiler works out what it depends on, and it stays current. No re-render ceremony, no manual subscriptions.
- Animation as declaration — springs and animators are attributes; states are named bundles of overrides you can be in or not in.
- Layout as an attribute — how a view arranges its children is a value you set, not a system you fight. And layouts are written in Declare too: a class with one method, place(), so a layout of your own is no harder to make than to use.
- Controls you can write — the library's buttons, sliders and menus are ordinary Declare classes. Extend Control and yours takes clicks, taps, focus and keys exactly as theirs do, so there is no cliff between using a component and building one.
- Drawing and effects in the same tree — draw(), clipping to any shape, filters and backdrop blur are attributes of a view, alongside its layout and motion, not a separate canvas or effects layer.
- Performance from the model — because the compiler knows every dependency in advance, the reactive core runs as a small C kernel: WebAssembly in the browser, native on the Mac. That cut update time 42–53% on every platform measured, and made a window drag’s updates five or more times faster.
- Data binding — UI replicates over data with a cursor (datapath), and two-way binding connects editable fields to the data they edit.
- One input model — pointer and keyboard events are routed identically on every renderer, with a built-in keyboard focus system.
It's a language, not a framework inside JavaScript. In React and its relatives, the UI is a function you re-run — the framework diffs the output and patches the page, and you manage the machinery (hooks, memoization, effects) that keeps re-running affordable. In Declare, the UI is a standing structure: values flow through it when things change, and nothing re-renders because nothing was ever a render. There is also no HTML/CSS/JS trinity — structure, style, and behavior are one tree in one language. Its nearest relatives are declarative-tree systems like SwiftUI and QML, but Declare is web-native, compiled, renderer-independent, and uses TypeScript — not a new scripting dialect — for logic.
The compiler parses the declarations, statically extracts every constraint's dependencies, type-checks every { } body as real TypeScript against every class's typed API, resolves the components you use (the standard library auto-includes by name), and emits a self-contained program. The benefits: errors surface before the program runs, with messages that name the fix; reactivity is derived by the compiler, not guessed at runtime, so behavior is predictable and analyzable by tools; production output is small; and programs are data — the same compiler powers the documentation system, static extraction for crawlers, and live editing. The compiler itself runs anywhere — in Node, or in the browser.
The [ ] structure is Declare; everything inside { } — expressions, event handlers, methods — is ordinary TypeScript, type-checked with full knowledge of every class's attributes. There is no new expression language to learn. The declarative layer contributes structure and reactivity; TypeScript contributes logic — and everything you (or your model) already know about it carries over.
A reusable view is a class: class PriceTag [ … ]. Its attributes are its public interface, and it reports back through events it declares. Composition is just nesting: your classes are used exactly like built-ins, including replication over data.
No — and that's a feature. Styling is part of the language: paint attributes on views (fill, cornerRadius, stroke, shadow, text styles), theme records that reskin an entire subtree from one place, and named style bundles a subtree can switch between at runtime. Your CSS knowledge transfers — colors (named or hex), font stacks, shadows all read the same — but there is no cascade, no specificity, no selector debugging. This is also what makes renderer independence possible: the canvas renderer couldn't consult a stylesheet the browser owns.
Side by side, yes; interleaved, no. A Declare app embeds in any page — including a React page. And inside a Declare program, the DOMIsland view hosts foreign content — a video player, a code editor, a React widget — in a box the Declare tree sizes and positions. What you can't do is put a React component inside the Declare tree as if it were a Declare view, or vice versa. The boundary is always an island, which is what keeps both sides comprehensible.
No router is needed: the URL is just an attribute. Declare location on your app and it follows the address bar. Setting it navigates, Back restores it, and a deep link is simply its starting value; the rest of the app derives from it like any other value. In the docs on this site, #guide/14-data opens that chapter directly. Deep-linked pages are crawlable too.
At build time, the compiler runs the app headlessly and bakes its content into the page as plain HTML: real headings, paragraphs and links. Crawlers and AI readers get that; people get the live app. No server needed: this site runs on GitHub Pages.
Because the compiler runs in the page, the page can edit and re-run itself: every sample on this site is live-editable — change the source and the app re-renders as you type. It restores view-source culture: "View and edit source" on any app here opens the viewer — a highlighted reader, the verbatim source, and a live-edit tab (?viewer=edit on the program's URL). And it means development needs no build step — the dev server and the browser use the same compiler with byte-identical output. For production you don't ship the compiler: the declarec build precompiles everything into a small artifact, no compiler aboard.
One program renders through managed DOM elements, directly to a single canvas, or in a native Mac host — same tree, same layout, same input handling, the three held to each other by a conformance suite (append ?render=canvas to any app URL to see it). The Mac host is a native macOS application, AppKit layers in place of DOM nodes, with the runtime's kernel linked in natively; you build it from the repository with one command (npm run build:mac). What this buys: the language owns its semantics — no DOM assumptions leak into your program; the canvas path suits environments where a DOM isn't available or isn't fast enough; and future renderers can be added without rewriting applications. Programs also run headlessly — no screen, no renderer at all — which is what powers static extraction and testing.
Concretely, measured from the deployed production artifacts:
- Wire size: the entire Declare calendar — application and runtime — ships at about 177 KB gzipped (the homepage reports the live figure, measured from the deployed production artifacts on every commit, which is why it can vary by a kilobyte).
- Source size: the tracker — a million issues, search as you type, editing in place with undo — is 1,508 lines: 983 of code and 251 of comments, and no dependencies. It ships at 194 KB gzipped before its data, which a DataSource fetches at run time.
- Responsiveness: when you drag an event, the constraint graph updates exactly what changed and paints — there is no virtual-DOM pass between your gesture and the pixels. Animations ride compositor-native paths (CSS transforms and painted properties on the DOM renderer; direct paint on canvas), so they run at the display's full rate — 120 fps on a ProMotion screen.
- Finding what's slow: the profiler (declare-profile, or the Inspector while you use the app) says which of your rules ran, how often and for how long, what each class cost to build, what drawings cost the GPU, and how long each frame took to reach the screen — in the program's own terms, its classes, attributes and source lines, never the runtime's internals. It runs in the browser or the Mac host.
- Startup: precompiled production builds start immediately. The live-compile pages (the editable samples) pay a one-time compiler download on a cold visit; warm visits start in around a tenth of a second. The one honest trade: a framework with no in-browser compiler wins the very first cold load — Declare's production path closes that gap by precompiling.
Live numbers are on the homepage, measured from the deployed artifacts themselves.
When machines write the code, the properties that matter change. Familiarity matters less; verifiability matters more. Declare's bet: give the model a small, closed, regular language, and give the toolchain the strictness to catch what the model gets wrong. Every compile type-checks every expression — a hallucinated attribute or misused API is an error with a message that names the fix, so the model's write-check-revise loop converges fast. And because an entire application is a few hundred lines of declarations, what the model produced can actually be audited — by a person or by another model. The calendar on this site reports its own number: zero lines written by hand.
Human-designed and directed, built with AI. The calendar was built by describing what was wanted and letting an LLM write the Declare — every line — with the compiler in the loop: each attempt was compiled and type-checked, the diagnostics steered the next revision, and the running app was the judge. The person's work was product design work — deciding what the calendar should be, reviewing behavior, and pushing back — not typing code. That's the workflow the language is designed around: the human owns intent, the model owns the text, and the toolchain keeps the text honest.
Two ways. First, the language is small enough that its entire definition fits in a model's context window — one file, docs/declare.md, about 21,000 tokens: a small fraction of a modern context window. A model doesn't need to have trained on a language it can hold the complete spec of while writing; recent research (cited in the essay on this site) shows models write never-before-seen languages competently from a spec in context. Second, most of what an LLM writes in a Declare program is covered by billions of lines of training data — the logic is ordinary TypeScript. Only the small declarative shell is new, and it's regular enough to learn from the spec. For agent workflows, the repository ships the same knowledge as an installable agent skill (skill/ — auto-discovered by Claude Code, usable as plain instructions by any coding agent).
verify command compiles, type-checks, boots the program in a real browser at laptop and phone sizes, and drives it with real input, so an agent can prove its output works before handing it back.
Starting cold from the repository, agents have built complete apps and passed hidden acceptance checks they never saw:
- A ticket-buying app — a drawn seat map, seats selling live while you choose, sign-in and checkout. Claude Code built it in Declare and, separately, in React. Both finished passing all 11 checks. The Declare app was 1,279 lines of code, the React app 2,666; the first Declare build that ran passed 11, 12.5 minutes in, where the first React build passed 6.
- A slide editor with a service behind it: 19 of 19 checks as delivered.
- The same ticket-buying app, built by OpenAI's Codex from the same cold download.
They are single runs, not yet a benchmark, and every one is published in full, the agent's own account included.
It's tested, not assumed. Agents are given a brief for a complete app and the repository, cold, and build it start to finish. Hidden acceptance checks they never see then drive the result through its controls' accessible names, both as delivered and at the first build that ran. The same brief goes to React, and to more than one vendor's model, and each run is published in full with the agent's own account of it, word for word. Failures feed back into the language, the diagnostics, and the documentation — several language changes exist specifically because evals showed models tripping. The documentation is written to double as training material: plain declarative prose, and every code fence in the teaching docs is compiled by the test suite, so nothing a model reads is stale or wrong. The result is a feedback loop — spec, diagnostics, evals — rather than a hope.
Declare is a from-the-ground-up modern successor to OpenLaszlo, the open-source rich-internet-application platform first released in 2002. OpenLaszlo had the core ideas remarkably early: interfaces declared as trees, attributes connected by live constraints, declarative data binding, compiled applications running in the browser — a decade before today's declarative frameworks. Declare keeps those convictions — the declarative tree, constraint-based reactivity, a real compiler — while rebuilding everything else for the modern era: a clean keyword-free syntax, TypeScript for logic, DOM and canvas renderers, in-browser compilation, and a design shaped from day one for the LLM era.
David Temkin, who co-founded Laszlo Systems and led the team behind OpenLaszlo. He writes about what led from one to the other at the end of the "Why Declare" essay.
Yes. Every sample on this site is live-editable in your browser — open one, change the source, and watch it re-render. The whole-page editor will even let you edit the homepage you're standing on. Nothing to install; the compiler is already in the page.
Node.js and git. Then:
git clone https://github.com/davidtemkin/declarelang.git && cd declarelang
npm install
npm start
That starts the dev server; write a program to my-apps/hello.declare and browse to its URL — on the dev server, the program's URL is the app's address. The repository ships prebuilt, so there's no build step before first run. (On a plain static host, the entry point is a page — index.html, or a production build's — rather than the bare .declare file; the site's service worker upgrades program URLs after a first visit.)
Yes. Declare installs into your project as an ordinary dependency, fetched from GitHub rather than the npm registry: npm install github:davidtemkin/declarelang (with pnpm, approve esbuild's install script when it asks). Your app lives in your own repository, next to the back end it talks to. The dev server serves your project at / and the platform at /declare/, declarec builds it, and the version is pinned in your lockfile, so you upgrade when you choose. Embedding Declare in a project has the whole setup. What you ship is fully standalone: a production build is a small set of static files you can host anywhere, with no trace of the toolchain. Cloning the repository, as above, is still the quickest way to try it, with your first programs in my-apps/.
Not yet as a standalone extension. The first-class editing surface today is the toolchain's own: every program URL opens a highlighted reader and a live editor in the browser, and the compiler — with its mandatory type-checking and fix-naming diagnostics — is the authority an editor plugin would defer to anyway. The language's surface is small enough that most editors' TypeScript support already helps inside { } bodies. A dedicated editor extension is an obvious gap, honestly labeled.
Your live edits belong to the session — copy the source out to keep it (it's one file). There's no playground permalink service yet: this site runs on a static host with no server to store shared snippets. The durable path is the ordinary one — save the .declare file, serve it from the dev server or any static host, and the program's URL is shareable from there.
With verify, which climbs six rungs, cheapest first, and stops at the first failure. The first three read the program: structure, names, types. The fourth boots it in Chrome, at a laptop's size and a phone's, and notes what it can see wrong in the layout — a control clipped out of reach, a child spilling out of its box, text running past its edge, a phone target smaller than 44 × 44. The fifth drives the app with real input from a script and checks what happens; the sixth compares screenshots against baselines. It suits CI and an agent's loop alike. Because programs settle deterministically, you can also drive one headlessly and assert on the resulting tree — that's how Declare's own test suite works. And a Declare app is a web page, so standard end-to-end tooling (Playwright, Puppeteer) applies unchanged. The platform itself is held to a stricter bar: a conformance suite holds the DOM, canvas and Mac renderers to the same answers.
Yes. The library's controls tell a screen reader what they are, what they are called and what state they are in, and keep it current; dialogs, sheets and menus hold and return the focus as their kind should; text fields are native, and say what they hold so autofill works. A control you draw yourself is given a role, and the compiler holds it to that role, as it does the library's. This is on the DOM renderer, the default; the canvas renderer does not yet carry it. The guide's Accessibility chapter has the detail.
The shape of the language: [ ] declares structure, { } holds TypeScript, attributes can be constraints, events get handlers. Then about twenty built-in classes and a standard library of controls. Start with Declare in 10 minutes, a twelve-screen tour of the model. Then the guide, in the docs, walks you through building real apps; if you know TypeScript, you'll be productive in an afternoon. The entire language reference is one readable file. Working with an LLM, you hand it that file (or install the packaged agent skill) and describe what you want — the compiler's diagnostics keep the loop honest either way.
Declaratively, like everything else. A Dataset holds inline or computed data; a DataSource fetches JSON. UI binds to data with a cursor — set datapath on a view and its descendants read relative to it; bind it to an array and the view replicates per element. Two-way binding connects a text field to the data it edits. When data changes, everything bound to it updates — the same reactivity as the rest of the language.
Large collections virtualize on one word. virtualize = true on a replicated node builds only the rows near the viewport and leaves the rest logical — same records, same paths, same behaviour, reconstructed indistinguishably as you scroll. There is nothing else to write: no row heights, no scroll wiring, no windowing library. It is a boolean and it is off by default, so that full replication keeps browser find-in-page working over every record — and like any other boolean it takes a { } constraint, engaging and disengaging as the answer changes. The tracker on this site is a million issues on that one word.
Declare is young and moving quickly. The language surface is deliberately small and increasingly settled; the toolchain — compiler, type-checker, dev server, production builds, docs, evals — is real and tested (this entire site ships on it). Expect evolution, and expect the repository to be the source of truth. If you're evaluating it for production, the honest advice is: try it on something real but bounded — and open an issue on GitHub with what you find; at this stage, that conversation shapes the language.
Modern browsers. Input is a unified pointer model, so touch works alongside mouse and keyboard; layouts are constraint-driven, so apps adapt to the window they're given. The DOM renderer produces real elements — native text selection, native form fields, built-in keyboard focus and tab navigation.
MIT. The complete source — compiler, runtime, standard library, documentation, and this site — is at github.com/davidtemkin/declarelang.
Declare · runtime architecture
A Declare front end is one self-contained artifact — small enough to hold in your head, strict enough for a machine to verify, rich enough for an ambitious application. This is what Declare’s runtime does to make that true.
Declare asks you to believe something unusual about an application’s front end: that it is complete. Everything the interface does — where each box goes, how a paragraph wraps, what a click landed on, how a view becomes the next one — is written in the one program, as relationships rather than steps, so a machine can verify it and a person can read it. That puts a demand on what runs underneath: if the browser decided the layout, or the DOM routed the click, the program would not be complete. So the runtime makes those decisions itself, once, and hands the browser what it does best.
What it looks like
What it is
Your .declare file
App program
the part you write
compiled: resolved, typed, dependencies extracted
A framework
Library — written in Declare
components, a store, re-rendering
Button, Slider, SimpleLayout: the same kind of code as yours
Runtime
decides everything below: ~137 KB gz
Kernel — C, as WebAssembly or linked natively
the settle and the geometry rules: 12 KB gzipped, as shipped
render seam · one interface, 71 methods
The browser
Host
does the real work
DOM
a <div> per view, geometry as inline style
Canvas
one <canvas>, its own compositor
Mac
a CALayer tree in a 7.8 MB app
Layout
Styling
Hit-testing
Hover & press

Gesture claims
Focus & Tab order
Text measurement
App
Library
Kernel
The compiler is where completeness is enforced. It parses the brackets into a tree, resolves every name to an explicit path, extracts what every { } body reads, and type-checks each body as TypeScript against the real interface of every class — so an attribute that doesn’t exist, or a relationship that reads something it can’t, is an error before anything runs. What it emits is not code that builds a UI but a description of one: the tree, a graph of which value reads which, and, for every body that is plain arithmetic, bytecode for a small stack machine. One line of the homepage’s bouncing-ball demo, compiled:
You wrote
to = { on ? lane.width - ball.width : 0 }
Resolved
to = { this.root.on ? parent.parent.parent.lane.width - parent.parent.ball.width : 0 }
Read-paths
0 this.root.on
1 parent.parent.parent.lane.width
2 parent.parent.ball.width
Kernel code
"=EL0 L1 L2 - K0 ?" — load 0, 1, 2 · subtract · push 0 · select
Because the program is data, it can be questioned as data. The Inspector on any page here — ⌥⌘D — reads this same graph: click a value and it shows the body that produced it, everything that body read, and their live values. The same completeness is what lets an AI’s work be checked: the compiler, the Inspector and verify all read one description of the whole interface, not a guess pieced together from files.
When a handler writes an attribute, nothing updates yet. When the handler returns, the runtime performs one update — a settle: everything that read what changed is recomputed, and whatever that changes in turn, until nothing more does; only then is the host told what moved. If you know React, it is the nearest thing to a commit, except that nothing was rendered to compare against. The meter below is live: raise its level, then step through what your click set in motion.
live · click it
Raise the level
Your code
Pointer arrives
Hit walk
It was a click
4
onClick()
5
Settle opens
6
bar.width
7
label.text
8
Surface calls
9
Pixels
Which view is under the pointer, and whether this was a click or the start of a drag, are the runtime’s answers, not the browser’s — one implementation under a finger or a pointer, on every renderer. And the settle itself runs in a kernel: one C file of about 1,400 lines that holds the graph’s tables and evaluates the compiled arithmetic, shipped as WebAssembly (10 KB gzipped) and linked natively on the Mac. A frame that only moves geometry never enters JavaScript.
03
A Spring is an attribute with a target; each frame it writes its next value, and that write is a settle like any other. Nothing about animation is special-cased, so a new target mid-flight is simply the next write: the spring retargets from wherever it is, and everything that read it follows. Interruptibility isn’t a feature of an animation system; it is what a reactive graph does when you write to it.
Every box is decided before the host hears of it — by arithmetic over attributes, by a layout (a class with one method, place()), or by the content’s own size. Text is the one place the host contributes a number, the width of a glyph; wrapping and height are the runtime’s, so a paragraph breaks into the same lines on every renderer. Because the runtime placed every box, it also answers the inverse question — what is under this point — and that one answer decides hover, press, and where a drag lands.
04
Because the compiler hands over the whole graph up front, the runtime doesn’t have to discover anything as it runs. Its reactive core (the graph, the settle, and the arithmetic of layout, springs and visibility) is a small C kernel: WebAssembly in the browser, linked natively on the Mac. Bodies that are plain arithmetic arrive as bytecode the kernel evaluates itself, without returning to JavaScript.
Moving the core there cut update time by 42–53% on every platform measured (Chrome, iOS Safari and the Mac host), and a window drag’s by 81–93%. The rules evaluated didn’t change; each one simply costs less.
05
Everything above happens on one side of a single interface, Surface — 71 methods such as setX, setFill and setText that the runtime calls and a backend implements. The DOM backend keeps an element per view and hands the browser what it does best: real text, the page’s own scroll, links, native fields. The canvas backend draws the tree directly to pixels. The Mac host is a Swift application with no web engine in it, applying each settle to Core Animation layers. Drawing (draw(d)) records rather than paints, so each host replays the same recording its own way.
Below, the calendar’s build drawn to scale by gzipped bytes: switch hosts and one block is exchanged while everything else holds still. Beneath it, the same desktop program on each host.
Mac host
Your program
13.1 KB
Library classes it uses
14.4 KB
Reactive core, instantiation & data
46.8 KB

Input, focus, gestures, scrolling

12.3 KB
137.3 KB
Browser · DOM
Browser · drawn to pixels (?render=canvas)
Mac host · no browser in the process
06
A web page embeds a Declare app with one attribute on a sized element; inside a Declare program, a DOMIsland gives a video player, an editor or a React widget a box of its own, and an AppIsland hosts another Declare program — this article is one, inside the homepage. In every direction the contract is the same: values provided, values exposed, and messages posted both ways.
07
In a browser a Declare app is a web page, not an imitation of one: text selects and finds, fields are native, scroll is the page’s own, links ⌘-click and Back tells the truth, and a crawler reads a static extraction from a plain host. What keeps the runtime honest is the second rendering: the DOM and the pixel renderer are held to the same picture on every change.
Reopen this page on the canvas renderer →
Read this page’s source →
This page is a Declare program; every figure on it is made of views. Sizes are measured from declarec production builds, gzipped at the default level, each time the site is built.

Rune: Rust VM for Emacs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
02:30:47 UTC
11:30:47 KST

R ust UN der E macs video introduction
This project is an experimental Emacs core written in Rust. The project is still at a very early phase but has the following goals:
- Bring multi-threaded elisp to Emacs
- Be “bug-compatible” with existing Emacs Lisp packages (everything should still work)
- Enable performance improvements (including faster GC, regex, and JSON) by leveraging the Rust Ecosystem.
See the design doc for more details.
The current goal of this project is to create an editor MVP. We have a basic elisp runtime, and we are working on adding basic editing functionality in a minimal GUI. This will include:
- buffer
- text insertion/deletion
- cursor
- line wrapping
- scrolling
- file IO
- display tables
If you want to contribute or have ideas for things to add, please open an issue.
Lisp files are currently pulled from
https://github.com/emacs-mirror/emacs/tree/emacs-29.1/lisp
Any modification for bootstrapping contain the tag RUNE-BOOTSTRAP.
- cargo run --release -- --repl
  Load the bootstrapped elisp and open the REPL
- cargo run --release -- --no-bootstrap --repl
  Open the REPL with only the builtin functions loaded
- cargo run --release
  Load the bootstrapped elisp and exit
Run the test suite with MIRI
MIRIFLAGS='-Zmiri-strict-provenance' cargo +nightly miri test
The project is defined by a main package rune, which depends on the crates included in the crates directory. One of those is the rune-macros crate, which defines the defun proc macro for defining builtin functions. The rest of the code is contained in src/. The modules are described below.
- objects
  The basic objects used in the interpreter. These are modeled after Emacs objects using tagged pointers with inline fixnums. Conversion between different primitives and object types is also found here.
- reader
  The Emacs lisp reader that translates a string to a cons cell. Due to the simple nature of lisp syntax, the reader is hand rolled and does not rely on any parsing libraries.
- env
  The global obarray. Currently, function bindings are global and immutable and value bindings are thread-local and mutable. When the ability is added to share data between threads, this will enable new threads to safely run functions without the need to copy them.
- gc
  Contains the allocator and garbage collector. All code for rooting and managing objects lives here as well.
- bytecode
  The bytecode VM. This uses the same opcodes as Emacs and uses the bytecomp.el to compile.
- interpreter
  The basic elisp interpreter. This is used only to bootstrap the elisp byte-compiler.
- fns, data, alloc
  These modules contain definitions of builtin in functions. Some of these are just stubbed out until the functionality is actually needed.
See the architecture doc for more info on the structure of Rust Emacs internals.
This project is moved forward by trying to load new elisp files and seeing what breaks. The best way to do that is with cargo run, which will load the currently bootstrapped files. The bootstrapped files are located in main.rs as part of the load function.
Usually what is needed is to implement more primitive functions. This is done with the defun macro. For example, if we wanted to implement the  substring function, we would first look at the lisp signature.
(substring STRING &optional FROM TO)
Then we would translate the types to their Rust equivalent. If the correct type is not known we can use Object. In this example we would write our Rust signature as follows:
#[defun]
fn substring(string: &str, from: Option<i64>, to: Option<i64>) -> String {...}
To load a new elisp file, run cargo run --release -- --load <file>.  Files that are not bootstrapped are not yet included in this repo, but are part of Emacs. Once the file is bootstrapped it can be added to the lisp directory.
Rune includes the `assert_elprop!` macro, which enables property testing against GNU Emacs. This macro works by sending the same Lisp form to both Rune and Emacs and then comparing their results. It can also be used in conjunction with the `proptest` crate.
#[test]
fn test_addition() {
    // Verify that (+ 40 2) evaluates to 42 for both Rune and Emacs
    assert_elprop!["(+ 40 2)"];
}
// Verify that make-vector works for any arbitrary byte
proptest! {
    #[test]
    fn test_make_vector_consistency(size in arb_byte()) {
        assert_elprop!["(make-vector {} ?A)", size];
    }
}
- Define WINEPREFIX: export WINEPREFIX=$PWD/.wine
- Add x86_64-pc-windows-gnu to rust toolchain: rustup target add x86_64-pc-windows-gnu
- Setup the wineprefix: `./scripts/setup-wineprefix`. This script will install Emacs inside the prefix as well
Now you can run the tests on Windows like that: cargo test --target x86_64-pc-windows-gnu. In case you use Nix enviroment you should skip 1 and 2 points.
Also, you can tell eglot to check the code marked with #[cfg(windows)]. Just add the following to .dir-locals.el and restart eglot:
((nil
       . ((eglot-workspace-configuration
           . (:rust-analyzer (:cargo (:target "x86_64-pc-windows-gnu")))))))
- tagged pointers in Rust
  My initial approach to creating tagged pointers in Rust. It serves as in intro to this project.
- implementing a safe garbage collector
  An overview of the garbage collector used in this project and how Rust enables safe GC abstractions.
- A vision of a multi-threaded Emacs :: Some ideas about how to add multi-threading to the existing language.
- Design of Emacs in Rust
  Some of the unique benefits that Rust could bring to Emacs.
- Remacs
  The original Rust and Emacs project. Remacs took the approach of enabling interop between Emacs C core and Rust, enabling them to replace parts of Emacs piecemeal. The project is currently unmaintained but is a big inspiration for Rune.
- emacs-ng
  The spiritual successor to remacs. This project integrates the Deno runtime into emacs, allowing you to write extensions in elisp or javascript. Which sounds cool if you happen to be a web developer. It really shows the power of integrating Emacs with a more modern ecosystem (which is part of the promise of Rust).
- helix
  A fast modern text editor written in Rust.
- crafting interpreters
  This was a big inspiration for this project, and it’s probably one of the best introductions to programming language implementations.

New York Bans Subscription and Post-Purchase Fees on Cars
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
02:27:02 UTC
11:27:02 KST

S.9348A/A.10411A Prohibits Car Companies from Charging for Built-In Features
Amid Rising Consumer Fees, New Law Increases Price Transparency and Puts Money Back in New Yorkers’ Pockets
Governor Kathy Hochul today signed a bill restricting motor vehicle manufacturers and dealers from charging consumers subscription fees after they purchase a vehicle. Amid rising consumer prices, the new law guards against a rising trend of car companies charging car owners fees to activate and use built-in features like heated seats, enhanced navigation, hands-free driving and entertainment options that do not present an ongoing expense for dealers and manufacturers.
“New Yorkers are already struggling with rising prices due to Trump’s affordability crisis, and we’re not going to let harmful business practices increase the cost of living even more,” Governor Hochul said. “People spending their hard-earned money on a car shouldn’t have to fork over even more money for features that should automatically come with the car. My message to car companies is clear: this kind of deceptive business model isn’t going to fly in New York State. Keep your hands out of New Yorkers’ wallets.”
S.9348A/A.10411A prohibits motor vehicle manufacturers, dealers or third-party service providers from adding subscription services or charging post-purchase fees for any motor vehicle feature that uses pre-installed components or hardware that do not have an ongoing cost. As vehicles become more advanced, the new law aims to establish clear rules preventing exploitative practices and prevent post-purchase fees as a future automotive business model.
New York Attorney General Letitia James said, “No one should have to pay recurring fees just to use features in a car they already bought. My office has secured millions of dollars for New Yorkers throughout the state who were charged illegal junk fees when purchasing a car. Unfair fees are fueling rising costs, and I am proud to work with Governor Hochul and the legislature to bring costs down.”
State Senator James Skoufis said, “People ought to know they’re able to use their already-installed remote start or heated seats during the winter without a monthly subscription. This new law will simply and clearly prohibit car manufacturers from ripping off New Yorkers who already paid for vehicle features when they purchased their car. It’s a common sense law and I’m grateful for the Governor and Assemblymember Magnarelli for their partnership enacting this legislation."
Assemblymember William B. Magnarelli said, "I thank Governor Hochul for recognizing the importance of this legislation, particularly as technology continues to evolve at a rapid pace. This law will help protect New Yorkers from unfair fees and ensure consumers aren’t paying extra for features already built into their vehicles.”
The law will take effect in 90 days on February 27, 2027.
This new law is the latest in Governor Hochul’s ongoing commitment to help address the cost of living by putting money back in the pockets of New Yorkers statewide. Under Governor Hochul’s leadership, she has:
- Secured reforms to lower auto insurance premiums for New Yorkers, battling fraud, limiting damages paid out to bad actors, and ensuring consumers, not insurance companies, are prioritized.
- Signed legislation requiring businesses to notify consumers of upcoming renewals and price changes as well as provide clear instructions on how to cancel subscriptions.
- Cut middle-class taxes to their lowest rates in 70 years.
- Tripled the Child Tax Credit, providing families up to $1,000 per child under the age of four and up to $500 for school-aged children, beginning in 2026.
- Made school meals free for every student, saving families $1,600 per child every year.
- Sent Inflation Refund checks to 8.2 million New Yorkers — up to $200 for individuals and up to $400 for families.
- Increased the maximum weekly unemployment benefit by $300 a week.

New York Bans Subscription and Post-Purchase Fees on Cars
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
02:26:23 UTC
11:26:23 KST

New York Bans Subscription And Post-Purchase Fees On Cars
Everything needs to be "smart" these days. Your refrigerator, your thermostat, your washing machine, etc., they all have some subscription fee or internet connectivity that doesn't actually help.
One of the most complained about "smart" devices though, is your car, but New York getting rid of that for good.
No Extra Fees!
On November 23rd, New York State Governor Kathy Hochul announced Bill S.9348A/A.10411A. This bill aims to put money back in New Yorkers pockets by prohibiting any vehicle manufacturers, dealers, or third-party providers from charging consumers more money for locked services or subscription fees.
This specifically targets newer vehicles that may lock pre-installed features (like heated seats or screen compatibility) behind a paywall or subscription service. This is becoming more and more of a problem as years go by, and consumers have been very vocal against the practice, but this is a tangible step that can be taken to end this practice before it gets too out of hand.
Why is This a Big Deal
As technology and pay-to-play services keep improving, consumers get less and less of what they paid for. What used to be an added feature in a vehicle now becomes a separate payment you have to take care of, sometimes monthly, instead of a down cost to install the service.
When asked about the bill, New York Attorney General Letitia James stated: "No one should have to pay recurring fees just to use features in a car they already bought. My office has secured millions of dollars for New Yorkers throughout the state who were charged illegal junk fees when purchasing a car. Unfair fees are fueling rising costs, and I am proud to work with Governor Hochul and the legislature to bring costs down."
The bill will go into effect on February 27th, 2027.

The Power of GraphQL, Without GraphQL
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-24
00:03:59 UTC
09:03:59 KST

COBALT — GraphQL from TypeScript, without writing GraphQL
The Power of GraphQL, without ``GraphQL``.  
The last framework that actually makes sense, in the age of AI. 
Why Cobalt?
It's simple and powerful.
It extends GraphQL but doesn't force you to use it manually.
You get the iteration speed of tRPC and the flexibility of GraphQL. This way you're not sacrificing your ability to craft complex & optimized GraphQL schemas for initial speed.
You can refine your API at every stage, without collecting dangerous tech debt.
Why not REST?
Endpoint Fatigue & Over-fetching
Managing hundreds of endpoints creates a maintenance nightmare. You fetch huge JSON blobs just to display a username.
The Cobalt Solution
One smart endpoint. The client specifies exactly what data it needs. The GraphQL feature everyone loves.
Why not pure GraphQL?
The Double Declaration Problem
You write a TypeScript interface. Then you write a GraphQL Schema. Then you write a Resolver. If they drift, your app crashes.
The Cobalt Solution
Single source of truth. Your TypeScript code IS your schema. No client-side codegen steps or schema sync required.
Why not tRPC?
Tight Coupling Trap
Great for monoliths, but your backend logic leaks into your client bundle. Scaling to microservices or exposing a public API later becomes a rewrite.
The Cobalt Solution
Loose coupling, magnetic types. The schema is the contract. You can move fast AND scale to complex backends.
The Cobalt Way
Magnetic Type Safety
Define your logic once. The Schema is inferred. The SDK is generated. The types are magnetically bonded across the network boundary.
The Cobalt Solution
The best of all worlds. The speed of tRPC, the flexibility of GraphQL, and the simplicity of REST.
Hate Typing?
Thirsty LLM?
Less is more.
A codebase that is fun to work with.
No Boilerplate
Stop writing glue code
Traditional frameworks require Controllers, manual serialization, and Service layers just to move data.
The Cobalt Solution
Write your logic. Cobalt generates the transport layer. No routers, no adapters, no manual schemas.
LLM Friendly
Context Rot
LLMs struggle with large, scattered codebases. Providing context across 50 files degrades performance and increases cost.
The Cobalt Solution
Dense, collocated code. Smaller context window footprint means smarter AI assistance and lower token costs.
Zero-Config SDK
Typed SDKs for free
Writing GraphQL queries manually is tedious and error-prone. Cobalt gives you a fully typed SDK out of the box.
The Cobalt Solution
sdk.query.user({ id })() - Autocomplete your entire backend, as if it was local code. No graphql tags, no codegen watchers.

Google alternative Kagi launches an email service

09:02:52 KST

Kagi, which already offers a paid alternative to Google Search and its own Orion browser as a rival to Chrome, is taking on another Google service: Gmail. Today, Kagi announced a public beta of Kagi Mail, a new email service that has “no surveillance, no ads, open standards, and no lock-in.”
Google alternative Kagi launches an email service
Kagi Mail is launching in public beta with a 60-day free trial.
With Kagi Mail, you get an @kagimail.com address, but the service also supports unlimited aliases and domains. It also offers 50GB of storage and a built-in calendar. On the roadmap, you can expect contacts and an address book in Q1 2027, but native mobile apps aren’t expected to arrive until Q3 2027.
“Does the world need another email service? We asked ourselves that question more than once,” Kagi says. “There are great ones out there, and we’re fans of what Fastmail and Tuta are doing. We still built our own, for the same reason we build everything at Kagi: technology should do its job and then get out of your way.”
Kagi Mail is available now with a 60-day free trial before you have to pay $99 per year. The service is available as part of a $25 per month Kagi Ultimate subscription.

Google Is Finally Retiring the Nest App Early Next Year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

Google Is Finally Retiring the Nest App Early Next Year (engadget.com) 20
			
		 	
				Google is shutting down its 15-year-old Nest app on January 25, 2027, requiring users to switch to Google Home to remotely control their smart home devices and receive notifications. "Then, on August 2, the company will retire Google Assistant on smart speakers and displays, with Gemini replacing the older tech for voice controls," reports Engadget. From the report:  If you've yet to migrate your Nest devices to the Google Home app, the company says your existing settings and schedules will transfer over automatically. The only thing you need to manually move over are any custom clips you might have saved over the years. You can do this by visiting the Nest website before January 25 and clicking the "Download all" button. Google says it will automatically migrate Nest Aware (1st Gen) subscriptions to its newer Google Home Premium package. "You will receive an email closer to the date of transfer detailing your subscription information," the company adds.  "The Nest app launched over 15 years ago, and home technology has changed a lot since then," Google says of the decision. "To give every Nest user Google's latest security protections and most advanced cyber defenses, plus new features from Gemini, we're bringing all Nest devices together in the Google Home app."

The hacker behind early GTA VI leaks has been sentenced — and released
Arion Kurtaj, who hacked Rockstar Games and leaked 90 videos of the game, was released after pleading guilty.
Prior to his sentencing, Kurtaj had already spent time in a hospital because of his autism and in prison.
“You are obviously a very talented young man,” the judge in the case said, according to Sky News. “Don’t waste those talents by spending the rest of your life in and out of prison.” Kurtaj cannot use platforms Telegram and Discord as part of a five-year Serious Crime Prevention Order, the BBC says.

The search for a top dark matter candidate
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
23:12:40 UTC
08:12:40 KST

The Search for a Top Dark Matter Candidate Was Losing Hope. Then Came a Potential Signal. Will It Be Enough to Energize the Struggling Field?
Physicists were growing weary with WIMPs, one proposed contender for the particles that make up dark matter. Despite their new cautious optimism, many challenges remain
Italy’s Gran Sasso National Laboratory is not a bad place to fret over the state of your career. The lab’s panoramic views of white-capped mountains and endless greenery are nearly inescapable. Physicists fuel their work with sharp espressos, rather than the watery coffee many Americans accept.
The lab also descends into the Gran Sasso mountain, forming one of the world’s largest underground research centers. Nestled beneath 4,600 feet of rock is an array of particle detectors. Thanks to the mountain’s thick skin, these instruments are shielded from cosmic rays, the high-energy particles produced by black holes, exploding stars and even our own sun. In the quiet depths of this laboratory, physicists hope to pick up on much more elusive particles—including dark matter.
For the past few decades, scientists have been hunting for dark matter, the invisible substance that makes up 85 percent of the matter in our universe. One primary target has been weakly interacting massive particles, or WIMPs—a hypothesized candidate for dark matter that passes through normal matter with barely a trace. Massive underground detectors like those at Gran Sasso are leading the search.
But no one has definitively found a WIMP, so no one is sure that they exist—and challenges to spotting them are building. As detectors get more sensitive, new background signals are beginning to cloud them, reducing the odds of catching WIMPs. Funding is growing more precarious, leaving many wondering how many years this endeavor has left.
When I visited the lab in May, the mood among some of these physicists was muted. “Of course, we were a bit more depressed,” reflects physicist Paolo Salomone, laughing. Despite his clear enthusiasm for the dark matter hunt, Salomone, a strikingly blue-eyed University of Chicago postdoctoral scholar, knew he was entering a field some might diagnose as terminal.
Then, in September, a pinprick of hope emerged. An American lab also searching for WIMPs released a preprint paper detailing a single—but intriguing—data point, rising above the expected background signals. Maybe, just maybe, it was dark matter.
Salomone was on vacation in Corsica when his phone started blowing up. Text notifications from physicist friends streamed in, asking him if he’d seen the news. “I was there on the beach trying to look at my phone, trying to zoom in on the plot,” he says. Watching the faraway excitement, he says, “I felt like I was missing out a lot.”
The finding was not a definite dark matter detection—one data point isn’t nearly enough to count as a discovery. Still, the result spawned headlines across the globe, a torrent of preprint papers and newfound hope for many physicists in search of WIMPs.
The question, now, is what’s next. Will this life raft be enough to overcome the field’s growing challenges? And will the few other detectors with similar sensitivity—most notably, one at Gran Sasso—be able to replicate the signal?
Nearly a century ago, Swiss astronomer Fritz Zwicky noticed a group of galaxies with some moving so quickly that they should have escaped the cluster’s gravity and flown apart. He theorized that some invisible material was gluing them together. Since then, scientists have accrued more evidence for this dark matter: stars speeding precipitously fast around a galaxy’s rim; unexpectedly strong gravitational lensing, in which distant light is bent by extreme gravity.
To explain it all, astrophysicists dreamed up WIMPs in the 1980s. As far as subatomic particles go, these would be relatively massive. In theory, they glide through space, on occasion lightly interacting with matter. Physicists have a pretty good sense of the density of dark matter scattered throughout our universe, but not much more about it. “It opens the possibility of having a lot of light particles or very few heavy particles,” says Salomone.
Directly detecting WIMPs, then, could clue us into the mass of individual dark matter particles and the number of them floating around, as well as how strongly these particles interact with normal matter. But finding them has been a challenge. Not only are physicists searching through a vast plain of possibilities, but they’re also looking for incredibly rare interactions that require extreme care in detection.
The smallest impurities can anger a detector. To illustrate the point, Marcello Messina, the technical coordinator for Gran Sasso’s XENONnT experiment, brought up the humble banana. While we don’t tend to think of a banana as radioactive, the fruits are rich in potassium, leading to a tiny shower of particle decays. “A banana can completely destroy our data table,” he said.
Messina was my guide for the day at Gran Sasso, driving me six miles through one of Italy’s longest tunnels and into the mossy interior of the mountain. He led me through the lab’s sterile halls, ending up at his even more sterile detector.
Messina’s XENONnT experiment is a hulking cylinder that holds a chamber of nearly 9.5 tons of xenon—mostly liquid—inside. The hope is that when a WIMP flies into the detector, it jostles one of the large and stable xenon atoms, which would in turn shoot out electrons and a light ray, alerting sensors to the strange particle’s presence.
XENONnT, which has been collecting data for around six years, is the strapping great-grandchild of the team’s original 2006 prototype. “XENON, for almost all its life, provided the most sensitive results,” Messina told me. “It was definitely leading the field.” Today, a few more rivals—like the American detector—have emerged.
We walked past another device that looked like a gigantic red Lego: a new detector Salomone is helping to build, DarkSide-20k, which will use a chamber of around 55 tons of argon instead of xenon. This enormous detector will reach a similar sensitivity to XENONnT and is set to begin collecting data in 2028.
Despite their materials’ purity, physicists expect some level of background fuzz—mainly radioactive decay products—and predict what that will look like. Sometimes, though, this “background” can behave in unexpected ways, so physicists must have a high bar for what anomalies in their data to take seriously.
Messina showed me a kind of memento mori for physicists: the latest generation of the DAMA experiment, which made a claim of dark matter detection that has been dismissed by most of the community.
In 1997, DAMA, which is made up of sodium iodide crystals, detected a signal that varied yearly, swooping in every summer—just as dark matter was expected to do. But while the team has continued to stand by their results, other experiments later ruled this out.
After DAMA’s false alarm, “I think people are just very cautious now,” says Ina Sarcevic, a physicist at the University of Arizona.
Despite the fear of more detector-cried-wolf scenarios, many physicists quickly pulled together theories to explain September’s announcement of a possible detection, Sarcevic says. The signal came out of South Dakota’s LUX-ZEPLIN (LZ) experiment, which is currently the world’s largest WIMP detector.
Physicist Richard Gaitskell, LZ’s spokesperson, has been searching for dark matter for nearly 40 years, starting with a detector “barely bigger than the top of my thumb,” he says. LZ, on the other hand, is a nine-foot-tall cylinder containing around 11 tons of xenon, packed within a 20-foot-tall outer structure.
To take advantage of LZ’s whopping size and sensitivity, Gaitskell says, the team “decided to really aggressively push” beyond the usual search parameters.
The longstanding assumption in these experiments has been that WIMPs swoop in at relatively low energies and bounce off the xenon like billiard balls. In this case, when a WIMP collides with a xenon nucleus, it scatters the atom’s protons and neutrons in what’s called a “coherent” manner, where their resulting energies add up, amplifying the signal. But if WIMPs were to barrel in at higher energies, the particles would scatter in a trickier way to track.
It was the switch to searching for these higher-energy collisions that led to LZ’s unusual detection. The signal also hinted that particles of dark matter would be quite heavy and collide with matter in a different way than many expected. Rather than rebounding like billiard balls, the WIMP may lose some energy (and mass) in the crash, exiting as a different, slimmer form of dark matter.
Notably, this detection couldn’t be explained by known forms of background events, like stray neutrons. The signal, then, could be dark matter, but it may also be a previously unknown type of background.
In announcing this event, Gaitskell says, the team was looking for input from the wider community, as they continue to pore through their own data. And input he did receive.
Amid the excitement and wave of preprint papers, though, many have retained a sense of caution. Messina says he was initially skeptical of the single data point. “The immediate reaction was, ‘Come on, one event?’” he says. But he recognized XENONnT’s potentially crucial role in the next step toward discovery.
Given their detector’s comparable sensitivity, the team is now rushing to replicate the finding, looking through the past two years of their own data for signals that chime with the American detection. Or they could find nothing.
Either way, Messina would like to let the team’s data analysis do the talking, rather than get too excited now. They hope to release a paper in the next few months. “Data matters,” Messina says. “All the rest is fake.”
When Messina led me around in the spring, he wasn’t optimistic about the future of his field. He was continuing to support the search for dark matter with XENONnT, but he had also been devoting time to detecting neutrinos, another kind of subatomic particle generated by stars, radioactive decay and the Big Bang. “Many people like me are thinking, ‘You know what, I want to investigate different fields,’” he said.
Some WIMP proponents point out that even though the detectors haven’t found one of these fabled particles, they have made a less obvious kind of progress over the decades. In their searches, scientists have ruled out many potential WIMP masses and densities, ticking away at the options and possibly making it easier to zero in on their true properties in the future.
But to many onlookers—including the funding bodies that control cash flow—the lack of clear progress in particle physics has been disappointing. “It’s quite hard to convince politicians to support you if you have a significant discovery each 30 years,” Messina said.
On top of the current fragile science funding ecosystem in the United States, he wasn’t sure how much more time WIMP detection had. He doubted the proposed next-generation detector, XLZD—which would dwarf LZ and XENON and merge the collaborations—would move forward.
After LZ’s finding, though, Messina’s tune has changed. While he still stresses the uncertainty that this signal will lead to a discovery, he believes it has imparted “some momentum” back into WIMPs.
Many physicists still see more promise in other dark matter models—the hypothetical WIMPs might not be the best (or only) explanation.
Ben Safdi, a theoretical physicist at the University of California, Berkeley, says that LZ’s detection may well be dark matter, but his initial excitement has waned. At first, the signal seemed to match an existing theory for WIMP behavior, but several preprint papers—including one of his—challenged this idea. “My personal belief that it is dark matter has gone down,” he says.
Safdi divides his research between WIMPs and a different dark matter candidate called axions. These theorized particles would move in a “continuous, almost fluid” wave through the universe, he says. Individually, axions would be super light—much less massive than WIMPs—but together, they could account for the missing mass of dark matter.
Attention to axions has surged as people have grown tired of WIMPs. While Safdi still thinks the WIMP hunt is worth continuing, “my heart is in axions,” he says.
Did you know? Other strategies to find WIMPs
- Some scientists hope to indirectly detect WIMPs by looking for neutrinos produced when they collide with their proposed antimatter counterpart and annihilate.
- Others are hoping to create WIMPs. According to some theories, WIMPs might be produced by energetic collisions between normal particles.
Even if interest in WIMP detection is revitalized, technological challenges remain. One specter still haunting the field is a fog of neutrinos that’s threatening to descend on the instruments.
With increased sensitivity, the detectors are starting to pick up the constant swarm of neutrinos. These tiny particles don’t interact much with matter—trillions move through your body every second—but they can clutter data taken by detectors that are precise enough to notice them. As this neutrino fog wafts in, potential WIMP signals could get lost in the haze. XENONnT reported its first glimpses of the fog in 2024.
This isn’t a huge problem for the detectors yet, and not everyone agrees it will be soon. But once neutrinos start meaningfully gumming up the signal, scientists don’t know what they’ll do.
Ronald Walsworth’s group is experimenting with a workaround: tracing the direction of incoming particles. WIMPs are expected to flood in like a “headwind” against the solar system’s path through the galaxy, he says. On the other hand, a lot of neutrinos emanate from the direction of the sun, produced by the churn of fusion.
A sea of liquid xenon functions “like an antenna,” says Daniel Ang, a quantum scientist in Walsworth’s group. The flash of light only clues scientists into a particle’s collision—not the direction it rode in. So to trace a signal’s source, physicists might have to take a whole new approach.
Walsworth decided to try this by using a more glamorous substance: diamonds. From his previous work, he knew that when particles cut through diamond crystals, they leave behind a finely detailed track—so their incoming direction is clear. “That’s the grandiose idea we had,” says Walsworth, a quantum physicist at the University of Maryland.
But the team has only tested their blinged-out detector on a small scale, and getting the funds to go bigger will be a challenge—especially given the “sagging of enthusiasm” for WIMPs, Walsworth says.
Once Salomone, the postdoc, got back from vacation, his excitement about LZ’s signal was slightly tempered by his boss, he says. “He was, of course, more skeptical and less enthusiastic than me.”
But even among Salomone’s liquid argon detector community, scientists are paying serious attention to the result. His collaboration has a regular meeting, which only one or two dozen people usually show up to. But at a recent one, some 100 people attended. Even people who never participated were interjecting with ideas, he says.
Salomone’s workdays are much like before, continuing to help build the DarkSide-20k detector, placing orders for special screws and popping over to the café for an espresso. But he feels a renewed excitement about the theory behind his daily tasks.
“You’re still doing what you were doing before but with a different mind-set,” he says. “Spending more time trying to read papers, spending more time trying to be even a better physicist, so that’s what I’m really happy about.”

Huang's Return Is the Biggest National Security Scandal in U.S. History
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
23:11:38 UTC
08:11:38 KST

Huang’s Return Is the Biggest National Security Scandal in U.S. History
Trump has put his pocketbook first and U.S. interests last.
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned in disgrace on Nov. 12 after his affair with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, was exposed and it became apparent that Nvidia was complicit in China’s evasion of U.S. export controls.
Now, under pressure from U.S. President Donald Trump, Nvidia’s board has reinstated Jensen Huang, saying that senior government officials promised that he posed “no national security risk.” This may be the biggest national security scandal in U.S. history.
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned in disgrace on Nov. 12 after his affair with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, was exposed and it became apparent that Nvidia was complicit in China’s evasion of U.S. export controls.
Now, under pressure from U.S. President Donald Trump, Nvidia’s board has reinstated Jensen Huang, saying that senior government officials promised that he posed “no national security risk.” This may be the biggest national security scandal in U.S. history.
That is a high bar. Consider the nearest competitor, the Iran-Contra affair. Officials in U.S. President Ronald Reagan’s administration, with the president’s knowledge, secretly sold weapons to Iran during the Iran-Iraq War, circumventing an arms embargo that Reagan publicly supported.
They did so both to secure Iran’s help in freeing U.S. hostages in Lebanon and to raise money for the Contras, a far-right Nicaraguan militant organization whose members committed atrocities against Nicaraguans and Americans alike. Once investigations began, officials orchestrated a cover-up. Reagan’s successor pardoned six individuals connected to the affair.
As morally foul as Iran-Contra was, it did not involve personal profiteering by the president and his associates. Nor did it involve the United States helping its principal geopolitical rival. Now, consider what is known about the Jensen Huang affair.
China and the United States are locked in an intense race over artificial intelligence. Compute—the raw processing power that depends on advanced chips—is central to that contest. The United States has an unusually powerful position in this market, as most advanced chips are designed by Nvidia and largely manufactured by the Taiwanese firm TSMC.
Both Nvidia and the White House were warned that Jensen Huang—a close Trump ally—was having an affair with a Chinese spy, including in a memo to senior officials overseen by U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio. But neither Trump nor Nvidia acted on these warnings.
Meanwhile, U.S. tech policy underwent a dramatic reversal. Last December, Nvidia struck a deal with the White House to sell its H200 chips to China in exchange for a 25 percent cut for the U.S. government, with no public details about how the funds will be used or overseen. Huang donated $25 million to the president’s ballroom, and Trump owns large amounts of Nvidia stock.
When news of the affair broke shortly after the U.S. midterms, it set off a cascade of revelations and investigations. Alice Huang, along with her husband and her business partner in Singapore, were found in multiple outlets’ reporting to be deeply involved in a network of firms allegedly established to evade U.S. restrictions and funnel advanced chips to China. Lawmakers also released information that revealed Nvidia had shipped more than 800,000 advanced chips to Chinese companies as of May 11, including to firms closely linked to the Chinese military.
A struggle broke out within Trump’s court, as Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick for failing to prevent China from accessing these chips. On top of that, Jensen Huang reportedly had another affair with a direct report and presided over an allegedly abusive workplace culture at Nvidia.
Trump’s response was to pressure Nvidia’s board for Huang’s reinstatement, potentially signaling that his administration would protect the company from punishment for its misdeeds. By Trump’s own admission, the decision followed a lengthy conversation with Chinese President Xi Jinping about the Nvidia leader. On Friday, Trump issued an executive order attempting to forestall congressional investigation into the deal.
The immediate beneficiary, apart from Nvidia’s stock price, is Beijing. China can now dismiss U.S. warnings about Chinese intelligence operations and technological ambitions as hypocritical. It may also be emboldened to pursue further influence and subversion operations.
Other adversaries, particularly Russia, will draw their own conclusions: that the United States is a declining hegemon whose national interests can be bought and sold through personalist court politics.
The scandal is unlikely to disappear quietly. Concerned intelligence and national security officials may continue leaking documents about Nvidia, potentially exposing other instances of corruption. Democrats will take this up as a major issue in the new Congress in January, and some Republicans may even join them.
The U.S. media now has a choice. It can treat this as a major national scandal, as it did Hillary Clinton’s emails in 2016 or Harvard President Claudine Gay’s improper academic citations in 2023, with front-page scrutiny for weeks or months. Or it can treat the affair as just another episode of the Trump show, or as a partisan political issue, and move on.
Supposed China hawks in the Trump administration, including Rubio and Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby, face a similar choice.
They can resign in protest, drawing national attention to the scandal and positioning themselves as a different kind of Republican for a future, cleaner administration. Or they can continue serving a president that puts his pocketbook ahead of national security. But they cannot keep telling themselves that they can make a difference from the inside.
James Palmer is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @beijingpalmer.bsky.social
