Both Nvidia and the White House were warned that Jensen Huang—a close Trump ally—was having an affair with a Chinese spy, including in a memo to senior officials overseen by U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio. But neither Trump nor Nvidia acted on these warnings.
Meanwhile, U.S. tech policy underwent a dramatic reversal. Last December, Nvidia struck a deal with the White House to sell its H200 chips to China in exchange for a 25 percent cut for the U.S. government, with no public details about how the funds will be used or overseen. Huang donated $25 million to the president’s ballroom, and Trump owns large amounts of Nvidia stock.
When news of the affair broke shortly after the U.S. midterms, it set off a cascade of revelations and investigations. Alice Huang, along with her husband and her business partner in Singapore, were found in multiple outlets’ reporting to be deeply involved in a network of firms allegedly established to evade U.S. restrictions and funnel advanced chips to China. Lawmakers also released information that revealed Nvidia had shipped more than 800,000 advanced chips to Chinese companies as of May 11, including to firms closely linked to the Chinese military.
A struggle broke out within Trump’s court, as Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick for failing to prevent China from accessing these chips. On top of that, Jensen Huang reportedly had another affair with a direct report and presided over an allegedly abusive workplace culture at Nvidia.
Trump’s response was to pressure Nvidia’s board for Huang’s reinstatement, potentially signaling that his administration would protect the company from punishment for its misdeeds. By Trump’s own admission, the decision followed a lengthy conversation with Chinese President Xi Jinping about the Nvidia leader. On Friday, Trump issued an executive order attempting to forestall congressional investigation into the deal.
The immediate beneficiary, apart from Nvidia’s stock price, is Beijing. China can now dismiss U.S. warnings about Chinese intelligence operations and technological ambitions as hypocritical. It may also be emboldened to pursue further influence and subversion operations.
Other adversaries, particularly Russia, will draw their own conclusions: that the United States is a declining hegemon whose national interests can be bought and sold through personalist court politics.
The scandal is unlikely to disappear quietly. Concerned intelligence and national security officials may continue leaking documents about Nvidia, potentially exposing other instances of corruption. Democrats will take this up as a major issue in the new Congress in January, and some Republicans may even join them.
The U.S. media now has a choice. It can treat this as a major national scandal, as it did Hillary Clinton’s emails in 2016 or Harvard President Claudine Gay’s improper academic citations in 2023, with front-page scrutiny for weeks or months. Or it can treat the affair as just another episode of the Trump show, or as a partisan political issue, and move on.
Supposed China hawks in the Trump administration, including Rubio and Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby, face a similar choice.
They can resign in protest, drawing national attention to the scandal and positioning themselves as a different kind of Republican for a future, cleaner administration. Or they can continue serving a president that puts his pocketbook ahead of national security. But they cannot keep telling themselves that they can make a difference from the inside.
James Palmer is a deputy editor at Foreign Policy. Bluesky: @beijingpalmer.bsky.social

====================================================================================================

Figma employee sues, alleging company forced her to confront harassers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
23:07:49 UTC
08:07:49 KST

A Figma engineer is suing the design software company and its co-founder and CEO, Dylan Field, over allegations that she faced sexual harassment and retaliation while managers and HR repeatedly told her to confront the very people she accused of misconduct.
The lawsuit, filed Monday in U.S. District Court for the Southern District of New York, alleges that Figma’s culture of “direct feedback” led managers and HR to make engineer Serry Park address harassment herself rather than investigate her complaints. Park, who joined Figma in 2022, alleges the company’s response damaged her career, compensation, and mental health. The lawsuit also alleges that Park, who is Asian, faced racial and gender discrimination, including performance criticism based on stereotypes that Asian women are meek, timid, and conflict-averse.
“Figma was my dream company for a long time,” Park told TechCrunch in an interview before the lawsuit was filed. “It was a place where I felt that I could level up in my career, working on a lot of really cool, interesting things at scale.”
Park is seeking damages, a jury trial, and a court order prohibiting Figma from requiring employees who report harassment to confront their alleged harassers. She also wants the company to adopt and enforce a process for investigating harassment complaints promptly and confidentially.
When reached for comment, a spokesperson for Figma said, “Figma does not tolerate discrimination or harassment. Figma also does not tolerate extortion. These claims have no merit, and we will present the facts in court.”
Faith in the Process
Park told TechCrunch she initially had faith in Figma’s complaint process, expecting that if she documented the alleged harassment and reported it to the right people, the company would act. But as an engineer in a male-dominated field, Park told TechCrunch, “I don’t feel that they took me seriously throughout this process.”
Park, who is still employed at Figma, joined its San Francisco office in October 2022. Within weeks, her onboarding mentor — a fellow engineer — asked her on a date and continued pursuing her after she declined, Park alleges. She initially feared reporting him, worrying that her manager would “dismiss her reports or view her as the problem,” according to the lawsuit.
Park says she tried to avoid her onboarding mentor. In early 2023, for example, she delayed choosing a work project until he had picked one. When her manager criticized her for not being a “team player,” Park explained that she was trying to avoid the colleague she accused of harassment, according to the suit.
Her manager allegedly dismissed her concerns, saying he had “met his wife at work too” and treating the behavior as “harmless flirtation.” When Park asked to be kept off projects with the onboarding mentor, HR allegedly instructed her to confront him directly about his behavior.
The lawsuit ties that response to Figma’s culture of direct feedback, which Park alleges Field established and enforced. It says the company’s People organization trains managers to direct employees, including those reporting harassment, to give feedback directly to the person involved.
In March 2023, her manager assigned him to a major project she was leading, despite her request to be separated from him. Park told her manager that she would “figure it out,” but alleges that having to work with him affected her performance. Her manager gave her a 2 out of 5 on her performance review, criticizing her for not speaking up enough or engaging with others and for lacking presence. Park was put on a coaching plan, which she completed in late 2023.
Changing managers didn’t help, according to the lawsuit. Her second manager also refused to separate them, allegedly telling Park that someone at her level should be able to resolve conflicts with teammates and that separating them would be “avoiding the problem.” Like her previous manager, he allegedly pointed out that he had met his partner at work.
Park alleges the harassment continued throughout 2023.
In April 2024, she transferred to another team, a move she claims set back her career by resetting her performance and promotion timeline.
On her new team, which had more women, Park said she felt better and no longer had to work with her alleged harasser. Her performance reviews improved, but in late 2025, she alleges another incident of sexual harassment, this time by her new manager.
The incident followed a night out for drinks with her wife and two teammates, including her manager. The next day, during a team lunch, the manager allegedly said Park’s sex life had been discussed the previous evening. Park, who said no such conversation had occurred in her or her wife’s presence, felt “horrified and humiliated” by comments she believed fetishized queer relationships.
A colleague reported the comments to HR as sexual harassment, according to the suit. Weeks later, Park’s manager apologized in a Slack message, saying she didn’t recall what she had said. When Park sought HR’s guidance, she was again told to address the matter directly with her manager.
During their conversation, the manager allegedly became defensive, and the encounter left Park feeling “crushed, anxious, and vulnerable.” The manager also allegedly said she would never make such a comment because she had “a lot of gay friends.”
The manager told HR that they had agreed it was a misunderstanding. HR closed its investigation without interviewing witnesses, according to the suit, even though Park disputed her manager’s account.
“Requiring an employee to confront her harasser as the price of complaining — and then closing her complaint — would dissuade a reasonable employee from complaining at all,” the suit said.
Park alleges her manager retaliated by publicly berating her, sometimes so aggressively that colleagues intervened or checked on her afterward, according to the lawsuit. The suit also alleges that other teammates left the team over concerns about the manager’s behavior.
Park again complained to HR about “escalating retaliation” and expressed concern the manager would “derail her career.” HR allegedly defended the manager’s conduct, saying the manager was “within her reasonable authority” and again instructed Park to address her concerns directly with the manager.
In early 2026, Park received another low performance review and a reduced bonus. The lawsuit alleges that similarly situated men continued to receive promotions, raises, and bonuses. When Park reported the negative performance review to HR in March, she was allegedly told her sexual harassment complaint against her manager could not be “substantiated.”
Park took medical leave in March to receive treatment for PTSD symptoms and generalized anxiety. Cleared to return to work in June, she was prescribed workplace accommodations, including a graduated work schedule and mandatory remote workdays.
Park told TechCrunch that Figma approved the accommodations but had not implemented them at the time she returned to work. In July, Park and her lawyers notified Figma of their intent to pursue legal action.
“It’s really scary for me,” she said about taking legal action. “I don’t know what’s going to happen next with this, I don’t know what the outcome is going to be, but at least it feels like this is my chance to do the right thing.”
This piece was updated to clarify when Park said Figma implemented the recommendations from her medical professionals.

====================================================================================================

Freeing the iPod Nano 5G
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
23:03:13 UTC
08:03:13 KST

A few years ago, CUB3D demonstrated unsigned code execution on the iPod nano 6G and 7G through ipod_sun, a font-based exploit. Whether the same approach would work on the 5G was still an open question.
I wanted to get Rockbox running on the nano 5G, so I set out to adapt the exploit. My motivation was simple: an up-to-date music player without an internet connection. I built a fully untethered boot chain that loads Rockbox from the nano 5G’s NAND, with no computer or button sequence needed after installation. I also found a way to make the existing exploit fully untethered on the nano 7G, and likely the 6G too.
Existing DFU exploits, including q3k’s wInd3x, provide code execution on the nano 5G, but booting custom code this way requires a computer each time. I used wInd3x to explore the device and develop a convenient, fully untethered exploit. The firmware details below refer to the latest nano 5G firmware, 1.0.2 (34A20020).
The catch with ipod_sun
The nano 5G’s NAND flash appears through a flash translation layer (FTL) as a logical disk with 4 KiB sectors. Its partition table separates a small private area for persistent device state, a firmware partition containing boot programs and resources, and a data volume for user files. The firmware partition has a directory of named images; the fonts live inside rsrc, a resource volume formatted as FAT16.
The original ipod_sun chain combines a resource-validation bypass with a font-parser vulnerability. Apple’s firmware directory identifies the normal operating system as osos, the disk-mode program as disk, and the resource image containing fonts as rsrc. The disk-mode boot path does not request the resource signature check used by normal OS boot.
Swapping the disk and osos directory entries exploits that difference: selecting disk mode now launches Apple’s regular OS under the disk-mode policy. The OS still reads its fonts, but the bootloader has not required a valid signature for the modified resource image.
The font then triggers CVE-2010-1797, the FreeType CFF stack-overflow vulnerability also known through the star exploit. A malformed font program corrupts the parser’s stack and redirects execution. My 5G port adapts that technique to the device’s font and execution context.
The swapped images remain on disk, but each restart still requires choosing disk mode with the device’s buttons. A normal boot selects the other entry. I wanted a better experience, with the modified font loading through the normal OS path.
Finding a bypass in the normal boot path
The bootloader is organized into EFI-style modules stored as Portable Executable (PE) images. The module that passes execution to the next boot stage decides whether to validate rsrc. It first asks the PrivateData module for FirmwareStatus, which comes from an authenticated record in the private NAND partition.
The decisive branch is below. This is reconstructed C from the reverse-engineered routine, with unrelated fuse handling and protocol setup omitted.
A value of 2 returns success before the resource-validation query. Bit 0 must remain clear: it is checked first, so 3 would reject the boot attempt. Tracing the restore OS shows that it sets the status to 1 during firmware transfer and clears it to 0 at the end. This suggests an update-in-progress guard that prevents booting an incomplete installation.
Apple’s resource-mount path writes status 2 only on the first successful boot, after rsrc has passed signature verification. My hypothesis is that this avoids repeating signature verification to shorten boot time or reduce energy use.
This is the second bypass. Instead of disguising the normal OS as disk mode, I can leave the firmware directory alone and supply a valid private record with bit 1 set. The normal boot path then accepts the modified resource image, and its font delivers code execution automatically.
The record lives in the private partition, identified by MBR type 0x3e. On the 5G layout I examined, the partition begins at logical block 1, with 4 KiB blocks. The bootloader reads a 512-byte header from its start. The status-bearing format begins with the little-endian magic PRVD and has this layout:
The integrity tag is device-specific, so creating and saving a valid PRVD record requires code execution on that iPod.
The tag is the first 16 bytes of the SHA-1 digest, encrypted with the device’s hardware AES UID key, selected through key slot 2. It authenticates the private header, but as the authentication is constructed on device we can falsify it if we have code execution.
Using emCORE to make it persistent
To inspect and update the private partition, I adapted emCORE, an older framework from freemyipod’s earlier iPod exploitation and boot work. It provided an existing USB-controlled environment to extend for the 5G.
I wrote the nano 5G NAND driver and integrated it into emCORE alongside the flash translation layer (FTL) and hardware AES access. I’ll cover the flash memory sequencer subsystem (FMSS), which enables NAND writes, in a future post. The FTL presents logical sectors over physical flash; it is the layer through which I read and write the private record and install the modified font.
The private-status tool verifies the existing header and tag, constructs the updated record, and obtains its new tag from the device. It rechecks the partition table and header before writing, then flushes the write and reads it back. The tool writes a complete 4 KiB logical sector even though the bootloader only consumes the smaller header. This works on nano 5G hardware.
Once the private record and modified font are installed, they remain in NAND across restarts. emCORE and the host computer are needed for the installation workflow, not for each subsequent boot.
A bootloader in less than 28 KB
Reaching the font parser automatically solved persistence. The next constraint was fitting something useful inside the font. I wanted it to load Rockbox from storage, so the payload needed a NAND driver, a flash translation layer, a filesystem reader, and enough startup code to hand off reliably.
To fit, I compiled with size and link-time optimizations and had the linker remove unused sections. The Rockbox kernel is stored on the device’s FAT32 partition and can be easily updated.
Taking control from the font parser
Code execution initially arrives in a damaged FreeType call frame. The stack has been corrupted, and Apple’s OS has left interrupts, DMA controllers, timers, caches, and address translation configured for its own use. Jumping straight into a large C program would carry that state into storage initialization.
I split the bootstrap into two images. The first is the 808-byte assembly stub at 0x22050000. Its job is to leave the parser context: mask IRQs and FIQs, switch to a private stack, stop inherited timer activity, quiesce DMA and clock domains, and clean up caches and the MMU before entering the main payload. It replaces the damaged stack and then jumps to the Rockbox kernel loader.
The second image is the main loader at 0x22060000. Its entry code disables the watchdog before starting the NAND scan, installs its own stack, and clears the uninitialized-data area before calling C. The watchdog matters because reconstructing the logical flash mapping can take several seconds; a reset in the middle would stop the boot before it ever found Rockbox.
This separation keeps the most fragile stage small. The assembly stub establishes a controlled execution environment; the main payload can then do the more substantial storage work in freestanding C.
Reading NAND and starting Rockbox
At the hardware level, the NAND driver uses FMSS, a programmable microcontroller, to communicate with the NAND chips. lemonjesus’s earlier work on the S5L8702 sequencer, published as S5L8702-FMISS-Tools, documents its instruction set and provides tools for inspecting and building bytecode. That research is important prior work for understanding this part of the storage hardware; my work here uses the sequencer to read NAND as part of the nano 5G boot path.
The loader first checks that the microsecond timer used for driver timeouts is running. It then initializes the read-only NAND and FTL stack, mounts the FAT filesystem, and opens /.rockbox/rockbox.ipod. The core sequence follows the Rockbox bootloader’s own file-loading path:
The file loader checks the Rockbox model identifier and checksum, then leaves the executable image at 0x08000000. The final handoff keeps interrupts masked, cleans and invalidates caches, drains pending writes, and branches to that address. Rockbox’s startup code takes over from there. If storage, mounting, or image loading fails, the payload shows the failing stage on the LCD and stops.
Result and limits
On my nano 5G, the result is a cold, battery-powered boot straight into Rockbox, with no USB connection and no disk-mode button sequence. The PRVD partition setting skips rsrc validation and, when combined with the star exploit, enables untethered boot straight into Rockbox. The main constraint is that the font payload is limited to 27,874 bytes. Installation and restoration procedures, along with the reverse-engineering notes and build details, are in the nano5g_bootstub and nano5g_emCORE repositories below.
I have confirmed that the private-data partition works the same way on the nano 7G, which suggests the method will likely work on the 6G too. I plan to get the nano 4G and 5G Rockbox ports into good shape before applying this method to the 6G and 7G.
References
- nano5g_bootstub: the font exploit, PRVD analysis, two-stage Rockbox loader, and exact size budget.
- nano5g_emCORE: the emCORE port, NAND read/write tools, authenticated private-record updates, and installation procedure.
- CUB3D’s ipod_sun: the earlier nano 6G/7G chain combining the disk-mode swap with a modified font.
- q3k, “wInd3x, the iPod Bootrom exploit 10 years too late”: the USB/DFU entry point used to run unsigned code during setup, with background on the iPod boot chain.
- Apple’s firmware update catalog: lists nano 5G firmware 1.0.2 (34A20020) under iPod software family 34.
- CVE-2010-1797: the existing FreeType CFF vulnerability used by the font exploit.
- freemyipod’s emCORE source: the original framework and earlier device targets on which the nano 5G adaptation builds.
- lemonjesus’s S5L8702-FMISS-Tools: earlier reverse engineering of the flash-controller sequencer, with instruction-set documentation and bytecode tools.
- n3g_fmss: my documentation of the nano 3G flash memory sequencer.
- n4g_fmss: my documentation of the nano 4G flash memory sequencer.
- Rockbox source: upstream firmware, storage, filesystem, and image-loading code reused in this work.

Trump Halts Army's Plans to Detonate Bomb-Making Chemicals In Iowa
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Trump Halts Army's Plans to Detonate Bomb-Making Chemicals In Iowa (arstechnica.com) 51
			
		 	
				President Trump has ordered the military to halt plans to detonate nearly five tons of unstable lead azide explosives at an Army ammunition plant in Middletown, Iowa. "Trump claimed that the fate of the unstable, highly explosive material is now in the hands of a federal court and Rob Sand, Iowa's governor-elect, who won't be sworn into office until January," reports Ars Technica. From the report:  The Army had planned to detonate the barrels on November 20, but delayed the blast on Thursday amid public outcry and a legal challenge from environmental groups. The explosion will launch a plume of toxic gases and neurotoxic lead that could reach up to 100 miles away from the Iowa facility, the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant (IAAAP).
Iowa residents and officials said they learned of the detonation only days beforehand. The environmental groups, led by Earthjustice, claimed in an emergency court filing that the Army had not fully considered safer options for disposing of the lead azide before planning the detonation.
Late Thursday, a federal appeals court granted Earthjustice's request to put a hold on the Army's permit from the Environmental Protection Agency, which allowed for the controlled detonation to be carried out. Around the same time, Acting Secretary of the Army Adam Telle said the detonation would be postponed until Monday so the military could communicate with Iowans about the situation and plans.

The Beauty of Artists' Studios
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:48:33 UTC
07:48:33 KST

issue 21 November 2026
Sir Frederic Leighton was president of the Royal Academy, one of the foremost sculptors and painters of his day. And yet one imagines his neighbours may still have been taken aback when, in 1881, he added to his home, on a quiet street in Kensington, an Arab Hall three storeys tall, topped with a crescent moon.
At least they could finally see what the artist had been up to on his annual solo trips to the east: shopping. Inside, Moorish, Turkish, Byzantine and Pompeiian tiles and styles mingle like layers on a cake, and yet the effect is beautiful, perhaps because Leighton commissioned star ceramicists Walter Crane and William De Morgan to bring all his finds together. The centrepiece was a tinkling fountain and impluvium, complete with goldfish.
Today the fish are gone, but Leighton House is lovingly restored, and still a bold aesthetic gambit. Opulently fitted out with chartreuse silk walls and considered effects of light at every turn, it was Leighton’s mini-museum and his stage set, where visitors stepped into the atmosphere of his paintings. Built to Leighton’s specification by George Aitchison, it was designed for display and hospitality on the grandest scale – Queen Victoria visited in 1869. But Leighton’s bedroom, opposite his studio, was plain and monk-like, with a little single bed, as if he were married, in a sense, to art.
A new show, Magic and Mess: The Artist’s Studio Revealed, which will be in the exhibition space until March next year, puts Leighton House in context, with 56 paintings, drawings and albumen photographic prints depicting artists’ studios from the long 19th-century. Although Leighton House is the only purpose-built Victorian artist’s studio-home now open to the public in London, it was once of a type, comparable with the bohemian palaces of his peers, those of G.F. Watts, Val Prinsep, Marcus Stone and J. Pettie (whose home, The Lothians, on Fitzjohns Avenue was a bold Scottish baronial) – all now lost and fabled.
Studios ranged from high to low, ‘basilica to bedsit’, as the catalogue has it. Antonio Canova’s studio and birthplace in Rome formed part of a must-see visitors’ complex, including consecrated temple. But at the other extreme, Sir Joseph Nollekens’s studio in Fitzrovia was shabby and troubled by rats, and the local brothel-keeper and supplier of life models, Mrs Lobb, kept coming in demanding money. All this was exposed in a tell-all memoir by a former employee published in 1829.
Most of the works on display in Magic and Mess come from Katrin Bellinger, who collects on the theme of ‘the artist at work’. Bellinger, formerly a dealer at Colnaghi specialising in Old Master drawings, is a benefactor of the arts, with a penchant for art about art; paintings about painting.
I say penchant, but her collection, growing since 1985, now includes 2,000 pieces. They rotate between being stored at her research facility, going on loan, and hanging on the walls of her home, not far from Leighton House. Initially she specialised in works on paper, but when her family expressed a preference for living with daylight, she started broadening her acquisitions.
‘I am fascinated by the mystique of the creative process,’ she tells me. ‘Artists on the road, artists in the studio, artists’ models – there are so many avenues to explore. That’s why I never got bored. I love the stories, the connections and finding great work by more obscure artists.’ For example, the painting of Alma-Tadema’s first London home, Townshend House, by Emily Epps Williams, the artist’s sister-in-law. It is a lovely, detailed panel painting – long and thin (82cm x 19cm), like looking through a keyhole. A sinuous blue hookah pipe hose lies negligently across the threshold, implying that the decor itself – the secret gleams of old gold in the dark corners, the daylight intensifying the red frescoed ceiling to blood – is in an altered state.
But in 1880s, when she painted this panel, Townshend House was already a memory, badly damaged when a passing barge on Regent’s Canal exploded, carrying nuts, coffee, petroleum and gunpowder: you can imagine the mess. The Alma-Tademas moved to St John’s Wood and began decorating afresh, with zeal; Emily Epps Williams completed no other known subjects.
The signifiers for a 19th-century artist included, of course, the brushes, the beret and the flaçon of wine. But also, the artist’s stove. It was a practicality, especially if a model was going to undress – and a symbol of passion and the creative spark. Once you get your eye in, you notice them in so many depictions of artists. Cézanne’s soot-black pot-boiler, grumpy, dumpy, but tenaciously alight, is on display, loaned from the National Gallery – an early work from about 1865, which he gave to Émile Zola, his childhood friend.
Successful artists’ studios were bedecked with objets: bronze jugs, feathers, rich fabrics, rugs, palms, taxidermy. There would be assorted hands, arms and casts. By the 1880s this was known as ‘Makartstil’ after the lavish interior of Hans Makart’s studio in Vienna. Leighton, though more restrained, still had a peacock (stuffed). Even the unsuccessful artist, the bohemian wannabe, had to have some objets, as we see in the satirical staged photograph ‘Glory and the Stew’ (Marie-Alexandre Alophe, 1858). Here, turning in one easy gesture from his easel to his lunch, the humble artist nevertheless has two useful treasures, hanging on the stovepipe behind him: two plaster-cast ears.
If windows were an obsession for artists, so, too, were window shades, seen here in opulent Mexican onyx (Alma-Tadema’s ‘In My Studio’, 1893) or Moorish tracery (Théodor Jacques Ralli, photographed in his Paris studio by Edmond Bénard, 1882). In ‘The Studio’, from c.1910, Sir William Orpen uses terrible light from badly controlled Venetian blinds to create a bold painterly pattern. But almost a century earlier, in Kalman Dery’s ‘The Painter’s Studio’ (1883), the struggling artist cannot yet see how to make everyday experience into art, and lies there, becalmed, with a drab improvised sheet over the window, plus some desiccated corn stalks representing, perhaps, his dried-up inspiration.
Artists’ paraphernalia range from the set squares and mahlstick that project such a calm air of mastery in ‘Caspar David Friedrich in his Studio’ (1811) by Georg Kersting, to the teetering piles of picture references and scattered pencils that surround Victor-Charles-François Mongodin’s ‘A Painter’ (1850). The last seems like chaos, but look closer and the artist is clearly in the ultimate flow state, the mountain range of mess his perfect working conditions.
‘When painters depict painters, they often bring their best game,’ says Bellinger. ‘My parents collected expressionists because that was what you collected in Germany after the war, because the Old Masters were tainted by the Third Reich.’ She went to art school but ‘thankfully stopped after that, as I had no talent. I love to see artists at work. I enjoy the sense of peering over their shoulder, seeing how they do it.’
The artists here are often wearing slippers, hunched over, their intense concentration amplified, twinned with that of the unseen artist who is painting them. But the albumen silver photographic prints on display in the final room of this exhibition show no such intimacy, with the master artists rarely depicted at work, but posing for the camera, like Lord Leighton, dressed smartly, and performatively holding his brushes and palette.
Leighton was fastidiously respectable – the first artist to join the peerage by dint of his talent – and his models had their own off-street entrance and staircase up to his studio, with a changing area and fireplace. It was a practical working studio: the ‘picture slot’ in the wall meant his largest canvases could be posted out and winched down to the pavement without having to be removed from their stretchers. The same space became an otherworldly concert hall at night, when Bach’s Chaconne was played under the gold-painted dome in the presence of the artist and his paintings.
But the maximalist, objet-stuffed studio was not to last, as we see from Matisse’s ‘Studio Under the Eaves’ (on loan from the Fitzwilliam Museum). Painted in 1903, the darkness of the empty attic implies that all the artist needs is the life-giving sunlight from the window, its ceaselessly fascinating reflections and shadows, and one flower. Fauvism and freedom are coming! The artist, as this absorbing exhibition reveals, often provides the most incisive commentary on art.
Magic and Mess: The Artist’s Studio Revealed is at Leighton House until 21 March 2027.

====================================================================================================

Ladybird, the first new browser engine in years, is now available to test
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:38:09 UTC
07:38:09 KST

Ladybird, an independent web browser with a built-from-scratch engine, is finally available for testing. On Monday, Ladybird developer Andreas Kling announced that the browser is launching in alpha on macOS and Linux following more than four years of development.
Ladybird, the first new browser engine in years, is now available to test
Looking for a web browser without Google Chrome underneath? You can try this independent web browser now on macOS and Linux.
The release makes Ladybird the first browser in years to arrive with an entirely new engine. We’ve seen many engines emerge over time, but the most popular browsers use just three today: Gecko, Blink, and WebKit. While Gecko, now used in Mozilla Firefox, first appeared in Netscape 6 in 2000, Apple’s WebKit arrived in Safari in 2003 as a fork of KDE’s KHTML. A decade later, the Google-backed Chromium project forked WebKit to create Blink, which now powers Google Chrome, Microsoft Edge, Opera, and more.
Ladybird is backed by a nonprofit organization with the goal of creating “an independent, fast and secure browser that respects user privacy and fosters an open web,” according to its website. Though Ladybird development initially began as part of a C++ project, its developers eventually began porting the code to Rust using AI.
In his post announcing the alpha, Kling notes that the browser built around the Ladybird engine “is modest but functional,” adding that “some websites won’t work” during the alpha. A Windows version of Ladybird will arrive during a “later stage” of the project.

====================================================================================================

Stalwart is now a drop-in replacement for Microsoft Exchange
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:32:29 UTC
07:32:29 KST

Stalwart is now a drop-in replacement for Microsoft Exchange
For more than twenty years, “the mail server” in most companies meant Microsoft Exchange, and “the mail client” meant Outlook. Today we are releasing Stalwart 1.2.0, and with it Stalwart becomes a drop-in replacement for Microsoft Exchange.
Stalwart now implements the protocols that Exchange clients speak: MAPI over HTTP, Exchange Web Services (EWS), Exchange ActiveSync and Autodiscover. Outlook, Apple Mail and the mail apps on phones and tablets connect to Stalwart as they would to an Exchange server, with mail, calendars, contacts, tasks and notes in one account. Users type their email address and password, Autodiscover does the rest, and they keep working in the client they already know.
Exchange support is part of the open source Community edition, under the AGPL.
Exchange on premises in 2026
Section titled “Exchange on premises in 2026”
Before going into what Stalwart now does, it is worth looking at where self-hosted Exchange stands today, because it explains why so many organisations are looking for a way out.
Microsoft has not announced the end of Exchange Server. On paper, on-premises Exchange still exists. In practice, every road Microsoft is building leads to Exchange Online:
- Exchange 2016 and 2019 are out of support. Both reached end of support on 14 October 2025. Microsoft sold a paid Extended Security Update programme for a while, and according to press reports of an Exchange team statement, it ended in October 2026 with no further extension. Servers still running those versions receive no fixes of any kind.
- No new features. Exchange Server Subscription Edition (SE) is now the only supported on-premises Exchange, and there will be no more major versions after it. SE does not receive the features developed for the cloud. Even its first cumulative update has slipped from late 2025 to an undetermined date in 2026.
- The new Outlook does not support it. Microsoft’s own documentation for the new Outlook for Windows says it plainly: “On-premises Exchange accounts aren’t supported.”
- The APIs are moving to the cloud. New functionality ships only on Microsoft Graph, a cloud API. Exchange Web Services in Exchange Online started being switched off in October 2026 and will be gone entirely in April 2027.
None of these steps forces anyone to move on its own. Together, they leave an Exchange administrator with a shrinking set of choices: keep running a server that will not grow, or move the organisation’s mail into Microsoft’s cloud.
For many organisations the second option is not acceptable. Email is the record of a company’s contracts, negotiations, legal advice, HR matters and customer data. Placing it with a US provider means placing it within reach of US law, including the CLOUD Act, which allows US authorities to compel American providers to hand over data they control wherever it is stored. Public administrations, healthcare providers, law firms, defence contractors and many European companies cannot accept that, or no longer want to. Data sovereignty means that you decide where your data lives, under which jurisdiction, and who can read it. A mail service in someone else’s cloud cannot offer that, and an on-premises server whose vendor is steering its customers toward that cloud offers less of it every year.
Outlook without Exchange
Section titled “Outlook without Exchange”
The obstacle to leaving Exchange has rarely been the server. It has been the client.
Many people have spent their whole working life in Outlook. They have years of muscle memory in it: their folder layout, their rules, their calendar views, their shared mailboxes, the way they book meeting rooms. Many organisations also have workflows built around it, such as delegated calendars managed by an assistant. Replacing Exchange with a standard IMAP and CalDAV server has always meant telling every one of those users to switch to a different mail client, or to reconfigure Outlook into a reduced IMAP mode where calendars and contacts no longer sync. For a migration project, that is often where the plan dies.
With Stalwart 1.2.0 the users do not need to change anything. The administrator moves the mailboxes and points the DNS records at Stalwart, and users connect with the same Outlook they used before.
Supported protocols and clients
Section titled “Supported protocols and clients”
Stalwart implements the following Microsoft protocols, all served on the same HTTPS port as JMAP and the WebUI:
- MAPI over HTTP (EMSMDB and NSPI), the native protocol of classic Outlook for Windows, in both cached and online mode. It covers mail, calendars, contacts, tasks and notes, incremental synchronisation, the global address list and the offline address book, delegate access, shared mailboxes, rules, junk email settings, reminders, search folders and read receipts.
- Exchange Web Services, schema versions Exchange 2010 SP2 to Exchange 2016: mail, calendars, contacts, tasks, folders, sharing and delegates, free/busy, inbox rules, out of office, name resolution, distribution lists, and streaming and pull notifications.
- Exchange ActiveSync 14.1 and 16.1: mail, calendars, contacts, tasks and notes, with push through Ping, meeting responses, search and out of office.
- Autodiscover in its POX, SOAP and JSON dialects, so clients configure themselves from an email address and a password.
That set of protocols is what the following clients use to talk to an Exchange server, and they can connect to Stalwart the same way:
- Classic Outlook for Windows (Outlook 2016 and later, including Microsoft 365 Apps), over MAPI over HTTP and EWS.
- Outlook for Mac, over EWS.
- Apple Mail, Calendar and Contacts on macOS, through an Exchange account over EWS.
- Mail, Calendar and Contacts on iPhone and iPad, through an Exchange account over ActiveSync.
- Android mail apps with Exchange account support, such as Gmail, Samsung Email and Nine, over ActiveSync.
- Thunderbird, with its built-in Exchange support for mail over EWS, or with TbSync for calendars, contacts and tasks over ActiveSync.
- Evolution (through evolution-ews) and eM Client, over EWS.
- Libraries and tools built on EWS or ActiveSync, such as exchangelib.
Shared data
Section titled “Shared data”
The Exchange protocols are not a separate silo inside Stalwart. They are another door into the same accounts. A message filed in Outlook is in the same mailbox an IMAP or JMAP client sees, a meeting accepted on a phone appears in the calendar that CalDAV clients read, and a contact added in Apple Contacts shows up in the CardDAV address book. A user can keep Outlook on the office desktop, the native mail app on a phone and a JMAP webmail in the browser, all on one account.
Rules created in Outlook’s Rules and Alerts are stored on the server as mail rules, which run as Sieve when the message is delivered. They apply when Outlook is closed and whatever client the user opens next. Delegates and folder permissions set in Outlook become regular Stalwart shares, so they apply to every protocol. Shared mailboxes open automatically in Outlook from group membership, as they do with Exchange.
Not every part of an Exchange deployment has a counterpart. Stalwart does not offer public folders, archive mailboxes, mobile device management, RPC over HTTP (Outlook 2010 and earlier) or NTLM and Kerberos sign-in: clients authenticate with a password, an app password or OAuth. The Exchange documentation covers configuration, DNS and Autodiscover, each family of clients, and the differences from Exchange that administrators should know about before a migration.
New Outlook
Section titled “New Outlook”
One Outlook is missing from the list above: the new Outlook for Windows, the one that Windows now installs by default and that classic Outlook keeps offering through a toggle in its title bar.
The new Outlook does not work with Stalwart, and it does not work with any self-hosted server, Exchange included. It is built to work through Microsoft’s cloud. Microsoft does not support on-premises Exchange accounts in it at all.
The new Outlook does accept IMAP accounts, and that might look like a way around the problem. It is not. When an IMAP account is added to the new Outlook, the connection to the mail server is not made from the user’s computer. As heise and c’t reported in November 2023, the new Outlook sends the IMAP server name, the user name and the password to Microsoft, and Microsoft’s cloud connects to the mail server on the user’s behalf. Every message then passes through Microsoft’s infrastructure on its way to the screen. Microsoft does not only see the mail; it holds the password to the mailbox.
For an organisation that runs its own mail server to keep its data under its own control, this defeats the purpose of the whole exercise. We advise against using the new Outlook with any self-hosted email deployment, whether it runs Stalwart or anything else. Users who see the “Try the new Outlook” toggle in classic Outlook should leave it off.
The same applies on phones. Outlook for iOS and Android reach on-premises mailboxes through a Microsoft cloud service as well, which requires a hybrid Exchange Online setup. On mobile devices, the native mail, calendar and contacts apps, which connect directly over ActiveSync, are the better choice.
Late to the party
Section titled “Late to the party”
The obvious question is why Stalwart is adding Exchange protocols now. EWS is being removed from Exchange Online, Microsoft’s own future for Outlook is a cloud client, and classic Outlook is reportedly supported until 2029. Why build support for protocols that will be obsolete in a few years?
Because the companies that run Exchange today cannot wait a few years, and because those protocols are the bridge they need to cross.
An organisation with a few hundred Outlook users cannot replace its mail server and its mail client in the same weekend. It can replace the server, though, if nobody notices. With Stalwart speaking the Exchange protocols, the server moves first: mailboxes are migrated, DNS records change, and on Monday every user opens the same Outlook and the same phone apps they used on Friday. The organisation’s mail is back under its own control from day one, and no one’s work is interrupted to get there.
The client can then change at the pace that suits the users. Over time, as classic Outlook reaches the end of its life and the new Outlook remains tied to Microsoft’s cloud, we expect users to move gradually to third-party email clients built on open standards, and in particular on JMAP. Outlook will stop being an option for anyone who cares about where their email lives, and by then the people who used it will have had the time to choose something else without a deadline over their heads.
Exchange support is not where Stalwart is going. It is how organisations get from where they are to where Stalwart is.
Footprint and modularity
Section titled “Footprint and modularity”
Exchange compatibility is not a small piece of work. It adds about 200,000 lines of code to Stalwart, and we know that some of you would rather not carry that weight if you never plan to see an Outlook client. We built it with that in mind, so that it costs as little as possible for those who do not use it, and so that it can be removed cleanly once the Exchange protocols are obsolete:
- It is a separate module. All of the Exchange code lives in its own crates, behind a single exchange Cargo feature. Build Stalwart without that feature and every line of Exchange code is left out of the binary. The compilation guide shows how. The day the Exchange protocols have no users left, removing them from the codebase is largely a matter of deleting those crates and that feature.
- It is disabled by default. Even in the official builds, which include the module, the Exchange protocols are off until an administrator enables them. While they are disabled, the server behaves as if they did not exist.
- It stores nothing until it is used. Outlook and the other Exchange clients keep properties that no standard protocol has a place for: extended properties, reminder state, client settings. Stalwart stores them as metadata attached to the item they belong to, using the JMAP Object Metadata extension, and only writes them when an Exchange client connects and sets them. Accounts that are never opened by an Exchange client carry no Exchange data at all.
- Its data can be removed. Because the Exchange-specific properties live in that metadata and not in the messages, events and contacts themselves, deleting the metadata removes every trace of them while the items stay intact for IMAP, JMAP, CalDAV and CardDAV clients.
Migration from Exchange and Microsoft 365
Section titled “Migration from Exchange and Microsoft 365”
A drop-in replacement is only useful if the data can follow. Vandelay, our account import and export tool, migrates mailboxes from both sides of Microsoft’s offering:
- Exchange Server on premises, through the EWS importer, which reads mail, calendars and contacts.
- Microsoft 365 (Exchange Online), through the Microsoft Graph importer, which reads mail, calendars, contacts and OneDrive files. The EWS importer also works against Exchange Online, for as long as Microsoft keeps its EWS endpoint running.
Vandelay copies each account into Stalwart over JMAP, and the domain moves to Stalwart when its MX records and client settings change. The migration guide for hosted services walks through the whole process, from the first test account to the cutover. With the Exchange protocols enabled on Stalwart, Outlook and the other Exchange clients then connect through Autodiscover and find the migrated mail, calendars and contacts.
Other changes in 1.2.0
Section titled “Other changes in 1.2.0”
Exchange support is the headline, but 1.2.0 brings several other features, most of them developed alongside it because an Exchange replacement needs them:
- Mail rules. Server-side rules in the familiar “when a message matches these conditions, do this” form, managed in the WebUI, over JMAP or from Outlook. Conditions cover senders and recipients, subject and body, headers, size, attachments, importance, message type, mailing lists, spam score, date ranges and weekly schedules. Actions include moving and copying, marking as read or as junk, forwarding, replying with a template and rejecting. Rules are compiled into Sieve together with the vacation response. Each account also gets junk email lists: blocked senders, safe senders and safe recipients. See Mail rules.
- Read receipts. Message Disposition Notifications (RFC 8098), including the JMAP methods for sending and parsing them (RFC 9007).
- Working hours. Per-account working hours, managed in the WebUI, over JMAP or through CalDAV. Time outside working hours appears as unavailable in free/busy lookups, and a new setting lets users of the same organisation see each other’s free/busy time without sharing calendars explicitly. See Availability.
- Rooms and equipment. Resource accounts with a capacity, a location and delegates who manage the calendar, with invitations booked automatically or approved by hand. See Resources.
- Native FoundationDB client. A new asynchronous Rust client replaces Apple’s libfdb_c library. It needs no client library on the host and is now part of the standard build and Docker image. In our benchmarks it delivers between 4.5 and 6.9 times the throughput of the previous client on point reads, with 5 to 7 times lower median latency and about half the CPU.
The changelog lists every change and fix, along with the upgrade steps: existing servers need to add the new permissions to their default roles.
Stalwart CLI 2.0
Section titled “Stalwart CLI 2.0”
Last week we also released version 2.0.0 of stalwart-cli, and its biggest addition is an interactive console. Run stalwart-cli with no arguments in a terminal and it opens a full-screen interface that covers the same ground as the WebUI: browsing and editing accounts, domains and every other configuration object, watching the dashboard, following live traces and running actions. It is aimed at administrators who live in SSH sessions, or who work on hosts where opening a browser is impractical.
The console is built with Ratatui, the excellent Rust library for terminal user interfaces, and it is schema-driven like the rest of the CLI: its screens, forms and field help come from the schema that the server publishes, so it picks up new objects without being updated. The CLI also learned to sign in with the OAuth device flow and to remember the session, so a single approval in the browser is enough for both the console and every subsequent command.
FOSDEM 2027
Section titled “FOSDEM 2027”
Stalwart will be at FOSDEM 2027 in Brussels. We will have a Stalwart stand, and on Saturday, 30 January 2027, we will give a talk about moving from Microsoft Exchange to Stalwart in the Modern Email devroom. If you are planning a migration away from Exchange, or are curious about what it takes, come and talk to us. We will post the time and title of the talk once the schedule is published.
Until then, the Exchange documentation has everything needed to try it, and we would love to hear how your Outlook clients get on with Stalwart.

====================================================================================================

Shared mailboxes open automatically in Outlook from group membership, as they do with Exchange.
Not every part of an Exchange deployment has a counterpart. Stalwart does not offer public folders, archive mailboxes, mobile device management, RPC over HTTP (Outlook 2010 and earlier) or NTLM and Kerberos sign-in: clients authenticate with a password, an app password or OAuth. The Exchange documentation covers configuration, DNS and Autodiscover, each family of clients, and the differences from Exchange that administrators should know about before a migration.
New Outlook
Section titled “New Outlook”
One Outlook is missing from the list above: the new Outlook for Windows, the one that Windows now installs by default and that classic Outlook keeps offering through a toggle in its title bar.
The new Outlook does not work with Stalwart, and it does not work with any self-hosted server, Exchange included. It is built to work through Microsoft’s cloud. Microsoft does not support on-premises Exchange accounts in it at all.
The new Outlook does accept IMAP accounts, and that might look like a way around the problem. It is not. When an IMAP account is added to the new Outlook, the connection to the mail server is not made from the user’s computer. As heise and c’t reported in November 2023, the new Outlook sends the IMAP server name, the user name and the password to Microsoft, and Microsoft’s cloud connects to the mail server on the user’s behalf. Every message then passes through Microsoft’s infrastructure on its way to the screen. Microsoft does not only see the mail; it holds the password to the mailbox.
For an organisation that runs its own mail server to keep its data under its own control, this defeats the purpose of the whole exercise. We advise against using the new Outlook with any self-hosted email deployment, whether it runs Stalwart or anything else. Users who see the “Try the new Outlook” toggle in classic Outlook should leave it off.
The same applies on phones. Outlook for iOS and Android reach on-premises mailboxes through a Microsoft cloud service as well, which requires a hybrid Exchange Online setup. On mobile devices, the native mail, calendar and contacts apps, which connect directly over ActiveSync, are the better choice.
Late to the party
Section titled “Late to the party”
The obvious question is why Stalwart is adding Exchange protocols now. EWS is being removed from Exchange Online, Microsoft’s own future for Outlook is a cloud client, and classic Outlook is reportedly supported until 2029. Why build support for protocols that will be obsolete in a few years?
Because the companies that run Exchange today cannot wait a few years, and because those protocols are the bridge they need to cross.
An organisation with a few hundred Outlook users cannot replace its mail server and its mail client in the same weekend. It can replace the server, though, if nobody notices. With Stalwart speaking the Exchange protocols, the server moves first: mailboxes are migrated, DNS records change, and on Monday every user opens the same Outlook and the same phone apps they used on Friday. The organisation’s mail is back under its own control from day one, and no one’s work is interrupted to get there.
The client can then change at the pace that suits the users. Over time, as classic Outlook reaches the end of its life and the new Outlook remains tied to Microsoft’s cloud, we expect users to move gradually to third-party email clients built on open standards, and in particular on JMAP. Outlook will stop being an option for anyone who cares about where their email lives, and by then the people who used it will have had the time to choose something else without a deadline over their heads.
Exchange support is not where Stalwart is going. It is how organisations get from where they are to where Stalwart is.
Footprint and modularity
Section titled “Footprint and modularity”
Exchange compatibility is not a small piece of work. It adds about 200,000 lines of code to Stalwart, and we know that some of you would rather not carry that weight if you never plan to see an Outlook client. We built it with that in mind, so that it costs as little as possible for those who do not use it, and so that it can be removed cleanly once the Exchange protocols are obsolete:
- It is a separate module. All of the Exchange code lives in its own crates, behind a single exchange Cargo feature. Build Stalwart without that feature and every line of Exchange code is left out of the binary. The compilation guide shows how. The day the Exchange protocols have no users left, removing them from the codebase is largely a matter of deleting those crates and that feature.
- It is disabled by default. Even in the official builds, which include the module, the Exchange protocols are off until an administrator enables them. While they are disabled, the server behaves as if they did not exist.
- It stores nothing until it is used. Outlook and the other Exchange clients keep properties that no standard protocol has a place for: extended properties, reminder state, client settings. Stalwart stores them as metadata attached to the item they belong to, using the JMAP Object Metadata extension, and only writes them when an Exchange client connects and sets them. Accounts that are never opened by an Exchange client carry no Exchange data at all.
- Its data can be removed. Because the Exchange-specific properties live in that metadata and not in the messages, events and contacts themselves, deleting the metadata removes every trace of them while the items stay intact for IMAP, JMAP, CalDAV and CardDAV clients.
Migration from Exchange and Microsoft 365
Section titled “Migration from Exchange and Microsoft 365”
A drop-in replacement is only useful if the data can follow. Vandelay, our account import and export tool, migrates mailboxes from both sides of Microsoft’s offering:
- Exchange Server on premises, through the EWS importer, which reads mail, calendars and contacts.
- Microsoft 365 (Exchange Online), through the Microsoft Graph importer, which reads mail, calendars, contacts and OneDrive files. The EWS importer also works against Exchange Online, for as long as Microsoft keeps its EWS endpoint running.
Vandelay copies each account into Stalwart over JMAP, and the domain moves to Stalwart when its MX records and client settings change. The migration guide for hosted services walks through the whole process, from the first test account to the cutover. With the Exchange protocols enabled on Stalwart, Outlook and the other Exchange clients then connect through Autodiscover and find the migrated mail, calendars and contacts.
Other changes in 1.2.0
Section titled “Other changes in 1.2.0”
Exchange support is the headline, but 1.2.0 brings several other features, most of them developed alongside it because an Exchange replacement needs them:
- Mail rules. Server-side rules in the familiar “when a message matches these conditions, do this” form, managed in the WebUI, over JMAP or from Outlook. Conditions cover senders and recipients, subject and body, headers, size, attachments, importance, message type, mailing lists, spam score, date ranges and weekly schedules. Actions include moving and copying, marking as read or as junk, forwarding, replying with a template and rejecting. Rules are compiled into Sieve together with the vacation response. Each account also gets junk email lists: blocked senders, safe senders and safe recipients. See Mail rules.
- Read receipts. Message Disposition Notifications (RFC 8098), including the JMAP methods for sending and parsing them (RFC 9007).
- Working hours. Per-account working hours, managed in the WebUI, over JMAP or through CalDAV. Time outside working hours appears as unavailable in free/busy lookups, and a new setting lets users of the same organisation see each other’s free/busy time without sharing calendars explicitly. See Availability.
- Rooms and equipment. Resource accounts with a capacity, a location and delegates who manage the calendar, with invitations booked automatically or approved by hand. See Resources.
- Native FoundationDB client. A new asynchronous Rust client replaces Apple’s libfdb_c library. It needs no client library on the host and is now part of the standard build and Docker image. In our benchmarks it delivers between 4.5 and 6.9 times the throughput of the previous client on point reads, with 5 to 7 times lower median latency and about half the CPU.
The changelog lists every change and fix, along with the upgrade steps: existing servers need to add the new permissions to their default roles.
Stalwart CLI 2.0
Section titled “Stalwart CLI 2.0”
Last week we also released version 2.0.0 of stalwart-cli, and its biggest addition is an interactive console. Run stalwart-cli with no arguments in a terminal and it opens a full-screen interface that covers the same ground as the WebUI: browsing and editing accounts, domains and every other configuration object, watching the dashboard, following live traces and running actions. It is aimed at administrators who live in SSH sessions, or who work on hosts where opening a browser is impractical.
The console is built with Ratatui, the excellent Rust library for terminal user interfaces, and it is schema-driven like the rest of the CLI: its screens, forms and field help come from the schema that the server publishes, so it picks up new objects without being updated. The CLI also learned to sign in with the OAuth device flow and to remember the session, so a single approval in the browser is enough for both the console and every subsequent command.
FOSDEM 2027
Section titled “FOSDEM 2027”
Stalwart will be at FOSDEM 2027 in Brussels. We will have a Stalwart stand, and on Saturday, 30 January 2027, we will give a talk about moving from Microsoft Exchange to Stalwart in the Modern Email devroom. If you are planning a migration away from Exchange, or are curious about what it takes, come and talk to us. We will post the time and title of the talk once the schedule is published.
Until then, the Exchange documentation has everything needed to try it, and we would love to hear how your Outlook clients get on with Stalwart.

Latent Introspection: LLMs Can Detect Prior Concept Injections
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:29:48 UTC
07:29:48 KST

Computer Science > Artificial Intelligence
  [Submitted on 23 Feb 2026 (v1), last revised 7 Nov 2026 (this version, v3)]
Title:Latent Introspection: Models Can Detect Prior Concept Injections
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:We uncover a latent capacity for introspection in a Qwen 32B model, demonstrating that the model can detect when concepts have been injected into its earlier context and identify which concept was injected. While the model denies injection in sampled outputs, logit lens analysis reveals clear detection signals in the residual stream, which are attenuated in the final layers. Furthermore, prompting the model with accurate information about AI introspection mechanisms can dramatically strengthen this effect: the sensitivity to injection increases massively (0.3% -> 39.9%) with only a 0.6% increase in false positives. Also, mutual information between nine injected and recovered concepts rises from 0.61 bits to 1.05 bits, ruling out generic noise explanations. Our results demonstrate models can have a surprising capacity for introspection and steering awareness that is easy to overlook, with consequences for latent reasoning and safety.

Latent Introspection: LLMs Can Detect Prior Concept Injections
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:29:48 UTC
07:29:48 KST

Computer Science > Artificial Intelligence
  [Submitted on 23 Feb 2026 (v1), last revised 7 Nov 2026 (this version, v3)]
Title:Latent Introspection: Models Can Detect Prior Concept Injections
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:We uncover a latent capacity for introspection in a Qwen 32B model, demonstrating that the model can detect when concepts have been injected into its earlier context and identify which concept was injected. While the model denies injection in sampled outputs, logit lens analysis reveals clear detection signals in the residual stream, which are attenuated in the final layers. Furthermore, prompting the model with accurate information about AI introspection mechanisms can dramatically strengthen this effect: the sensitivity to injection increases massively (0.3% -> 39.9%) with only a 0.6% increase in false positives. Also, mutual information between nine injected and recovered concepts rises from 0.61 bits to 1.05 bits, ruling out generic noise explanations. Our results demonstrate models can have a surprising capacity for introspection and steering awareness that is easy to overlook, with consequences for latent reasoning and safety.
