Trump engineered a risky comeback for Jensen Huang
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:25:12 UTC
07:25:12 KST

After Nvidia’s board accepted Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO, following reports that Huang had an affair with an alleged Chinese spy, President Donald Trump, too, appeared to submit to the new reality. Appearing on Stephen A. Smith’s radio show shortly after the news came out, Trump said that Huang would “end up on his feet.”
Trump engineered a risky comeback for Jensen Huang
Trump got his wish to reinstate Jensen Huang at the helm of Nvidia — but investigations are still looming.
Trump engineered a risky comeback for Jensen Huang
Trump got his wish to reinstate Jensen Huang at the helm of Nvidia — but investigations are still looming.
“You know the expression, anybody can be replaced,” Trump said, floating Apple’s ex-CEO Tim Cook as a potential CEO candidate.
It didn’t take long for Trump to begin softening his approach, making it clear he’d welcome Huang back into the role of CEO even if Nvidia’s own board wasn’t ready to do so. Behind the scenes, the Trump administration was working to grease the landing for Huang’s return. And soon, Nvidia’s board changed its tune: Barely a week after insisting they all agreed to shut the door on Huang’s return, they voted unanimously to reinstate him as CEO, president, and board director.
“There’s nothing wrong with a comeback,” Trump said in a celebratory call to CNBC.
Trump then posted an image on Truth Social of Huang transposed onto Trump’s own body in the infamous photo of him raising his fist after being shot at a campaign rally. In his raised fist, Huang-on-Trump’s-body is clutching a scarlet bra, holding it high in the air.
“There’s nothing wrong with a comeback”
Even after a decisive blow to the president’s power as Democrats swept the midterm elections, Trump appears to maintain his position as a corporate kingmaker. Trump said he spoke with Huang the night prior to his reinstatement and had been in communication with the board both “directly and indirectly.” White House communications director Steven Cheung said in a statement that “President Trump was totally involved” in the board’s decision — Trump decided near the end of last week that he wanted to lead efforts to get Huang reappointed, per The New York Times, so he asked Vice President JD Vance to act as a liaison between Huang and Nvidia’s board, providing reassurances that the move wouldn’t pose a security risk. Trump also reportedly dialed into Nvidia’s Sunday night board meeting to make the case for his return. “I made the deal,” he told CNBC on Monday.
News of Huang’s reappointment, nearly as dramatic as Sam Altman’s return to his CEO role after being voted out by OpenAI’s board in 2023, was a widespread shock to experts, analysts, and investors, particularly after Nvidia’s board had assured stakeholders on its quarterly earnings call last Tuesday that the board “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.” On Monday morning, a surge in NASDAQ-100 futures hit a circuit-breaker limit and caused a halt in trading over the volatility.
Trump has meddled in other messy incidents, including by repeatedly — and perhaps illegally — extending the deadline for TikTok to complete its sale to a US entity, and, he admitted on Monday, protecting Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk. Although he said on CNBC that Musk is “80 percent super genius, 20 percent he has other problems,” Trump said it’s important to protect people like him and Huang. “We have to protect our brilliant people because we don’t have enough.”
But the upcoming Democratic-majority Congress could test both Huang and Trump’s influence. As Republicans prepare for an open presidential primary in 2028, at least one potential candidate appears to be breaking with the president in their skepticism of Huang and Nvidia. And Democrats seem eager to probe both Nvidia and the Trump administration.
The past couple of weeks have been tumultuous for Nvidia. Huang admitted he had had an affair with Alice Huang (no relation), a former executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed whom some US intelligence officers reportedly believed to be a potential Chinese spy. There were nearly immediate reports of an additional affair with a direct report, as well as an alleged third affair with a second employee. Megaspeed has reportedly also been investigated for a broader attempt to illegally funnel advanced Nvidia chips to China. Huang himself has pushed to open up more chip sales to China, and he reportedly lied to the Nvidia board about his infidelity. He’s scheduled to testify before Congress on December 1st.
The Nvidia board tried to address concerns about Huang in its announcement Monday, writing that members “received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.” It made assurances that the independent investigation it had kicked off about Huang and foreign intelligence would “proceed to conclusion” with Huang’s cooperation.
The board also said it had recently formed an entirely new National Security and Export Control Committee “comprised solely of independent directors to oversee trade compliance and national security risks,” which is working with legal counsel and advisers who have national security experience. Nvidia will be hiring for a new C-suite position, the board said: chief compliance officer. The executive will “oversee compliance” across the company and report to the Nominating and Corporate Governance committees. “NVIDIA will continue to cooperate with ongoing government investigations and inquiries,” the board added.
Still, the reaction to Huang’s return reflects how small the chance of Huang’s return seemed before Trump’s efforts. Although Trump had previously cast doubt on multiple reports that Alice Huang was a spy, The New York Times reported on Sunday that she “kept in contact with an official of China’s top spy agency for more than a year.” According to the Times, which cited multiple people with knowledge of US intelligence reports, Kang Wei — Alice Huang’s business partner and, by some reports, husband, who reportedly introduced her to the Nvidia CEO — worked with the same Chinese intelligence agency. But on Sunday evening, Trump still argued for Huang’s return and managed to continue flipping Nvidia’s board in his favor after a weekend’s worth of efforts.
“[I]ndefensible, irresponsible and incomprehensible”
If the board maintained any hang-ups about bringing Huang back as CEO, Trump’s power over the executive branch — and the pressure he could exert with it — might have offered a compelling counterargument. The Securities and Exchange Commission had previously opened an investigation into whether Nvidia “misled investors,” according to The Wall Street Journal. But in a post on Truth Social Monday, Trump said that the attorney general “has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct.” Reached for comment, the DOJ did not provide an on-the-record response in line with The Verge’s editorial policy.
Key Democrats have indicated that both Nvidia and the Trump administration will be under fire when the new Congress is sworn in — and momentum has built behind GPU export controls, which Huang lobbied extensively to loosen. “Democrats will wield their full power to investigate any wrongdoing by the Trump Administration, Jensen Huang, other NVIDIA executives, and anyone selling out our national security for financial gain.” Senate Majority Leader Chuck Schumer (D-NY) said in a post on X, adding they’d work “on a bipartisan basis to continue advancing policies that stop the flow of our most powerful technology to the Chinese military and other adversaries.” Speaker-designate Hakeem Jeffries (D-NY) called the board’s decision “indefensible, irresponsible and incomprehensible” and pledged to investigate “business malfeasance by NVIDIA and corrupt abuse of power by Howard Lutnick and the Department of Commerce.” He called for “traditional Republicans” to join in the effort.
“The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company’s board still owe Congress answers about the company’s compliance with U.S. export controls,” Sen. Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said in a statement. “Jensen Huang’s return only increases the urgency of passing bipartisan export control bills into law and making sure NVIDIA does not lobby to kill that legislation and put our national security at risk.” Rep. Jamie Raskin, the top Democrat on the House Judiciary Committee, said, “A real Congress will find out” whether Trump and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick had any alternative incentives for their role in Nvidia’s dealings. Sen. Mark Warner, the top Democrat on the Intelligence Committee, called Trump’s intervention to bring back Huang “outrageous.” Rep. Joaquin Castro (D-TX), a member of the House Intelligence and Foreign Affairs Committees, which are interviewing Huang next week, said he wants to know how Trump stands to benefit from “personally champion[ing] Huang’s return.”
“We need to know why Trump put his thumb on the scale to have Jensen reinstated as CEO”
At closed-door hearings next week, Democrats say they intend to press Huang and the administration further, and several accused the administration of corruption. “We need to know why Trump put his thumb on the scale to have Jensen reinstated as CEO and why his administration failed to restrict the PRC’s access to our most sensitive technology,” Rep. Gregory Meeks, the top Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said in a statement to The Verge. The board’s decision is “outrageous,” said Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi (D-IL), a member of the House Select Committee on China. “We still don’t know what information Chinese intelligence may have obtained from him, and neither the government nor Nvidia’s own board has completed its investigation,” Krishnamoorthi said in a statement, calling for Huang to testify publicly. “Reinstatement is no substitute for accountability,” Rep. Kathy Castor (D-FL), another China Committee member, said in a statement.
California Attorney General Rob Bonta also intends to continue an investigation into the company. “The reinstatement of Jensen Huang does not change the fact that the American people deserve to know what happened here — whether Nvidia misled investors and the public,” California Department of Justice spokesperson Elissa Perez said in a statement.
Last week, before Huang was reinstated, at least two Republican China hawks opposed the idea. Sen. Josh Hawley (R-MO), a rumored potential presidential candidate, told reporters that he would not like to see Huang return to the top post, but would “defer to the board how they want to run their company. If it were me and a guy who just was caught up in a Chinese honeytrap — I think is the technical term for it — I don’t think that I would impose massive security concerns, not just for the company, but for American national security.”
While it was up to the board to decide whether to bring Huang back, Hawley said, “We in Congress ought to be concerned about the American national security implications and I think we should be looking into that.”
Huang “has a serious credibility problem”
House China Committee Chair John Moolenaar (R-MI) said on stage last week at the Aspen Cyber Summit in Washington, D.C., that Huang “has a serious credibility problem, and I don’t believe he would be as effective now as he was before.” His office did not immediately respond to a request for comment after Huang’s reinstatement.
Sitting beside Moolenaar on stage last week, Rep. Greg Stanton (D-AZ) gave his advice to the board: “Don’t do it. Don’t bring it back. And at the very least, slow your roll, because there are so many questions that have to be answered. What else is out there that Jensen Huang has done to put US national security at risk?” Stanton warned that Congress is just beginning to probe what happened here. “There are going to be very serious investigations with very serious ramifications if in fact it is as it seems,” he said. “The company needs to know this ain’t going away for a while. This is a very serious issue.” After news of Huang’s return, Stanton doubled down on calls for investigations.
Other Republicans — some of whom are leaving Congress — notably disagreed with Trump’s jubilance for Huang’s return. “It’s inexplicable to me why they want this guy back as CEO,” retiring Rep. Don Bacon (R-NE) told CNBC. “To me it’s a failure of character.” Sen. Pete Ricketts (R-NE) wrote on X that Huang’s “behavior does not support reinstatement,” and said he must still testify before Congress. “It also doesn’t absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips,” he wrote. Senate Intelligence Committee Chair Tom Cotton (R-AR) wrote on X that he’s not done investigating. “The controversy with Nvidia and Megaspeed is just the tip of the iceberg, and it’s not resolved because Jensen Huang has his job back,” he said. “Congress has much to investigate and we will pass overwhelmingly bipartisan chip export-control bills, including my Chip Security Act, one way or the other.”
“Congress has much to investigate”
Market analysts have been watching and waiting for a Huang comeback since reports that he had assembled a “war room” of allies to help with his possible reinstatement. Not only did Huang cofound one of the world’s most valuable companies and arguably the most important one in the AI boom, but he has personally helped secure hundreds of billions of dollars in infrastructure funding from US financial institutions. Demand for Nvidia chips constantly outpaces supply, and Huang has had the president’s ear for months. Despite all that, though, Huang’s clout seemed like it wouldn’t help him up until the past 24 hours.
Lindsey Stewart, director of institutional insights at Morningstar, wrote in a note that although investors may consider this a “good move given his history of value creation at the company,” questions remain about the best way to provide corporate governance and oversight at such an important company.
Jacob Bourne, an eMarketer analyst specializing in ad tech and consumer technologies, said in a statement to The Verge that it was difficult to gauge “how much protection Trump’s assurances will ultimately provide Nvidia.” And Heather West, head of Eurasia Group’s geo-technology practice, highlighted the fact that Trump recently met with Chinese President Xi Jinping at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum. The timing offers Democrats and China hawks “a great deal to work with in 2027 as they begin scrutiny into the administration, potential corruption, and large tech companies[‘] actions here,” said West.
Analysts at Yorkville Ives wrote in a note Monday that they consider Nvidia a $300 stock with Huang and a $225 stock without him, due to his hard-to-replace influence in the sector. They said an interim structure “could not replicate” his supply relationships with TSMC and others, as well as his place in AI industry dealmaking.
“This is a board that reversed itself because the alternative, a founder-less Nvidia at the start of the Rubin cycle with a CEO search running into next year, was the bigger risk to shareholders, and it chose to pair the reversal with the governance architecture investors will want to see around him,” the Yorkville Ives analysts wrote.
Even Trump admits, though, that a true comeback story can’t be called in a single day. “I’ve seen some pretty good comebacks but I’ll tell you, what you have to have for a comeback, really, is longevity,” he told CNBC. He described the pep talk he gave Huang the night before. “I said, ‘You’ve got to promise me one thing. You’ve got the brain, you’ve got everything going, you’ve got to work hard. And if you work hard nobody can beat you.’”
According to Trump, Huang told him, “I’m going to work harder than I’ve ever worked.”

Levent Alpöge comments on his Riemann Hypothesis proof
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:22:56 UTC
07:22:56 KST

Stupid things I did:
1. 250,000+ pages was a joke guys!, I find big number extremely hilarious. I tried to make it clear by the level of pedantry of the reals total ordered statement in the main theorem, but people ran with it unfortunately and I didn’t feel like talking on social media in the days after the announcement, though I did have a good time with the memes. Sorry!!! (Similarly with our YM4 existence I found it extremely funny that we were slowly working through 60,000 pages of slop, though the committee asking for Lean made me feel dumb about wasting our time with that. But 60,000 funny.) Tao advises to not joke in papers, see also the Big Lebowski reference I shoehorned, I now understand why… I didn’t feel comfortable having any slop writeup attached to this problem, and I figured I can’t produce anything readable anyway, so I went off the Lean to lengthmaxx.
2. The Lean is full of garbage obviously, but compiles, but all criticism of such garbage is totally reasonable and accepted, I should have cleaned it up but it was randomly difficult to for various strange reasons. Mea culpa…
3. @scottnarmstrong clocked me, indeed even as a student there were theorems that took me the better part of a decade to write up, but with this one I should hopefully be more responsible. (I really was yoloing a lot in there, I bet most is unnecessary in the end, admittedly the lazy thing to do would be to wait because it’ll simplify massively…)
4. I forgot to emphasize what I think is the point. Arguments like these, cool, but they are the raw inputs when interpolating definitions. We don’t have the / an “H^1 object” over number fields, and probably for a layperson the fact that we fight hard for these arguments for the purpose of definitions is not clear a priori (that’s at least my philosophy). Just like I knew I must fight for an utterly simple Weil / Deligne, I feel Riemann should be easy, and any real difficulty is an indication our setup is just dumb at the moment.

Levent Alpöge comments on his Riemann Hypothesis proof
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:22:56 UTC
07:22:56 KST

Stupid things I did:
1. 250,000+ pages was a joke guys!, I find big number extremely hilarious. I tried to make it clear by the level of pedantry of the reals total ordered statement in the main theorem, but people ran with it unfortunately and I didn’t feel like talking on social media in the days after the announcement, though I did have a good time with the memes. Sorry!!! (Similarly with our YM4 existence I found it extremely funny that we were slowly working through 60,000 pages of slop, though the committee asking for Lean made me feel dumb about wasting our time with that. But 60,000 funny.) Tao advises to not joke in papers, see also the Big Lebowski reference I shoehorned, I now understand why… I didn’t feel comfortable having any slop writeup attached to this problem, and I figured I can’t produce anything readable anyway, so I went off the Lean to lengthmaxx.
2. The Lean is full of garbage obviously, but compiles, but all criticism of such garbage is totally reasonable and accepted, I should have cleaned it up but it was randomly difficult to for various strange reasons. Mea culpa…
3. @scottnarmstrong clocked me, indeed even as a student there were theorems that took me the better part of a decade to write up, but with this one I should hopefully be more responsible. (I really was yoloing a lot in there, I bet most is unnecessary in the end, admittedly the lazy thing to do would be to wait because it’ll simplify massively…)
4. I forgot to emphasize what I think is the point. Arguments like these, cool, but they are the raw inputs when interpolating definitions. We don’t have the / an “H^1 object” over number fields, and probably for a layperson the fact that we fight hard for these arguments for the purpose of definitions is not clear a priori (that’s at least my philosophy). Just like I knew I must fight for an utterly simple Weil / Deligne, I feel Riemann should be easy, and any real difficulty is an indication our setup is just dumb at the moment.

Welcome to elite impunity, Jensen Huang
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:09:26 UTC
07:09:26 KST

I suppose I must issue a hearty congratulations to Jensen Huang. Huang is CEO of Nvidia again, to the surprise of basically everyone except President Donald Trump and Vice President JD Vance, who made sure it happened.
Welcome to elite impunity, Jensen Huang
Consequences are for the poors.
Welcome to elite impunity, Jensen Huang
Consequences are for the poors.
Now, those of us who had some kind of basic education on what a “free market” is might find it objectionable to have the American president strong-arming a major American company. But our billionaire tech overlords care about the “free market” exactly as much as they care about “free speech,” which is to say not at all. Watching Silicon Valley cheer for what is, effectively, a sign of a command economy has certainly been bracing. But then, why would they give a shit? Their only real beliefs are that they should remain rich and above the law. Jensen may not have exchanged emails with Jeffrey Epstein, but he’s squarely at the Epstein table now.
The deal was reportedly brokered by Peter Thiel’s very own boy
I have argued here, in the past, that the Epstein case laid bare the real politics of the revanchist American right wing: total elite impunity. Well, here it is again! If you are close enough with Trump, it doesn’t matter if you may have jeopardized national security by fucking an alleged Chinese spy. You won’t even be investigated for it before the American president calls in to your board of directors’ meeting to demand you be reinstated. I mean, it is the era of gangster tech regulation — what were any of us expecting? The deal was reportedly brokered by Peter Thiel’s very own boy, Vance.
Just so we can be absolutely clear about what transpired at Nvidia, let’s quickly go through the facts as we know them. A Chinese citizen named Alice Huang acquired the company she renamed Megaspeed, the Singaporean data firm at the center of this inquiry, in 2023. At some point, Huang met Huang, and the two were seen together at a dinner that December. From 2024 to 2025, Megaspeed received more than 200 Nvidia shipments.
In September 2025, the US government began investigating Megaspeed for possible violations of chip export controls; that October, the State Department told Trump of Jensen Huang’s affair with Alice Huang, who was described as a Chinese intelligence asset. Also in October, The New York Times first reported on Megaspeed’s relationship with Nvidia. In December, Trump — who has a close relationship with Jensen Huang — allowed Nvidia to sell more powerful chips to China. In March 2026, Marco Rubio warned the government about Alice, and Trump nonetheless invited Huang to a visit in China. In October, The New York Times reported on the affair. Shortly after Huang sent a note to Nvidia admitting to it, several other reports of infidelity — this time with Nvidia employees — surfaced. On November 12th, Huang resigned. On November 17th, during the Nvidia earnings call, the board’s chair said that the company “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.” On November 23rd, Huang was back.
This level of open corruption is genuinely impressive
What changed between last week and this week? A week was not enough time to truly review all the information on Huang and his paramour, Alice “No Relation” Huang, the international woman of mystery. The explanation seems to be that Nvidia’s board succumbed to a pressure campaign from Huang’s fellow billionaires, and a direct intervention from the president and vice president of the United States of America.
Sure, all these CEOs ostensibly wanted Huang back at Nvidia to keep the AI trade going. Still, watching the likes of Epic CEO Tim Sweeney, former Uber CEO Travis Kalanick, investing loudmouth Bill Ackman, and Elon Musk — who actually did appear in the Epstein files, and who seemed to be pretty friendly with Epstein! — pitch a fit last week was instructive. These men worried that they might be punished for wrongdoing, that they did not get big enough to be above the law. They have now been reassured, and I suppose that is what really matters. After all, Trump said on CNBC that he gave Nvidia’s board assurances that Jensen is “absolutely fine.”
This level of open corruption is genuinely impressive, and not just because Huang is CEO of Nvidia again. Almost in passing, Trump noted that he had “protected” Musk in his first term from “a certain incident [that] took place, a very tough incident.” In his own inimitable style, Trump told Jim Cramer, “You wouldn’t have Elon if it wasn’t for me.” Surely there is no possible downside when the CEOs of two of the biggest corporations on Earth owe their jobs to Trump personally.
Surely there is no possible downside when the CEOs of two of the biggest corporations on Earth owe their jobs to Trump personally
One thing that has perhaps emboldened this crew is the stance from professional politics-knowers — and House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries — that holding members of the Trump administration accountable might be a hindrance to governing. That accountability was part of the reason for the blue wave in November seems to have bypassed these pundits (and the presumptive House majority leader). But then, why should we believe that Democrats will thoroughly investigate this? Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer is claiming to want an investigation, but I am skeptical of how deep that investigation will go as long as Schumer leads it, and only partially because he’s been allied with Silicon Valley for more than a decade. He also has a daughter who works at OpenAI, a company that Nvidia invests in and that is dependent on Nvidia chips. How hard is he really willing to go?
Of course, there may be more to this than just pure economic self-interest. In celebrating Huang’s return, Trump posted a remarkable image. It is an altered picture of Trump himself, taken after a failed assassination attempt in 2024. Trump’s head has been replaced with Jensen Huang’s head. The American flag has been replaced with the Nvidia logo. Huang is waving a bright red bra. It seems I owe Sigmund Freud an apology. In the end, these men fear nothing more than losing power for sexual shenanigans — and especially losing power to a woman.
Leave aside the national security issues for a minute and focus on the two Nvidia employees. Dating subordinates is a serious conflict of interest; the CEO of Nestle lost his job for just that last year. This is a major violation for CEOs of normal companies, but it is common practice among the sleazeballs of Silicon Valley: Bill Gates is famous for it and it was plainly rampant at Google, a tone set by Sergey Brin himself. I am sure that you will be shocked, shocked to discover that in the wake of MeToo, Brin was among the men who suddenly swung right. I mean, Musk himself announced he was a Republican the day before Business Insider reported that he exposed himself to a flight attendant and paid hush money to cover it up.
If you’re rich enough, and connected enough, everything is permitted
The Republican Party has made the abuse of women without consequences one of its main issues; that the lesser violation of sleeping with subordinates would also be something the revanchists would defend seems obvious. That was clear from the botched investigation of the Epstein affair itself, the botched release of the Epstein files, and the assorted confirmation hearings of Pete Hegseth, Brett Kavanaugh, and Todd Blanche. (To say nothing of the Andrew Tate business.) But in all this, let’s not forget that Trump has been accused of sexual misconduct by no fewer than 28 women. So I’m sure it was an affront to all these guys that not only did Huang’s misconduct include a series of affairs, but he was also replaced by Colette Kress, who is known to be a woman.
I suppose we might as well pass by the various reports that Huang allegedly terrorized his employees. In light of everything else, this seems minor by comparison.
Anyway I had that all in mind when I saw Epic’s Sweeney celebrating the state intrusion into theoretically private companies by posting “Back in Black,” an oblique reference to Huang’s leather jackets that is also from the later, lesser period of AC/DC, after Bon Scott died and AC/DC became an AC/DC tribute band. He also called The New York Times’ reporting into Huang “lies.” Musk continued his streak of Sephiroth posting: “The mistake has been corrected.” Jason Calacanis, the All-In podcast’s village idiot, posted, “GPU Jesus has risen!” along with an openly blasphemous, AI-generated image of Huang as Christ.
Best of luck to Joon Kim, the Wachtell Lipton partner and former federal prosecutor who is conducting “a thorough and independent investigation” into Huang’s alleged misconduct on behalf of Nvidia’s board. I assume Kim has, like the rest of us, gotten the message loud and clear: If you’re rich enough, and connected enough, everything is permitted. Or perhaps, more succinctly: lol nothing matters.

VP ≠ VNP over Every Field
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:09:10 UTC
07:09:10 KST

For every field, circuits for the Hamiltonian cycle polynomial, and for the permanent outside characteristic 2, need more than m^(c (log m)^(1/3)) gates for all large m. The same holds for approximating circuits. So VP is a proper subset of VNP, and VNP is not contained in the closure of VP. This does not settle P vs NP.
The result was discovered by an internal research Claude model. This folder holds the paper and its Lean 4 formalization. Both were written by AI models, and no human mathematician has checked the proof.
Research artifact. Not maintained and not accepting contributions.
paper.pdf: VP ≠ VNP over every field, by Claude (Anthropic). The full proof is its appendix.
1. Read the statement. Statement.lean states all of the above and imports only Mathlib. Lean checks the proofs, but no machine checks that this file says what is claimed. Appendix Q of paper.pdf lists the Lean counterparts of the numbered statements (Table 3) and says where they differ.
2. Check the proofs. With elan installed, in this folder:
lake exe cache get
lake build
lake env lean scripts/PrintAxioms.lean
The build prints sorry warnings from Challenge.lean only, which states the theorems without proofs for the fuller check. The last command should name no axiom other than propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound.
The fuller check is the one this repository's CI runs, bash ../.github/scripts/comparator-check.sh (Linux): Comparator compares the statements with Challenge.lean and re-checks the proofs with a second, independently written kernel, nanoda.

Anthropic launches Claude Ship, letting AI agents review, fix, and approve code changes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:05:50 UTC
07:05:50 KST

Anthropic is moving from having Claude write code to helping developers decide when that code is ready to ship. On Monday, the newly public AI company announced the launch of Claude Ship, a suite of AI agents for automating code reviews, fixes, and approvals of routine code changes.
The latter is the more interesting part of this new set of tools, as AI coding tools already offered code review features before now. What’s notable about Claude Ship is not just that it can review proposed code changes and identify and address problems, but that it also includes an agent that can approve code changes automatically based on policies set by a development team.
The launch arrives at a time when AI coding agents have become increasingly capable of generating substantial amounts of code, leading to bottlenecks in reviewing and approving the code changes before they go live. With Claude Ship, the idea is to automate even more of the work involved in the development process, essentially by delegating approval authority to an AI agent itself.
“Most teams want a person to approve every change, and that’s easy to keep up with when people write every line. But a lot of developers build with agents now,” a company blog post explains. “On the Claude Code team, Claude writes most of our code, and we wanted everything after the PR to move that fast too.”
Claude Ship is launching in beta, allowing early adopters to use it to review pull requests (PRs), and then decide what’s ready to merge.
The new offering consists of Code Review, which is already generally available, and two new agents now in beta: PR Steward and Claude Approvals. PR Steward focuses on addressing issues such as failing CI checks, merge conflicts, and feedback from Code Review. It also comments after every push on what it changed and how to roll it back, if needed.
Claude Approvals then applies an approval policy that the team writes as a file in the repository. This approval process can incorporate various signals, including Code Review findings or the company’s own classifiers, with policies written in natural language.
Because the offering is still in beta, Anthropic suggests running the approval functionality in observe-only mode first. Teams can also choose to start with just one repository and set monthly spending limits to have more control over the process.
Anthropic says teams will also still remain in control of where Claude Ship runs, which review settings to use (e.g., Quick, Standard, or Deep), and which changes should be escalated to a human for a decision.
Claude Ship is rolling out to Claude Team and Enterprise plans for repositories on GitHub. Code Review is generally available, while PR Steward and Claude Approvals are in beta.

====================================================================================================

Supertake raises $20M to let AI agents turn your market predictions into stock portfolios
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
22:02:05 UTC
07:02:05 KST

AI investing startup Supertake has an interesting premise: What if your “takes” on the market could make you money without you having to pick stocks yourself? The service lets people turn their ideas into real investment portfolios, using frontier AI models and trading agents.
Now, the company has raised $20 million in seed funding to grow its business. The round was led by venture firm Adjacent (Nico Wittenborn), with participation from Coinbase Ventures and Union Square Ventures (USV). Numerous angel investors also joined, including Nat Friedman, who’s currently head of product at Meta Superintelligence Labs; Vercel CEO Guillermo Rauch; renowned product manager Nikita Bier; investor Scott Belsky; and Nemil Dalal, a former Coinbase exec who is now a visiting partner at Y Combinator.
Michael Mignano originally incubated the startup at USV. He is the co-founder and former CEO of the podcasting platform Anchor, which Spotify acquired in 2019 for over $150 million. After leading Spotify’s podcasting business as “Head of Talk” for a few years, he joined the team at Lightspeed as a venture partner, betting on AI companies like Suno and Granola. He also co-founded Oboe, an AI education startup, and joined USV as a general partner in April.
“We really believed there was an opportunity to make investing more democratized…and give everyone and anyone the ability to invest in their own insights and beliefs and ideas,” Mignano said. While he will continue in his role as founder and executive chairman, he’s now brought in a team to run the company day to day.
The team includes co-founders Lincoln Murr (CEO) and John Peterson (CTO). Both joined from Coinbase, where, Mignano notes, they were the heads of AI product and engineering, leading launches like Coinbase advisor, Coinbase for agents, agentic wallets, and others.
The startup arrives as AI continues to make inroads into financial services, but allowing AI to take over people’s actual investments requires a new level of trust. Supertake is betting that people will ultimately be willing to take that leap, given recent improvements in frontier AI models.
Currently, Supertake works with Coinbase and Robinhood, and the company intends to expand to more brokerages over time. The idea is that you can explain, in broad, natural language, what you believe about how the world is going to change, and Supertake’s AI will iterate with you back and forth to turn that idea into a “take” that’s investable.
Under the hood, the startup is leveraging frontier AI technology and tools, combined with its own proprietary software that directs and limits what the AI can do.
Supertake’s users’ ideas are tracked on a leaderboard on the site, so others who agree can invest alongside them. For instance, there’s currently an idea on the leaderboard about looksmaxxing and longevity becoming more of a trend, where the portfolio includes certain pharma and cosmetic companies that could potentially benefit.
For now, these portfolios consist of stock picks, but the team wants to expand the service over time to handle other types of transactions, like shorts, derivatives, perpetuals, long exposure, or even prediction markets — the latter handled through partnerships with companies in that space.
Despite these “takes” seeming like bets on what could happen next in a given market, Murr stresses that this doesn’t make Supertake itself a prediction market.
“We think it’s a novel and very much improved way to create what was previously considered the robo-advisor experience,” he says. “When that came out, it had this amazing vision and direction of allowing people to have digital asset management. We think now is actually the time for that, with AI agents being so widely proliferated.”
“I don’t think this is necessarily a prediction market by any means. The idea of natural language translation into an underlying set of assets feels different in that regard,” he says.
Still, the startup could wade into short-term predictions if a user’s take involves something they believe could happen in the near future, like who might win an election.
“We’re primarily focused on the longer, medium-term holdings today. But that being said, we don’t want to preclude people from building out very hyper short-term strategies that might exist,” Murr adds.
To address people’s concerns over AI managing their money, Supertake’s team says it’s implementing certain guardrails and spend limits. For instance, the AI has no ability to deposit or withdraw funds on a user’s behalf without their explicit consent. There are also drift limits in place so the portfolio stays balanced, plus allow and deny lists (meaning which assets the AI may and may not trade), real-time monitoring for anomalous activity, tools to set trade size limits, and a restriction that keeps agents from seeing the user’s brokerage credentials.
“We view it as somewhat analogous to self-driving cars, actually, where there is a spectrum here of both trust and technological innovations that are happening,” says Murr. “Where a few years ago you may not feel comfortable getting in a car that has lane assist, but today you get in a Waymo, and it’s like nothing. And we think that there’s a very similar progression happening with the agentic finance space, and we’re far further along in the technological spectrum than people think.”
Access to Supertake is still behind a waitlist while in beta, but the founders said they’ve put their money where their mouths are. Murr and Peterson are letting it manage “tens of thousands” and “thousands” of dollars, respectively.
Mignano, meanwhile, says he has Supertake managing “hundreds of thousands” of dollars.
With the new funding, which follows a $2 million pre-seed round, Supertake aims to expand its team in areas like engineering, design, growth marketing, and others. Mignano and Murr are in New York, while Peterson is in the Austin area but commutes to New York at times. The team says it hopes to figure out a hybrid work model as it grows.

Anthropic Launches Claude Ship to Review, Fix, and Approve Code 49
			
		 	
				Anthropic has unveiled Claude Ship, a new feature in Claude Code that allows AI agents to review pull requests, fix bugs, resolve merge conflicts and approve code changes according to policies established by development teams. 9to5Mac reports:  Claude Ship is comprised of three agents: Code Review, PR Steward, and Claude Approvals. "Together, they take a pull request through review, fixes, and an approval decision so teams don't have to manage each step manually," Anthropic says. "Developers can try Claude Ship on their own pull requests with the new /ship command in Claude Code," Anthropic says.

Elon Musk and others rejoice as Jensen Huang is reinstated at Nvidia amid scandal
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
21:47:41 UTC
06:47:41 KST

A handful of tech CEOs, including Elon Musk, are celebrating Jensen Huang’s return to Nvidia. Despite being caught in three affairs — including one with an alleged Chinese spy that has triggered multiple congressional investigations over national security concerns — Huang was reinstated as CEO and president of Nvidia on Monday morning.
“The mistake has been corrected,” Tesla and SpaceX leader Elon Musk wrote on X about Huang’s return.
Musk had been among the most vocal tech leaders to advocate for Huang.
“I think it’s like kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak, and it just doesn’t seem like a good thing for shareholders,” Musk said last week at the DealBook Summit.
OpenAI CEO Sam Altman had taken a more neutral stance, commenting last week, “It’s hard to imagine the AI industry without Jensen.”
Altman had experienced a similar situation when he was ousted as CEO of OpenAI, then reinstated days later. After the announcement that Huang would return, Altman simply posted, “Jensen!” Many other tech CEOs have similarly refrained from commenting on the matter, given the shifting politics surrounding Huang’s ouster and reinstatement, as well as the national security concerns at the center of the controversy.
In addition to Musk, Binance co-founder and former CEO Changpeng Zhao (CZ), who served prison time for failing to stop criminal activity on the Binance crypto exchange, is also supportive of Huang.
“Glad to see @JensenHuang back,” CZ posted on X with a clapping hands emoji.
Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, who had been among 200,000 signatories on an open letter calling for Huang’s reinstatement, wrote on X, “Jensen’s return will be a demonstration of American resiliency to corporate, political and journalistic missteps.”
Another signatory on the open letter, former Uber CEO Travis Kalanick, who had similarly been driven out of the company he founded after scandals, also voiced his support for Huang on X.
When Nvidia’s stock tanked in reaction to Huang’s ousting, billionaire hedge fund manager Bill Ackman said he believes that the Nvidia board was “not acting in the best interest of shareholders nor in a manner consistent with their fiduciary duties.” As Nvidia’s stock began to rebound Monday morning, Ackman wrote, “The wisdom of the crowd prevails.”
The attitude toward Huang’s return is not as celebratory outside of the tech industry. Across the political spectrum, some elected officials are skeptical about President Donald Trump’s claims that Huang’s return does not threaten national security.
“No national security risk? Based on what?” wrote Senator John Cornyn (R-TX).
Senator Pete Ricketts (R-NB) added, “Nvidia’s decision today doesn’t excuse Jensen Huang from testifying before Congress. It also doesn’t absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips. I look forward to Mr. Huang coming to the Senate in December.”
Huang is still expected to testify publicly before the Senate Banking Committee on December 3, as well as privately before three House committees.

The AI conversation we're not having
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
21:46:33 UTC
06:46:33 KST

8 Things Two Years in AI Taught Me That Nobody Posts About
AI is threatening everyone. That is the feeling, anyway.
I’ve been working in AI for about two years now. Most recently that’s been at Arcade.dev, helping teams get their agents into real production use. I’m still figuring a lot of it out, like everyone else.
Over these two years, the same questions keep finding me. A friend over dinner wondering if their job is safe. A customer trying to figure out where to even start. Someone new to tech, and someone with decades in it, both quietly asking what actually matters now. I don’t have all the answers, but I’ve collected a few honest ones. Here are eight things I keep coming back to.
1. Do not doubt the skills you already have
What you have built over years is legitimately useful, even if the ground is shifting under it. Your old skill may be transcending into something else. Figure out what that something else is. But do not start by throwing away what you have.
Compounding still works in skills. It is still working.
And you’re not the only one feeling it. Everyone is adjusting to how fast things are moving. Everyone is going through a disruption in their career and their life, and everyone is working hard to get through it. It is not the whole world ending. It is the whole world changing at the same time.
And if you’re just starting out, you have something the rest of us don’t: no baggage. You haven’t spent ten years learning “the right way” to do things that may not matter anymore. Use that. Try the weird idea. Just remember that a fresh idea only counts once you’ve built it.
2. Meet people in person
The digital world is getting weird. More automation, more bots, more noise. So do more of what AI cannot do.
Have food with people. No robot is going to split a pizza with you and ask how your family is doing. Go meet a friend in person and say hello. It opens up perspectives you will never get inside the tech bubble.
That is how I got my job. I met people in person. A LinkedIn profile is a vastly different thing from the person standing in front of you.
3. Your originality is the leverage
There is exactly one person in the world who is like you. No one can replace that. So go after it, big time. I know it is hard. Go after it anyway.
If someone comes after your originality, they have already lost, because they are admitting they do not have their own.
There is always some value you can add in a given situation, regardless of money, title, or anything else. And that value compounds too. Test it.
4. Build something real. Not something fancy.
I see a lot of people talk about agents. Some are even trying to build critical agent infrastructure, and they have not built two agents for themselves.
Building agents is hard. It is not running a prompt. There is deployment, scaling, quality, evals, design, model choice, prompting, cost. Do not underestimate any of it.
If you’re new, this is your edge. Almost every hiring manager will ask the same question: what have you built? So build. Contribute to an open source project. Ship a rough idea. Put something out there that works, even if it’s ugly.
But don’t confuse building with building a lot. Just because you can build something doesn’t mean you should. Knowing what not to build is a skill too. Building what people actually need is vastly different from building something fancy that no one is going to use.
Talk is cheap. Build the right thing.
5. Adoption is slow. Wait for it.
Almost none of this technology has reached real scale yet, outside a few model companies. True adoption happens slowly, because habits are hard to break.
At the end of the day, humans are the consumers of this technology. They pay from their own pockets. They are the economy. AI will take time to make money, and that is exactly where you can make money too, if you are in the industry.
So do not conclude too early. Do not get frustrated. Do not build FOMO. I am not an AI optimist. I am skeptical of many things. But doomsday is not coming either. There will be a place for you to keep adding value.
Wait for habits to break. Wait for people to embrace agents. Wait for people to understand the patterns.
6. Join problem solvers, and ask hard questions
In any industry, look for the people solving a really hard problem. Find that group and join it.
Do not be fooled by a fancy marketing site. In my experience, the people who do not know the depth of building a product end up with the fanciest marketing. The people building great products often do not have time to polish theirs.
So when a company vibes well with you, question it deeply. Who are their customers? How will they sustain long term, financially? How do they make money? If the market crashes, do they have enough funding secured to survive it? What are their case studies? Do they have a sales team?
Ask those questions before you join, not after.
7. Study the past
History repeats itself. That is why we call something a pattern: because it is one.
You have all this fancy AI. Use some of it to study the past. What happened when the internet arrived? When cars were invented? When world wars reshaped everything? You will find clues about what is going to happen to you. Use them as a wedge to reinvent yourself. There is no other choice.
8. Nobody retires from what they love
Billionaires will tell you money will become invalid and nobody will need to work. Regardless of all that, your hard work, your focus, and your perspective are not going away. Your energy is always going to be there. You have to put it on something.
Do not fall for the retire-at-forty trap. Nobody actually retired. Some people made more money. Some stopped working for a company. But nobody ever retired from their passion. Nobody retired from doing the thing they love.
Working for yourself, building something meaningful, that is not going away.
Do something meaningful.
So, who are you going to meet next week? And what agent are you going to build tomorrow?

====================================================================================================

Shirudo: OpenAI and Cisco's open-source runtime for shielding unpatched Linux
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
21:30:41 UTC
06:30:41 KST

OpenAI and Cisco introduce Shirudo
An open-source runtime that helps operators protect running Linux systems without needing to wait for the patch or restarts
OpenAI and Cisco are launching Shirudo, an open-source runtime that helps teams protect Linux systems while they roll out patches. In a controlled test(opens in a new window), a Codex-generated shield blocked a Java4Shell exploit attempt against a running Elasticsearch service without requiring a restart.
We’re inviting developers, security researchers, and infrastructure operators across the industry to help develop the project further. The project brings together OpenAI’s frontier AI, security and infrastructure research and Cisco’s infrastructure and security expertise to help protect the systems people rely on every day.
Testing and deploying a patch can still take days or weeks. Protecting a service in the meantime can be challenging because an exploit and a legitimate request may rely on the same Linux operation. Blocking that operation entirely could take the service offline.
Shirudo (Japanese for “shield”) is designed to help security teams navigate this tradeoff. Teams can test whether a temporary safeguard, called a shield, blocks the behavior that exposes a vulnerability while allowing legitimate requests through. Once an operator approves the shield, it can be deployed while the service continues running.
Shirudo uses eBPF, a Linux technology that allows small programs to run at specific points inside the operating system. This makes it possible to observe suspicious activity and apply targeted security protections to running systems.
OpenAI and Cisco are designing Shirudo around four core capabilities:
- Live protection: eBPF shields can block vulnerable code paths without restarting the system. Security teams can monitor how a proposed shield would affect applications before enforcing it.
- Defense at machine speed: OpenAI and Cisco are designing Shirudo to give agents programmable controls to investigate vulnerabilities, develop targeted shields, and validate their effectiveness against exploits and real workloads. Security teams control what gets deployed.
- Trusted deployment: Shields are cryptographically signed and verified before loading. Linux's eBPF verifier checks eBPF programs against its safety rules, while kernel security controls restrict unauthorized changes.
- Hardware-backed verification: Shirudo uses a Trusted Platform Module (TPM) to support a verifiable record of protections being loaded or removed. Operators can use this evidence to check whether systems meet their security requirements.
The Shirudo technical walkthrough(opens in a new window) from the Linux Plumbers Conference shows how to apply a shield and verify it on the target host(opens in a new window). The Java4Shell vulnerability is another example illustrating how a targeted shield can protect a running application while a permanent patch is being prepared. Using OpenAI's Codex, the team generated a shield that added an input check to Java's Base64 decoder and attached it directly to a running Elasticsearch process. When attempting to exploit the vulnerability, the shield rejected the malicious input, blocking the attack while Elasticsearch continued running. You can read more here(opens in a new window).
Shirudo is a first step toward infrastructure that can adapt to emerging threats while remaining reliable. We want to strengthen the operating system foundations needed to deploy these protections at scale, including hardening the kernel and core services, limiting workload permissions, and making updates and recovery safer.
Our long-term goal is to enable defenders and AI agents to quickly identify affected systems, validate protections, and deploy eBPF shields that block exploits without disrupting workloads. Applications should run with only the permissions they need, governed by policies that are automatically generated and maintained.
We will combine frontier AI with security and infrastructure research to discover vulnerabilities and test defenses against realistic attacks. We aim to make it easier for operators to detect and contain compromises, prevent attackers from moving across systems, and restore services to a verified, known-good state. Hardware-backed attestation and independent network controls provide additional assurance even if individual systems are compromised.
OpenAI and Cisco are open-sourcing Shirudo(opens in a new window) so developers, security researchers, and infrastructure operators can inspect the code, test protections, and contribute to its development. Shirudo is licensed under BSD 2-Clause, with its BPF programs dual-licensed under BSD and GPL.
Our goal is to make tested shields available alongside vulnerability disclosures, giving operators a way to evaluate and deploy temporary protections while preparing permanent patches.
- Try Shirudo: Follow the VM quickstart(opens in a new window) to sign, load, and verify a test protection.
- Get hands-on: Explore Shirudo through Isovalent's self-guided lab(opens in a new window).
- Join the technical deep dive: Register for the December 15 webinar(opens in a new window).
We’re building Shirudo in the open with the people who build, run, and secure Linux systems. We welcome contributions to the runtime, new shields, and improvements to Linux and eBPF. If you'd like to get involved, take a look at the code on GitHub(opens in a new window), read the docs(opens in a new window), and let us know what you find(opens in a new window).

====================================================================================================

Megaspeed, the company at the center of the Jensen Huang scandal, appears headed for a U.S. blacklist
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
21:25:42 UTC
06:25:42 KST

Megaspeed, the company that Alice Huang — the woman at the center of the alleged Chinese espionage scandal involving Nvidia — led for over two years, will be placed on a U.S. Commerce Department blacklist, Semafor reported Monday.
The company will be added to the Commerce Department’s Entity List, the outlet writes. This list “contains the names of foreign parties” subject to certain export restrictions designed to ensure that “sensitive technologies do not fall into the hands of those who would threaten U.S. national security or American citizens.”
Functionally, what this will mean is that U.S. companies will not be able to do business with or sell products to Megaspeed without first gaining a special license from the federal government. Semafor cites anonymous sources familiar with the matter to note the move is designed to stop the company “from obtaining US-made semiconductors.”
Megaspeed is a Singapore-based cloud and AI infrastructure provider that, during the period that Nvidia’s boss, Jensen Huang, self-admittedly had an affair with Huang, was one of Nvidia’s biggest chip customers. Last week, the Commerce Department also announced it had launched a criminal probe into the company. Authorities in both the U.S. and Singapore are now probing whether the company defied export restrictions and illegally smuggled Nvidia chips to China. The company has denied wrongdoing.
TechCrunch reached out to the Commerce Department for comment on the reporting and will update this story if it responds.
After lobbying from President Trump, Jensen Huang was reinstated as Nvidia CEO on Monday after a short period in which he was ousted by the company’s board over the scandal involving Huang.

====================================================================================================

AI data centers throw another wrench into chaotic UN climate talks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
21:09:37 UTC
06:09:37 KST

The clean energy revolution hinges on electrifying the entire global economy just as power-hungry data centers are surging. It’s a challenge delegates from all over the world had to grapple with during the United Nations climate conference, called COP31, that came to a chaotic and unsatisfying close in Antalya, Turkey, on Sunday.
AI data centers throw another wrench into chaotic UN climate talks
Global climate negotiations fell apart as delegates tried to tackle AI’s growing carbon footprint.
Each year, attendees try to make good on the commitment to stop climate change as part of the 2015 Paris climate accord. Preventing extreme weather from growing more terrifying, coastal communities from flooding, and droughts from wrecking food and water systems requires slashing greenhouse gas emissions. But in order to power homes or cars with solar and wind instead of oil and gas, they need to go electric.
“Every climate minister in the world is thinking about this”
To that end, one of the most anticipated outcomes of this year’s negotiations was a deal to raise electricity’s share of global energy consumption to 35 percent by 2035, compared to about 20 percent today. That becomes harder to do, however, when data centers are causing electricity demand to skyrocket. That made the rush of new AI data centers a climate issue alongside talks on how to transition from fossil fuels to renewable energy.
“Every climate minister in the world is thinking about this, and it will inevitably come up in the discussions. It’s part of the broader energy transformation,” Chris Bowen, Australia’s minister for climate change and energy and the president of COP31 climate negotiations, told Politico in an interview at the start of the COP31 summit.
Despite becoming the longest COP yet, with debates lasting two days longer than scheduled, official negotiations fell apart spectacularly. Delegates ultimately failed to garner enough support to pass the “pact” they’d been bargaining on for two weeks. The EU, UK, and several smaller nations most vulnerable to climate change rejected the deal that they saw as too weak. The pact omitted the term “fossil fuels” despite many nations calling for stronger commitments to phase out oil, coal, and gas. And the US, the epicenter of the data center buildout and the biggest climate polluter historically, was a no-show after the pro-fossil-fuel Trump administration pulled the country out of the Paris accord (twice).
With official negotiations falling apart, and countries only making voluntary commitments on AI and electrification, some environmental and humanitarian groups called this year “the worst COP” ever.
To meet the Paris goals, nations will need to cut their greenhouse gas emissions roughly in half by 2030 before reaching net zero by the middle of the century. Many tech companies made similar commitments in recent years, including Google, Microsoft, and Meta. But thanks to the AI boom, those goals have fallen by the wayside.
Meta is “re-evaluating” its timeline to reach net zero emissions as it focuses on AI, the company said in its annual sustainability report on Thursday. Meta reported a 45 percent year-over-year increase in climate pollution. Google’s and Microsoft’s carbon footprints are also growing despite climate commitments the companies have made. Data centers’ electricity consumption is forecast to double by 2030, according to a forecast by the International Energy Agency.
“That is a huge climate risk: rising power demand from data centres is not yet sustainable, and an AI boom cannot be allowed to become a fossil fuel boom,” Bruce Douglas, CEO of the Global Renewables Alliance, tells The Verge in an email. Data centers were “a key topic” at COP31, according to Douglas, whose organization cohosted a panel on the challenges during the event.
In the US, the country with the most data centers, power sector emissions could increase 42 percent by 2030 as energy companies build more fossil fuel infrastructure to meet data center electricity demand, according to a recent analysis by the Sierra Club. Already, hyperscale data centers in the US create more climate pollution than the country of Denmark, a separate study by Harvard researchers recently found.
To stop data centers from creating more pollution, they would need to be powered by additional new sources of renewable energy, Douglas says. He adds that data centers are currently competing with everyone else for grid connections, finance, and electricity generation capacity — and against arguably more essential tasks like heating and air conditioning, as well as electrifying transportation.
Many tech companies match their electricity use with renewable energy purchases. But unless they’re helping to build new sources of green energy to meet their added electricity demand, they might just end up hogging limited renewable energy capacity. And if more fossil fuels make up the difference, then greenhouse gas emissions continue to rise overall.
“Should new green electrons be used for air-conditioning, electrification or data centres?”
“In many cases, datacentres are outbidding other consumers to get speed to power,” Douglas says. “Should new green electrons be used for air-conditioning, electrification or data centres?”
There was some progress. Around 80 nations signed onto a voluntary pledge to increase electricity’s share of energy consumption to 35 percent over the next decade, according to COP31 president Murat Kurum. The list of countries that includes, however, hasn’t been published yet, Carbon Brief reports.
Another voluntary pledge to keep AI from derailing climate action by calling on data centers to run on less polluting energy also failed to garner much support. Douglas notes that by using the term “low-emission energy,” the pledge still leaves the door open for natural gas (which produces less carbon pollution than coal when burned but is a significant source of methane pollution) to keep powering data centers.
“The Antalya A.I. pledge is a step in the right direction to address an important piece of the impacts, but needs teeth in order to translate into stronger protections for people and the planet,” the nonprofit Natural Resources Defense Council said in a statement when the pledge was first announced. “The speed, scale and scope of this challenge is bigger than perhaps anything before it, and the guardrails must be commensurate.”

The Pantry Ingredient That Saves Bland Gravy Every Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

The Pantry Ingredient That Saves Bland Gravy Every Time

====================================================================================================

The horse made the state
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
20:59:12 UTC
05:59:12 KST

Listen to this essay
58 minute listen
Transportation is the backbone of civilisation, which is why controlling the Strait of Hormuz grants global leverage. Because oil fuels more than 90 per cent of global transit, the source of this power lies not in the waterway, but in the control of global mobility. Halting all oil supplies paralyses virtually all transport, communication and supply lines: cities collapse, states disintegrate, societies break down and populations die off, throwing humanity back to the pre-industrial age. Without transport animals, humanity would still be back in the Stone Age.
No city or state can exist without transportation, for there can be no concentration of population and resources. No transportation means no extrahuman transportation, leaving walking and carrying as the only means of transit. This baseline determined the size and structure of human communities. Production was limited to what could be carried home, circumscribing the areas where humans could settle and colonise. Production capacity and resource availability kept communities small, forcing them to split and disperse. Because size dictates structure, small meant undifferentiated and egalitarian. Ultimately, the inherent limit of the physical capability of travel and portage formed a natural immunity to inequality: it kept communities small, egalitarian and autonomous, leaving their lands beyond conquest.
As long as this constraint existed, communities resembled those of the Amazon Basin or the New Guinea Highlands. The domestication of animals and the invention of boats liberated humanity, making long-distance mobilisation possible. With this newfound capacity, communities no longer needed to fit their immediate environments; instead, they could build cities and integrate expansive areas – all roads lead to Rome. Transportation – not grain – is what brought the state and civilisation into existence, and what continues to maintain them. Let’s examine this power by looking from the present back to the origin of the state.
Whoever rules the waves rules the world! Without its navy and air force, the United States cannot command global reach or project power worldwide. The same was true of European colonial empires; ultimately, warships and gunboats forged them. In earlier periods, Vikings built their empire on longships, Aztecs on canoes, Egyptians on riverboats, and Romans, Greeks and Phoenicians on galleys.
Conquering the world on horseback is easy, as the saying goes. On land, equids – donkeys and horses – forged states and civilisations, while the llama did the same for the Inkas. Ur’s kunga chariots project the military might of Mesopotamian cities, and the Abydos donkeys demonstrate their vital role in early Egyptian kingship. By the end of the 3rd millennium BCE, Central Eurasian nomads were entombing entire horse chariots – the high-tech, high-value war machines of their time – to honour their kings in Sintashta. Horse chariots later symbolised the power of the Shang and the Zhou; the latter deployed 4,000 chariots to overwhelm the former in 1046 BCE.
Building a state does not require grain, but does demand extrahuman transportation
‘The rider appears on the stage of history like a new breed of man’ – a centaur, as the German sociologist Alexander Rüstow put it in the 1950s. Eurasian nomads had long bred their mounts into the modern domestic horse, paving the way for an age of empires that would fundamentally reshape the world. Horses dominated human history for nearly four millennia. Even for maritime powers, horses remained key to conquest; from Carthage to the British, all relied on horses for inland expansion. Francisco Pizarro’s conquest of the Inkas is a prime illustration of the power of horses against an essentially pedestrian empire built on llama transportation. Horses also enabled the Comanche in North America to forge their empire and compete with European powers for nearly a century.
The Comanche Empire? Did they actually have grain to build a state? No, they had no grain – nor any agriculture, for that matter. Horse husbandry and bison hunting sustained their livelihood. In fact, all the Eurasian steppe powers – including the Mongols – relied on pastoralism. What they enjoyed most was an abundance of extrahuman transportation; what they lacked most was agricultural produce. Ultimately, building a state does not require grain, but it does demand extrahuman transportation.
Look at the political geography of the Roman and Mongol empires: the Romans could reach where ships could reach, and the Mongols could reach where horses could reach. The same is true of European colonial empires; it all comes down to the power of extrahuman transportation. Donkeys, horses and all other herd animals are the creations of pastoralists. Extrahuman transportation made the state and civilisation possible; this places pastoralism at the origin of the state and civilisation, and the Eurasian steppe at the centre of human history.
Yet the Sinocentric paradigm – the predominant vision of human history and civilisation with China at its centre – structurally erases extrahuman transportation and, with it, pastoralism, steppe empires and charisma from state formation, thereby negating the Eurasian steppe as the prime centre of human history. This framework shapes our current understanding of world history, leaving us with a view defined by entrapment, doom and despair. Becoming aware of this paradigm is essential if we are to break free from this bleak outlook, better comprehend the world we live in, and chart a brighter future.
While scholars critique world history for its Eurocentric bias, its conceptual framework and narrative are profoundly Sinocentric. This is because the origin of the state – and consequently the birth of civilisation – is the question that shapes our vision of world history. The latest authoritative iteration of this view is James C Scott’s Against the Grain: A Deep History of the Earliest States (2017), along with his The Art of Not Being Governed (2009). ‘The Origin of the State: Land Productivity or Appropriability’ by Joram Mayshar and colleagues is its economic restatement. Scott, readers might counter, focuses almost exclusively on Mesopotamia and rarely mentions China. Yet his underlying framework actually rearticulates the paradigm established by the American Orientalist Owen Lattimore’s influential work, Inner Asian Frontiers of China (1940). Precisely for this reason, Scott not only frequently mentions ‘the loess soils along the Yellow River’, but also, directly quoting Lattimore, claims that ‘irrigation was spectacularly rewarding in the loess core of ancient China’.
Lattimore sought to legitimise the Republic of China’s claims over Mongolia, Manchukuo, Tibet and Eastern Turkestan. To this end, he advanced the grain thesis to locate the origins of statehood and civilisation in the Yellow River Valley, positioning China as the Cradle of the East. By mapping Frederick Jackson Turner’s American frontier thesis onto the region, Lattimore framed China as the civilised centre and reduced the aforementioned territories to ‘Inner Asia’ – a mere geohistorical frontier akin to the American West. In doing so, Lattimore abandoned the traditional conquest theory – a framework he had previously espoused – which held that both China’s early states and most of its subsequent empires were forged by Eurasian nomadic conquerors rather than indigenous dynamics. The rejection of the conquest theory was a repudiation of extrahuman transportation and pastoralism as forces that led to statehood, and an exaltation of sedentism and grain agriculture instead.
The paradigm owes its formation to Cold War politics and is at odds with archaeological evidence
The theory that entrenched Lattimore’s thesis in academia is Robert Carneiro’s circumscription hypothesis, published in the journal Science as ‘A Theory of the Origin of the State’ (1970). Not just conventional narratives, but a wide array of prominent thinkers – including Michael Mann, Jared Diamond, Francis Fukuyama and Yuval Noah Harari – popularised Carneiro’s theory in different guises. Even David Graeber and David Wengrow ultimately remain within this framework.
Drawing on Carneiro’s concept of circumscribed agricultural land, Mann’s The Sources of Social Power (1986) posits ecological and social cages with no ‘escape route’, while Diamond’s Guns, Germs, and Steel (1997) similarly focuses on regions with ‘nowhere to flee’ to isolate the environmental conditions that generate the population pressure needed for the rise of the state. Fukuyama’s The Origins of Political Order (2011), however, is far more direct in delineating the primary conditions for pristine state formation: ‘that population needs to be physically constrained so that it increases in density’ and ‘that subjects cannot run away when coerced’. When Diamond wrote in Nature in 2002 that agricultural storable food surpluses ‘led to social stratification, political centralisation and standing armies’, he was merely rationalising the Lattimore-Carneiro population-pressure dogma as an ‘autocatalytic’ process. Harari’s Sapiens (2011), with its disease-ridden ‘luxury trap’, Scott’s The Art of Not Being Governed, along with his Against the Grain, and Graeber and Wengrow’s lamentation that ours is a species ‘enslaved to its crops’ all ultimately stem from the same grain thesis.
Indeed, the Lattimore-Carneiro framework relied on a population-pressure model, arguing that agriculture and sedentism triggered overpopulation, forcing the creation of hierarchies and states. However, Carneiro’s circumscription thesis required areas such as Mesopotamia, the Nile Valley and the Peruvian coast to be entirely overcrowded. Instead, empirical settlement surveys revealed that these areas lacked the demographic density to exhaust arable land, with some even experiencing depopulation during early state-formation. Because no population pressure existed to catalyse state formation, archaeological findings invalidated Carneiro’s theory.
Scott’s novelty lies neither in his rhetoric of ‘political crops’, nor in his laser-focused ‘circumscribed ecological niches’ of ‘alluvial or loess soils’, but rather in his theoretical sleight of hand: dynamic climatic circumscription. His fertile alluvial or loess soils mirror Carneiro’s circumscribed agricultural land, though on a minuscule scale, engineered by a millennium-long drought rather than geographic barriers. Ultimately, Scott repurposes Carneiro’s model of resource concentration – a resource-rich habitat that draws people from surrounding regions – yet he supercharges it with the driving force of drought. In sum, drought – simultaneously reconfiguring the physical terrain into a localised cage and driving populations inward – enables Scott to isolate the minuscule, 250-hectare footprint of Uruk as the circumscribed agricultural land. Scott’s model here is a clever manoeuvre to salvage the Sinocentric paradigm. That paradigm, as we shall see, owes its formation to Cold War politics and is at odds with archaeological evidence.
Whether climatic or geographical, the agricultural cage is inherently an anti-mobility framing that structurally erases pastoralism from the formation of the state. This erasure is, however, not just a conceptual flaw; it is written into the paradigm’s DNA, as the framework is fundamentally built against mobility, and actively argues for its exclusion. The erasure takes the form of epistemic theft by way of attributing the authorship of domestication of livestock and pastoralism to farmers, delaying the date of the advent of pastoralism through hyper-restrictive definitions, and relying on exclusionary framing. While the initial framework was a politically motivated, instrumental scheme, subsequent models have resulted from paradigmatic biases.
The question that led to this erasive framing was not the origin of the state in general, but its emergence in what is now China – specifically, whether it was indigenous or born of nomadic conquest. This issue was linked to a raging political dilemma: did Manchuria, and by extension the Daiching Empire (also known as the Qing dynasty), constitute a Chinese or an Inner Asian empire? This debate was a political question concerning the definition of China and the delineation of its territory. Specifically, should China be defined as ‘China proper’, restricted to the Ming provinces, or as ‘China imperial’, encompassing the domains of the Daiching Empire.
The controversy escalated into a heightened political crisis with the declaration of Manchukuo. Japanese scholarship argued that the Daiching Empire was an Inner Asian state that had conquered China, and that the Manchus were a distinct people deserving independence. The Chinese nationalist regime and its scholarship countered, arguing that the empire was inherently Chinese, and that Manchuria had been an integral part of China since antiquity. Meanwhile, contemporary critical scholarship essentially agreed with the Japanese view; the dominant framework for the origins of the state in China was the invasion theory, which argued that early Chinese states, as well as many subsequent empires, were forged by Eurasian nomadic conquerors. Crucially, until then, Lattimore himself had been a proponent of this theory.
The Republic of China was a legal fiction enabled by the Open Door policy (authored by US diplomats in 1899). Yet, this very regime claimed sovereignty over Mongolia, Manchuria, Eastern Turkestan and Tibet. By the 1930s, however, the Open Door order was in tatters: Outer Mongolia had declared independence in 1911, Tibet in 1912, and Manchukuo had broken away in 1932, absorbing Eastern Inner Mongolia. The rest of Inner Mongolia was ruled by a Japanese-allied government, while Eastern Turkestan declared independence in 1933.
Had Mongolia and the others remained states, they could not have been conceptualised as frontiers, but rather as independent neighbours
Recognising the Daiching Empire as an Inner Asian state meant forfeiting China’s claim over all of these regions, stripping the regime of what Lattimore estimated at nearly two-thirds of its claimed territory. Worse still for the West, the Soviet Union was poised to become the ultimate beneficiary – an outcome the regime and its primary architect, the US, were determined to prevent. The regime had to ensure that its territory emerged as inherently Chinese, and scholarship was needed to substantiate this claim. This required refuting the invasion theory and reframing China as the Cradle of the East. Lattimore’s book, Inner Asian Frontiers of China, accomplished precisely this.
