Likewise, there can be no bureaucracy and no bureaucrat by virtue of appropriation, election or designation; the appropriation of office and bureaucracy are mutually exclusive. If rulership excludes appointment, appropriation excludes bureaucracy. Bureaucracy follows rulership and serves it; bureaucracy is merely the administrative apparatus of the state. Ultimately, no rule is law if it is not enforced, no land is territory if it is not under sovereignty, and no authority is a bureaucracy if it is not appointed by a ruler. It is rulership that creates law, territory and bureaucracy. By equating the state with bureaucracy, the Sinocentric paradigm not only turned Weber’s theory upside down but also misled us.
Ironically, Weber actually conceptualises the premodern ‘centralised and highly developed administration’ as a product of conquest rule. To maintain domination, the state develops a repressive apparatus to domesticate its subjects. By necessity, it becomes ‘strongest in conquered territories, because of the continuous threat to’ the conquerors. Weber notes:
It is no accident that the Norman states, especially England, were the only feudal states in the Occident with a really centralised and highly developed administration; the same was true of the Arabic, Sassanid and Turkish warrior states, which were most highly organised in conquered areas.
He similarly attributes bureaucratic administration ‘in the Orient’ to ‘new conquerors’ who ‘counteracted quite effectively the otherwise natural process of decentralisation and appropriation.’
Conquerors by necessity had to monopolise power, especially military power, themselves. Yet, because of linguistic, cultural and other local barriers, they had to rely on local interlocutors. However, giving them hereditary military power amounted to creating rival power centres, as the early Manchu creation of the Three Feudatories demonstrates. The most effective means to concentrate power in their own hands, and at the same time efficiently domesticate and administer the conquered population, was to appoint easily removable civilian administrative officials.
However, scholars – including Scott, Fukuyama, Graeber and Wengrow – equate the state with bureaucracy, and search for traces of bureaucracy to find the state. Thus, Scott confidently asserts that ‘it was only in such settings that the first bureaucratic states were likely to arise.’ Graeber and Wengrow go further to advance a novel conceptualisation that essentially defines the state as bureaucracy, ‘as a combination of sovereignty, bureaucracy and a competitive political field.’ Clearly, Jean Bodin’s and Thomas Hobbes’s sovereignty or Weber’s monopoly of violence is not enough to constitute the state. Indeed, Graeber and Wengrow discourse ‘on sovereignty without “the state”’ and search for ‘a great bureaucratic machine’ to find a state. Ultimately, to be a state is to be a bureaucracy. For this reason, Fukuyama in 2011 declares that ‘China alone created a modern state in the terms defined by Max Weber … in developing a centralised, uniform system of bureaucratic administration’. Consequently, by declaring: ‘I take China as a paradigm of state formation and ask why other civilisations didn’t replicate the path it followed,’ Fukuyama positions China as the norm, thereby casting the rest of the world – including Greece, Rome and Europe as a whole – as a ‘divergent’ abnormality, that is, what Lattimore called a ‘barbarian plague’. The Sinocentric paradigm thus places China at the centre and the summit of ‘All under Heaven’.
We set out to learn the origins of the state by studying its preeminent theories and the deep history of the earliest states. Yet, have we truly learned anything about the state’s origins? Or, have we instead, as Scott notes, merely learned what ‘can serve as a basis for taxation’ – namely, its ‘productive base’? Mayshar and his colleagues, who similarly argue that ‘hierarchy arose as a result of the shift to dependence on appropriable cereal grains’, also demonstrate how grain serves as a basis for taxation. This is Scott’s theory of the origin of the state, and it is – one might say – fundamentally a theory of the state’s productive base, not of its origin. Yet, this is the preeminent theory of state origins – the reigning evolutionary, materialist, population-pressure paradigm that I call the Sinocentric paradigm. Ultimately, this paradigm represents a colonial legacy that displaced conquest theory.
What, then, is conquest theory, and what does it say about the origin of the state? What exactly is the state, and how is it defined? Remarkably, both theories accept the same definition of the state. That the state is a human community that (successfully) claims the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force within a given territory is a universally accepted definition. Weber defines the state sociologically ‘only in terms of the specific means peculiar to it, as to every political association, namely, the use of physical force,’ that is, ‘the monopoly of the legitimate use of physical force’ (italics original). In short, the state is the institution that exercises a monopoly on legitimate violence. Therefore, when looking for the origin of the state, we should examine how this institution arose. That is exactly what conquest theory does.
Therefore, in looking for the origin of the state, conquest theory examines the rise of physical force – that is, the institution of violence – and the establishment of a monopoly over it. This framework locates that physical force in the warband and the monopoly in the warlord, and explores how that monopoly becomes both necessary and legitimate. Specifically, it finds the monopoly in the need for leadership and organisation within the warband, and the legitimacy in the warband’s need for security. Finally, it explores the conditions that give rise to, or necessitate, this force, and locates those conditions in a state of war.
By rendering the security of the warband paramount, war makes violence legitimate
Making life and liberty scarce, war necessitates physical force in the form of a warband. A warband emerges as a warrior consociation – a community of common political struggle over life and death, and a warrior brotherhood – where individuals are expected to face death in the group’s interest. War also forges the warband into a rational normative community: in Weber’s words, a ‘pressing need for security against internal and foreign enemies induces a growing rationalisation of lawmaking and lawfinding.’ Thus, war creates the warband as a warrior fraternity and a rational-legal consociation.
Demanding unity, organisation, discipline and leadership, war not only moulds a warband into a cohesive group but also fashions the warlord as its leader. In turn, the unity, organisation and discipline – which require both centralised authority and the monopolisation of power – render the warlord a monarch who commands and compels obedience. Ultimately, by rendering the security of the warband paramount, war makes violence legitimate. As Weber writes: ‘Violence acquires legitimacy only in those cases, however – at least initially – in which it is directed against members of the fraternity who have acted treasonably or who have harmed it by disobedience or cowardice.’ Therefore, a warband is a human community that successfully claims a monopoly on the legitimate use of physical force. As such, the rise of the warband marks the beginning of the state. Thus, in the rise of the warband, conquest theory locates both the military differentiation and the military constitution – the archetype of the state. A victorious warband that has successfully established a monopoly of violence over a population in a stretch of land becomes the state.
Therefore, the state is fundamentally a relation of political domination – specifically, domination by virtue of authority (defined as the power to command and the duty to obey) – based ultimately upon a monopoly of violence over life and liberty. It is a domination founded on both the threat to and the protection of life and liberty; this is the fundamental reason for the monopoly of violence, which constitutes sovereignty. In short, war brings kingship as a war power in the form of a warband, and the king as a war leader in the person of a warlord. As Weber observes, ‘Everywhere the king is primarily a warlord,’ and ‘kingship is normally charismatic war leadership that has become permanent and has developed a repressive apparatus for the domestication of the unarmed subjects.’ This is entirely Weber’s theory, and this reading makes him one of the most conceptual, yet profoundly misunderstood, conquest theorists.
It is not resource surplus, scarcity or appropriability that leads to the rise of the state; instead, it is the direct and unmediated monopoly over life and liberty. This domination is not mediated by resource control, but by direct control over life and liberty. For this reason, not only does the political exist on its own terms, but political relationships are also primary and fundamental. It is the political constitution that determines and defines both the social and the economic. Consequently, private property, land scarcity, social stratification and social classes – and, indeed, society and economy – are consequences of the state rather than preconditions for it, institutions ultimately forged by conquest, subjugation, expropriation and political integration.
However, it is not just war per se or armed conflict, but conquest – or wars of conquest – that gives rise to the state, which is why the theory is called conquest theory. This model sees mobility or extrahuman transportation as the decisive revolutionary force. Without extrahuman transportation, forming a large society is impossible, and communities are forced to split and disperse, as observed in Amazonia. Ultimately, extrahuman transportation is what enables conquest and state-building.
The state first arose among nomads, who then conquered neighbouring farming regions
Extrahuman transportation empowered those who possessed it, and made mobilisation – and, consequently, the domination of man over man – possible over large areas. By making mobilisation and concentration possible, extrahuman transportation not only led to the rise of the warband but also, by facilitating conquest, fundamentally changed the nature of war. War was transformed into campaigns of conquest, an enterprise of state-building that rendered communities and their lands the prime targets for subjugation and forced integration into a larger society. Conquest and state-building created what in the 1950s Rüstow termed an ‘opening configuration’. This ‘new configuration’ served as ‘a new natural aggregation of external and internal factors of human life’, providing the ‘opening configuration for the development of all high cultures’ – the state and society.
Because pastoralists possessed extrahuman transportation in the form of herding animals, the conquest theory argues that the state first arose among nomads, who then conquered neighbouring farming regions. For this reason, the conquest theory is primarily a theory of nomadic conquest over sedentary farmers. In addition to high mobility, the theory identifies military leadership, organisation, discipline, martial skills and a warrior ethos as key cultural factors in the rise of the state, noting that these qualities are highly developed among pastoralists. Undeniably, frequent long-distance migration with large herds under harsh and unpredictable conditions naturally fosters these leadership and martial attributes.
While conquests by equestrian warrior nomads were the most consequential in the rise of the state and civilisation, the conquest theory maps a broader historical sequence. It posits a series of conquests over time: first of hunters over cultivators, then of herdsmen over planters and peasants and, finally, of European colonial powers. Conquest theory by no means valorises conquest; instead, it views it as creating despotic, two-strata class states whose toxic legacies we must confront and overcome. Indeed, conquest theory offers a radically different vision and framework to make sense of the human journey and its historical trajectory. It presents a vision neither of ever-evolving progress nor of inevitability, but equally not of permanent entrapment or doom.
Through this lens, much like conquests, each and every progressive political, social, economic and cultural achievement of the modern era, just like each conquest of the previous era, stems from a charismatic revolution – be it political, social, economic or scientific – aimed at dismantling the bitter legacies of institutional injustice and natural limitations. From the early foundational shifts of liberal revolutions and constitutionalism, to global decolonisation, to human and environmental rights frameworks; and from the Industrial Revolution, to the rise of STEM, to the advent of AI – all are, for better or worse, the fruits of charismatic ideas, individuals, endeavours and movements. Ultimately, it is not the invisible engine of evolution that drives history, but the fire of human agency.
Though the human journey remains a persistent pursuit of freedom, its trajectory is conceptualised as a transition from the despotic, two-strata, class-based ‘State of robbery’ to the ‘Freemen’s Citizenship’ of the future (as Franz Oppenheimer put it in 1914) – a self-governing society – progressing through a series of states, including constitutional ones. However, the path of progress is neither linear nor consistent. Every step of liberation has been shaped by historical contingencies, facing severe setbacks, systemic backlash and profound failures along the way. Moreover, the achievements of today’s charismatic revolutions routinely become tomorrow’s institutional trap and bulwark: the liberating ideas of one era harden into the systemic barriers of the next. Progress, therefore, remains trapped in a perpetual cycle of revolution and institutionalisation. Despite these fractures and constraints, the core drive remains unyielding: charisma – a deeply human, deeply individual, and deeply personal impulse for freedom.
Through this lens, the Sinocentric paradigm misconstrues the human past, present and future; it rationalises the past as a product of ecology and evolution; and it naturalises the present as given, and the future as fated. It misguides us, blaming our numbers for our conduct. It attributes what was, and is, cultural to nature, and supposes what was, and is, natural to be cultural.
Conquest theory arms us with a framework to not only break free from the Sinocentric paradigm but also to critically re-evaluate human history, fully reclaim our agency, and purposefully chart our own future. Only by overcoming the Sinocentric paradigm and embracing conquest theory can we construct a human history that fully appreciates the significance of the extrahuman transportation outlined at the outset of this essay. Such an account will be more than the new history of humanity that Graeber and Wengrow deemed necessary; it will provide a framework that finally rescues human history from being treated as an anomaly.

And Now Once More My Travels Have Ended
Posted on November 23, 2026 Posted by John Scalzi 13 Comments
After three weeks of being on the road with only a limited amount of time home, I have no more business travel for the rest of 2026. I’m going the spend most of the rest of the year in a bathrobe, blanket over me, cat on my lap, probably watching movies. You know, as one does for the holidays.
If I saw you on the road this November, thank you for coming out to see me and for supporting my weird little monster book. I really appreciate both. And I hope the rest of your 2026 is also restful and enjoyable.
PS. I am in fact writing this in a bathrobe with a cat laying on me. She is blocking my view of the keyboard. Please excuse any errors.
— JS

Apple renews Ted Lasso for season 5
No word yet on when it might be streaming.

Ted Lasso has proven to be one of the tentpole series for Apple TV, and it returned in 2026 after a three-year hiatus. Nvidia employees greet Jensen Huang like a hero as he returns to company’s HQ
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
20:38:53 UTC
05:38:53 KST

Jensen Huang returned to Nvidia’s offices on Monday to an applauding crowd of hundreds of employees, according to several videos circulating online.
The embattled Nvidia founder had stepped down earlier this month amid a series of scandals — including an affair with an alleged Chinese spy, which has triggered multiple Congressional hearings over national security, and two affairs with Nvidia employees — but was reinstated as CEO after pushback from the tech community and an intervention by President Donald Trump.
After basking in chants of his name, Huang briefly addressed the crowd.
“Thank you all for the second chance,” he said. “I’m going to be a better CEO the second time around.”
Over 200,000 people, including around 10,000 current Nvidia employees, had signed an open letter advocating for Huang’s return. Other tech leaders also pushed for Huang’s reinstatement, including Elon Musk, who said last week at the DealBook Summit that letting Huang go is like “kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak.”
Amid this increased scrutiny, however, other reports have depicted a different side to Nvidia’s top exec — one where Jensen is not seen as a beloved leader, but as a brutal bully. Based on decades of court documents and over a dozen interviews with current and former Nvidia employees, The New York Times reported that Huang “used humiliation as a tool” and verbally belittled staff at times.

Su Blackwell Enchants with Folkloric Stories That Spring from Vintage Books
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
20:33:23 UTC
05:33:23 KST

Su Blackwell Enchants with Folkloric Stories That Spring from Vintage Books
From vintage tomes and encyclopedic volumes, British artist Su Blackwell carves, slices, and reconfigures text-laden pages into enchanting sculptures. Her latest body of work, opening this week in a solo show titled Pathway of Crumbs, draws on fairytales and folklore, a fitting topic for such dreamlike scenes.
Nodding to the enduring children’s story of Hansel and Gretel, the exhibition roots in nature and the stories we draw from the world around us. “In Flight,” for example, stretches nearly 3.5 feet as a swarm of butterflies emerges from the flared pages of a book. “Break off my Branches” is similarly dynamic, with a flock of multi-colored birds perched on the spindly stems that burst from a splayed edition.
Blackwell selected this particular set of books because they’ve been in her collection for several years, allowing the artist to digest their contents and ruminate on how they might emerge over time. Some she’s left in the same condition since purchasing them from a secondhand shop, and others she’s minimally cut and spliced for earlier projects. All, though, have remained a compelling artifact of human knowledge and a collective desire to create, one she transforms anew into striking compositions at the intersection of nature and culture.
Pathway of Crumbs is on view from November 25 to January 25 at Long & Ryle in London. Find more from Blackwell on her website.

Next gaming monitor should still be an OLED
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
20:30:46 UTC
05:30:46 KST

They're more complicated and more expensive than LCD screens, but your next gaming monitor should still be an OLED
Go for the burn
Your next gaming monitor should be an OLED display. There, I've said it. And I know the notion won't please everyone. After all, a large grey proboscidean lumbers into the room whenever OLED technology is mentioned. Put simply, almost any risk of the dreaded burn-in is an immediate deal-breaker for some. I do get that.
Likewise, despite my musings just the other day on how affordable gaming monitors have generally become and the fact you can now snag an OLED panel, specifically, for sub £300 / $300, it's a stretch to say OLED gaming comes cheap. And yet here I am arguing firmly in favour of OLED.
The point is that OLED is fundamentally superior to LCD, the latter being the dominant display tech on the PC for decades and also, as it happens, an unambiguous technological kludge. Understanding why is the relatively easy bit. Where things get extraordinarily, even belligerently, complicated concerns the current OLED gaming monitor market and the baffling array of panel types and monitor models on offer.
We'll get to all that. But let's start with the core appeal of OLED, which comes down to two factors: lighting control and speed. In both regards, OLED technology completely annihilates LCD. The thing about OLED panels and lighting is that each pixel is its own individually controlled light source. Actually, each subpixel is.
That means literally perfect lighting control. There's no leakage, no need for bodges like local dimming. Just the ability to have immediately adjacent pixels doing absolutely opposite things, one fully lit up, the other totally off. The impact of that on contrast is epic. In short, if you want a true HDR experience that combines sizzling bright highlights with properly controlled black levels, only OLED can do that.
The problem with LCD, you see, is that it's a transmissive technology, so light is pushed through the pixels from a big, dumb backlight behind. The liquid crystals in each subpixel are then oriented by an electric charge, controlling the quantity and colour of the light output. In practice, there are oh so many limitations to that approach.
Chief among these problems is that the backlight leaks through in all kinds of unintended ways. And let's be clear, technologies like mini-LED backlights with full-array local dimming don't get anywhere near entirely compensating for LCD's shortcomings. For starters, even a mini-LED backlight with 2,000 zones is very, very low resolution in terms of lighting.
With that kind of zone count, you're looking at a backlight resolution of roughly 55 zones by 35 zones in a 16:9 monitor. Imagine if the actual image resolution was 55 by 35 pixels. Yuck. In the context of a 4K monitor, then, each zone is shared across approximately 4,000 pixels. That's a lot and it translates into visible blooming, weird brightness gradients, dimming zones noticeably popping on and off and all that messy, ugly stuff.
While we're talking liquid crystals twisting in the electrical wind, that's also the reason why LCD is so much slower than OLED. It involves the physical rotation of those rod-shaped crystals to update the image. Like I said, the pixels in an OLED panel are their own light-emitting diode sources, and very rapidly reacting ones at that. So, OLED response times are essentially instantaneous from a human perception perspective.
I will admit, it's still impressive how good modern LCD panels can look, despite their numerous baked-in limitations. But in lots of important ways, OLED is inherently more suited to gaming displays than LCD. The catch is that it, too, is imperfect, ye olde burn-in being the most obvious shortcoming.
The "O" in OLED refers to "organic", as in Organic Light Emitting Diode. The "organic" bit translates into carbon-based molecules that degrade with use. In short, the harder you drive an OLED pixel, the more its ability to output light is worn out. Over time, the whole panel will wear to some degree, degrading maximum light output. But the real fear is differential wear that leads to unevenness across the screen. The classic scenario is static on-screen interface furniture, perhaps the Windows task bar or persistent parts of a game interface like a health bar or minimap. Eventually, those elements will lead to uneven panel wear.
Pretty much all current OLED gaming monitors have various burn-in mitigation technologies, such as pixel shift, refresh cycles, edge dimming and so on. But none entirely solve the problem. As for real-world expectations of just how bad burn-in can be, there are several independent assessments of OLED monitor burn-in. For instance, Monitors Unboxed has been running one that's now at the 30-month mark, testing absolute worst-case usage on the MSI MPG 321URX, which is from the first generation of 4K, Samsung-made QD-OLED panels.
For what it's worth, my overall view - based on both personal experience with numerous OLED panels over the last few years, and what you might call the emerging consensus - is that burn-in is a real problem, but shouldn't stop most of us buying an OLED. The aforementioned worst-case scenario usage includes driving the panel hard in terms of brightness settings, routine use of persistent interface elements and even failing to allow burn-in mitigation measures to run optimally. Basically, doing all the wrong things. And the evidence is that even this doesn't lead to truly catastrophic burn-in.
After a year of really heavy daily use, you'll probably see some very slight burn-in that's only visible if you go out of your way to look for it. In the second and third years, the burn-in will become more apparent, but only gradually, and even then it will likely still only be visible in certain situations, like an application window with a uniform grey background. The panel will also experience a touch of overall brightness drop off, but not enough to be subjectively noticeable.
All of that applies to early OLED monitors released three to four years ago, starting with the Alienware 34 AW3423DW. By definition, it hasn't been possible to test recently released panel generations over several years. But each new generation has arrived with promises of improved burn-in resistance.
If that's an overview of generic OLED tech, what about the specifics of the stuff available to buy? It turns out all OLED gaming monitors use panels from just two sources, LG and Samsung. Strictly, there is a new player just arriving on the scene, TCL CSOT, and with it a novel so-called inkjet OLED tech. But there's little to nothing you can actually buy right now with that technology in the gaming space.
So, LG and Samsung it is. LG's tech has broadly been branded "WOLED", where the "W" stands for "white", while Samsung's panels have been branded "QD-OLED", with the "QD" referring to quantum dot technology. Whether you buy an OLED monitor from Asus, MSI, Alienware or anywhere else, it'll use one of those two basic panel types.
Indeed, until recently, it's been relatively simple to generalise about the two options. LG's WOLED panels used an additional white subpixel to boost peak brightness. Samsung's QD-OLED, meanwhile, had the edge for colour volume and vibrancy, thanks to its strategy of sticking with standard red, green and blue subpixels. Quantum dot tech also more efficiently converts the blue light generated by Samsung's emissive OLED layers into coloured light, compared to the simple filters LG uses to convert white light into coloured light.
Both shared essentially the same near-instantaneous 0.03ms pixel response. Combine that with high refresh rates and the result is razor-sharp motion clarity. However, there were and remain some further detailed differences. Samsung QD-OLED suffered from so-called purple tinting in bright ambient light due to the structure of the panel and the use of quantum dots, which can be activated by ambient light. Basically, stick one of the first three generations of QD-OLED panel next to a window on a bright day and it turns a bit purpley-grey, compromising black levels and overall contrast. On the other hand, the colours in LG's WOLEDs could look a little washed out, due to the use of that white subpixel to boost brightness.
Over those early panel generations, LG also had the edge when it came to image sharpness. Computer OSes like Windows function under the assumption that the subpixels in a digital display are arranged in a vertical RGB-stripe arrangement. LG's "RGWB" stripe, or red, green, white and blue subpixels arranged in vertical stripes, was much closer to that ideal RGB-stripe than Samsung's QD-OLED panels, which had an oddly triangular RGB subpixel structure. The upshot was that those triangular subpixel QD-OLED panels suffered from text fringing and a very slight softness in image quality, comparatively.
However, much of that has changed for the most recent generations of panels released in the last six to 12 months. The hot new thing, at least in terms of branding, is "tandem" OLED technology. Thus, LG has its new "Tandem WOLED" panels and Samsung has "Penta Tandem QD-OLED" tech.
In both cases, you might imagine the "tandem" bit implies fully dual-layer OLED panels, like those used by Apple in its iPad Pro tablets and the new PROLED MacBook Pro display. But actually, it refers to added emissive layers within the subpixels. LG's latest panels actually now have four layers, while Samsung's have five, hence the latter's pointed "Penta" branding flex. Either way, the added layers are claimed to increase brightness and improve durability.
Early monitor models based on either OLED panel type (like the aforementioned OG Alienware) tended to top out at around 250 nits of full-screen brightness, which I'd argue wasn't really good enough. The newer "tandem" generations have upped that to 300 and 335 nits for Samsung and LG respectively, which is closing in on the 400 nits I personally think is required to consider the full-screen brightness problem solved. Just note those figures are for pure white brightness, a metric that naturally favours LG's panels with their added white subpixel.
When it comes to colour brightness - and, in practice, most images you look at in-game are coloured, not pure white - Samsung's QD-OLED tech is often brighter. Don't, then, assume a higher outright brightness rating means brighter games. It's more complicated than that. The arrival of the Samsung Penta Tandem QD-OLED generation also brought with it an improvement, but not a total eradication, of the purple-tint issue in bright ambient light.
If you've followed all that. and honestly, props if you have, I'm afraid to say this is where things get really baffling. Both LG and Samsung have also released pure RGB-stripe subpixel variants of their newest "tandem" OLED panel tech. For LG, that means dropping the white subpixel for the sake of both better colour volume and more precise rendering, but slightly to the detriment of peak brightness. For Samsung, that means a switch from the triangular subpixel arrangement to vertical RGB-stripe and mostly just an improvement in pixel precision and text quality.
Not all of these panel types are available in all shapes, sizes and resolutions. But it gets confusing really fast. Right now, for instance, there are no fewer than six different 32-inch 4K OLED panel types to choose from. From LG you can have its original RGWB WOLED in, say, the Asus ROG Swift PG32UCDP, the new RGWB Tandem WOLED (for instance, the Asus ROG Strix XG32UQWMS) and then finally the RGB-stripe variation on Tandem WOLED ( see the Asus ROG Swift PG32UCWM). From Samsung, meanwhile, you can choose between its original triangular subpixel QD-OLED (like on the MSI MAG 321UP), the Penta Tandem follow up (of the MSI MPG 321UP X24) and then the all-singing RGB-stripe Penta Tandem (of the MSI MPG OLED 322URDX36, which is just about to be released).
Just to complicate things even further, LG's RGB-stripe "tandem" panels are still branded "WOLED", because the "W" in WOLED doesn't and has never referred to the white subpixels LG had hitherto used, but instead the white colour of the light produced by the emissive layers within each subpixel before conversion by the RGB filters. Got that? Phew.
The long and short of all this is that if you're comparing the very latest "tandem RGB-stripe" panels from LG and Samsung, they are closer now in terms of the subjective experience than ever before. Up until this generation, I always favoured QD-OLED for its superior colour vibrancy while accepting the purple tint issue. But as I peck out these words on the new 39-inch 5K2K Alienware AW3926QW and its Tandem WOLED RGB-stripe panel, I think I might just lean LG right now for its superior contrast in bright ambient light conditions, as well as this slightly more neutral subjective colour balance.
Anyway, as much as all that may feel like an exhaustive (and exhausting) dissertation, there is actually a fair bit I don't have space to fully cover. There are slight variations in the panel types, for instance, in terms of issues like uniformity and graininess, colour shift as opposed to outright burn-in, and also OLED flicker. But I wouldn't consider any of those even near to being deal breakers.
Really, it's just the long-term outlook that would have me thinking twice. First, that involves burn-in. I'd be pretty confident of getting at least three years out of an OLED. The big brands all have three year burn-in cover in any case (which is a very good reason to favour a big, familiar brand that you can be fairly certain will honour that warranty down the road). And it wouldn't surprise me if five years was very doable with the latest panels. The catch is that you can't, currently, be entirely sure about that.
Second, OLED technology is developing fast. It's come a long way in the last few years and I suspect that the next few will see comparable progress. Two or three years from now, I think full-screen brightness will likely be a totally solved problem, for instance, burn-in will be barely any concern at all, and prices will have come down even further. Ultimately, however, I wouldn't let that expectation stop me buying an OLED. It may not be entirely without flaws. But OLED is still by far the best PC display tech available today.

====================================================================================================

Congress shreds Nvidia's 'outrageous' return of Huang: 'Failure of character'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
20:13:18 UTC
05:13:18 KST

Members of Congress from both parties on Monday expressed outrage and incredulity, and doubled down on their calls for investigations, after Nvidia announced it has reinstated Jensen Huang as its president and CEO.
The resurrection of Huang, who had resigned from the artificial intelligence behemoth just 11 days earlier, raises more questions about the company's decision-making and the Trump administration's national security assessments, on top of existing concerns about Nvidia's advanced AI chips being diverted to China, the lawmakers said.
"It's inexplicable to me why they want this guy back as CEO," Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., a China hawk who is retiring from Congress, told CNBC in a phone interview. "To me it's a failure of character."
President Donald Trump told CNBC in an extended interview later Monday morning that he personally intervened to convince Nvidia's board to bring Huang back, saying, "I made the deal."
Bacon, chairman of the House Subcommittee on Cyber, Information Technologies, and Innovation, said he wants the next Congress to conduct thorough oversight, while questioning the Trump administration's motives.
"Somebody's responsible for this, and it may be" Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose department is responsible for setting U.S. export controls on AI chips, Bacon said. "But I think we should get to the bottom of this."
Huang, who is scheduled to testify before multiple committees on Capitol Hill starting Dec. 1, is "going to get high scrutiny in a bipartisan way when he comes next week," Bacon said.
Huang's closed-door appearance before a trio of House committees is on the schedule, as the panels seek answers on the national security implications of Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, MS NOW reported. The Senate banking committee announced after Huang's reinstatement that he will appear for a public hearing Dec. 3.
John Fisher of Trident, a spokesman for Huang, declined to comment on the Nvidia CEO's scheduled appearances.
"He better be there," Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., a member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told CNBC about Huang's scheduled Dec. 1 appearance. His reinstatement will only intensify congressional probes into Nvidia and its government connections, Amo predicted.
"I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query, and it makes Howard Lutnick and his dealings — supporting his nepo children's operation at Cantor Fitzgerald — party to inquiry as well as any other administration official that has facilitated a status quo in which chips can be sold to China or Chinese operated intermediaries who don't intend good things for the American people," said Amo, referring to the financial firm run by Lutnick's sons that has business ties to Nvidia.
A Cantor spokesperson didn't comment directly when asked about Amo's comments, saying the firm "holds no material proprietary position in Nvidia" and noting its Nvidia positions "arise primarily from equity swaps executed for clients."
The Commerce Department didn't immediately respond to requests to comment on Bacon's and Amo's remarks.
Nvidia in a statement Monday said it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident that Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."
David Rudenstine, a constitutional law professor and national security scholar at Cardozo School of Law, said the frenzy around Huang's comeback is secondary to lingering national security concerns.
"The real responsibility for national security is with the administration and not with the CEO of a private company," Rudenstine told CNBC.
"You're opposed to China getting the chips. You recognize that the intermediary companies are sending chips to China, and you don't put a stop to it. I don't get it," he said. 
Capitol Hill doubles down on criticism
The CEO's reinstatement, while celebrated by the White House and in Silicon Valley, led to heightened calls on Capitol Hill to seek answers on AI chip exports to China and what the Trump administration's role has been in facilitating that.
"We are sending high-end computer chips to China, and yet we're saying we want to win the AI war. It's a contradiction, and it's a failure of leadership in the White House," Bacon told CNBC.
Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, told CNBC in an emailed statement that Huang's return "proves that under Trump, Vance, and Rubio, national security is for sale to the highest bidder."
"We will all pay the price of letting China plunder America's cutting edge technology. Democrats will be pushing for accountability for anyone in the administration that put corrupt deals over our country's security," Wyden, who is also a Senate Intelligence Committee member, said.
Trump in a Truth Social post Monday added that Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."
Lawmakers, who have already launched multiple investigations related to Huang, Nvidia and related entities, quickly filed in to question those conclusions.
Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., a close Trump ally and a former CEO, criticized the move and called for a congressional briefing "as soon as possible" on Huang's reinstatement.
"Nvidia's board is free to vote however they want, but the federal government owes the American people answers about what actually happened," Scott told CNBC in an emailed statement. "This looks terrible on its face, and it drives home the need for Congress to be informed on the full impact of Jensen Huang's actions on our national security and economy."
Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., a member of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, told CNBC that it's still not clear to what degree Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy might have compromised national security.
"The fact that the president of the United States is involved in putting him back in there, presumably because maybe he has stock or he just cares about the stock market, it's really reached a bizarre place," Peters said. "And obviously that man has not learned anything from the election about what people think of him, or he doesn't care."
Monday's move also gave fuel to lawmakers who have already questioned Trump, his administration or Nvidia.
"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement via the Senate Banking Committee, where she is the top Democrat and is expected to become chair in January.
Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, more tersely questioned the official assurances that Huang poses no risks to national security.
"No national security risk? Based on what?" he wrote on X.
Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi, D-Ill., who serves on the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, in an X post called Nvidia's decision to reinstate Huang "outrageous."
"We still don't know what information Chinese intelligence may have obtained from him, and neither the government nor Nvidia's own board has completed its investigation," wrote Krishnamoorthi, who is also a member of the House Oversight and Intelligence panels.
— CNBC's Dan Mangan, Garrett Downs and Luke Fountain contributed to this report.

CrowdStrike to Buy Zscaler In $44 Billion Deal 25
			
		 	
				CrowdStrike has agreed to acquire network security provider Zscaler in a $43.9 billion all-stock deal. "The deal marks CrowdStrike's biggest acquisition to date and is among the largest in the sector as businesses consolidate and ramp up defenses to fend off AI-powered attacks," reports CNBC. The acquisition is expected to close in the second half of CrowdStrike's fiscal 2028. From the report:  "With Zscaler, we're building cybersecurity's operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely," CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said in a statement. "We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen."
CrowdStrike has been a major beneficiary of skyrocketing demand for AI security, with shares soaring about 80% this year as of Monday's open. Zscaler shares are up about 5% year to date after Monday's surge. Their combined market value exceeded $265 billion as of Friday's close, behind market leader Palo Alto Networks at $282 billion.
[...] Analysts view the Zscaler deal as a major AI security opportunity, allowing CrowdStrike to capture more customer security spending and close a significant gap with Palo Alto. "The addition of ZS to CRWD's platform could bring more breadth in an era of vendor consolidation and, more importantly, network security to position the company more effectively for rising demand for AI Sovereignty," wrote analysts at JPMorgan.

Jensen Huang says he’ll be a ‘better CEO’ as staff applauds his return
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:58:27 UTC
04:58:27 KST

Jensen Huang returned to Nvidia’s office as CEO today to a crowd of applauding employees who stood and filmed the cofounder’s first moments back.
Jensen Huang says he’ll be a ‘better CEO’ as staff applauds his return
Nvidia’s reinstated CEO thanked employees for the ‘second chances.’
“Thank you all for the second chances. I’m gonna be a better CEO the second time around,” Huang said, as shown in a video of his return.
Huang resigned as CEO on November 12th following reports of affairs, including with an alleged Chinese spy as well as a direct report at Nvidia. Huang admitted two days before that he was “unfaithful to my wife.”
President Trump, who had advocated for Huang to be reinstated ahead of it actually happening, was “totally involved” in his return, the White House said. In its press release about Huang’s reinstatement, the board of directors said it “received input from senior levels of the United States government” that made it “confident” that Huang’s reinstatement “poses no national security risk.” Trump asked Vice President JD Vance to help with the reinstatement over the weekend, including assisting the board to get “the assurances it needed,” The New York Times reported.
“This is my first day at work,” Huang told staff today, according to a video reported on by Business Insider. “I’m terribly sorry for putting all of you through all of this.”

How Jensen Huang ended up as Nvidia CEO again
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:57:18 UTC
04:57:18 KST

The Jensen Huang era of Nvidia nearly came to an end this month. Huang, who three decades ago cofounded what’s come to be considered one of the most important companies in AI, resigned from his role on November 12th over mounting scrutiny around his alleged affairs. Huang admitted he had been “unfaithful” to his wife and that he “created problems for the board.”
How Jensen Huang ended up as Nvidia CEO again
In the span of less than a month, Nvidia lost and reinstated its CEO.
How Jensen Huang ended up as Nvidia CEO again
In the span of less than a month, Nvidia lost and reinstated its CEO.
Huang’s resignation came after weeks of escalating problems, both personal and professional. First, reporting from The New York Times said he had a “close relationship” with a woman passing intelligence to China. Subsequent reporting said that China’s intelligence agency had enlisted that woman — Alice Huang, then an executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed — to spy on Nvidia’s leader, and that the Singapore-based company was under investigation for potentially smuggling AI chips to China. Stories followed that indicated Huang was having an affair with Alice as well as an Nvidia executive, vice president of healthcare Kimberly Powell.
So when Huang resigned less than two weeks ago, the decision seemed final. Even Nvidia’s board appeared unwilling to budge. “The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO,” board chair Harvey Jones said during an earnings call last week.
But in the days following Huang’s resignation, the founder’s allies — including President Donald Trump — mounted a pressure campaign that brought him back to the company he created and sent Nvidia’s share price rocketing upward, almost as though he had never left.
The campaign in support of Huang began almost immediately after his resignation. A coterie of tech leaders began posting in support of Nvidia’s founder: Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney questioned the motives of journalists who broke the stories about his affairs. Tesla CEO Elon Musk likened Huang’s departure to “kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak.” OpenAI CEO Sam Altman later said on stage, “It’s hard to imagine the AI revolution without Jensen.”
“If I didn’t get involved, you’d probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come.”
— Donald Trump
Meanwhile, some of those tech leaders began questioning aspects of the reporting that led to Huang’s exit. Venture capitalist and former White House AI czar David Sacks cast doubt that Alice Huang was a spy, citing a lack of confirmation from US officials. Trump exacerbated the uncertainty, telling reporters, “I don’t know that she is a spy,” despite reports saying the assessment had relied on US intelligence sources.
Nvidia’s board, meanwhile, found itself in a tough position. The company’s shares crashed following Huang’s departure, and they didn’t pick up even after a rosy earnings report. The board was also scrambling to find a new CEO, something industry analysts speculated would prove exceptionally difficult. Reports indicated that Nvidia was considering hiring Arm CEO Rene Haas and Apple hardware chief Johny Srouji, both of whom already have powerful roles in the chip industry.
Trump quickly warmed up to Huang’s return, too. The president — whose investment accounts traded up to $97 million worth of Nvidia stock since returning to office — initially floated the idea of having former Apple CEO Tim Cook take Huang’s place, before saying that it “certainly isn’t a bad idea” to bring Huang back.
This past weekend, Trump appears to have inserted himself into the situation directly. A report from The New York Times says that Trump asked Vice President JD Vance to work with Huang and Nvidia’s board to “resolve the situation,” while the Trump administration reassured the board that it “did not believe that Mr. Huang himself posed a national security risk.” Semafor reports that Trump even called in to Nvidia’s board meeting to advocate for Huang’s reinstatement. Sunday evening, Trump said in a post on Truth Social that he hopes Nvidia’s board “rehires Jensen Huang.”
Hours later, Nvidia’s board did just that. The board announced on Monday that it reinstated Huang as its CEO, president, and board member. In its statement, the board said that it received input “from senior levels of the United States government,” and determined that “Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.”
Trump immediately took credit for Huang’s reinstatement, with White House spokesperson Steven Cheung saying the president “was totally involved” in the decision. “If I didn’t get involved, you’d probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come,” Trump said during an interview with CNBC. He added in a post on Truth Social that the US attorney general confirmed that it doesn’t have evidence that Huang “engaged in any unlawful conduct.”
Nvidia’s shares rose on the news of Huang’s reinstatement, while tech leaders celebrated his return on social media. But the saga is likely far from over. The board said it will finish its investigation into Huang, the Securities and Exchange Commission is looking into Nvidia’s “accounting practices,” and Megaspeed is being investigated by the Justice Department. Even Trump has said that Nvidia should continue its internal investigation about export controls compliance.
Trump’s apparent involvement also leaves plenty of room for scrutiny from Democratic lawmakers, who will control the House and Senate next year. Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) called Nvidia’s decision “reckless” and urged the Trump administration to explain the “input” government officials provided to the company’s board.
Huang’s stunning reinstatement still leaves many questions unanswered. Even though the backing of Wall Street, the White House, and Silicon Valley may have brought Huang back as CEO, a new wave of scrutiny from federal regulators and lawmakers is just getting started.

‘Outrageous,’ ‘reckless,’ ‘wow’: Here’s what politicians are saying about Jensen Huang’s return to Nvidia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23

04:31:41 KST

Jensen Huang is back. The Nvidia CEO and founder, who was ousted from his company a little over a week ago after revelations of extramarital affairs (including, critically, a dalliance with a suspected Chinese spy), has apparently been deemed too big to fail.
Huang, who co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and subsequently grew the chipmaker into one of the most powerful companies in the world, has now been reinstated by the company’s board. The move was one that President Trump lobbied for. On Monday, after Huang’s return was announced, the president posted an AI-generated meme of the CEO holding a woman’s bra, an image that recalled the famous photo from Trump’s own assassination attempt.
Just how and why all of this has happened is a question that everybody is still scrambling to figure out. Because Nvidia is an economically important company, it is also a politically important one. Yet there’s a lot of disagreement over just how important Huang, himself, is — and whether his importance outweighs the alleged dangers of Chinese espionage to which his affair with Alice Huang may have exposed the company.
Even if Huang is back for now, the Nvidia founder may face an uphill battle given the changing political landscape wrought by the “blue wave” of Democratic victories that swept the U.S. midterms. Huang, now aligned with Trump, will face increased scrutiny from congressional Democrats — starting with a public Senate appearance scheduled for next week. He is also expected to testify privately before several House committees next month, including the Intelligence Committee, the Foreign Affairs Committee, and the China Select Committee.
If initial responses from current political leaders are any indication, Huang has his work cut out for him. As news of his return broke on Monday, politicians and public figures from across the political spectrum weighed in — and many didn’t seem too pleased.

HBO Max is offering one of the best Black Friday deals we’ve seen this year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:19:08 UTC
04:19:08 KST

If you’ve been meaning to catch up on Lanterns or the latest season of The Gilded Age, Thanksgiving weekend is the perfect time to do so. And thanks to HBO Max’s new Black Friday deal, new and returning subscribers can now watch both shows for less. The streaming service is currently offering a 50 percent discount for Black Friday, bringing the price of its Basic With Ads plan down to $5.49 per month (about $6 off) for one year. The promotion runs through November 30th, and you can subscribe either via HBO Max or Amazon.
HBO Max is offering one of the best Black Friday deals we’ve seen this year
Whether you’re into Lanterns or Sinners, HBO’s holiday discount makes it cheaper to catch up on your favorite content.
HBO Max has plenty of great movies and shows to keep you entertained well into the new year, no matter which plan you choose. You’ll find a wide selection of TV shows, including Heated Rivalry, Dune: Prophecy, The White Lotus, Euphoria, and Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, alongside longtime favorites like Game of Thrones and The Big Bang Theory. There’s more on the way, too, including HBO’s highly anticipated Harry Potter series, which premieres on Christmas.
There’s plenty to watch beyond TV, too. HBO Max’s extensive movie catalog currently includes films like Sinners, One Battle After Another, Marty Supreme, Backrooms, and Wuthering Heights. And if you’re a sports fan, both the Standard and Premium plans also include access to select live events from MLB, U.S. Soccer, the NCAA, and more.
What you get ultimately depends on the subscription tier you choose. HBO Max’s Basic with Ads plan lets you stream on two devices at once but doesn’t include live sports. The ad-free Standard plan ($18.49 a month), meanwhile, adds live sports and up to 30 offline downloads. The Premium plan ($22.99 a month) adds a few more perks, including 4K streaming and Dolby Atmos with select titles, up to 100 downloads, and the ability to stream movies and shows on four devices simultaneously. That said, HBO currently isn’t offering discounts on either the Standard or Premium plans… at least not yet.

Per Arm's request, this site will be discontinued on 4 December 2026 :(
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:17:55 UTC
04:17:55 KST

PER ARM'S REQUEST, THIS SITE WILL BE DISCONTINUED ON 4 DECEMBER 2026. :(
Arm's lawyers have notified me to take down this site or face legal action. 
To all of the fans of this site over the years (even the ones at Arm): thank you for your support! I'm glad I could provide a resource so many people found value in. I will look into solutions that can potentially keep bookmarks and links working. 
— Jon
    
This site offers reference documentation for the AArch64 instruction set and system registers defined by the Armv8-A and Armv9-A architectures.
You can use the search field below (and at the top of every page) to search for an instruction or system register. Alternatively, you can browse the full listings manually:
      If you find this site useful or have any suggestions, don't hesitate to
      let me know.
      
If you see this note instead of a search field, your browser either does not support WebAssembly or it has been disabled, e.g. in Apple's Lockdown Mode.

New magical shows worth watching instead of Harry Potter
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:17:11 UTC
04:17:11 KST

Since we wrote earlier this year about J.K. Rowling using her money to support groups and policies harming transgender people, she has accused the United Nations of promoting “sex denialism” and described trans rights activists as “elitists” whose work creates “a climate of fear and intimidation.” Also, her upcoming HBO Harry Potter series has not looked particularly good in any of the clips that have been released so far.
New magical shows worth watching instead of Harry Potter
Now’s a great time to get into some of this year’s best streaming fantasy series.
This take on Rowling’s novels appears to be a visually uninspired rehash of Warner Bros.’ film adaptations that’s designed for people who can’t free themselves from the shackles of nostalgia. But this year, there have been plenty of other new and returning shows that are filled with the kind of magic and mystery that gets fantasies going. And with the holiday season coming up, it’s the perfect time to give some of these less-problematic series a watch.
Percy Jackson and the Olympians
The second season of Disney’s adaptation of Rick Riordan’s fantasy series picks up at a time when all Percy Jackson (Walker Scobell) wants it to get back to Camp Half-Blood and reunite with his best friends Annabeth (Leah Sava Jeffries) and Grover (Aryan Simhadri). But when Percy arrives at the camp, Annabeth acts like she wants nothing to do with him, and Grover has gone missing under mysterious circumstances. The peril Grover seems to be in prompts Percy to embark on an adventure to the Sea of Monsters to save him. It’s the kind of quest that turns demigods into legendary heroes, but it’s one that pushes Percy to his limits. Compared to last season, this chapter of Percy Jackson and the Olympians appears to be more epic in scale as it fleshes out its world of Greek gods and monsters. And now that the new season is airing, it’s a must-watch for fans of Riordan’s novels.
Coven Academy
When Briar Roberts (Malina Weissman) first arrives at Crescent Veil Academy in New Orleans, she assumes that the place is just a ramshackle school for delinquent teenagers. Briar thinks she’s been shipped off to Crescent Veil because of her anger issues and run-ins with the police. But headmistress Emma Graves (Tiffani Thiessen) and the other students quickly make it clear that they — like Briar — are powerful witches whose magic makes them targets for evil monsters. Coven Academy’s a pretty classic coming-of-age story that feels like a cross between American Horror Story: Coven and Degrassi: The Next Generation. It’s cheesy, but it’s an easy watch if you’re looking for something lighthearted on Disney Plus.
Witch Hat Atelier
Adapted from Kamome Shirahama’s manga series, Witch Hat Atelier tells the story of Coco (Anjali Kunapaneni) — a young girl who dreams of becoming a witch even though she doesn’t seem to have any innate mystical abilities. In the land of the Zozah Peninsula, only a select group of people are allowed by the Great Hall of Witches to practice any sort of magic. But when Coco accidentally casts a powerful spell by reading from a book, the witch Qifrey (Joshua A. Waters) takes her in as his latest apprentice. Witch Hat Atelier is one of the most gorgeous fantasy anime to hit Crunchyroll in 2026. Even if witchcraft isn’t really your thing, it’s a tremendous show that’s worth a watch.
VisionQuest
As a synthezoid powered by genuine artificial intelligence, Vision (Paul Bettany) prefers to think of himself as a man of logic and reason. The former Avenger understands he is a machine built by a team of engineers, but on some level, he also knows that the Scarlet Witch’s magic played a role in his becoming the newest version of himself. Even with that knowledge, Vision has a difficult time processing his feelings when he crosses paths with Thomas Shepherd (Ruaridh Mollica), a Scottish orphan who seems to have a spiritual connection to the robotic hero. And to make things worse, Vision has to deal with his homicidal father, Ultron (James Spader), trying to make him relive traumatic memories. Following Agatha All Along, VisionQuest cements the WandaVision trilogy as one of the most riveting stories Marvel has produced to date. The series is brilliant on its own, and it portends some very promising things for the MCU’s future.
Bass X Machina
Set in an alternate, steampunk past where people are trying to settle the Wild West with the help of autonomous machines, Netflix’s Bass X Machina follows bounty hunter Bass Reeves (Brian Tyree Henry) as he finishes his last gig before retiring. Reeves is trying to leave the gunslinging life behind him in order to spend time with his family, but with money being tight, he can’t afford to give his robotic shooting arm a rest just yet. If Reeves had his way, he would just bag a couple of ordinary outlaws, collect their bounties, and be on his way. But fate has other plans, and Reeves winds up journeying into Choctaw Country on a mission to investigate a local man’s mysterious disappearance. Though Bass X Machina initially seems to be a straightforward steampunk anime, it takes a surprisingly magical turn that infuses its story with intrigue. And the show’s visuals are stunning — which isn’t a surprise given that it’s produced by Studio Mir.
Carrie
Mike Flanagan’s adaptation of Stephen King’s seminal novel for Amazon isn’t exactly fantasy, but it does present Carrie White (Summer H. Howell) as part of a group of women who have been historically persecuted for having talents that seem otherworldly. When Carrie comes across a book about women accused of being witches, she can’t help but see herself in their stories and she wonders if she, too, might be burned at the stake if people learn her secret. Though this spin on Carrie stays fairly faithful to the novel, Flanagan modernizes the story in order to tackle the realities of how kids bully one another in the age of social media. And while the show definitely takes some creative liberties that might confound King’s fans, it features one of the most terrifying (in a good way) depictions of Carrie’s fateful prom.
Small Prophets
Though Michael Sleep’s (Pearce Quigley) nosy neighbors know that he’s up to something strange in his shed, they have no idea that he’s experimenting with a recipe to grow prophetic homunculi in jars. According to a book of alchemy owned by Sleep’s father, Brian (Michael Palin), properly raised homunculi have the supernatural ability to accurately answer any question they’re asked. Sleep isn’t entirely convinced that what he’s doing will work, but he toils away in hopes that the humanoid creatures will be able to help him locate his long-lost girlfriend. As much as Small Prophets is a comedy, it’s also a touching drama about grief and the challenges of building new friendships as people enter the latter stages of their lives. Like many British series, Apple TV’s Small Prophets is a pretty quick watch, and it has already been renewed for a second season.
The Vampire Lestat
In its third season, AMC’s Interview With the Vampire gets a rebrand that mirrors the way Lestat de Lioncourt (Sam Reid) reinvents himself as the front man of a rock band. Lestat’s music career gives him a way to continue thumbing his nose at vampires’ laws about secrecy and to take his mind off his former lover, Louis de Pointe du Lac (Jacob Anderson). Try as they might, it’s hard for Lestat and Louis to stay away from one another, even though it strains their romantic entanglements with others. And the closer the two get to reconciling their relationship, the more innocent people around them get gravely hurt. Though this series has always been phenomenal, The Vampire Lestat reaches new emotional heights that make you feel how much these characters have evolved over time. And this season sets the stage for yet another surprising pivot into one of Anne Rice’s best stories.
American Horror Story: 13
Rather than committing to a completely new story the way previous chapters have, American Horror Story: 13 brings back a number of characters from past seasons. Almost every single cast member reprises multiple roles, and the show spins a wild tale that occasionally feels like a return to the series’ glory days — particularly when it’s focused on Supreme witches Cordelia Goode (Sarah Paulson) and her mother, Fiona (Jessica Lange). Though all of the witches can sense that a powerful evil is rising, they don’t know that Constance Langdon (also Lange) plays a pivotal role in the strange happenings across the world. Constance herself doesn’t fully understand what her new boss Mr. Nas (Paul Anthony Kelly and Fedor Steer) is trying to accomplish, but she follows his orders because she has faith that their desires are aligned. American Horror Story: 13 is at its best when it leans hard into camp and lets its ensemble cut loose as they move through the season’s ridiculous twists. And while it’s nowhere near as good as American Horror Story: Coven, it’s just great to see Lange hamming it up on the small screen again.
The Witch Farm
Inspired by real-world events, the BBC’s four-part series The Witch Farm tells the story of an English family who move to a remote house in the Welsh wilderness for a fresh start. At first, Liz (Gabrielle Creevy), her husband Bill (Michael Socha), and their son Jonathan (Lewin Lloyd) love their rustic new home, but it isn’t long before they begin to suspect that a malevolent presence is lurking in the shadows. While Liz and Bill don’t believe in ghosts, they can’t ignore the way people in their small town speak about their house as a place of evil. And as the couple learns more about the house’s history, they start to realize that they might have really wandered into a nightmare. The Witch Farm is only available to stream on BBC iPlayer, and it looks like a solid spooky treat for folks who love haunting horror.

====================================================================================================

Google Search’s AI Mode can now tap into Drive and Wallet for more personalized responses
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:14:46 UTC
04:14:46 KST

Google announced on Monday that it’s expanding Personal Intelligence in Search’s AI Mode with integrations for Google Drive, Google Wallet, and the Gemini app. Personal Intelligence is designed to offer users more personalized, context-aware answers by connecting to their Google apps and drawing on information from their digital lives. The feature can already connect to apps like Gmail, Google Calendar, and Google Photos.
By bringing Personal Intelligence in AI Mode to Drive, Wallet, and the Gemini app, Google is leveraging the wealth of user data that’s already within its ecosystem. Since users rely on these services, the tech giant can deliver more personalized experiences that rivals aren’t able to match. Of course, not everyone wants AI looking at their Wallet and Drive, so connecting the apps is optional, and you can turn Personal Intelligence on or off at any time.
Google says that connecting Google Wallet to Personal Intelligence lets users track their financial goals, cut everyday spending, and make better use of credit card rewards. The tech giant notes that if users choose to connect their Wallet, AI Mode can only read information from accounts and isn’t able to move or manage their money.
As for Drive, the company says important information related to a project or major decision is often scattered across documents and spreadsheets. By connecting Google Drive to Personal Intelligence, AI can draw on those files to provide more relevant, personalized answers, such as creating a tailored plan for tax season.
Users will also be able to share context between their Gemini app and AI Mode in Search, which means that if they explore an idea in the Gemini app, they won’t have to start over with AI Mode because it will already have the information, and vice versa.
Additionally, Google says users will soon be able to view the context that AI Mode uses via a new “Personalization” icon that will appear below responses.
The company also announced on Monday that users will start to see more personally relevant responses in AI Overviews and AI Mode that will be tailored to users’ recent search context.

Nothing’s upcoming Ear Note is a pair of earbuds with AI transcription
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
19:04:52 UTC
04:04:52 KST

Nothing’s new AI device is the Ear Note, a set of earbuds and their microphone-equipped charging case that can record and transcribe conversations, with a connected app to manage the recordings, transcriptions and AI-generated summaries, similar to Apple’s new Watch feature and devices like Amazon’s Bee wearable. Nothing CEO Carl Pei demonstrated the new device to Bloomberg, but said the company hasn’t yet settled on a price ahead of a planned launch on December 8th.
Nothing’s upcoming Ear Note is a pair of earbuds with AI transcription
The new AI device from Nothing can record and transcribe audio with AI from the earbuds or via microphones on their charging case.
The report also says Nothing’s rumored plans for an AI smartwatch and smart glasses are delayed, while it will be “pursuing a more aggressive smartphone release schedule next year than it did in 2026.” In a post on X, Pei said this is “the first of many products building a hardware context layer for AI. Rather than ask you to carry a new product, we took something you already wear and trust and made it intelligent.”
According to Bloomberg, the earbuds will “support interactions with various AI agents, including a more basic voice-control system developed by Nothing, letting users engage in back-and-forth conversations.” Users will also be able to record themselves with a “Talk” button on the case, like Nothing previously included on the Ear 3, which also has recording capabilities. Earlier this year, Nothing introduced an Audio Snapshot recording feature in the Ear 3A. Anker’s Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro Max can also record directly to the earbuds’ case and generate AI transcripts and summaries in an app.

Trump Is Said to Have Directed Effort to Reinstate Nvidia Chief (nytimes.com) 82
			
		 	
				President Trump reportedly directed a behind-the-scenes effort to reinstate Jensen Huang as Nvidia's CEO, enlisting Vice President JD Vance to secure assurances from federal officials that Huang was not facing criminal charges or considered a national security risk. According to The New York Times, the intervention came despite intelligence concerns surrounding Huang's alleged relationship with a suspected Chinese spy linked to an investigation into illegal Nvidia chip shipments to China. From the report:  [...] Only a tiny handful of officials at the highest level of the government were aware of these conversations. On Sunday night, Nvidia's board told the administration that it would be announcing Mr. Huang's reinstatement on Monday morning before the markets opened. The announcement included a note about a new compliance committee, intended to offer public assurance that the company was taking seriously the investigation into the possible smuggling of advanced chips. Mr. Trump was said to have been thrilled that the plan to restore Mr. Huang was executed without any leaks.
In directing Mr. Vance to engage with Mr. Huang and his peers, Mr. Trump was turning to one of the members of his administration with the strongest relationships in Silicon Valley. The vice president is close to some of Mr. Huang's biggest supporters and admirers, such as David Sacks, the investor and co-chairman of the President's Council of Advisers on Science and Technology. Mr. Sacks previously served as Mr. Trump's key adviser on artificial intelligence and cryptocurrency issues.
On Monday morning, Mr. Trump rejoiced in Nvidia's action, saying on his social media website Truth Social that the company "made the correct and proper decision." The president explicitly connected the reinstatement of Mr. Huang to the health of the U.S. economy. "The Country needs Jensen's leadership at NVIDIA in order to ensure the Success and Prosperity of not only the Company, but the American Economy," Mr. Trump wrote.

GTA VI’s pretentious puzzler makes indie developers feel ‘part of larger culture’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
18:55:06 UTC
03:55:06 KST

One of the most intriguing games of the year is called Tenebrae Lux. It’s a moody, black-and-white title with obtuse puzzles and a main character that constantly narrates to himself with dramatic language. Its credits attribute pretty much everything to a developer named Johannes Zuch. It’s generated a lot of discussion for seemingly mocking games like Jonathan Blow’s The Witness or The Chinese Room’s Dear Esther. It’s also a game that only exists inside of Grand Theft Auto VI.
GTA VI’s pretentious puzzler makes indie developers feel ‘part of larger culture’
Tenebrae Lux is a gag that has inspired some larger conversations about the gap between indie games and big-budget blockbusters.
Developer Rockstar Games has stuffed a lot of satirical media inside of GTA VI, including TV shows you can watch, books you can read, and radio shows you can listen to. It’s all in service of making the world feel like a funhouse-mirror version of our current reality — Ted Lasso star Jason Sudeikis plays a right-wing radio host, for example. There are more games you can play inside GTA VI in addition to Tenebrae Lux, including a zombie-themed roguelike. But Tenebrae Lux, which you have to see as part of GTA VI’s main story, is the one that has struck a chord, including with indie developers like Sam Barlow and Robert Yang.
“I have to admit it’s kind of funny for the biggest blockbuster game of all time to go after arthouse indie,” Yang tells The Verge, noting that it’s “like a fighter jet getting obsessed with an ant, petty in a funny way.” (Given that GTA VI earned $3.79 billion in its first day, the gulf between Rockstar and indie game studios that are struggling to get by is vast.)
But Yang suggested that Rockstar developers are probably well versed in the types of games they’re lampooning in GTA VI. “I don’t think gamers understand how much indie experimental stuff any decent AAA dev plays,” Yang said, pointing out that Rockstar’s environmental artists and level designers “all probably love Dear Esther,” the influential first-person game that helped popularize the “walking sim” concept when it launched in 2012.
“I was mostly taken with what it meant that the millions of people playing the game were having to sit down and play through an extended gag about indie games,” Barlow tells The Verge. “That made me feel like we were part of larger culture.” He added that he hopes some of these players might “seek out what these arty indie games might be.”
Rockstar is self-aware enough to use its video games to poke fun at the medium itself. As part of a long conversation between protagonist Jason Duval and his friend Cal Hampton before they start playing Tenebrae Lux, Cal hypes the game by saying that none of it is “just sitting around waiting for people to finish talking.” Initially, you only play Tenebrae Lux for a few minutes, which is enough time to get stumped on the initial puzzles, before the game cuts back to Jason being bored. “Got anything else, a little less, uh… y’know?” he asks. They play the zombie game, titled Street Trash 3 Dezombifier, right after. (You can go back and play the entirety of Tenebrae Lux later if you want.)
Barlow said that the cut “felt very true to the character and story. I gather there is more to the game, but it felt natural to me to hit a wall with the pretentious puzzle design and then move on!”
Yang also pointed out that from a design perspective, GTA VI has more in common with Tenebrae Lux than Street Trash 3 Dezombifier. GTA VI is “basically a walking sim where you walk around Florida and listen to people talk,” Yang said. “What’s the difference? Well, just like a billion dollars I guess.”
