Iranian authorities flog singer who performed without hijab, lawyers say
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
18:48:38 UTC
03:48:38 KST

Iranian authorities flog singer who performed without hijab, lawyers say
A prominent singer who performed without the mandatory hijab and four members of her band and production team have been flogged by Iranian authorities, lawyers say.
Parastoo Ahmadi, three musicians and a producer were each given 74 lashes in the city of Qom, a member of their defence team told the BBC.
Ahmadi, 29, wore a sleeveless dress during a concert that was livestreamed in December 2024. Iran's laws ban public performances by women and require them to wear headscarves and modest clothing.
This June, a criminal court in Qom convicted Ahmadi and eight others of "offending public decency" through the publication of "obscene and indecent material", which they had denied.
In addition to being sentenced to 74 lashes, they were banned from audiovisual activities and from leaving the country for two years.
At the end of September, an appeals court in Qom rejected the defendants' appeal and confirmed the sentence, her lawyers said.
There was widespread condemnation of the sentences from other Iranian artists and human rights activists, who warned that flogging constituted cruel, inhuman and degrading punishment, and violated the international prohibition on torture.
On Monday, one of the defendants' lawyers told the BBC that Ahmadi and four of her male co-defendants had been flogged at the third branch of the Criminal Sentence Enforcement Office in Qom.
"We did everything in our power to have the verdict overturned, or at least to secure a reduction in the sentence or a suspension, but it didn't work out," he said.
"The other lawyers and I even tried to get the enforcement of the sentence postponed, but the enforcement branch insisted on carrying it out immediately."
Pro-reform daily Etemad cited another defence lawyer, Mostafa Nili, as identifying the four men flogged alongside Ahmadi as Soheil Faghih-Nasiri, Amin Taheri, Amirali Pirnia and Mohsen Lashkari.
"Parastoo Ahmadi's flogging for singing and dressing as she chooses is a flagrant violation of her fundamental rights and a chilling demonstration of the Iranian authorities' determination to suppress women's freedoms and to violently punish anyone who dares to challenge their discriminatory laws and policies," said Bahar Ghandehari, director of advocacy at the US-based Center for Human Rights in Iran (CHRI).
Under Iranian law, which is based on the country's interpretation of Sharia, women and girls above the age of puberty must cover their hair with a hijab and wear long, loose-fitting clothing to disguise their figures.
In 2022, protests swept across the Islamic Republic following the death in custody of Mahsa Amini, a 22-year-old Kurdish woman who was detained by morality police in Tehran in September that year for allegedly wearing her hijab "improperly".
Women burnt their headscarves or waved them in the air at the nationwide demonstrations against the clerical establishment, during which hundreds of people were killed in a crackdown by security forces.
Since then, many women and girls have stopped covering their hair in public altogether in open defiance of the dress code. Authorities have responded with periodic waves of arrests, warnings, business closures and other measures.
In January this year, thousands of people were killed and tens of thousands were arrested when security forces crushed another wave of mass anti-establishment protests, this time sparked by the country's economic crisis.
Since the start of Iran's war with the US and Israel in late February, authorities have intensified their use of the death penalty against protesters.
According to the Norway-based group Iran Human Rights, at least 39 men convicted over January's protests and two convicted over the 2022 unrest have been executed, along with 16 people allegedly affiliated with banned opposition groups and 17 others convicted of espionage or collaborating with foreign states.
Another 121 protesters detained in January, including six women and five juvenile offenders, have been sentenced to death and are at imminent risk of execution, it says.

Apple removed my open-source app from EU App Stores after 9 years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
18:48:10 UTC
03:48:10 KST

You signed in with another tab or window. Reload to refresh your session. You signed out in another tab or window. Reload to refresh your session. You switched accounts on another tab or window. Reload to refresh your session. Dismiss alert
For those who don't know me, let me introduce myself. I'm the independent developer behind FSNotes, an open-source note-taking app for macOS and iOS.
I've been developing FSNotes for 9 years. And what happened a few weeks ago came as a complete shock.
Apple removed my app from every European App Store. Without a clear explanation or any way to resolve the issue promptly.
It all started when I didn't receive my October sales payout. This had happened before, but I was usually able to resolve it by contacting Apple Support. Not this time.
I opened two support cases:
- November 5 — Case ID: 23353628
- November 17 — Case ID: 23573022
Since November 5, I haven't received a single response. Not one.
I've gone through everything I could find in App Store Connect, completed the necessary compliance information under the EU Digital Services Act (DSA), and submitted official documents from Ukraine's government business registry.
I'm a legally registered sole proprietor in Ukraine. I run my business officially and pay my taxes. But apparently, that's not enough. Or perhaps nobody is even reviewing my documents.
And you know what's the most frustrating part?
While Apple remains silent, everything I've spent nine years building is being destroyed.
My app is losing its positions in App Store rankings and search results. The visibility, reputation, and discoverability I've worked so hard to build over the years are disappearing right before my eyes.
Even if Apple restores my app tomorrow, who's going to give me those rankings back? Who will compensate me for the lost time, users, and revenue?
I'm not asking for special treatment. I'm not asking Apple to bend the rules for me. I simply want someone to review my documents, explain the restrictions, and give me a chance to resolve the issue.
It's especially painful to see how difficult it has become for a Ukrainian developer to keep working. Sometimes it feels like Ukraine is the country under sanctions, not Russia.
FSNotes is still available for free on GitHub. I'll continue providing access to the source code and free builds. But the reality is that without revenue, it will be extremely difficult for an independent project like this to survive.
I'm asking you to support this open letter to Apple with a like or a repost. I'm not asking for donations or special privileges. I just want to bring this situation to the attention of the App Store team.
You cannot simply remove an app with a nine-year history from sale, ignore a developer's support requests for weeks, and provide no explanation of what's happening.
This kind of treatment is killing indie development.

Oregon and Virginia sue SpaceX over Grok’s porn deepfakes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
18:26:47 UTC
03:26:47 KST

In separate but similar lawsuits filed on Monday, Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield and Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones accused SpaceX of violating state consumer protection laws by ignoring industry-standard safeguards and falsely promoting the safety of its X social media platform and Grok AI features. The complaint filed in Virginia accuses Musk of willfully ignoring growing concerns around AI-generated sexualized deepfakes, and “instead saw a market opportunity for more profits,” describing the weak safeguards around Grok as “not a failure but a feature.”
Oregon and Virginia sue SpaceX over Grok’s porn deepfakes
State AGs called the weak safeguards built into Elon Musk’s AI image generator ‘not a failure but a feature.’
“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false. Instead, the tool permitted users to routinely create and circulate non-consensual intimate imagery and sexualized images, and child sexual abuse material (CSAM), causing harm to individuals and children,” Jones said in a press statement. “Companies that deploy powerful tools don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation.”
The complaints largely center around Grok Imagine, a tool that allows Grok and X users to generate images and videos. In August 2025, Grok maker xAI (which Musk has since merged with SpaceX) launched a “spicy” mode for Grok Imagine that spat out sexualized content including nude deepfakes of celebrities like Taylor Swift. In January 2026, Grok gained a feature that allowed X users to instantly edit any image without requiring consent from the original poster — resulting in X becoming flooded with nonconsensual, sexualized deepfakes of adults and minors.
xAI made several changes to restrict access to Grok image editing, moving some capabilities behind a paywall and blocking certain requests for sexual poses, swimwear, or explicit scenarios. Our testing at the Verge following these restriction updates found they did little to prevent the bot from creating sexualized deepfakes, however.
Both of the Oregon and Virginia lawsuits are seeking financial damages.
The X Safety team said in a statement released after the lawsuits were announced that it takes appropriate measures to address users who generate sexual content involving minors:
“The people who tried to use Grok Imagine to generate this material did so by deliberately violating our Terms of Service and working aggressively to circumvent our safeguards. When some of these bad actors found ways to misuse Grok Imagine earlier this year, we acted promptly to tighten its protections, which are designed to refuse any request to generate explicit images of minors or real people. To stay ahead of this disgusting and indefensible conduct, we keep testing and adjusting those protections as these abusers change tactics. We also implement sophisticated classifiers that screen every prompt before an image is generated and check every output after.”

YC Startup Working On 'Cost Per Kill' For Missiles 84
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Wired: At 16 years old, Henrijs Dandzbergs is too young to vote. Or drink. Or smoke. Or drive unsupervised. But he's not too young to win half a million dollars for his AI-guided-missile startup. In September, Dandzbergs, who was born in Latvia, was admitted to Y Combinator's fall 2026 cohort. The startup accelerator counts companies like Airbnb, Stripe, DoorDash, and Reddit among its alumni; Dandzbergs is likely the youngest solo founder in its vaunted history. His startup, Polemos Industries, is attempting to develop a new class of precision-guided missile that can be easily and cheaply manufactured, can be fired from portable launchers, and can resist jamming attempts. "I think missiles are the coolest thing," Dandzbergs tells WIRED. "A lot of people that have found success in engineering early on have had a passion for blowing stuff up."
In conversation, Dandzbergs is poised and articulate. He talks about "attacking hard problems," breaking things down to "first principles," and how the Ukraine conflict has forever altered the theater of war -- a personal fixation, given his country's proximity to Russia. He projects an unassailable confidence in his own abilities. "He's a pretty exceptional person," says Harj Taggar, a YC partner assigned to mentor Dandzbergs. "I'm still somewhat in disbelief that we're talking about a 16-year-old." Dandzbergs has resolved to apply these unusual talents to figuring out how to optimize targeted killing. His startup's guiding ambition, according to its investor one-pager, is to bring down the "cost per kill." Dandzbergs hopes to demonstrate a prototype missile by December. Despite concerns about the ethical implications of autonomous weapons and his young age, he remains undeterred. "I want to be part of it. I want to move with the future of warfare," he says.
"Please don't say I'm sacrificing my childhood," says Dandzbergs. "This is the childhood I dreamed of."

====================================================================================================

How should an LLM see a computer?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
17:19:25 UTC
02:19:25 KST

Introduction
Claude Code and Codex can both use your computer. Under the hood they work in a similar manner: both can give the model a screenshot and an accessibility tree, a text description of every button on screen, and let it click by position or by naming an element.
The text version of the screen was built decades ago, for people who couldn’t see it. In 1984, two IBM mathematicians, Jim Thatcher and his former thesis adviser, Jesse Wright, who was blind, started a side project to read the PC’s screen out loud. When GUIs turned the screen into pixels, apps had to start describing themselves instead. Microsoft shipped Active Accessibility in 1997, Sun started AT-SPI for Linux in 2001, and Apple added its Accessibility API in 2002.
This text has been useful for agents too. On the 2024 OSWorld benchmark, models looking only at screenshots completed about one task in twenty, whereas most models reading the screen as text performed better.
How do those results hold up with stronger models? We tested screenshots on their own and with the accessibility tree on seven models, and used the results to decide how our QA agent, TREX, should see the screen.
What's so hard about clicking a button?
A person clicks a button by moving the mouse to it. A model, on the other hand, looks at a screenshot and has to write the coordinates out as text (e.g., ‘x=523, y=217’) before clicking on it. Getting these numbers off by a few pixels can mean it clicks the wrong thing.
The same button sits at different coordinates on different screen and window sizes, so the guesses get worse when the resolution changes.
Also, the model’s vision works against it as it doesn’t see a screenshot pixel by pixel. The image is initially shrunk to fit the model's size limit, then cut into tiny squares, each dozens of pixels wide. But a click still has to land on one exact pixel.
Small targets are hard. On ScreenSpot-Pro, a 2025 benchmark with tiny targets on high resolution screens, the best model at release hit the right target only 18.9% of the time. Anthropic’s own docs warn that Claude “might make mistakes or hallucinate when outputting specific coordinates.”
When OSWorld came out in 2024, human testers were able to complete 72% of its tasks, and the best model about 12%. The authors found that models struggled with two things: turning ‘click Save’ into the right point on the screen, and knowing how to operate the software.
The first problem has a seemingly obvious fix. The accessibility tree already reveals each button's location, so the model doesn't have to go through the trouble of finding pixels. Go one step further, and the model doesn’t even need coordinates; it can pick a button by ID and let the harness click it. To test this, we built three setups, each building upon the last:
- Screenshots alone
- Adding an accessibility tree (clicking by coordinates)
- Adding clicks by element IDs
Every setup used the same agent on 172 OSWorld tasks, with one attempt per task, a 50-step limit and low reasoning effort, scored by OSWorld’s own graders.
Starting with screenshots
The model runs in a loop where it first sees a screenshot and past actions, picks an action (click coordinates, type or press keys), and then looks at the changes. It’s the simplest way to give a model a computer, and the one most agents start with. OSWorld’s baseline agent supports it, and Anthropic described its first computer use model as looking at screenshots and counting how many pixels to move the cursor.
We ran the same screenshot agent across seven OpenAI models:
On our 172 tasks, GPT-4.1 scored a mere 5.4% with screenshots alone, while the more recent GPT-6.1 Sol scored 83.8%. On the much harder OSWorld 2.0 offline set, OpenAI reports a score of 71.4% for GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. The gap between the two models raised questions: do stronger models even need the tree, and can it help weaker ones catch up?
Adding the accessibility tree
Here, the model sees the screenshot with an accessibility tree beside it. We were interested in the tree because it can just tell the model what’s there, like a button labeled “Save” and where it sits. The model still clicks by coordinates here. The tree lists each element's position and dimensions in pixels, so the model can work out where to click after doing the math. With the clicking kept constant, the only thing that changed was what the model saw.
OSWorld runs on Linux, and so our agents read AT-SPI. The app's toolkit, such as GTK and Qt, describes what's on screen and passes on those descriptions to any program that asks for them, without prompting the user for permissions. Also, while testing, we realized that GIMP was missing from the tree until we added back an environment variable set at login.
In the evals, trees helped every model that struggled with screenshots. GPT-5.2 went from 15.8% with screenshots alone to 36.2% after the tree was added, and GPT-4.1 from 5.4% to 20.6%. On the other hand, stronger models barely moved. GPT-6.1 Sol got 82.7% when the tree was added, slightly lower than with screenshots alone (83.8%) and none of the three strongest models changed by a statistically significant amount.
Clicking by ID
Next, we gave each element in the tree an ID. The model can simply name the element it wants and the harness clicks it. This bypasses the problem of clicking the exact pixel.
For weaker models, ID clicks boosted performance on top of the tree. GPT-5.2 went from 36.2% with the tree + coordinates to 52.8% when it clicked by ID, and GPT-4.1 from 20.6% to 29.3%. Put together, the tree and ID clicks more than tripled GPT-5.2’s score with screenshots alone (15.8%).
Again, this experiment didn't do much for the strongest models. GPT-6.1 Sol scored 82.7% clicking by coordinates and 82.1% clicking by ID.
It depends on the model
For the three strongest models, neither step made a difference. Across both steps, the four weaker models gained 9 to 37 points with the tree and ID clicks, more than doubling their scores in three of four cases. If you’re building on weaker models, the tree is still worth adding.
Some apps publish no tree at all. A canvas app like Figma publishes its toolbar buttons, but the canvas itself is just pixels, and a game like Doom is pixels from top to bottom. Even in apps with a good tree, some bugs only show on screen, like text that overflows its box. The screenshot covers all of these.
TREX Computer Use
Code review catches bugs in the diff. Some bugs only exist when the software is running: a button that can’t be clicked, a label that runs off the edge of the window. Until now, catching those took a person opening the app and simulating the user flow. By writing more code and touching more of the UI than any team can check by hand, coding agents have made it hard for programmers to keep up.
That’s why we built computer use into TREX. It runs the changes in your pull request on an actual desktop and uses the app the way a person would, so you can see that the feature you asked for is the one that got built.
How TREX sees the screen came out of these experiments. It runs on the best models and relies on the screenshot at every step. It uses the accessibility tree to click by ID when it can, and coordinates when the tree doesn't have the element, like a canvas or a game.
How a model should see the screen depends on the model, so we'll rerun these experiments each time a new one comes out and update TREX when the answer changes.
When something breaks on screen, TREX catches it, and shows you what happened.
Try TREX on your next pull request.

====================================================================================================

How should an LLM see a computer?

Microsoft shipped Active Accessibility in 1997, Sun started AT-SPI for Linux in 2001, and Apple added its Accessibility API in 2002.
This text has been useful for agents too. On the 2024 OSWorld benchmark, models looking only at screenshots completed about one task in twenty, whereas most models reading the screen as text performed better.
How do those results hold up with stronger models? We tested screenshots on their own and with the accessibility tree on seven models, and used the results to decide how our QA agent, TREX, should see the screen.
What's so hard about clicking a button?
A person clicks a button by moving the mouse to it. A model, on the other hand, looks at a screenshot and has to write the coordinates out as text (e.g., ‘x=523, y=217’) before clicking on it. Getting these numbers off by a few pixels can mean it clicks the wrong thing.
The same button sits at different coordinates on different screen and window sizes, so the guesses get worse when the resolution changes.
Also, the model’s vision works against it as it doesn’t see a screenshot pixel by pixel. The image is initially shrunk to fit the model's size limit, then cut into tiny squares, each dozens of pixels wide. But a click still has to land on one exact pixel.
Small targets are hard. On ScreenSpot-Pro, a 2025 benchmark with tiny targets on high resolution screens, the best model at release hit the right target only 18.9% of the time. Anthropic’s own docs warn that Claude “might make mistakes or hallucinate when outputting specific coordinates.”
When OSWorld came out in 2024, human testers were able to complete 72% of its tasks, and the best model about 12%. The authors found that models struggled with two things: turning ‘click Save’ into the right point on the screen, and knowing how to operate the software.
The first problem has a seemingly obvious fix. The accessibility tree already reveals each button's location, so the model doesn't have to go through the trouble of finding pixels. Go one step further, and the model doesn’t even need coordinates; it can pick a button by ID and let the harness click it. To test this, we built three setups, each building upon the last:
- Screenshots alone
- Adding an accessibility tree (clicking by coordinates)
- Adding clicks by element IDs
Every setup used the same agent on 172 OSWorld tasks, with one attempt per task, a 50-step limit and low reasoning effort, scored by OSWorld’s own graders.
Starting with screenshots
The model runs in a loop where it first sees a screenshot and past actions, picks an action (click coordinates, type or press keys), and then looks at the changes. It’s the simplest way to give a model a computer, and the one most agents start with. OSWorld’s baseline agent supports it, and Anthropic described its first computer use model as looking at screenshots and counting how many pixels to move the cursor.
We ran the same screenshot agent across seven OpenAI models:
On our 172 tasks, GPT-4.1 scored a mere 5.4% with screenshots alone, while the more recent GPT-6.1 Sol scored 83.8%. On the much harder OSWorld 2.0 offline set, OpenAI reports a score of 71.4% for GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. The gap between the two models raised questions: do stronger models even need the tree, and can it help weaker ones catch up?
Adding the accessibility tree
Here, the model sees the screenshot with an accessibility tree beside it. We were interested in the tree because it can just tell the model what’s there, like a button labeled “Save” and where it sits. The model still clicks by coordinates here. The tree lists each element's position and dimensions in pixels, so the model can work out where to click after doing the math. With the clicking kept constant, the only thing that changed was what the model saw.
OSWorld runs on Linux, and so our agents read AT-SPI. The app's toolkit, such as GTK and Qt, describes what's on screen and passes on those descriptions to any program that asks for them, without prompting the user for permissions. Also, while testing, we realized that GIMP was missing from the tree until we added back an environment variable set at login.
In the evals, trees helped every model that struggled with screenshots. GPT-5.2 went from 15.8% with screenshots alone to 36.2% after the tree was added, and GPT-4.1 from 5.4% to 20.6%. On the other hand, stronger models barely moved. GPT-6.1 Sol got 82.7% when the tree was added, slightly lower than with screenshots alone (83.8%) and none of the three strongest models changed by a statistically significant amount.
Clicking by ID
Next, we gave each element in the tree an ID. The model can simply name the element it wants and the harness clicks it. This bypasses the problem of clicking the exact pixel.
For weaker models, ID clicks boosted performance on top of the tree. GPT-5.2 went from 36.2% with the tree + coordinates to 52.8% when it clicked by ID, and GPT-4.1 from 20.6% to 29.3%. Put together, the tree and ID clicks more than tripled GPT-5.2’s score with screenshots alone (15.8%).
Again, this experiment didn't do much for the strongest models. GPT-6.1 Sol scored 82.7% clicking by coordinates and 82.1% clicking by ID.
It depends on the model
For the three strongest models, neither step made a difference. Across both steps, the four weaker models gained 9 to 37 points with the tree and ID clicks, more than doubling their scores in three of four cases. If you’re building on weaker models, the tree is still worth adding.
Some apps publish no tree at all. A canvas app like Figma publishes its toolbar buttons, but the canvas itself is just pixels, and a game like Doom is pixels from top to bottom. Even in apps with a good tree, some bugs only show on screen, like text that overflows its box. The screenshot covers all of these.
TREX Computer Use
Code review catches bugs in the diff. Some bugs only exist when the software is running: a button that can’t be clicked, a label that runs off the edge of the window. Until now, catching those took a person opening the app and simulating the user flow. By writing more code and touching more of the UI than any team can check by hand, coding agents have made it hard for programmers to keep up.
That’s why we built computer use into TREX. It runs the changes in your pull request on an actual desktop and uses the app the way a person would, so you can see that the feature you asked for is the one that got built.
How TREX sees the screen came out of these experiments. It runs on the best models and relies on the screenshot at every step. It uses the accessibility tree to click by ID when it can, and coordinates when the tree doesn't have the element, like a canvas or a game.
How a model should see the screen depends on the model, so we'll rerun these experiments each time a new one comes out and update TREX when the answer changes.
When something breaks on screen, TREX catches it, and shows you what happened.
Try TREX on your next pull request.

Kagi Mail (public beta)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
17:17:01 UTC
02:17:01 KST

We are proud to announce something that our small team of very dedicated people at Kagi has been building for a long time – the public beta of Kagi Mail. A private email and calendar with a @kagimail.com address, or with your own domains.
If you’ve already swapped Google Search for Kagi and Chrome for Orion, your inbox is probably the last big piece of your digital life still at Google. Now you can move that too.
Try Kagi Mail free for 60 days
$99/year (+ Sales Tax) after that. Included with Kagi Ultimate.
Why we built it
Does the world need another email service? We asked ourselves that question more than once. There are great ones out there, and we’re fans of what Fastmail and Tuta are doing. We still built our own, for the same reason we build everything at Kagi: technology should do its job and then get out of your way.
For email, that job is to be fast, private, and secure. On top of that, it should help you stay on track with what’s important: bring forward what you need to act on, respond to, or acknowledge, and quietly handle the rest.
Kagi Mail also has to treat you the way Kagi Search and Orion do: no surveillance, no ads, open standards, and no lock-in.
Already a Kagi member?
Kagi Mail is included in Ultimate. Sign in with your Kagi account, and you’re set. AI features draw from your existing Allowance. On another plan, or no Kagi account yet? After the free 60-day trial, you can get Kagi Mail for $99/year (+ Sales Tax).
How it works: A service and a client, on open standards
To deliver the experience we believe in, we built the full stack: hosting for your addresses and domains, plus the mail and calendar apps. It runs on open standards and open-source infrastructure (the Stalwart server, IMAP, JMAP, and CalDAV), so you can use Kagi’s apps or any compatible client you already like.
The basics
- A @kagimail.com address
- Unlimited aliases
- Unlimited domains
- Built-in Calendar
- 50 GB of storage
Intelligence
- Organize your mail in your own words. Brief it the way you’d brief an assistant: “Label anything where beta users send product feedback. It might come from @kagimail.com, but use judgment.” You decide what permissions each rule gets (read the content, or skip the inbox).
- Inbox Highlights show what needs you today, and learn from what you dismiss.
- Ask your inbox in plain language and get direct answers with the source email, including text inside scanned PDFs and photos.
- Newsletter view reads newsletters like a feed, out of the way of real mail.
- Responsible AI, with one switch to turn it all off. You control what AI is allowed to do and infer.
Private by default
- Tracking pixels and remote images are blocked or proxied, so senders can’t tell when or where you opened their mail.
- Sender screening holds new senders until you approve them, so they never reach your inbox uninvited.
- Suspicious senders trigger a phishing warning, while verified senders show their (BMI) logo.
No ads, ever. No profiling, no selling, no sharing your data.
For teams and families
- Shared addresses on your domain (team@, info@, family@): create an alias, then choose which people have access to it
- Sign in through a social login, or your organization’s own identity provider (OIDC)
- Coming soon: Shared mailboxes
Kagi integration
- Kagi Translate is built in
- Email Assistant: Write to assistant@kagimail.com for answers about your mail (actions coming soon)
- Proofread and translation included while you write emails
For power users and developers
- Keyboard-first: command palette, shortcuts, and search operators
- Rule-based filtering with Sieve, no AI involved
- MCP server with read access to your mail
- Works with Apple Mail, Thunderbird, and any IMAP/JMAP/CalDAV client, with automatic setup
What’s not here yet
We will keep adding new capabilities – here’s what to expect over the next few quarters.
Until the mobile apps arrive, use the mobile web app or any IMAP/JMAP client, such as Apple Mail or Thunderbird.
Our “no lock-in” promise to you
Leaving Kagi Mail is as easy as joining it. Your mail, calendar, and workflows are yours, and you can export them at any time, through standard protocols and a one-click export experience. You’ll keep access to your mailbox for six months after leaving Kagi Mail. You can send and receive emails for the first 60 days, then read and export your mail with one click for the remaining four months. We build an ecosystem so things work better together for those who want it, not to trap anyone.
Try Kagi Mail free for 60 days
$99/year (+ Sales Tax) after that. Included with Kagi Ultimate.
We want Kagi Mail to be the first email account we’d create for our children. We’re not there yet, but it is a high bar we hold ourselves to, and every feature in this beta is a step toward it: a calm, safe place to communicate, and a window onto an internet that treats you as a person, not a product who’s been tracked, targeted, and sold to.
We can build it this way because of you. Kagi has no ad business, and no one pushing us to monetize your inbox; you’re not a dataset for ads or AI. You are here because you believe the internet can be built differently. Thank you.

Meta throws out its net-zero goal — its competitors should be furious
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
17:13:45 UTC
02:13:45 KST

Meta’s new sustainability report buries the lead: The company is almost certain to miss its own goal to reach net zero by 2030, and it’s refusing to commit to a new timeline.
“We are re-evaluating our timelines, sequencing, and interim milestones — including the 2030 timeframe for our net zero emissions goal and our near-term intensity targets — so our approach reflects current operating realities while keeping progress transparent year over year,” wrote Rachel Peterson, vice president of data centers at Meta, and Blair Swedeen, global head of net-zero and sustainability at Meta, in the introduction to the company’s 2026 sustainability report.
That’s a lot of words to say that Meta will continue to pollute if that means it can continue its AI rollout.
Such is the risk of voluntary targets. Companies can make and break them with little to lose, betting — often rightly — that they can ignore them with just a small hit to public opinion, which is fickle anyway. (Remember the Cambridge Analytica scandal? Most people don’t.) There’s almost no risk to the bottom line.
“We remain committed to net zero, but are re-evaluating the 2030 timeline and interim milestone. We’ll continue investing in emissions reductions and reporting our progress annually,” a Meta spokesperson told TechCrunch.
A few years ago, Meta was arguably one of the better corporate stewards of the climate. The company has been one of the largest purchasers of clean power, and it set ambitious goals for water conservation.
More recently, though, its multibillion-dollar bet on natural gas at its Hyperion data center in Louisiana has undone much of that progress. Natural gas may burn cleaner and more efficiently than coal, but its impact on global warming can be just as bad when the leaky infrastructure needed to burn it is factored in.
Despite years of clean power procurement, Meta’s greenhouse gas emissions have more than doubled in the last four years. Between 2024 and 2025, its data center emissions increased more than 12 times, and Hyperion isn’t online yet.
That commitment to natural gas led to the company’s departure from RE100, a corporate renewable energy initiative, in July. The company had been part of the group for a decade, though the two split after RE100 started requiring more rigorous reporting toward renewable energy targets.
“When we set our net zero emissions goal, we anticipated steady growth, falling clean energy costs, and changes in the broader system that would enable progress at the pace we envisioned,” they added. “The reality has been far more complex. Demand for AI infrastructure has grown faster than almost anyone projected, arriving on a timeline measured in quarters rather than decades, and meeting it has meant building at a pace and scale the industry has never attempted.”
That’s all true, but when put that way, it sounds like Meta has little agency in the matter. But the company’s decision to chase AI was a conscious one.
You could argue that Meta has a fiduciary duty to chase after its peers, which have also dived headfirst into AI. But there’s precious little data to support the idea that AI will be a profit driver for a company like Meta, which is almost entirely consumer-focused.
CEO Mark Zuckerberg is optimistic that improving Muse’s energy efficiency will turn that around, but how much will everyday people be willing to pay for software that translates your offhand thoughts into surreal memes? Or monitors your emails so you won’t forget to reply to your mom? Meta is betting that it’ll be enough to support a $200 billion-plus data center, and probably more.
The choice of the fuel to power those AI ambitions was also a conscious one. Natural gas power plants, besides being able to run 24/7, are actually not a great fit for AI data centers. Turbines like to run at constant speeds, while AI data centers’ energy requirements surge and sag depending on the load. They need batteries as a buffer, which begs the question: Why not just connect to the grid and use batteries to bridge any gaps during times of peak demand? That’s what one expert recommends.
Meta’s bet on natural gas appears mistimed. Long-duration energy storage is coming into its own — and geothermal energy appears poised to become the next major power source on the grid in the U.S. Companies like Fervo, Mazama, and Quaise have been logging milestones at a rapid clip, potentially unlocking tens of gigawatts of capacity. The technology isn’t competitive with natural gas today, but it very well could be in the mid-2030s, just a few years after Meta completes Hyperion. Geothermal is also not vulnerable to supply chain shocks, like what happened with natural gas and the Iran War.
Beyond that, the company’s decision to abandon its 2030 net-zero goal risks fomenting the sort of backlash that leads to regulations with real teeth. Such regulations won’t just apply to Meta, and the broader industry would be justified in their frustration.
In that light, Meta’s timing couldn’t be worse. Surveys show the general public doesn’t hold AI or data centers in high esteem, rightly sensing that the infrastructure development is driving a rapid and often-unchecked expansion of polluting infrastructure.
The tech industry has spent over a decade trying to prove that it would be different from previous industrial juggernauts like oil and gas or automobile manufacturing. In a single PDF, Meta might have undone much of that goodwill. After this, who will trust another tech company’s voluntary net-zero goals?

Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
17:10:02 UTC
02:10:02 KST

Anthropic is launching some new Claude AI features to help software engineers ship code. As part of a new Claude Ship product being announced today, Claude now can review, fix, and approve pull requests.
Claude AI can review pull requests and ship fixes on its own now
Anthropic is bringing new AI tools to software engineers with Claude Ship.
Engineers have already been able to take advantage of Claude’s code review capabilities thanks to the Code Review feature, which was announced in March and became generally available in October. What’s new alongside Claude Ship are the PR Steward and Claude Approvals agents, which are launching in beta today.
PR Steward “jumps in on failing CI checks, merge conflicts, and review feedback from Code Review, other review tools, or team members” and leaves comments on what it changed, according to Anthropic. Claude Approvals can approve routine changes if an organization’s policy allows it. “Teams can have Claude Approvals consider a variety of signals in its decision-making process, including Code Review findings or their own classifiers using natural language,” Anthropic says.
Claude Ship is now available for Claude Team and Enterprise customers and works with repositories on GitHub.

US Intelligence Chief Predicts More Tech Outages As Systems Undergo Upgrades
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

US Intelligence Chief Predicts More Tech Outages As Systems Undergo Upgrades (reuters.com) 54
			
		 	
				Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton warned Sunday that Americans should expect more technology outages as companies and government agencies modernize aging infrastructure. Speaking on CNN, Clayton said the upgrades are necessary to improve performance and cybersecurity but will inevitably cause "glitches, gaps, [and] interruptions in service." Reuters reports:  A string of technology failures this month disrupted critical U.S. systems, including a nationwide halt to air travel, a Microsoft outage that hit email services, a technical glitch that affected several major banks, and a temporary disruption to the National Security Agency's network.
Speaking on the ABC program "This Week," Clayton reiterated his previous statements that those outages were not the result of coordinated or state-sponsored actions, attributing them to technical upgrades. Clayton declined to discuss specifics of separate reports of cyberattacks on the FBI and Department of Agriculture, saying he expects investigators to provide answers "in due course."   Clayton told CNN: "The American people should be prepared for a great deal of investment in upgrading our systems; they should be prepared for investment in cybersecurity, and they should be prepared that any time there is a step change in technology, a step change in both compute and applications, there will be bumps in the road."
Microsoft President Brad Smith issued a similar warning on CBS News' Face the Nation: "I think we have to be prepared, frankly, for the coming months because this isn't the type of thing that just gets done in a couple of weeks ... The whole information infrastructure of the world is in effect being upgraded right now."

Jensen Huang isn’t out of the woods yet
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:57:40 UTC
01:57:40 KST

It’s safe to say no one expected this.
Monday morning, Jensen Huang returned as CEO of Nvidia, after resigning the position following news of multiple affairs, including one with a subordinate and one with a woman tied to Chinese intelligence services. With so much of the AI industry built on top of Nvidia’s AI infrastructure, Huang’s departure looked like an imminent threat to the business — and the mood on his return has been ecstatic. Share prices are rocketing back up, particularly for neoclouds like CoreWeave and Nebius, and the Nasdaq rose 7% on the news.
Still, tech leaders should be clear-eyed about all the problems likely to come with Huang’s reinstatement. President Trump is already telling reporters that the U.S. government found no national security concerns resulting from the affair, but the broader national security concerns aren’t so easy to dismiss, and Trump’s involvement may do more harm than good for Nvidia and its partners. This isn’t the end of the problems caused by Huang’s affairs; it’s likely just the beginning.
Most importantly, serious questions remain about how Nvidia chips were smuggled into China, and how much Nvidia knew about it. Among Jensen Huang’s defenders, it’s become common to question whether Alice Huang was really a spy, but it’s clear that a lot of restricted chips were flowing into China through her company. Just this weekend, The New York Times reported sustained contact between Alice Huang and a member of China’s Ministry of State Security during the same period she was having an affair with Jensen Huang.
With Democrats in control of Congress, including investigative bodies like the House Oversight Committee, there are plenty of ways to force the issue. Nvidia’s role in violating U.S. export controls would likely have been the subject of prolonged uncomfortable investigation, whether Huang returned or not. But with him back in the CEO’s seat, there will be a lot more riding on any revelations that come up — and any new revelations will damage Nvidia as a company alongside Huang himself.
Then there are the questions raised by Trump’s intervention. It’s unprecedented in American history for a sitting president to intervene to clear a CEO accused of being a national security risk. Simply pressing the Department of Justice to exonerate Huang would have been a scandal, but calling the Nvidia board goes significantly further. Huang has already donated $25 million to Trump’s ballroom project, so it’s easy to make the case that Trump was intervening on behalf of a donor, setting national security risks aside for his personal benefit.
All of this is happening just as Democrats retake Congress, with all of its associated investigative powers. Even if they can’t fire Huang or remove Trump, there are lots of uncomfortable questions that are still unanswered. Was there any Department of Justice inquiry into whether Huang had committed a crime, or did Trump make the call on his own? Did the intelligence services make any official assessment that could be subpoenaed into the congressional record? Investigating Democrats will be particularly interested in Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who oversees export controls and retains significant financial ties to the industry through his firm, Cantor Fitzgerald.
If all of this sounds exhausting, you’re getting a sense of what the next year will be like for anyone following national news. Congress will keep a steady drip of embarrassing revelations, milking as much political juice as possible out of Huang and everyone associated with him. Trump will keep lashing out, often making things worse for everyone involved. (Trump’s statement to CBS, which defends Huang in racist terms while also accusing him of treason, is an early taste.) It’s going to be ugly and sad, and the most successful tech company of the last decade will be right at the center of it.
I have to assume that this was the scenario the board was concerned about when it decided to remove Huang two weeks ago. Since then, the cost of removing him has gone up: an open letter from Nvidia’s employees and partners, a tanking stock market, and, ultimately, direct intervention from the president of the United States.
But nothing has lowered the cost of keeping Huang, and the ugly scenario the board foresaw earlier this month is still very much in place. We’re about to see it come to life.

Trump reportedly called into Nvidia board meeting to advocate for Huang’s return
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:39:10 UTC
01:39:10 KST

A close ally of Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang, President Donald Trump reportedly dialed into an Nvidia board meeting to push for the ousted CEO’s return, Semafor reported on Monday. Trump got his way, as Nvidia announced Huang’s reinstatement shortly afterward.
“I spoke to them, let me put it that way … And I convinced them,” the president told CNBC.
Huang was forced to step down from his role as CEO, which he held for 33 years, after news broke that he had affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and two Nvidia employees, including a direct report.
“President Trump was totally involved and this is a great decision by the NVIDIA board. We have to protect our geniuses,” White House communications director Steven Cheung told Semafor.
The president took to his social media app, Truth Social, to remark that “the United States government has no reason to believe that Jensen’s return to Nvidia poses any risk to national security.”
According to Nvidia’s press release about Huang’s reinstatement as CEO and president, the company claimed that “the Board received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.”
While Trump has declared that Huang “did absolutely nothing wrong,” many government officials aren’t buying the president’s point of view. Huang is still expected to testify in closed-door conversations with three committees of the House of Representatives on December 1. He is also slated to appear before the Senate Banking Committee to testify publicly, though that hearing has not been scheduled yet.
“People say, is it a threat to have him? No, it’s a threat to lose him,” Trump said to CNBC.
Trump has maintained this position through the scandal, even denying that he was ever told about Huang’s relationship with an alleged Chinese spy named Alice Huang (no relation). The New York Times reported, on the contrary, that Trump’s secretary of state and national security adviser Marco Rubio briefed him aboard Air Force One in May.
Earlier this spring, Rubio also supervised the drafting of a memo that circulated among top policymakers, warning that Alice Huang was spying for China’s Ministry of State Security.

White House brags that Trump was ‘totally involved’ in getting Jensen reinstated
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:30:11 UTC
01:30:11 KST

It sure looked like President Donald Trump played a role in Jensen Huang’s reinstatement as Nvidia CEO — and now the White House is making sure everyone knows he was. “President Trump was totally involved and this is a great decision by the NVIDIA board,” White House communications director Steven Cheung said in a statement, which was first reported by Semafor. “We have to protect our geniuses.”
White House brags that Trump was ‘totally involved’ in getting Jensen reinstated
Trump said Nvidia’s board ‘made the correct and proper decision’ to bring back Jensen Huang.
Trump later went on CNBC with a similar statement. “We have to protect our geniuses,” he said on the air. Trump said he “believed very strongly” that Huang should be reinstated. “We’ve done a very thorough investigation, and the investigation showed that he did — I mean, he had bad judgment having to do with somewhat unrelated incidents. But he did nothing wrong.”
Trump also said, “If I didn’t get involved, you’d probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come.”
Nvidia said in its announcement that its board “received input from senior levels of the United States government” that made them confident reinstating Huang would not pose a national security risk, following reports about his involvement with an alleged Chinese spy.
Trump also posted on Truth Social that Nvidia’s board “made the correct and proper decision” to reinstate Huang, adding:
“The United States Government has no reason to believe that Jensen’s return to NVIDIA poses any risk to National Security. The Attorney General of the United States has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct.”
Less than 12 hours earlier, Trump said in a separate post that Huang’s reinstatement “would be the best thing” for Nvidia and the US, and urged the board to rehire him. Reporting from Semafor now says that he was calling in to Nvidia’s board meeting around this time to make the same argument.

CrowdStrike to acquire Zscaler for $44 billion
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:20:11 UTC
01:20:11 KST

Cybersecurity giant CrowdStrike has agreed to buy Zscaler in a $43.9 billion all-stock deal, the companies announced Monday.
CrowdStrike and Zscaler said that the deal will create the “largest single addressable market” for cybersecurity, which will enable the companies to better position themselves for the AI era.
Zscaler is a San Jose, California-based tech company that provides cloud cybersecurity products to about 8,000 enterprise companies. The company offers zero-trust technology, a security feature that requires employees to authenticate each and every time they access an internal system or resource. The aim is to protect against hackers from stealing credentials and using them to gain unfettered access to a company’s network.
CrowdStrike, which secures the computers, laptops, phones, and other devices connected to a company’s network, said the deal will unite its endpoint protection software with Zscaler’s zero-trust platform to better defend their customers against data breaches. By combining the two products, CrowdStrike and Zscaler say that they can offer “superior protection” to their customers.
In remarks on Monday, the companies said that the acquisition will help customers safely adopt AI.
The deal, which is subject to regulatory approval, is expected to close around this time next year.

Tech leaders are celebrating Jensen Huang’s return
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:14:04 UTC
01:14:04 KST

Let’s say you’re Jensen Huang. You (allegedly) cheated on your wife with an alleged Chinese spy, allegedly had an affair with not one, but two subordinates, and (allegedly!) “terrorized” your employees, a series of indiscretions that led to your ouster from what was once the world’s most valuable company. (The stock price has recently taken a tumble, for obvious reasons.) And now you’re back, baby — and all your friends are so eager to congratulate you.
Tech leaders are celebrating Jensen Huang’s return
Huang is getting by with a little help from his friends.
Elon Musk, for one, is thrilled.
And why wouldn’t he be? As a fellow leather jacket enthusiast who has a checkered history with women — including those who work for him — it’s clear that he and Huang have a lot in common beyond their positions as tech CEOs. Shivon Zilis, who has two children with Musk, also got in on the fun.
OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, too, is a man who can relate to Huang’s struggle.
Gavin Baker, founder and chief investment officer of Atreides, is so impressed by Huang’s rise from the ashes that he’s going to buy a leather jacket of his own.
Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeny chimed in with an oblique reference to Huang’s outerwear.
Shopify CEO Tobi Lütke took what one could call a moral stance.
And then there’s billionaire investor Bill Ackman, a real wife guy if there ever was one, but one who, for better or worse, seems to understand that the market comes first.
Sequoia Capital partner Shaun Maguire also pointed out the obvious market-bending powers of Huang’s reinstatement.
Of course, no one has been more effusive than the man who may have orchestrated it all. President Donald Trump posted his lengthy congratulations to Huang on Truth Social. “I have gotten to know Jensen, and he is a Great Patriot and National Treasure,” Trump wrote. He commended Nvidia’s board for making the “correct and proper” decision in reinstating Huang — a decision that, the board admits, was made with input “from senior levels of the United States government,” who determined that “Huang’s reinstatement poses no security risk.” Isn’t that great news?
Trump expects Huang to make him proud. “I think you’re going to see some tremendous numbers out of this company,” Trump said on CNBC. “I talked to him last night. I said, ‘Jensen, just promise me one thing: You’re going to work hard.’ I spoke to him last night because I knew what was going on.” In the same appearance, Trump said “we need to protect our geniuses.”
Marc Andreessen didn’t officially comment on the reinstatement, but the a16z cofounder changed his X bio after the news broke. It now reads: “We have to protect our geniuses.”
Trump also seems to be relishing in Huang’s alleged affairs. In another Truth Social post, he superimposed Huang — red bra in hand — into the iconic photo of his reaction to being shot at a rally in Butler, Pennsylvania.
For Canadian venture capitalist Chamath Palihapitiya, Trump’s intervention is just further proof of Huang’s innocence. Certainly an interesting take!
The market’s reaction may be the most jubilant of all. Per Odd Lots host Joe Weisenthal, NASDAQ-100 futures jumped so quickly that trading was halted. Congratulations to everyone who bought the dip, I guess.
Update, November 23rd: This post has been updated with reactions from Shivon Zilis, Tobi Lütke, Shaun Maguire, Chamath Palihapitiya, and Marc Andreessen.

Ladybird is now in alpha
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:13:12 UTC
01:13:12 KST

Hello friends! Ladybird is now in alpha. You can download it for macOS and Linux.
I’ve been looking forward to writing this post for a long time. :^)
Ladybird is a browser with a new engine. The engine is our own, built from the ground up, and not a fork. The browser around it is modest, but functional.
This is an alpha, so some websites won’t work, and it will crash sometimes. When that happens, tell us! You can report a broken site from inside the browser. After a crash, you can read the crash report and decide whether to send it. Our privacy policy has the details.
We want security nerds to hack it! A new browser gets hacked, over and over, and that’s part of the process, so we make no promises about security today. When you pwn us, please tell us how so we can harden Ladybird!
The alpha is a rolling channel, and we’ll be pushing out automatic updates regularly. This is the part I’m most excited about. Ladybird improves quickly, and until now you had to build it from source to see that.
If you build websites, try yours in Ladybird and tell us what breaks. It supports WebDriver for automated testing, as well as the Firefox DevTools protocol.
Thank you to our sponsors and donors, and to everyone who has tested, reported bugs, and written code over the years. There’s still a mountain of work ahead of us, but I’m very happy that you can finally try Ladybird for yourself!

Buckle Up the AI Bubble Is Popping
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
16:01:20 UTC
01:01:20 KST

Buckle Up! The AI Bubble is Popping
Last week’s Anthropic IPO was the height of the bubble. From now on it’s chaos and, unfortunately, eventually a recession. The news this morning that Trump reinstated, or twisted Nvidia’s board to reinstate Jensen Huang is just his typical and predictable way of thinking of the market as a proxy of his manhood. It won’t solve any problems even though markets are temporarily up.
Because the main problem is that very very rich people in Silicon Valley only just heard the news, via the midterms, that normal people hate them, and hate data centers, and are stopping them from happening. Literally, until now, the nerds were still computing the costs of data centers in terms of the cost of land and equipment and maybe the workers who they needed to build them. They think that way because they are trained to think that way. They have no sense of politics whatsoever until they get (consistently) surprised by it. I know this because I used to be a nerd like that. The only difference is that I didn’t think politics was stupid, I just realized I had no idea how it worked. Whereas the God-complex infused AI founders really think that, because they are good at engineering, they are smarter and wiser than whatever happens in DC. It means they have no finger on the pulse of what is actually happening.
The consequences of this data center construction slow-down are important, and somewhat subtle. I don’t work at a hedge fund anymore, but if I did I would be backing out of the market values and circular financing deals and interest rates (including long-term interest rates) the following question: what is the implied rate of growth of compute that all of the dealmaking implies?
It’s not just one number, it’s a graph of the rate of growth of compute over the next 5 years. And that graph is being challenged by the pushback of data center protests and new laws and fights against permitting for the electric grid, access to water, and concerns over noise and air pollution.
What it means is that there’s a new graph of expected compute growth, and it is smaller than what the market thinks it is. The delta between those two graphs is about half the size of the larger graph. It’s not an estimation error, it’s a big deal.
And so it’s not going to be adjusted by a small or even major shift in market values of the big companies. Because there’s an avalanche of debts that depend on this graph being right, and when the first few loans go into default, or even before they do but their companies are pushed to provide proof that they will get paid, their failures will put other debts into doubt, and the spaghetti plate of circular financing will slowly and then quickly unwind, with investors getting the fuck out of the way while they still can.
The only thing in our economy that’s really growing right now is the healthcare sector, basically because people are getting older and want to stay healthy. But even that sector is going through chaos due to the ridiculous policies of the greedy Trump administration.
And everything I just mentioned has nothing to do with the internet going down, which I still think has a real, non-zero chance of happening soon. If that happens, I plan to start a soup kitchen on the park next to where I live in Somerville called Prospect Hill Park. It was the site of a lookout during the Revolutionary War, and then it housed northern troops during the Civil War. We’ll have a library and places to exchange information and a way to listen to the solar powered radios that me and the other preppers will happily share. I’ll call it Occupy 2.0.

Dutch privacy authority urges consumers not to buy ‘pervert glasses’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
15:29:49 UTC
00:29:49 KST

The Dutch Data Protection Authority is urging consumers not to buy AI-enabled camera glasses, warning that they compromise the privacy of nearby bystanders and risk the safety of their users.
The authority, whose recent statement falls just short of an outright ban, said that use of the internet-enabled and always-on glasses to record people in public is “almost always prohibited.”
Critics have dubbed these smart wearables as “pervert glasses” for their propensity to be used to video people without their permission, particularly to harass women. While some glasses have warning lights when users are recording, privacy advocates have argued that these alerts are not clear enough. Some people have even made phone apps capable of detecting nearby smart glasses wearers.
The Dutch data protection authority warned that the glasses also produce wrong results and unsafe results. In one test carried out by the Dutch authority, a pair of unnamed glasses “gave dating advice to an adult who indicated that they are interested in a minor.” The glasses also incorrectly read a medicine label and claimed to have called an ambulance when it had not.
The Dutch government’s advice follows a similar move by the Norwegian government, which in September said it was considering a ban on camera-enabled devices.

====================================================================================================

One Man's Quest to End Daylight Savings
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
15:28:41 UTC
00:28:41 KST

At a rally in Mississippi not long ago, Donald Trump complained about the reason for his frequent tardiness. “I’m always late. You know, I had a big meeting and I was an hour late. I said, ‘How?’ ” he told the crowd. “Daylight-saving time. We change it twice a year. . . . It’s ridiculous.” Trump has been urging the Senate to approve the Sunshine Protection Act—which was passed by the House in July and would make daylight-saving time permanent—before the bill expires at the end of the current Congress, on January 3rd.
His speech recalled an episode of HBO’s “Veep” in which Congressman Jonah Ryan is two hours late to a meeting after adjusting his clock the wrong way. “That’s impossible,” Ryan says. “I set my watch back for daylight-savings time. I springed backwards!” Told that he should have sprung forward, he’s irate: “Have you ever watched girls’ gymnastics? That makes no sense!”
“It’s a naturally funny topic,” Scott Yates said late last month, the day before the clocks were set back. Yates, a sixty-one-year-old former journalist, has spent a decade trying to end the changing of the clocks. “Some people plan their vacations around golf,” he said. “I use frequent-flier miles to fly to Michigan to testify.”
He was sitting outside a former chapel in Liberia, Costa Rica, to which he relocated from Colorado, in 2024. The country does not change the clocks. “It’s not the No. 1 reason I moved here, but it’s on the list,” he said. He wore Birkenstocks and no watch. Yates began his crusade after he read the book “A Complaint Free World.” “The idea is, if you can’t do something about it, you shouldn’t complain about it,” he said. “Robbing an hour of sleep just always annoyed me.” So he started a blog (locktheclock.net) in which he compiles research on the topic.
Yates found that studies linked the “spring forward” clock change—where people lose an hour of sleep—to a rise in heart attacks and medical incidents involving human error, while the “fall backward” clock change—where it gets dark earlier—led to an increase in fatal auto accidents. Both changes are associated with a spike in strokes.
Within a couple of years, legislators in more than a dozen states started contacting Yates about his writing. He’s helped draft bills, spoken at hearings, and, last year, testified before the Senate Commerce Committee, advocating to end clock changing. (His proposal would give states two years to decide which time to lock in.) He concluded his testimony with a story about how scientists often avoid changing lab rats’ schedules after a clock change because it disrupts results. “At long last, can we treat our citizens with the decency that we treat our lab rats?” he asked the senators.
Yates often gets e-mails from people with their own ideas about how to solve the problem. Computer programmers reach out suggesting that we stop using time zones and time changes altogether and put the whole world on Coördinated Universal Time, the international time standard. In New York, this might mean waking up at noon and having lunch at 6 P.M. “It’s never going to happen,” Yates said.
The idea of daylight-saving time was first implemented in Germany during the First World War, to save on energy costs, but it had built steam earlier in Europe as a health movement and was helped along by a golf-loving Englishman named William Willett, who liked to go riding early in the morning. “He was me a hundred years earlier,” Yates said. In 1907, Willett distributed a pamphlet called “The Waste of Daylight,” promoting a plan for four twenty-minute advances in the clock throughout the year. It went nowhere.
Around the same time, the National Baseball League wanted more light for evening games, while movie-theatre executives wanted less, thinking that darkness might drive people into cinemas. In the states, daylight-saving time was implemented as a fragmented system throughout the World Wars and assorted energy crises. In New York City, in 1951, Grand Central Terminal was home to two railroads that used different times. In the early sixties, there were twenty-three different combinations of start and end dates for daylight-saving time in Iowa alone. The Times described it as “time schizophrenia.” To remedy the clock anarchy, the Uniform Time Act of 1966 standardized the dates for daylight-saving time and gave states the option of moving to permanent Standard Time. Only Hawaii, Arizona (except the Navajo Nation), Puerto Rico, and a few other U.S. territories do not change the clocks.
“Time doesn’t exist,” Yates said. “The thing that we call time is just what we all agree on.” The Sunshine Protection Act is the closest he has come to getting his way, but the House bill is slightly different than what he advocated for. “Washington is just so dysfunctional,” he said. “If this was a thing with money behind it, it would’ve been worked out.” He added, “I’m so worried about Trump being in favor of it, because I don’t want to ruin it for another fifty years.” ♦

Dance into the Week with an Entirely Hand-Drawn Animation for the Osees

My trusty 27-inch Alienware QD-OLED gaming monitor is just $280
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
15:24:22 UTC
00:24:22 KST

Alienware’s AW2726DM QD-OLED gaming monitor was a great deal even at its original $349.99 price. The low price and impressive specs were enough to convince me to get one at launch, and I’m glad that I did. I’m a little jealous that you have the opportunity to save $70 on the monitor I’ve been using for work and play for several months. Best Buy and Dell are selling the 27-inch 1440p monitor for $279.99 ahead of Black Friday.
My trusty 27-inch Alienware QD-OLED gaming monitor is just $280
The 1440p 240Hz panel looks and performs like a monitor that’s hundreds more.
Alienware AW2726DM QD-OLED monitor
If you’re on a tight budget, but want spectacular contrast and brightness control that only an OLED can provide, here’s a deal for you. Alienware’s 1440p 27-inch QD-OLED monitor with 240Hz refresh rate is selling at its lowest price yet. Read our hands-on.
The AW2726DM has more features than other similarly-priced monitors that I was comparing it to, and that still holds true when comparing it to more modern models. It has a 1440p QD-OLED screen that delivers tremendous contrast and deep blacks, and its 240Hz refresh rate provides a high ceiling for my graphics card to deliver fast frames. It’s equipped with HDMI and DisplayPort inputs, and really one of its only flaws is that it lacks USB-C video support. The monitor works great for PC games, as well as console titles. I think it’d be a sweet gift to yourself or to a gamer in your life this holiday.
