How one router caused the worst ground stop since 9/11
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
15:15:12 UTC
00:15:12 KST

Today I’m talking with Darryl Campbell, a freelance journalist, aviation expert, and author of the book Fatal Abstraction: Why the Managerial Class Loses Control of Software.
How one router caused the worst ground stop since 9/11
Journalist Darryl Campbell breaks down the mundane management failures behind the FAA’s nationwide meltdown, and what it will take to fix a system held together with duct tape.
Darryl covers aviation and the FAA, and earlier this month, when the US suffered a sudden nationwide ground stop on air travel, Darryl was all over the story. He published a great report for us on The Verge about what happened, what and who was to blame, and what we might do about the underlying infrastructure failures in the aviation business.
So ahead of Thanksgiving, one of the country’s major holiday travel weeks, I wanted to have Darryl on the show to walk us through this incident on Friday, November 13th, when the FAA grounded every departing flight in the country for more than four hours.
You’ll hear us work backward from how a single router could cause this kind of damage to why our aviation infrastructure is so painfully out of date and what the government plans to do about it. Also, why incidents like this are creating a kind of swirling paranoia about AI and cyberattacks, even when the real culprit is far more mundane.
Before we get going, do me a favor and subscribe to Decoder on YouTube, where we put out new episodes every Monday and Thursday.
Okay: aviation journalist Darryl Campbell on the FAA ground stop. Here we go.
This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity.
Darryl Campbell, you’re a journalist, you’re an aviation expert, and you’re the author of the book Fatal Abstraction: Why the Managerial Class Loses Control of Software, which is potentially the most Decoder title in all of publishing history. Welcome to Decoder.
Thanks very much. Longtime listener, first-time caller. So thanks for having me.
I love that book title. I love all the ideas that it suggests. We’ll have you back to talk about the book, but I feel like we’re going to use that idea to talk about air traffic control today on Decoder with you.
Yeah, I think so much of what we’ve found out, even in the week since my report came out, has really just been a lot of boring management failures that have somehow cascaded to affect millions of people on thousands of flights. And it’s just the importance of actually having people who know what they’re doing in charge.
There’s a lot there already. There’s a whole hour of Decoder right there. But what you’re referencing is the fact that you wrote a story for us about the nationwide ground stop the FAA had to call because of a bad router. Tell us what we know. What happened here? Why did air traffic control ground you all?
Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here.
I’m going to take you back to Friday the 13th in a part of the country that nobody really associates with aviation, which is a little place called El Dorado, Arkansas. It’s the very tail end of part of this big backbone of data and telecommunications that’s really a private network run exclusively for air traffic control by a company called L3Harris, who runs it on behalf of the FAA.
They’re just going in there, and they’re replacing what is essentially like 50-year-old infrastructure. If you remember back in the ‘90s, early 2000s when you made your first switch from DSL to fiber optic, that is exactly what they’re trying to do there, except-
So just to remind everybody, DSL ran on the phone lines.
Yeah, so this is like a cable modem. So I’m old enough to remember that, and maybe some of you out there are. But yeah, this is like as soon as you plug in fiber, your speeds get five to 10 times faster. And that sounds great, but in the private telecom world, that happens on entirely separate networks.
Your DSL provider isn’t your fiber provider, or if they are, they’re running on parallel networks. The FAA system has to do both at the same time, and so you have to figure out how we get all of these different cables, different routers, different protocols for how these things transmit data to all play nice with the same reliability and uptime? Because you need that when you’re managing tens of thousands of flights every single day.
One of the things that I think is really important to understand here is that the FAA runs its own private network. It’s called the FAA Telecommunications Infrastructure. It was built by this contractor back in the ‘90s and early 2000s. So it’s old, and it is actually due to be replaced, correct?
Yeah, and that’s part of the challenge, because you have to have it to keep the aviation system going; you can’t just shut it down for a week, rip everything out, and replace it. So they’ve been doing piecemeal upgrades over time, and a lot of it is done by this L3Harris contractor, who’s one of the biggest defense companies in the world. They’ve been working with the FAA for a long time. You would think that they would know how to do a simple router swap without causing everything to go down, but that’s exactly what happened on Friday.
If you go back and look at everything that exactly caused the outage, it’s like a comedy of errors. No single thing would have caused the entire system to crash, but every little missed opportunity, failed connection, or decision that somebody made with no knowledge of what happens downstream from everything, really led to the point where all of a sudden the most congested airspace in the world could not figure out where the airplanes were. They couldn’t talk to people who are en route, and that’s a serious safety problem that could have put thousands of people at risk.
Let’s work backwards from the actual precipitating incident, and then let’s talk about the government’s response to this crisis. So there’s a router swap that happens in Arkansas. In your story, you say they swapped in a fiber router for an old copper router, and that thing started sending out too many requests for the network it was connected to.
Yeah.
Explain exactly what that is.
So L3Harris is trying to replace what is essentially a telephone switch from the 1960s with a new modern fiber optic data router. And so they’re removing the cabling, they’re removing the switch, and replacing it.
Now, if you’re on, let’s say, a perfectly modern networking system, and you’re switching out a fiber optic for a fiber optic line, the data routing is the same; the protocols that the routers use to communicate with each other are the same. Do you remember that DSL to fiber optic comparison we made? That’s not really an issue because everything can handle huge amounts of data with high throughput.
When the manufacturer sends out these new routers, there’s a factory setting on it that says, “Okay, when you’re making a communication attempt for the first time, assume that you are doing it with a high-throughput fiber optic system.” L3Harris forgot to turn off that switch, and so as soon as they plug in-
That’s it?
Yeah, from what I can tell and from all the reports that have come out, that is literally it. So L3Harris has said, “Hey, we did what we were supposed to. The router manufacturer said everything was working as it’s supposed to. There was no problem with the machine. It’s just that someone forgot to say, ‘Hey, you’re connecting to an old system.’” So when it starts pinging what it thinks is a high-capacity fiber optic data network, it’s just sending thousands and thousands of communication requests.
Now, on their own, and if this wasn’t a critical infrastructural system, it probably would have done what I compare to a FaceTime call with bad Wi-Fi. All these connection requests start displacing some of the normal data packets that come through the network, and you start losing a couple of data packets, and so maybe the image quality degrades, or the audio starts to skip or something like that. That’s fine if you’re just trying to talk to someone in a normal one-on-one setting.
But again, this is the air traffic control system for the world’s largest air traffic network, and you really can’t have any kind of data lag. And so what pilots start to see is the air traffic control routing centers that handle the high-altitude airplanes, the ones who are in cruise control and whatever; first, they start seeing some transmission loss. Sometimes it’s a radio transmission that cuts out. Over time, it gets worse as this router keeps pinging the system and keeps blasting it with all of these connection requests that cause data loss.
And then that starts cascading. So it’s not just radio traffic. It then becomes radar data, too, and then it becomes so overwhelming that entire centers are going offline. At the peak of the crisis, when Secretary Sean Duffy decided to start this ground stop, 17 out of the 22 en route control centers around the country had gone dark, and they were unable to communicate with airplanes that were en route, or see where they were, without having to rely on a backup system.
That strikes me as all very bad, and as we saw, there was a ground stop. Why did it take quite as long as it did for anyone to say what happened?
Yeah.
I’m thinking about [Transportation Secretary] Sean Duffy and DHS Secretary Markwayne Mullin and their, frankly, absurd press conferences where they were saying conflicting things back to back from one another. They were not ruling out cyber attacks. What happened there? Why did it take so long to come up with what seems like a simple explanation for a very complicated problem?
Yeah, well, if it sounded like people didn’t know what they were talking about, it’s because they didn’t. If you remember back in, I think, February of 2025, Elon Musk going in there with DOGE and firing a couple hundred people at the FAA. Now, you remember at the time — and in fact, I wrote a piece about it for The Verge — they said, “These aren’t safety-critical people. These aren’t the radar technicians. They’re not air traffic controllers.” That was true. But what they were was network engineers, data technicians, telecom specialists. These are the people who would have been overseeing the work that L3Harris was doing, and they simply did not exist.
So there was a CNN report that came out about this trail of evidence, where usually what would happen is the contractor comes in with a router, and then an FAA technician meets them and oversees the work. They do some tests to make sure that everything is going okay and that nothing is going to disrupt the entire system. Everybody signs off, and then everyone goes home, and nobody really knows that anything happened.
Well, guess what didn’t happen? The FAA technician did meet the contractor, but they didn’t oversee any of the work. The contractor didn’t say that they were done. They didn’t let the FAA know that they were going to start testing. All of these decisions and all of these theoretically simple, easy tasks related to basic good management and governance didn’t happen. By the time people figured out what was going on, we were already four hours into what was the worst ground stop since 9/11.
How did we figure out what was going on?
So eventually L3Harris started isolating parts of the data management system — basically taking routers offline. That’s why one of the initial reports came out and said, “Oh, we think we’ve isolated the problem to a part of the country that spans Arkansas and Texas.” And then, just one by one, taking these routers dark, they eventually turned off the single router that had gone and started creating these connection requests.
Once that was offline, which honestly took them until around 7:40PM or 8PM ET on Friday, they were able, almost instantaneously, to start getting data and radar connections back. And then they decided to release the ground stop at that point. But it was really just your classic IT debugging of turning things off and seeing if anything changed until they found the right one.
When all else fails.
That’s right. Turn it off, turn it back on again.
I said I wanted to move backwards. You stepped back to DOGE. Elon Musk comes in with DOGE. He takes the chainsaw to bureaucracy. He fires a bunch of network technicians. It seems like a bunch of processes go out the window. But this is a long story, right? This is a rickety system that we’ve known needed to be upgraded for years. Is it really just DOGE that brought us to the brink of incompetence, or is there a story beyond that?
Yeah, I think it’s not just years, but decades. I mean, the main problem is that the money, effort, and sustained attention required to do all of these kinds of boring network upgrades operate on time horizons that are like five to 10 years. Whereas in politics, your time horizon is maybe a year or two max, every time you’re going back to budgeting, to getting the political will to get things passed.
Because those things are so far out of sync, number one, it’s hard to just get the money in the first place to do these expensive upgrades. Number two, keep the money going so that the project is perfectly budgeted and the contractors have started on it, then get the money to keep doing the work that they need to do for a couple of years. And then number three, to maintain that momentum across administrations, across congressional changes, and really across party shifts. That’s been the big problem — that whenever a sort of smaller government, anti-regulation administration comes in, they start cutting some of the funding, and usually the FAA is on the chopping block.
It’s almost kind of ironic that pilots, air traffic controllers, and the people who operate the system behind the scenes have actually gotten so good at making up for the FAA and the air traffic control system’s flaws — just because their machines are literally falling apart — that we’ve kind of papered over some of the issues and gone around some of the problems. If these had surfaced maybe five or 10 years ago, maybe we would be a little bit faster about getting the upgrades that we need today.
Just continuing the theme of moving backwards, it’s not as though people didn’t know, right? There was funding to modernize the system. That funding was controversial in the way that whenever Democrats pass funding and Republicans take power, the funding becomes controversial.
It’s funny. There’s a video of Sec. Duffy saying the Biden administration was wasting money that they should have been spending on the FAA. And then after this incident, he’s begging for $50 billion at a press conference. It seems like everyone knew about the problem, right? We have a plan to fix it. There’s a date to replace the system. Why isn’t there more urgency?
Yeah, so I think there are two parts to your question here. I think number one is the political will required to get the budget in the first place, which is kind of like a two- to three-year process where you have to identify the company that can actually do the work. That’s part of the reason why we’ve got all these big incumbent defense companies because you can’t have some startup in San Francisco figure out how to change 3,000 miles of cabling overnight. That just can’t be done.
And then you have to go through the work of getting it passed through Congress, through all of the accountability layers like the Government Accountability Office and all of the other procedural guardrails that are designed to keep government waste somewhat under control. That’s largely good, except that by the time we get the reports and actually figure out, okay, here’s what we need to do, here’s the right vendor, and so on, usually that’s after a midterm or after an administration change, and the political will has already kind of gone out the window.
So I think that’s one part of it. And I think the second part is the FAA is just kind of suffering from death by a thousand cuts over the last 20, 30, 40 years. Even if they do the right thing, which is that they should be replacing a lot of these older switches with new routers, by the time that happens, all of the other issues with the administration and the agency have kind of filtered down to the point where they just don’t have the people in place.
Let’s say you’re a network engineer who really knows their stuff and who could be in the right place for it. After three to five years of just sort of will they, won’t they, am I going to have a job next year, and all of the other things that get in the way of you being able to do your job, you’re probably going to go into private industry at that point. So I think there’s a talent problem and there’s this kind of overall political problem that’s really causing a lot of these issues.
I want to rewind to the beginning of the second Trump administration, which feels like 10 million years ago, but it was not so long ago. It was two years ago. Elon comes in with DOGE; he’s got the chainsaw for bureaucracy, and he’s firing these people. He’s maybe getting rid of some of the talent you’re talking about.
But there’s also a bunch of aviation catastrophes that happened, right? Planes were crashing, there were near misses at airports, and we heard a lot about how, because Elon was in the government, we would instantly modernize our infrastructure. We were going to rip Verizon out and replace it with Starlink. We were going to AI vibe code all the software. Did anything come of that, or did anything happen because of that?
A couple of things have happened, but not to the extent that they’re addressing some of the major problems with the system. I think the biggest challenge here, and it’s kind of a quirk of the U.S. air traffic control system, is that our regulator and also the entity that operates all of the infrastructure and air traffic control systems are one and the same.
It’s actually quite a hard thing for, in particular, Republicans and small government conservatives to get the idea that it’s good to fund the FAA when, in their mind, it’s a regulator as much as it is kind of the operator. That already creates a lot of challenges.
I think the second thing that we talk about, and specific to DOGE, was that a lot of the contracts that they gave out were from people in that Silicon Valley orbit. So you think about Palantir; there’s a new company called ASI that’s now running the SMART AI air traffic control system.
Oh, we’re going to go to ASI.
Yeah, that’s been kind of a big conspiracy theory thing. But those are great, and they’re important systems, but the biggest challenges for the FAA are physical in nature. How do we replace this gigantic network? How do we get these telephone switches out of the ground and replace them with fiber optics? How do we replace radar and communications technology that is, in some cases, literally held together with duct tape and aluminum foil? And that part you’re never going to be able to do as kind of a small startup operating entirely in the digital and software space.
I mean, the people that they gave contracts to and the people that benefited the most were attacking some small part of the problem, but not the one that’s related to how do you keep airplanes from not running into each other or how do you make sure that air traffic control radios are working so that helicopters don’t crash into commercial flights. Those are really hard, intractable, physical world problems that this sort of Silicon Valley AI vibe coding thing is never going to solve.
There was some amount of drama about Verizon being ripped out and replaced with Starlink. The idea that we could just upgrade the networks and equip all the planes with new sensors — there was energy around that in the beginning of the Trump administration. It appears to have just completely fizzled.
It’s part of this contractual incumbent thing. You can say whatever you want about replacing a vendor you don’t like with the one from your buddy down the hall, but it’s going to take years. Verizon, I think, did get moved out of some contracts, but they were replaced with another big telecom company, not Starlink.
I mean, I’m pretty sure that there’s one Starlink system, and it’s operating in the middle of nowhere in Alaska. So on paper, yeah, Starlink has something, but again, they’re just not equipped to handle some of the big problems with all of this sort of legacy cabling and networking.
You mentioned your buddy’s company down the hall. That brings us directly to ASI, which is the center of one of the hottest conspiracy theories about the ground stop that exists. Sean Duffy’s son, Michael Alfonso, is running for office. ASI contributed to his campaign.
People have drawn this connection. They think there’s some sort of kickback, but ASI was hired to implement some AI-based system, or SMART system, that was going on while the ground stop happened. Are these things connected in any way?
In a word, no. A lot of the conspiracy theories that have come up around this SMART system really came about in this gray space because the messaging from the administration was so bad, and there’s this natural antipathy towards AI. But all that SMART is doing is replacing some of the manual printouts and the routing that happens at the level of air traffic controllers in sort of control towers and facilities.
They’re not actually plugged into the data backbone. They’re not handling any of the radar or the sensitive radio data. They’re just kind of a parallel system operating out in space. Now that being said, it’s because you’ve got the Secretary of Transportation and the Secretary of Homeland Security out there kind of shooting from the hip at their press conference that inspired exactly zero confidence in the messaging that they were giving.
To give him a little bit of credit, Sean Duffy did say a couple of accurate things, but the way that he said them and then literally in the next sentence walked it back and said, “But it could be 10 other things,” does not inspire confidence at all. I think networking is a hard thing to explain to your average person because their eyes may glaze over, and then you have someone who really doesn’t understand it, who’s trying to do the right thing. But in his mind, the main point of that whole press conference was to get to that $30 or $50 billion shakedown of the government. So he’s kind of blowing past the technical details in the first place.
It was just not a great look, and I think that’s why some of this conspiracy theory stuff has started to thrive.
You mentioned the natural antipathy towards AI. Obviously, this ground stop happened in the context of many other outages. Microsoft Outlook went down. United had an issue that brought it down for several hours. There were other outages this week. There was a Chase Bank outage that people overread into thinking it was a banking system outage.
There’s a lot of AI antipathy out there. There’s a lot of, “Well, this must be either AI-powered cyber attacks or everyone’s vibe coding garbage software and pushing into production; it’s crashing.” The top comment on your story is, “I call BS on this. It was AI, but we won’t hear it. They’re using this to beg for money they need after Elon Musk tore air traffic control apart.” Is there an AI element to this story at all?
I would say to that commentary that it gets two out of the three, so that’s pretty good. The AI component, I think, is really more about what happens in three to five years when we fully transition, I’m sure, to this AI-powered system. I think that’s the part where we’re really going to see how good it is.
Right now, air traffic controllers are still using legacy processes to route planes and keep track of everything. And those processes work pretty well. I mean, I’ve talked with hundreds of air traffic controllers over the years. They’re uniformly professional, very skilled, and more importantly, they know the ways that their systems will break down so that when something happens, they can immediately figure out how to keep things going without putting everybody at risk.
When you do eventually move to the SMART AI system, that’s going to be a whole unknown. And I think that’s really kind of where my concern would be: when they turn it on for a larger portion of the air traffic control system, where they say, “Okay, from now on,” I’m just making this up, “Chicago is going to be on the SMART system, and we’ll see what happens.” That’s the day that maybe I would avoid flying for a little while.
I don’t have the data to back this up, but it just feels like flying has gotten more chaotic and dangerous in the second Trump administration than before. There have been more crashes. Is that related to management incompetence? Is that just a string of bad luck?
Chaotic for sure. I think dangerous, probably not. I mean, if you think about the incentives that line up, airlines do not want to have a crash. Passengers don’t want to fly on something dangerous. So with whatever’s going on at the administration level, there’s enough pushback within industry and within the passenger market that nobody is really going to take steps that they think will actively degrade the system.
That being said, I would say you’re 100 percent justified in thinking that you should be less confident in the regulation and operations leadership side today than you would be five years ago. You heard it on Friday night and Saturday morning that the people in charge don’t really care what’s going on, and they just want the optics to look good. Even if you push them on the details, the people who knew what they were doing are really not in place anymore. I think we’ll probably see more of these disruptions over the next couple of years. I don’t think we’ll ever see anything that gets to the point of being materially more unsafe.
In large part, I think the ground stop was probably justified. I mean, I talked to a bunch of controllers who said, “We could have handled it. We could have gone to backup systems, and in 20, 30, 40 minutes, things would’ve been more or less back to normal.” And I believe them. I think they absolutely could have handled some of these outages without causing any real safety risks.
But from a passenger standpoint, you also don’t want to hear, and that’s part of the reason I started the story with that anecdote, “Hey, this is your pilot. We can’t communicate with anybody. We’re going to kind of see how it goes.” That is a very scary thing to hear.
I think that really fuels a lot of it: that any kind of perceived safety risk in aviation just awakens this primordial fear in people. That’s totally justified. And I think it helps keep some of these anti-regulation impulses in check because people know that once a critical mass of people are afraid of flying, you kind of undo an industry that holds the entire United States and, in many respects, the world together.
One of the things that’s really interesting about this dynamic, this whole story, is that if you were to design an air traffic control system from scratch today, it would be vastly more automated, right?
Yes, absolutely.
You would absolutely have an enormous amount of computer control over where the airplanes are. You would build vastly more powerful communication between planes and air traffic control centers in between them, and you would maybe even just let ATC control the planes directly, right?
It’s a closed ecosystem where everyone wants to participate and has aligned incentives. You would just say, “Okay, the robots are going to fly all the planes for us, and they’re going to be perfectly safe.” But it doesn’t seem like there’s a pathway from here to there.
At least not in the United States. I think there are so many counterarguments to some of the good intentions that people have, and frankly, a lot of people are just trying to get in because they want a hand of the government payout pie. I would be very worried if someone said, “Hey, tomorrow we’re going to launch robot planes coming out of Atlanta.”
I do think a potential competing model is what we see in China, where they don’t have some of these competing economic or political gamesmanship kind of counterforces going on, where they are able to build what is essentially a new air traffic control system largely from scratch and with updated technology. And I think we are seeing some of the ways that automation is making that more efficient.
That being said, I would bet you $100 that would never happen in the US, at least not until something materially changes in the way that the US thinks about air travel. I mean, we think about it as a right. We think about it as the primary way that we get from point A to point B other than driving a car for 100 miles, and we think about it as something that everybody should have access to.
So, that means we want it cheap, we want it fast, and we’re kind of able to tolerate some of these large-scale disruptions as long as it doesn’t affect our Thanksgiving plans in a week. I think that also just kind of keeps the system the way it is in ways that maybe are not the case in some other countries that are newer to large-scale aviation.
I like that you made reference to the cliché: you can have it cheap, fast, or good. Now pick two.
Or maybe none.
That’s what you’re saying. In this case, maybe none.
There’s another argument I’ve heard, and this is in Project 2025. You mentioned the industry has a lot of incentives to figure this out, to make it good, to make sure people feel safe. In Project 2025, the idea that we should privatize air traffic control exists.
At the beginning of the second Trump administration, as Elon was slashing air traffic control and the FAA, the notion that this would lead inexorably to privatized air traffic control that could then impose a bunch of this stuff, because the industry would be doing it to itself, was everywhere. I heard about it constantly. That doesn’t seem to have happened. Is there a push, is there an argument to be made for private air traffic control?
We definitely saw that, and I think with Project 2025, that’s been a plank of The Heritage Foundation’s plan for government for more than two decades, if not since the late ‘80s when Reagan fired the air traffic controllers.
I think privatization is a difficult concept for a lot of people to get their head around. But I do think the core idea, which is that you kind of need to separate the FAA as regulator from FAA as air traffic control operator, is fundamentally sound, whether that means moving it into a new government department or some other structural change.
A lot of places around the world do have privatized air traffic control, so Canada, the UK, Germany, or they have a different system entirely. In Brazil, for example, air traffic control is actually controlled by the military.
China is really the only big country that has a similar structure, but when you’re in a one-party system, it doesn’t really function as it does in the US. That is kind of a weird quirk that needs to be solved one way or another.
The quirk is that the regulator also oversees air traffic control.
Correct. But I just don’t know what the right pathway is because there’s also… I mean, you’re seeing it today with some of these contractors who are coming in and essentially scamming the government about everything, and that doesn’t seem to me like a safe way to operate the air traffic control system. How do you compensate for that human/corporate nature that we seem to have here, where you do kind of want to maximize profits even past the point of producing a good product?
The airline industry is not full of shy characters. Ed Bastian, the CEO of Delta, is not a reserved figure. These are big personalities; they run big businesses, they battle with each other, and they spend enormous amounts of money. As you said, they’re the backbone of the global economy in many ways. Why can’t they push for it? Why aren’t they leading the charge?
Ah, so there’s an interesting one. They are leading the charge publicly, while in private not really committing any considerable support to it. So let me put this in the context of aviation security. It costs the US government about $10 to screen for baggage, security, and whatever. But if you look at your airline ticket, your average FAA 9/11 fee, whatever it’s called today, is $1.50. That was because airlines pushed so hard against any kind of thing that would increase their fares that we’re essentially losing money on every security scan.
Now, whether or not security works is a whole other story, but that’s kind of the mentality that they have. It’s great for them to pontificate about what we need, but if you ask the airlines to shoulder some of the burden, they’re going to say, “Well, this is going to blow up our entire economic model.” That’s a difficult thing to swallow.
I mean, in Canada, they just make them pay for the fees. The security fee is about 10 times higher. The fees for their private air traffic control system also pass through to the passengers, but that means that they don’t have your $70 “go somewhere cheap this weekend” kind of fares that we do in the US. So there are trade-offs, and I think the industry isn’t yet willing to part with some of that margin in order to upgrade the air traffic control system. They think it should be a government burden to pay.
If the listener is keeping track of us going backwards, we started with one router that was replaced with a misconfigured setting, and we’ve arrived at how we should split up the FAA and change the fee structure for airlines. That is a long and winding road, but it feels like a very direct connection.
Yeah, absolutely. I mean, these kinds of changes take decades to do. If you’re in that short-term mentality, that one where you’re just trying to make it to the next election or you just need this particular line on the budget, you’re never going to get to a world where we can have a fully functional air traffic system.
Part of the thing is that people do hide behind this complexity to both extract money from the government funds that are available or to just be able to… Case in point, Sec. Duffy, who, when he was a congressman, voted against funding for the FAA. Now six, seven, eight years later, he’s saying, “Oh my gosh, what a terrible idea. Whoever did that in the first place should be banned from the government. By the way, I also need $50 billion more to do it.”
You can kind of see how short-term memory, the political cycle, and everything else line up to make sure that we kind of get the system we deserve in a way.
Let’s say Sec. Duffy was massively competent. He was the single best network design engineer that has ever existed. This is a lot for a cast member of The Real World, but so it goes. What if you gave him $50 billion? If you dumped $50 billion on the FAA today, could it accomplish building a system that was reliable, stable, much more automated, and safer?
I think it could happen in a couple of years, but it’s not just the money that you would need. I think you would need a couple of other things too. Number one, you just have to have the managers who can do it. So you remember Mark Zuckerberg; his whole thing was like, “Let’s get rid of all these middle managers.” But they have a function. And especially when you’re dealing with legacy systems, complex technical installations, and the management of just large networks of people, not just data, but people.
You kind of need those people who are in these glue functions to make sure that, hey, maybe let’s not do something on a Friday afternoon when even a small failure is going to bring down a busy air traffic controller. Maybe let’s have a technician on site who knows what they’re doing and who can oversee the work. Maybe let’s have a plan that says, hey, let’s check the factory settings when you plug in this thing.
It’s not just these giant top-line initiatives of a lot of money. It’s the baseline competence process and the ability to take things slowly when you need to. All of that is missing right now. If you think too hard about it, you can get really frustrated and despair about the future of air travel.
The thing that gives me confidence is that we’ve done it so far, even in these conditions where we’re not having massive numbers of crashes, and that we are willing to pause the system when it makes sense to do so. But you can only go on for so long like that.
I think this is really when we’re coming to a decision point, and at some point the equipment itself, regardless of what the politicians say, regardless of what the budget says, the equipment itself is going to tell us, “This is unsustainable. We can’t go on anymore.” I would rather we do the work to not get to that point if we can, because I don’t want to be the person on the airplane who hears the pilot say, “Oh, we’ve lost communication with the FAA and really have no idea what’s going on.” That’s a very scary and dangerous thing to hear.
We’re publishing this the Monday before Thanksgiving, which is historically one of the busiest air travel weeks of the entire year. Should people feel that the system is reliable right now?
I think we’ll get through Thanksgiving.
[Laughs] That was a long pause, dude.
Well, I was going to say that I’m staying put this Thanksgiving, so make what you will of that. I think the system, airlines, know what’s going on. Now people are on guard for that. And from what we’ve heard from the FAA, they’re not installing any new routers anytime soon. That particular risk is pretty much eliminated.
I would say your safety has never really been at risk, and people are still able to manage these crises when they happen. So I wouldn’t worry about safety. I would say give yourself a couple of extra hours either way.
For example, I flew to Boston over this past weekend. Saturday morning, even though the ground stop had been lifted, we were still delayed over an hour. It was not because of any communications problem, but because so many things had happened to put airplanes and people out of place that they were literally calling flight attendants on their day off to come in and work, and that’s what my aircraft was waiting for.
Recovery will happen. Disruptions can be managed to a certain extent. But I think if we get to another year from now and we’re still paralyzed in this world of, “Well, let’s just spend the time pointing fingers rather than doing anything meaningful,” then I think it really starts to call into question whether we do have the capacity to fix what’s wrong with the air traffic system.
FTI, the network at issue here, is due to be replaced in 2028. Just to wrap it up, what are the markers you’re looking for to see if that process is happening as intended and we are going to wind up with a better, more reliable network?
I think by the middle of next year, we’ll be able to see the pace at which some of these projects are being implemented. And so we’ll know if there’s a problem with implementation that’s going to push out timelines for the next two to three years. I mean, the FAA has gotten really good at overpromising and underdelivering over the years. There was an entirely new air traffic control system that was supposed to be put into place between 2000 and today that just died because of a lack of political will.
One of the good things that the FAA has done is actually created a website tracking a lot of these projects. And if you’re nerdy like me, you can go on there and see, okay, this week they put a router in there in Arkansas or wherever. And so that’s really going to tell us and give us the clarity that we’ve lacked over the last couple of years: the pace of some of these projects. And to give them credit, it is creating some accountability where there wasn’t really any.
But I think it will really be monitoring over the next six to nine months to see whether this has fundamentally changed the FAA’s appetite for taking risks that will get to a better system down the road, but that may cause disruption today. And that’s really what I’d be worried about: that it’s easy and it’s practically agency policy at this point to kick the can down the road. And so if this has created so much blowback that the FAA is now completely risk-averse, there may be a world where they don’t accomplish some of these things until the 2030s.
There is going to be a massively democratic Congress in power in just a few months here. All they can really do is oversight. I don’t think you want members of Congress doing the router upgrades.
Yeah.
They can do oversight. Do you think that oversight will lead to any meaningful change here? Or do we just have to wait until Sean Duffy and his band of merry managers are out?
The one thing that I would like to see from the incoming Congress is a lot of the restoration of the jobs that were cut by DOGE back in 2025. These are the people who should be doing this, who should be overseeing it. I don’t care what the Secretary of Transportation or Congress is or isn’t doing, but you do need these managers in place.
I think there’s a loss of those 400 people in the DOGE cuts, plus another thousand to 1500 retirements or just people quitting the FAA. You really want those people back because this is such a unique structure, and it’s such a unique kind of amalgam of partially obsolete, partially new technology. It’s the only thing like it in the world that I’m aware of. There’s only a certain number of people who even know enough to deal with it. You need those people in place before you can make those changes.
So I think if Congress is willing to make changes to bring those people back, to restore those jobs, and protect the funding for those jobs from the next random idea that someone has, then I would say that’s a good change. If it’s just more theatrics and pushing budget lines from one section to another, that would give me less confidence.
Darryl, this has been great. I feel like I could talk to you about this all day and all night. The book is called Fatal Abstraction: Why the Managerial Class Loses Control of Software. I feel like the ideas that we’ve talked about here are directly related to ideas in your book.
Yeah, and there’s a whole chapter on org charts in there, just for Decoder listeners.
Perfect Decoder bait. We’re going to definitely have to have you back very soon. Thank you so much for being on Decoder.
All right. Thanks, Nilay.
Questions or comments? Hit us up at decoder@theverge.com. We really do read every email!
Decoder with Nilay Patel
A podcast from The Verge about big ideas and other problems.

Trump endorses Jensen Huang’s return to Nvidia, says US has no evidence of wrongdoing
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
15:04:01 UTC
00:04:01 KST

President Trump praised Nvidia’s decision on Monday to reinstate founder Jensen Huang as the company’s CEO and president, saying on his social network Truth Social that “the United States Government has no reason to believe that Jensen’s return to NVIDIA poses any risk to National Security.”
The Nvidia board’s statement had also referred to consultations with “senior levels of the United States government” as informing its decision. It said that, based on that input, Mr. Huang’s reinstatement was deemed not a national security risk.
In his comments on Monday, Trump claimed that U.S. Attorney General [Todd Blanche] had found no evidence that Mr. Huang had engaged in unlawful conduct. It’s an unusual statement for a U.S. president to make, as the attorney general typically conducts those investigations independently. Notably, Attorney General Blanche has not yet made a formal statement on the case.
There’s also indication that President Trump may have been aware of the decision before the announcement by the Nvidia board. Speaking to a PBS journalist over the weekend, he said of Huang “he’s back where he should be and we see he did absolutely nothing wrong.” Asked to clarify about whether he meant Huang would be reinstated, Trump said, “I hope so, I hope so.”
Huang was accused of having multiple extramarital relationships, including one with a direct report at Nvidia and another unnamed employee. However, the affair of most concern was the one with the alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang (no relation). Ms. Huang was an executive at a Singapore-based company called Megaspeed, which had purchased billions in Nvidia chips over the past couple of years. This relationship had raised national security concerns about whether the CEO ties to Ms. Huang could have compromised sensitive information or facilitated China’s access to advanced U.S. AI chips, which are subject to export restrictions.
Trump said last week that he believed this relationship didn’t threaten the U.S.’s national security, and instead said that no one had proven she’s a spy. “She may not be a spy,” the president said, adding that “China plays games with us, and we play games with China.”
Trump’s full statement on Huang’s return, released on Monday, is below.
The NVIDIA Board of Directors has made the correct and proper decision to return Jensen Huang to the position of CEO. The Country needs Jensen’s leadership at NVIDIA in order to ensure the Success and Prosperity of not only the Company, but the American Economy. The United States Government has no reason to believe that Jensen’s return to NVIDIA poses any risk to National Security. The Attorney General of the United States has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct. On a personal note, I have gotten to know Jensen, and he is a Great Patriot and National Treasure. Separately, NVIDIA should continue its thorough internal investigation of the Company’s compliance with U.S. export controls in order to ensure that it is fulfilling its legal obligations and Patriotic Duty to protect U.S. National Security. Congratulations to Jensen, the NVIDIA Board, to the People of the United States — and, indeed, the World! Thank you for your attention to this matter. President DONALD J. TRUMP

Apple’s AirPods Pro 3 are a whopping $100 off ahead of Black Friday
At $100 off, they’re the same price as the less impressive AirPods 5.
It’s a weird situation that, right now, the feature-packed AirPods Pro 3 are the same price as the newer (but less impressive) AirPods 5 that include the wireless charging case. The Pro 3 deliver considerably more bang for your buck.
This model is Apple’s best-sounding, longest-lasting AirPods, and they ship with five silicone ear tips. The Verge’s favorite holiday gifts that cost $50 or less
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
14:41:34 UTC
23:41:34 KST

You’ve got $50 to spend on a friend or loved one this shopping season, and you’re not sure what to spend it on. First off, that’s actually really generous of you. While this price tier can sometimes be challenging to shop for — caught between affordable accessories and big-ticket items — there’s plenty of gold to be mined for clever shoppers. And we’re here to help.
The Verge’s favorite holiday gifts that cost $50 or less
If you’re on a budget but still looking for a thoughtful gift, we’ve compiled some picks from Verge staff.
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
This guide includes a wide range of cool finds at $50 or less, from mainstream hits like KPop Demon Hunters on Blu-ray and popular tech like the DJI Mic Mini to more eccentric picks like VidaBay’s E Ink fridge magnets and the Werewolf USB-C power adapter. We think you’ll be surprised how far your $50 can stretch.
If someone on your list collects Game Boy, Game Boy Color, or Game Boy Advance cartridges, the GB Operator might be the perfect gift. The slick, transparent USB-C device makes it easy to play and manage classic titles on a macOS or Windows machine via emulation, with or without achievements.
OhSnap Grip Stand
Pricey yet endlessly useful, the Grip Stand is the ultimate phone accessory. The ultra-thin magnetic attachment is a better gift than simpler PopSocket-style holders, especially since it can serve as an ad hoc kickstand. That makes it convenient for both video calls and YouTube.
Wacaco Minipresso GR2
Espresso in the woods? Yes, please. The GR2’s magical mechanics make it possible, letting you turn 12 ounces of grounds into espresso without power. Its carrying case houses all the accessories needed to get that sweet shot of bean, making real coffee available virtually anywhere.
New Zealand artist Aldous Harding has another eccentric album on her hands with this year’s Train on the Island. The slow-burning LP is gorgeously recorded and delightfully weird, and it feels tailor-made for vinyl, making it an easy pick for Harding fans (or anyone who loves quirky indie jams).
Zepath USB-C batteries
Rechargeable AA batteries are great for the environment and your budget, but not all models are created equally, so we did our own testing. With top-tier capacity, breezy usability, and a compact USB-C charging case, Zepath’s batteries are the best we’ve tried.
Give the gift of security with this USB-C smart camera, which offers a wide field of view, night vision, microSD storage, and free Wi-Fi livestreaming. The optional subscription adds cloud storage and smart object detection, letting users upgrade as needed.
Moft Invisible Laptop Stand
Moft’s origami-inspired “invisible” stand provides a sleek way to prop up your tablet or laptop (up to 14 inches) and to keep your working space looking more organized. The design has features like ventilation gaps and firm grips to work on a variety of surfaces and devices.
This affordable dongle kit provides an almost magical way to power nearly any DC device from a USB-C charger or portable battery. Program it from a mobile app, connect it to your old gear, then everything from a PC monitor to an original Game Boy can accept USB-C charging.
Hoto 12-in-1 electric screwdriver
Hoto’s electric screwdriver is a thoughtful gift for the builder in your life. The compact, easy-to-use tool includes 12 S2 steel bits, a circular LED to illuminate your project, and a soft, yet grippy handle. Three torque settings allow for a variety of jobs, and a long-lasting battery (with USB-C charging) keeps the driver going for hours.
Big Walk
It can be hard to keep in touch with friends, but Big Walk might be the game that gets the gang back together. The cooperative multiplayer title drops players into a wacky world as spherical humanoids who must solve puzzles by chatting and teaming up. You’ll want at least two copies, and sessions support up to 12 players.
This handy Japanese organizer is a thoughtful gift for folks who prefer a physical way to keep track of everything, from upcoming events and projects to random thoughts and creative ideas. It comes in multiple sizes and can be customized with artistic covers in a variety of colors and designs. Take your pick.
SwitchBot Bot button pusher
SwitchBot’s futuristic block, which is designed to activate physical switches from afar, is as useful as it is eccentric. Also available in a rechargeable configuration, the app-controllable button pusher clips onto almost anything with a switch and lasts up to 600 days on a single 3V CR2 battery.
Everyone needs a basic toolkit, and Ron Swanson would agree. I’m not sure he’d go for the micro-sized set from iFixit, but we can back it. It’s an excellent entry point for folks living in small homes or apartments. Headlined by a 32-bit screwdriver set, it includes two pairs of tweezers, dual spudgers, and more.
EasySMX makes our favorite Nintendo Switch 2 controllers, and the S10 Lite adds a major upgrade: native system functionality. That makes it simpler to connect and customize its back paddles than its rivals. The Lite skips a few features, like amiibo support and high-quality rumble, but otherwise provides a solid value for those on a budget.
DJI Mic Mini
As any experienced content creator will tell you, capturing good audio is more than half the battle. DJI’s Mic Mini gives aspiring creators a leg up with a sleek mic and transmitter set, enabling up to 10 hours of recording time, plus powerful noise canceling to capture the narrative, not the unwanted noise.
This collapsible stand converts your Steam Deck or Switch 2 into a desktop-like experience. Wherever you want to post up with your handheld, the Desk Stand is a high-quality aluminum stand that’s a great buy. It includes cable guides and works with Mechanism’s accessories for attaching battery banks and more.
These self-looping USB-C cables from SuperCalla might be just what your gift recipient needs. Instead of flat cables that often become unwieldy, these are capped with slim magnetic threads that stick together in a tight coil, making cable organization a breeze. They’re a bit pricey, but worth it.
KPop Demon Hunters (4K Blu-ray)
So what if you’ve heard “Golden” more times than you can count? Netflix’s massive animated hit is a lot easier to gift now that it’s officially available on 4K Blu-ray disc. The story about three pop stars who band together to save humanity from a demonic boy band is an instant classic, with fantastic music and striking animation.
These nifty fridge magnets might revolutionize grandma’s refrigerator. Designed to look like Polaroid pictures, they each feature an E-Ink screen and NFC communication, letting owners easily upload new photos to the frame by simply tapping their phones on them. It feels like magic, but it’s just a really cool application of some modern tech.
Cast iced tea glass
Sure. You could put iced tea in them, but no one’s stopping you from sipping pulpy OJ or rich chocolate milk from Kinto’s stylish glassware, which was crafted with an “uninterfering design” in mind. Needless to say, a full set makes a lovely addition to any minimalist’s ensemble.
Let your loved ones sling their new Apple gear safely and in style with these official, well-made Apple Wrist Straps. Compatible with AirPods Pro 2 and iPhone 17 cases or newer, they’re a practical way to carry phones and earbuds when pockets won’t do.
LeapFrog My First Gamer
This durable handheld system for preschoolers and up is the perfect gift for your favorite budding gamer. With an ABC keyboard, built-in games like go-kart driving, and educational ones to challenge their memory, it’s a great tool to stimulate young minds and keep them occupied without letting them loose in Roblox.
While Zepath’s batteries are the top AA rechargeables we’ve tested, Paleblues are a convenient alternative for those after USB-C charging on each cell instead of in a case. They come with a four-headed cable to charge a four-pack from a single USB-C port.
Knockaround Premiums sunglasses
For the person on your list whose hatred for smart glasses runs deep, Knockaround’s hip, tech-free sunglasses should hit the spot. They’re available in a variety of fun styles and offer lightweight frames and polarized lenses, with both UVA and UVB shielding for attractive eye protection.
D&D Stranger Things: Welcome to the Hellfire Club
RIP, Eddie Munson. This radical D&D set celebrates the classic Stranger Things character and his policy of accepting nerds from all walks of life. It’s a fun way to remember the long-running Netflix series and a solid gift for any D&D fan.
Bring Nintendo’s popular Mario Kart racing game to life with this collectible RC car. Beyond the kart, the package includes a jewel display case, a controller and batteries, and can be accompanied by rivals like Toad, Luigi, Peach, and Yoshi. Race on!
Ikea Iskarna
For your next Secret Santa gift swap at work or in your friend group, we submit this zany head-shaped lamp from Ikea. Its color-changing profile looks cool, so long as you don’t mind having a head as decor. Beyond that, it provides a useful way to store items like hats and sunglasses.
Saiji nonslip leather lap desk
In the age of remote work, you can get the job done from just about anywhere at home. With this clever laptop desk, your favorite worker can easily log on from bed or on the couch without having to awkwardly prop their machine on a throw pillow. It offers adjustable legs, a foldable design, and a nonslip surface.
Let your favorite gamer turn their phone into a Game Boy, of sorts. 8BitDo’s controller pad emulates Nintendo’s classic handheld and slides directly into your phone’s USB-C port, perfect for emulation. It offers a soft backpad that won’t scratch your screen, and its hinged connector lets it detach with ease.

Nvidia reverses course and brings Jensen Huang back as CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
14:21:59 UTC
23:21:59 KST

In a stunning reversal, Nvidia’s board said on Monday that it had reinstated Jensen Huang as the company’s CEO and president, less than two weeks after he resigned following his admission of at least two extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman whom the U.S. government suspects to be a Chinese spy.
The decision marks an extraordinary comeback for one of the tech industry’s most influential executives, whose abrupt departure raised questions about national security and the future of the world’s most valuable chipmaker. Notably, the U.S. government itself played a role in his return: Nvidia’s board said it consulted senior government officials before determining that Huang posed no national security risk.
Nvidia shares, which had already shot up in premarket trading, opened 30% higher. Nvidia shares were priced at $159.95 a share, up 27%, at 9:51 a.m. ET.
In the days since Huang resigned, President Donald Trump has repeatedly expressed his support for the ousted executive, who is now back at the helm of the AI chip giant. Trump said in a post on Truth Social that the board “made the correct and proper decision to return” Huang to the position, noting that the U.S. government has no reason to believe that Nvidia poses any risk to national security.
Trump also said the U.S. attorney general confirmed that the U.S. government doesn’t have any evidence that Huang engaged in any illegal activity.
A long list of high-profile tech executives were pushing the board — in social media posts and publicly aired interviews — to bring Huang back, including SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk, Replit CEO Amjad Masad, Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, Atoms founder Travis Kalanick, and Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan — all of whom signed a petition pushing for reinstatement.
The company said the board’s unanimous decision followed a series of discussions and meetings with Huang, after which they were “satisfied with respect to Mr. Huang’s candor and ability to lead Nvidia.”
Just a few days ago, the board had seemed immovable in its choice to push Huang out. During the company’s third-quarter earnings call, Nvidia board chair Harvey C. Jones said they didn’t intend to reinstate Huang. Nvidia had even retained executive search firm Heidrick & Struggles to find a permanent replacement.
Jones, along with the rest of the board, now has a different view. In a prepared statement, Jones said the board believes Huang is the right person to lead Nvidia, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of its stockholders, employees, partners, and customers.
“We have every confidence in Jensen and look forward to working with him as he resumes his leadership of the company,” Jones said. “We are grateful to Colette Kress for her outstanding service as interim CEO, in addition to her continuing responsibilities as Nvidia’s Chief Financial Officer.”
In a statement, Huang said he was honored to return to his role as the head of the company he co-founded in 1993.
“I take this responsibility with the greatest humility and renewed commitment,” Huang said. “I want to thank the Nvidia Board for its confidence and deliberation through this process. I am especially grateful to Harvey Jones for his leadership as Chair, and to Colette Kress and our executive staff for leading Nvidia during this period. I am also deeply thankful to our employees, shareholders, leaders across the industry and partners around the world for their extraordinary support. Above all, I am grateful for the love and support of my family. I can’t wait to get back to work.”

Building a wireless network adapter for the Super Nintendo
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
14:16:37 UTC
23:16:37 KST

00 Why?
I always come back to the Super Nintendo (SNES). It was my first console and it’s still my most potent source of nostalgia. It inspired my early interest in electronics as a teenager, designing circuits for mods and flash carts that I hoped to build one day. I followed the launch of Hackaday, Adafruit, and the progress of bunnie Huang’s Chumby and knew exactly what I wanted to do.
I dropped out of my final years of high school to study hands-on electronics at college, and continued on to university. Then, finally equipped with those skills I’d aspired to, I went off-track and spent the next fifteen years working in data, ML and software. A lot has changed in that time but I’m back now, picking up exactly where I left off.
The Super Network Adapter is an exercise in ‘16-bit futurism’, it aims to be a faithful peripheral to the original SNES hardware and what we imagined the future might be back then: a wireless link to the net, sync’ing saves across devices and an uplink to AI.
01 Prototyping
The SNES never had a dedicated network port. The closest it came was the Satellaview, a modem peripheral connected to a hidden expansion port that could receive game broadcasts via satellite. There was also the XBAND, a modem that plugged into the cartridge slot and supported pass-through patching of specific games for online play. Reproducing the hardware of either of these today would be challenging, however most peripherals for the SNES took a much simpler route, they used one of the controller ports.
Controller Serial Link
      The SNES controller ports are small synchronous serial buses that a game can control, setting and reading individual lines and driving the clock. These ports have seven lines but in a typical controller cable, only five tend to be present, with the wires for D1 and IOBit left out. To make sourcing parts for this project easier, my design also only relies on the remaining five wires.
      
 ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╮
 │ ┌──────────────────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────────╮ │
 │ │  ●       ●       ●       ●   │  │   ●       ●       ●   │ │
 │ └──────────────────────────────┘  └───────────────────────╯ │
 └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╯
      1       2       3       4          5       6       7
      5v     CLK    Latch     D0         D1    IOBit    GND
    
    First I needed a protocol for talking over this serial link, which I iterated with Mesen and a virtual network adapter written in Lua simulating the controller port link. This protocol has the SNES writing to the Latch line and reading from the adapter on D0, one bit at a time (‘bit-banging’). Even with some optimisation (unrolling loops and ignoring mid-stream replies) this approach tops out at about 30 KB/s, but that’s enough for the ’90s.
    
Breadboard Version
The first physical prototype was built using a Pico 2 W dev board. The additional circuitry needed was very simple, just some pull-up resistors for CLK and Latch. The board’s RP2350A can handle the console’s 5v logic on its pins directly, and it has two cores: one can maintain the console link, while the other maintains the Wi-Fi network link, without juggling interrupts.
Controller port pinout
The prototype runs from USB, with the console’s 5v only feeding the pull-ups. I did not connect the SNES 5v to the Pico’s VBUS or VSYS while USB is plugged in. The Pico was powered on before the console so the RP2350’s pins were ready to handle 5v. The PCB design later can run on either supply.
02 Save sync
Adding Cloud Save or ‘Save Sync’ seemed the most practical use for a Wi-Fi connection. I often go back to the SNES and start a new game, but if I could continue playing that save file in RetroArch on my phone and then back to the SNES, it would fit into my life much more easily.
      Game save data is stored in battery-backed SRAM and in order for a game to send that through my adapter, I needed some of my own code to run. I already have a programmable flash cart for the SNES, so I wrote a utility to patch ROMs and inject this ‘save sync’ code into the reset vector. At power-on and whenever the console is reset, it syncs the save data through the adapter to a WebDAV server as saves/<core>/<name>.srm (the same path RetroArch uses) so the console save can be continued on the phone, and vice versa.
    
Joining Wi-Fi
First though, the adapter needs to join the local Wi-Fi network. I wanted an era-appropriate UI that can be run from a setup ROM (fig.2), which has you pick a network and type its password with an on-screen keyboard, one letter at a time. That felt most ‘authentic’ but I also made a second iteration that shows the typical QR code on the TV for setup via mobile phone.
The second version has the adapter set up a temporary network. Scanning the QR code with a phone joins the network and it then does the usual captive portal flow to open the adapter’s setup page, where the network and WebDAV auth details can be input in a much more user-friendly way.
Pushing and Pulling Saves
An important detail to get right was checking whether the save on the cartridge was newest, or whether to download a newer one from WebDAV. RetroArch keeps a manifest file with a hash of every save, so the adapter updates that too, comparing hashes to decide whether the cartridge or the server has the newest save. If both sides have changed, nothing is overwritten and the cartridge’s save goes to a conflicts folder to play it safe.
SRAM saves are small but this sync still has to run whenever a game starts, potentially introducing a delay. To avoid this, the save is split into chunks that each have their own checksum, so only the chunks that have changed are sent over the controller port. In practice this makes the save sync delay imperceptible, and only when an existing save is pulled by the SNES for the first time is there a 1-2s load screen.
03 AI dialogue
Naturally in 2026, an internet connection also means a connection to LLMs. The dialogue in SNES-era games tends to be fixed and unresponsive. In A Link to the Past, the villagers don’t react when you smash their pots (unlike in subsequent Zelda games). With the network adapter though, we can hook the game’s dialogue text renderer to swap out the original text for an in-character line generated by an LLM.
In order to prompt for dialogue, the LLM needs to be passed the game context, such as the character being interacted with and recent events. Since Link to the Past has been fully disassembled, it was possible to patch it to accumulate this state in RAM and push it out over the controller serial to the adapter and on to an LLM (via a proxy I ran on a local machine that holds the API key).
I tried a few different models and settled on Claude Opus 5.5, as cheaper models would break character or leak spoilers despite being prompted not to. I used LLM as a Judge evals to measure the proportion of responses that fit the context - Haiku scored 66%, Sonnet 90% and Opus 98%. The challenge was the time to first token of the LLM, for Haiku it was about 0.8 s but for Opus it averaged 2.2 s.
To avoid game-breaking delays from the LLM response times, I switched to speculatively generating the dialogue when Link approaches an NPC, storing the responses in a cache and invalidating them as the context updates. The delay from using a larger model was hidden and most NPC interactions had the response ready. This is a great way to burn tokens so probably not something to do for the full game duration!
04 Hardware
When designing the PCB version of the adapter (fig.0) I replicated the same architecture as the prototype, an RP2350A microcontroller that handles the 5v lines and maintains the serial link, with an ESP32-C3 Wi-Fi coprocessor that handles the Wi-Fi connection. These communicate with each other over UART, allowing each link to be maintained by a dedicated core.
┌─────────┐ port 2 ┌─────────┐ uart ┌──────────┐ wi-fi ┌────────┐
│ SNES    │──────▸ │ RP2350A │────▸ │ ESP32-C3 │~~~~~▸ │ cloud  │
│ 65816   │◂────── │ PIO     │◂──── │ TCP/TLS  │◂~~~~~ │ WebDAV │
└─────────┘        └─────────┘      └──────────┘       └────────┘
  3.58 MHz           150 MHz          160 MHz
      A PIO state machine on the RP2350 watches clock and latch, queueing the data and filtering out glitches shorter than 100 ns. This frees up the cores to prepare data to be sent over D0 while also talking to the C3.
    
The USB socket was included not just for flashing the board but also to support debugging it while it is powered from the SNES. This means the board needs to safely support taking a 5v supply from either the controller port or USB, and avoid backfeeding if both are connected. This is done with a LM66200 dual ideal diode. Supply can be taken from either source and we avoid the voltage drop that would come with using actual diodes. I’d seen some brownouts in tests on the breadboard, so designed the PCB with plenty of headroom for the LDO regulator even if the supply rail sags on a Wi-Fi burst.
While prototyping I also had issues when using series resistors on the link lines. This may be related to erratum RP2350-E9 in the A2 revision (stepping) of the RP2350A on my Pico 2 W. In the assembled PCB order I switched these to 0 Ω links, as they’re just for protection and I wasn’t sure which stepping the PCB would be assembled with. The boards turned out to have the fixed A4 stepping, so real resistors can go in next time.
05 Enclosure
The case is just a tray and a lid that sandwich the PCB, though I did want it to nod toward an era-appropriate design language. It was generated in code (build123d) using the PCB’s STEP files from KiCad, so that the design could be tested within a single 3D model.
The two halves join around the cable and the USB socket, and there are M2 pillars that align with the drilled holes in the PCB to hold everything together.
Since the PCB has a status LED, this design has a light pipe that channels that light up to become the dot of the etched Wi-Fi icon. It’s not the prettiest of designs, but I thought that was cute.
I’ve ordered a batch of these enclosures to be 3D printed and will update with actual photos once they arrive and the adapter is fully assembled. I’m also considering how to fabricate a single assembly that can insert directly into the controller port. Reach out at [email protected] if you’re interested!
06 Build log
- 65816 toolchain, headless emulator tests
- Breadboard link to port 2 working
- First save uploaded from the console
- PCB designed and ordered from JLCPCB
- PCBs arrive and work perfectly!
- Ordered 3D printed enclosures
- Awaiting delivery of enclosures

====================================================================================================

Building a wireless network adapter for the Super Nintendo
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
14:16:37 UTC
23:16:37 KST

00 Why?
I always come back to the Super Nintendo (SNES). It was my first console and it’s still my most potent source of nostalgia. It inspired my early interest in electronics as a teenager, designing circuits for mods and flash carts that I hoped to build one day. I followed the launch of Hackaday, Adafruit, and the progress of bunnie Huang’s Chumby and knew exactly what I wanted to do.
I dropped out of my final years of high school to study hands-on electronics at college, and continued on to university. Then, finally equipped with those skills I’d aspired to, I went off-track and spent the next fifteen years working in data, ML and software. A lot has changed in that time but I’m back now, picking up exactly where I left off.
The Super Network Adapter is an exercise in ‘16-bit futurism’, it aims to be a faithful peripheral to the original SNES hardware and what we imagined the future might be back then: a wireless link to the net, sync’ing saves across devices and an uplink to AI.
01 Prototyping
The SNES never had a dedicated network port. The closest it came was the Satellaview, a modem peripheral connected to a hidden expansion port that could receive game broadcasts via satellite. There was also the XBAND, a modem that plugged into the cartridge slot and supported pass-through patching of specific games for online play. Reproducing the hardware of either of these today would be challenging, however most peripherals for the SNES took a much simpler route, they used one of the controller ports.
Controller Serial Link
      The SNES controller ports are small synchronous serial buses that a game can control, setting and reading individual lines and driving the clock. These ports have seven lines but in a typical controller cable, only five tend to be present, with the wires for D1 and IOBit left out. To make sourcing parts for this project easier, my design also only relies on the remaining five wires.
      
 ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╮
 │ ┌──────────────────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────────╮ │
 │ │  ●       ●       ●       ●   │  │   ●       ●       ●   │ │
 │ └──────────────────────────────┘  └───────────────────────╯ │
 └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╯
      1       2       3       4          5       6       7
      5v     CLK    Latch     D0         D1    IOBit    GND
    
    First I needed a protocol for talking over this serial link, which I iterated with Mesen and a virtual network adapter written in Lua simulating the controller port link. This protocol has the SNES writing to the Latch line and reading from the adapter on D0, one bit at a time (‘bit-banging’). Even with some optimisation (unrolling loops and ignoring mid-stream replies) this approach tops out at about 30 KB/s, but that’s enough for the ’90s.
    
Breadboard Version
The first physical prototype was built using a Pico 2 W dev board. The additional circuitry needed was very simple, just some pull-up resistors for CLK and Latch. The board’s RP2350A can handle the console’s 5v logic on its pins directly, and it has two cores: one can maintain the console link, while the other maintains the Wi-Fi network link, without juggling interrupts.
Controller port pinout
The prototype runs from USB, with the console’s 5v only feeding the pull-ups. I did not connect the SNES 5v to the Pico’s VBUS or VSYS while USB is plugged in. The Pico was powered on before the console so the RP2350’s pins were ready to handle 5v. The PCB design later can run on either supply.
02 Save sync
Adding Cloud Save or ‘Save Sync’ seemed the most practical use for a Wi-Fi connection. I often go back to the SNES and start a new game, but if I could continue playing that save file in RetroArch on my phone and then back to the SNES, it would fit into my life much more easily.
      Game save data is stored in battery-backed SRAM and in order for a game to send that through my adapter, I needed some of my own code to run. I already have a programmable flash cart for the SNES, so I wrote a utility to patch ROMs and inject this ‘save sync’ code into the reset vector. At power-on and whenever the console is reset, it syncs the save data through the adapter to a WebDAV server as saves/<core>/<name>.srm (the same path RetroArch uses) so the console save can be continued on the phone, and vice versa.
    
Joining Wi-Fi
First though, the adapter needs to join the local Wi-Fi network. I wanted an era-appropriate UI that can be run from a setup ROM (fig.2), which has you pick a network and type its password with an on-screen keyboard, one letter at a time. That felt most ‘authentic’ but I also made a second iteration that shows the typical QR code on the TV for setup via mobile phone.
The second version has the adapter set up a temporary network. Scanning the QR code with a phone joins the network and it then does the usual captive portal flow to open the adapter’s setup page, where the network and WebDAV auth details can be input in a much more user-friendly way.
Pushing and Pulling Saves
An important detail to get right was checking whether the save on the cartridge was newest, or whether to download a newer one from WebDAV. RetroArch keeps a manifest file with a hash of every save, so the adapter updates that too, comparing hashes to decide whether the cartridge or the server has the newest save. If both sides have changed, nothing is overwritten and the cartridge’s save goes to a conflicts folder to play it safe.
SRAM saves are small but this sync still has to run whenever a game starts, potentially introducing a delay. To avoid this, the save is split into chunks that each have their own checksum, so only the chunks that have changed are sent over the controller port. In practice this makes the save sync delay imperceptible, and only when an existing save is pulled by the SNES for the first time is there a 1-2s load screen.
03 AI dialogue
Naturally in 2026, an internet connection also means a connection to LLMs. The dialogue in SNES-era games tends to be fixed and unresponsive. In A Link to the Past, the villagers don’t react when you smash their pots (unlike in subsequent Zelda games). In order to prompt for dialogue, the LLM needs to be passed the game context, such as the character being interacted with and recent events. The challenge was the time to first token of the LLM, for Haiku it was about 0.8 s but for Opus it averaged 2.2 s.
To avoid game-breaking delays from the LLM response times, I switched to speculatively generating the dialogue when Link approaches an NPC, storing the responses in a cache and invalidating them as the context updates. The delay from using a larger model was hidden and most NPC interactions had the response ready. This is a great way to burn tokens so probably not something to do for the full game duration!

When designing the PCB version of the adapter (fig.0) I replicated the same architecture as the prototype, an RP2350A microcontroller that handles the 5v lines and maintains the serial link, with an ESP32-C3 Wi-Fi coprocessor that handles the Wi-Fi connection. These communicate with each other over UART, allowing each link to be maintained by a dedicated core.

│ SNES    │──────▸ │ RP2350A │────▸ │ ESP32-C3 │~~~~~▸ │ cloud  │
│ 65816   │◂────── │ PIO     │◂──── │ TCP/TLS  │◂~~~~~ │ WebDAV │
